IMO & International Instruments (10)
T01-Q01 · Describe the structure of IMO. Specify the responsibilities and work done by the Assembly, the Council and various main committees. What role do the sub-committees perform in supporting the main committees? Define a convention. Explain the differences in international instruments such as Convention, Protocol, Code and Resolution, with an example of each.
1. Organizational structure and allocation of responsibility Assembly — all Member States; policy, budget, Council election Council — executive supervision; currently 40 States Five main committees — MSC • MEPC • LEG • TC • FAL Seven technical sub-committees support MSC and/or MEPC Secretariat supports the organs throughout this structure Assembly - policy and overall governance. All Member States participate. It normally meets once every two years, approves the work programme and budget, determines financial arrangements and elects the Council. It considers the work of the Organization and adopts resolutions within its competence. This ensures that IMO's priorities and resources are set collectively by governments. Article 15 of the IMO Convention sets out its functions. [1,2] Council - executive supervision. The Council supervises IMO's work under the Assembly, coordinates organs, examines programme and budget proposals, and submits reports and recommendations. It appoints the Secretary-General subject to Assembly approval. Between Assembly sessions it carries out the Assembly's functions except the reserved function under Article 15(j) concerning recommendations to governments on maritime safety and pollution prevention. [1,2] Current Council: 40 States, elected for two-year terms. Category (a) has 10 States with the largest interest in providing international shipping services; category (b), 10 with the largest interest in international seaborne trade; category (c), 20 with special maritime interests, ensuring geographic representation. India is in category (b) for 2026-2027. The 2021 constitutional amendments provide for a 52-member Council and four-year terms, but the current structure published by IMO remains 40 and two years. Write the operative arrangement, then identify the adopted expansion separately. [2,3] 2. The five main committees Committee Principal responsibility Examination example MSC - Maritime Safety Technical safety and security of ships: SOLAS, STCW, mandatory safety Committee construction, equipment, navigation, codes, casualty-investigation dangerous cargoes, training and safety-related standards. manning. Exercises treaty amendment powers within its remit. MEPC - Marine Prevention and control of ship-source pollution, MARPOL amendments, ballast-water Environment Protection including air pollution, energy efficiency and and anti-fouling work. Committee GHG measures. LEG - Legal Committee Maritime legal issues, especially liability, CLC, LLMC, wreck removal and fair compensation and related legal instruments. treatment of seafarers. TC - Technical Helps States build the administrative, legal and Training officials, institutional Cooperation Committee technical capability needed to implement IMO assistance and implementation instruments. projects. FAL - Facilitation Simplifies arrival, stay and departure FAL Convention, maritime single Committee formalities for ships, cargo and persons, windows and harmonized electronic balancing efficiency with necessary controls. data. Important distinction: IMO membership and participation in a treaty amendment vote are different questions. Treaty Parties that are not IMO Members may participate as provided in that treaty. The treaty's voting clause, rather than ordinary committee attendance alone, determines who votes on its amendments. [1,2,35] 3. Role of the seven technical sub-committees Sub-committees provide specialist analysis, assess experience, develop draft regulations and interpretations, and prepare recommendations for MSC and/or MEPC. Working and correspondence groups allow detailed technical work between plenary sessions. They support decisions; a draft emerging from a sub-committee is not automatically an adopted mandatory requirement. [2] Sub-committee Main technical contribution HTW - Human Element, Competence, training, certification, watchkeeping and human-element matters. Training and Watchkeeping III - Implementation of IMO Implementation, survey and certification, PSC, casualty analysis and lessons Instruments from State audits. NCSR - Navigation, Navigation systems, radiocommunications, GMDSS, routeing and SAR. Communications and Search and Rescue Sub-committee Main technical contribution PPR - Pollution Prevention and Pollution prevention technology, harmful substances and response matters. Response SDC - Ship Design and Structural design, stability, load lines and construction issues. Construction SSE - Ship Systems and Fire protection, life-saving appliances and shipboard systems/equipment. Equipment CCC - Carriage of Cargoes and Packaged dangerous goods, solid bulk cargoes, containers and relevant Containers cargo/fuel codes. The Secretariat, led by the Secretary-General, supplies administrative, legal, technical, translation and conference support. It maintains records and treaty notifications and supports the organs; it does not replace governments as the decision-makers. [1,2] 4. Convention, protocol, code and resolution Instrument Meaning and legal effect Example and why it matters Convention A treaty governed by international law, setting SOLAS 1974: broad safety agreed obligations for States bound by it. obligations, technical regulations Binding force depends on consent and and certification requirements. entry-into-force provisions. Protocol A treaty instrument associated with an existing 1997 MARPOL Protocol: convention, which may amend, supplement or introduced Annex VI on air pollution. substantially revise it. It has its own Becoming party to the parent treaty participation and entry-into-force provisions. does not by itself establish participation in every later protocol. Code A systematic body of detailed requirements or ISM Code, mandatory through recommendations. Its title alone does not SOLAS IX; STCW Code Part A establish whether it is mandatory. mandatory and Part B guidance. Always identify the provision giving binding effect. Resolution The formal record of a decision adopted by an MSC.287(87) adopts GBS; IMO organ. It may adopt amendments, a code, MEPC.377(80) adopts the 2023 guidelines or a policy. Its effect depends on its GHG Strategy. A policy resolution content and legal basis. and a resolution adopting mandatory treaty amendments have different effects. How to read a resolution number: MSC.287(87) identifies an MSC resolution, sequential number 287, adopted at its 87th session. The number helps locate the original text; the adoption date, acceptance date and entry-into-force date must still be checked separately. 5. How the structure produces an onboard requirement A technical problem is supported by evidence and submitted to IMO. The responsible committee allocates detailed work; the sub-committee develops a draft; the competent organ approves and/or adopts it under the relevant procedure. Once applicable, the Flag Administration implements the requirement and may delegate specified statutory tasks to an RO. The owner incorporates it into designs, procedures and resources; the Master and Chief Engineer implement the shipboard requirements. PSC verifies compliance within its lawful powers. IACS interpretation point: a relevant Unified Interpretation (UI) assists consistent application of ambiguous technical wording. It is read with the convention and any Flag instruction. A UI does not determine IMO's constitutional structure or amend a treaty. No UI is needed to redefine Assembly or Council powers. [26] 16-mark writing outline Suggested emphasis, not an official marking scheme: introduction (1); Assembly and Council including 10/10/20 (4); five committees (4); seven sub-committees and Secretariat (3); instrument comparison with examples and legal effect (4). Draw the organization diagram and finish with IMO develops standards; States implement and enforce. Avoid these errors: saying the Council already has 52 members; calling every code mandatory; calling every resolution advisory; or saying a sub-committee directly enacts international law. T01-Q02 · Trace in brief the origin of IMO and elaborate on the authority vested in IMO. Explain briefly the ratification process. Write short notes on the Casualty Investigation Code and the IMO Member State Audit Scheme.
1. Origin and development International shipping crosses jurisdictions, so isolated national rules cannot produce consistent safety standards. Early international cooperation addressed collision prevention, tonnage and safety. The Titanic disaster in 1912 prompted the first SOLAS Convention in 1914, before IMO existed. The post-war need for permanent intergovernmental cooperation led to the IMO Convention. [1,5,6] Milestone Significance 6 March 1948 Convention establishing the Inter-Governmental Maritime Consultative Organization (IMCO) adopted at Geneva. 17 March 1958 Constitutive Convention entered into force. 1959 Organization began work; first Assembly met. 1960 New SOLAS Convention adopted under its auspices, a major early safety achievement. 1967 - Torrey Canyon Highlighted pollution and liability gaps; helped drive development of the Legal Committee and international compensation arrangements. 22 May 1982 Name changed from IMCO to International Maritime Organization. 2. Authority vested in IMO The IMO Convention provides the institutional mandate to promote cooperation in maritime regulation and encourage high practicable standards of safety, navigation efficiency and pollution prevention. IMO provides a forum for drafting conventions, agreements and other instruments, convening conferences, adopting recommendations and performing functions entrusted by maritime treaties. [1] Technical authority: committees develop standards, review casualty and operating experience, adopt amendments where the relevant treaty permits, and issue interpretations and guidance. Examples are MSC action under SOLAS and MEPC action under MARPOL. Administrative and supporting authority: IMO acts as depositary for many maritime treaties through its Secretary-General, circulates treaty actions, maintains information systems, provides technical cooperation and conducts the Member State audit scheme. These functions help make international obligations workable and measurable. Limits of authority: IMO does not operate as an international coastguard, issue every ship's statutory certificate or routinely prosecute individual owners. Flag, port and coastal States implement and enforce within their jurisdiction. An RO acts only within the authority delegated by the Administration; the State retains its treaty responsibility. Do not confuse international standard-setting with shipboard command or domestic enforcement. [9,10] 3. Ratification: international consent after national preparation Ratification is the international act by which a State establishes its consent to be bound, following a signature subject to ratification where the treaty provides that route. It is not simply a delegate voting for a text. Acceptance and approval can have the same international effect under the treaty's clauses. [4] Technical/legal assessment Required domestic approval Instrument executed by competent authority Deposit with treaty depositary Entry into force for that State National implementation and enforcement The maritime administration examines legal gaps, cost, fleet implications, training and enforcement needs. The government follows its constitutional process and prepares the formal instrument. Deposit with the designated depositary establishes the treaty action; the treaty's own clause determines when obligations become effective for that State. Domestic approval and international deposit are related but distinct acts. For India, explain the roles of the Government and maritime administration and the need for supporting legislation, rules and notifications. Do not state that every treaty requires a separate Act of Parliament merely to deposit an instrument; legislative change is needed where necessary to give domestic effect to obligations or powers. Article 253 of the Constitution empowers Parliament to legislate for implementing international agreements. [4,44] 4. Casualty Investigation Code: purpose, force and scope The Code of the International Standards and Recommended Practices for a Safety Investigation into a Marine Casualty or Marine Incident was adopted by MSC.255(84). SOLAS XI-1/6 makes Parts I and II mandatory; Part III contains recommended practices. The mandatory arrangement entered into force on 1 January 2010. Related obligations include SOLAS I/21, MARPOL Article 12 and Load Lines Article 23. [12,45] Purpose: identify causal factors and prevent recurrence. A marine safety investigation aims at safety learning rather than apportioning blame or determining liability. Investigators must nevertheless report causal factors fully even if fault or liability can be inferred. Separate criminal, civil or disciplinary proceedings may also exist. Very serious marine casualty: a casualty involving total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment. Under Code chapter 6, a marine safety investigation shall be conducted into every very serious marine casualty. The Flag State is responsible for ensuring that it is conducted; States substantially interested cooperate and agree the investigating arrangements. Serious non-fatal casualties should not be dismissed merely because they fall outside this minimum mandatory category. Core requirements: notification and cooperation; an identified investigating State or States; suitably qualified and impartial investigators; access to evidence; protection and appropriate use of safety information; factual analysis, conclusions and recommendations; and reporting to IMO. Under chapter 14, the final report of every investigation into a very serious casualty must be submitted to IMO. GISIS provides the reporting mechanism. MSC.390(94) amended the Code, and A.1075(28) provides implementation guidance. [12,45] Chief Engineer's sequence - practical application: first protect life, stabilize machinery and support emergency response under the Master's command. Inform the Master immediately and provide accurate technical facts. The Master/company activate the prescribed reporting chain, including DGCOM for the Indian casualty-reporting route, and MRCC/Coast Guard for urgent SAR or response needs, as applicable. Preserve alarm histories, engine data, logbooks, samples and failed parts; record necessary emergency alterations. Cooperate truthfully with authorized investigators and implement corrective actions through the SMS. Evidence preservation must never delay rescue or essential safety measures. [13] 5. IMO Member State Audit Scheme - IMSAS IMSAS evaluates how effectively a State fulfils its obligations as Flag, coastal and port State. The benchmark is the III Code, A.1070(28). Mandatory auditing began on 1 January 2016 through amendments to the relevant instruments, including SOLAS chapter XIII. This is an audit of State implementation, not the company's ISM certification audit. [9,10] Scope: implementation of applicable SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, Load Lines, Tonnage and COLREG obligations. Auditors assess legislation, organizational authority, qualified personnel, enforcement, surveys and certification, RO oversight, investigations and reporting. An enacted rule without staff, resources or enforcement evidence is insufficient. Preparation and self-assessment Independent audit and evidence Findings and root causes Corrective action plan Follow-up and continuous monitoring Current update: A.1211(34), adopted 3 December 2025, replaced A.1067(28) from 30 June 2026. It introduces the IMSAS Continuous Monitoring Mechanism (ICMM) and more risk-based audit planning. Verification information, gap analysis and the State audit questionnaire support assessment. Depending on demonstrated implementation, the process provides for limited or full on-site audits. The framework provides periodic audits within a cycle not exceeding nine years; use the actual IMO schedule for a particular State. [10,11] Importance: identifies systemic weaknesses, directs capacity-building, supports consistent treaty implementation and feeds lessons back into IMO rule-making. For a Chief Engineer, reliable survey records, casualty evidence and statutory compliance support the national system, but the Chief Engineer neither conducts nor certifies the State's IMSAS audit. 16-mark writing outline Allocate approximately: history and dates (3); IMO authority and limits (3); ratification process (3); Casualty Code, mandatory parts and very serious casualty rule (4); IMSAS scope, III Code and 2026 framework update (3). Give every requested short note space; do not spend the entire answer on IMO history. Memory anchors: 1948 / 1958 / 1959 / 1982; XI-1/6 = casualty investigation; XIII = implementation verification; A.1070 = audit standard, A.1211 = current audit framework. T01-Q03 · List and explain the four main stages of an IMO convention. With examples, explain the delay that occurs between the adoption date and coming-into-force date of a convention. What can be done to reduce this delay.
1. Explain the stages without confusing different legal acts For a complete lifecycle answer, use adoption, entry into force, implementation/enforcement and amendment. Ratification or accession is the crucial consent step between adoption and entry into force. Some teaching notes group the four steps as adoption, ratification, entry into force and enforcement; explicitly explaining consent and subsequent amendment covers the substance whichever terminology the examiner uses. These are explanatory groupings, not four stages prescribed by one universal IMO regulation. [4,42] Develop and adopt text States consent; thresholds reached Convention enters into force Implement and enforce Review experience and amend 2. Stage 1 - Development and adoption Governments identify a problem such as casualties, pollution or inconsistent standards. Technical work establishes the risk, possible controls, feasibility, costs and international implications. Drafting may involve committees, sub-committees, intersessional groups and consultation with relevant organizations. A diplomatic conference or competent organ considers the final text and adopts it under the applicable procedure. Adoption settles the agreed text; it does not alone bind every participating State. A delegate may support adoption while the government still needs domestic approval before accepting the treaty. The instrument normally includes substantive obligations, technical annexes, participation provisions, entry-into-force conditions, amendment procedures, depositary functions and denunciation provisions. [4,42] 3. Stage 2 - Consent and entry into force States express consent through the methods permitted by the treaty: definitive signature, ratification, acceptance, approval or accession. For signature subject to ratification, later deposit of the required instrument completes the international consent step. The depositary then verifies the entry-into-force formula. Maritime technical treaties commonly combine a minimum number of States with a specified proportion of world merchant tonnage. Some add another sector-specific condition. A prescribed waiting period follows achievement of the thresholds. The clock does not necessarily start on the adoption date. [4,6,7,8] 4. Stage 3 - Implementation and enforcement The State provides domestic legal authority, survey/certification systems, trained personnel, reporting arrangements, equipment approval mechanisms and sanctions. The owner supplies compliant ships and resources; the Master and Chief Engineer apply operational controls and maintain evidence. Flag State: ensures compliance of its ships wherever they operate. Port State: inspects visiting ships within the convention's powers and addresses serious non-compliance. Coastal State: meets its service and protection obligations and exercises jurisdiction within international law. Implementation must include monitoring and feedback; mere ratification does not demonstrate effective enforcement. [9] 5. Stage 4 - Amendment and improvement Operating experience, casualties, scientific findings and new technology reveal gaps. The treaty's amendment article determines circulation, voting, acceptance and entry into force. Express acceptance requires positive State acceptances; tacit acceptance allows eligible amendments to be deemed accepted after a stated period unless the prescribed objection threshold is met. Tacit acceptance is particularly useful for technical annexes because frequent updates would otherwise remain pending for years. It does not remove national consent to the parent treaty or automatically bring a newly adopted convention into force. SOLAS Article VIII and MARPOL Article 16 are major examples, but their detailed thresholds are not interchangeable. [6,35] 6. Verified examples of adoption-to-entry delay Instrument Adopted Entered into force What the example demonstrates SOLAS 1974 1 November 1974 25 May 1980 Agreement on text was followed by the required national consent, fleet coverage and waiting period. BWM Convention 2004 13 February 2004 8 September 2017 Number-of-State and tonnage criteria both mattered; equipment approval and implementation concerns also required extensive work. Hong Kong Convention 15 May 2009 26 June 2025 An additional 2009 recycling-capacity condition made participation by relevant recycling States important. SOLAS Article X: at least 25 States, representing at least 50% of world merchant gross tonnage, followed by 12 months. BWM Article 18: at least 30 States, representing 35%, followed by 12 months. Hong Kong Article 17: at least 15 States, at least 40% of world merchant gross tonnage, plus the specified recycling-volume test, followed by 24 months. The recycling test uses the combined maximum annual recycling volume of the relevant States during the preceding ten years, amounting to at least 3% of their combined merchant gross tonnage. It is not simply 3% of world tonnage. [6,7,8] 7. Why delay occurs Domestic legal work: the administration must identify gaps, allocate powers, draft rules and obtain the necessary constitutional approvals. New inspection powers or penalties may require legislation. Economic and technical readiness: retrofits, approved equipment, yards, shore infrastructure and competent personnel take time and money. Governments may hesitate where technology performance, test methods or costs are unsettled. Participation thresholds: many small registries can satisfy the State-number requirement while leaving the tonnage criterion unmet. Conversely, a few large registries cannot satisfy the minimum number of States alone. Unequal impacts and political priorities: benefits and costs are distributed differently among shipowning, trading, island, developing and recycling States. Competing legislative priorities also delay action. Administrative delay and prescribed lead time: interdepartmental consultation, preparation and deposit of instruments take time. Even after all thresholds are met, the treaty may intentionally allow a further implementation period. These are general mechanisms; they should not be presented as proven causes for a named State without supporting evidence. 8. How delay can be reduced Start national gap analysis during negotiation, nominate a lead ministry and set deadlines for legal, technical and financial preparation. Use model legislative assistance and IMO capacity-development programmes. Train surveyors and operators and prepare approval and certification procedures early. Resolve uncertainty: issue clear test standards, implementation guidance and appropriately phased application dates; conduct trials and build essential infrastructure. Engage major flag, trading and affected industry States so that all entry criteria can be achieved. Improve treaty design: negotiate realistic thresholds and transition provisions when drafting a new treaty. Existing thresholds cannot simply be ignored for convenience. For eligible future technical amendments, use the treaty's tacit-acceptance procedure. Use lawful early implementation where appropriate, while clearly distinguishing voluntary preparation from a binding international obligation. Depositary reminders, transparent status reporting and interagency coordination can reduce avoidable administrative delay. Faster adoption must still leave enough time for safe, credible implementation. 16-mark writing outline Cover lifecycle and consent distinction (5), three dated examples and entry criteria (4), reasons for delay (4), and practical remedies (3). Essential sentence: adoption agrees the text; consent binds the State through the permitted treaty action; entry into force activates obligations on the treaty's terms. Avoid these errors: treating signing and ratifying as identical; starting every waiting period at adoption; saying tacit acceptance solves the initial ratification threshold; or claiming the Hong Kong Convention is still awaiting entry into force. T01-Q04 · Discuss the procedure of entry into force of an IMO convention after its adoption. State the provision and its importance towards entry into force of the convention. For a convention of important technical nature, state the general rules/conditions observed by states for its entry into force. Explain the terms (i) Accession (ii) Signature subject to Ratification, acceptance or approval.
1. The controlling provision After adoption, a convention becomes legally operative according to its entry-into-force article in the final clauses. That article specifies the required consents, any fleet or other threshold, the waiting period, and usually the treatment of later-joining States. There is no single entry-into-force formula applicable to every IMO convention. [4,42] The clause is essential because it prevents uncertainty over when obligations start and secures a sufficient level of international participation. For a technical shipping treaty, combining State numbers and merchant tonnage helps avoid a regime supported by many States but covering little shipping, or by only a few large registries without broader acceptance. 2. Procedure after adoption Step 1 - Authenticate and open the text for participation. The agreed text is finalized in its authentic languages. Signature is possible during the period and on the terms specified in the convention. The depositary circulates the text and records treaty actions. Step 2 - National assessment. Each government examines compatibility with domestic law, administration, fleet requirements, equipment availability, costs and enforcement capacity. It follows its constitutional approval process. For India, Article 253 is relevant to Parliament's power to implement treaties; actual implementing powers and rules must be identified for the subject concerned. [44] Step 3 - Express consent in an authorized way. Where signature is subject to ratification, acceptance or approval, the State deposits the corresponding instrument after completing its internal process. A State may instead accede if the convention permits. A signature by a person without the necessary authority does not automatically establish valid consent. Step 4 - Depositary checks the conditions. The depositary records qualifying instruments and calculates whether all State-number, tonnage and any special criteria have been met. Each required condition must be satisfied, not merely one of them. Step 5 - Waiting period and notification. The prescribed period begins when the required conditions are achieved. The depositary notifies States of the resulting date. This interval allows governments and industry to prepare for implementation. Step 6 - Global and State-specific entry into force. The initial date activates the convention for the relevant consenting States. A later-depositing State becomes bound on the date prescribed by the later-accession clause. For example, SOLAS Article X(b) provides a three-month period after deposit for a State whose instrument is deposited after the Convention has entered into force. Do not assume that every treaty uses three months. [6,55] Step 7 - Determine application to the particular ship. A convention in force does not mean every provision immediately applies to every vessel. Check ship type, size, voyage, construction or delivery date, equipment installation date, survey triggers, exemptions and transitional provisions. This is where many examination and onboard errors occur. 3. Typical technical-convention conditions, with examples Convention and Participation threshold Waiting period provision SOLAS 1974, Article At least 25 States and 50% of world merchant 12 months after conditions met. X(a) gross tonnage. BWM 2004, Article At least 30 States and 35% of world merchant 12 months. 18(1) gross tonnage. Hong Kong 2009, At least 15 States, 40% of world merchant 24 months. Article 17(1) gross tonnage, and the specified 3% recycling-volume criterion. The Hong Kong example demonstrates that a technically specialized convention may require participation by States with the industrial capability to make it effective. Its recycling-volume criterion is measured against the combined merchant shipping tonnage of those States, using the Convention's ten-year maximum-volume method. [7,8] 4. Accession Accession is a permitted act by which a State expresses consent to be bound without first signing subject to ratification. It has the same international legal effect as ratification, acceptance or approval once validly completed; the procedural route differs. It is especially useful for a State that did not sign during the signature period. [4] The government performs its national assessment, executes the instrument of accession and deposits it with the depositary. Whether accession is open, and when the treaty becomes effective for that State, are determined by the treaty. Accession need not always wait until the treaty has already entered into force internationally if its clauses permit earlier accession. Illustration: a government that did not sign a convention during its signature window can still join through accession if the convention allows it. It does not need to recreate a past signature or obtain an ordinary ship certificate from IMO to become a Party. 5. Signature subject to ratification, acceptance or approval This signature indicates the State's participation and intention to consider becoming bound but does not itself complete consent to be bound. It gives the government time to complete domestic consultation and approval. A later formal international act is required. [4] Under Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties, Article 18, a State that has signed subject to ratification must refrain from acts defeating the treaty's object and purpose, pending its decision, until it makes clear its intention not to become a Party. This limited obligation should not be confused with full performance of all treaty provisions. Articles 11-16 explain methods and establishment of consent; Article 24 addresses entry into force. [46] Acceptance and approval serve the same international function as ratification where permitted; the terminology often reflects a State's internal constitutional arrangements. Definitive signature, where the treaty permits it and the relevant conditions are met, can itself express consent. Therefore, saying “signature never binds a State” is also incorrect. 6. Distinctions the examiner expects Act What it establishes What it does not automatically establish Adoption Agreement on the treaty text. Consent of every State to be bound. Signature subject to Preliminary signature with later confirmation Full treaty obligations already in ratification required. operation. Ratification / Consent through the permitted international Immediate entry into force acceptance / approval / act. regardless of the treaty clause. accession Entry into force Treaty becomes operative for the relevant Universal application of every States on its terms. regulation to every ship. National Domestic mechanisms to give effect to A substitute for depositing the implementation obligations. required international instrument. Chief Engineer's application: on receiving a company circular, verify the applicable instrument, amendment resolution, operative date and ship-specific trigger. Advise the Master and superintendent of any gap, obtain Flag/RO interpretation where needed, then arrange equipment, training, procedures and survey evidence. A press report of “IMO approval” is not sufficient authority to treat a draft as mandatory. 16-mark writing outline Entry clause and purpose (3); seven-step procedure (5); technical treaty examples (3); accession (2); signature subject to ratification, acceptance and approval (3). Highlight international consent, entry into force and ship-specific applicability as three separate checks. Source: MEO_ClassI_Section1_IMO_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 1 Oct 2026

T01-Q05 · Referring to the IMO Instruments Implementation Code: briefly discuss the objectives of the code and the strategy recommended to the flag state to achieve the objective; which all IMO instruments are covered in the code; what are the key performance indicators for evaluation of flag state performance as per the code; briefly discuss the flag state, coastal state, and port state responsibilities.
1. Identity, objective and legal framework The IMO Instruments Implementation Code (III Code) was adopted by resolution A.1070(28), 4 December 2013. Its objective is better maritime safety and protection of the marine environment through effective State implementation of IMO instruments. It is also the audit standard for IMSAS. Mandatory application is established through amendments to the relevant treaties, including SOLAS chapter XIII, rather than by assuming that every Assembly resolution is automatically binding. [9,10] The Code has four parts: common areas, Flag States, coastal States and port States. A State must consider all its applicable roles. India has a registry, coastline and ports, so its implementation arrangements involve all three capacities. The Code applies in relation to the instruments by which the State is bound; geography and maritime activity affect which obligations are relevant. 2. Recommended overall strategy - paragraph 3 The strategy has three linked elements: define how obligations will be met; monitor and assess implementation; continuously review and improve performance. This is more than a policy statement. It needs assigned responsibility, adequate legal powers, personnel, resources, measurable objectives and evidence that the system works. [9] Identify obligations and gaps Assign powers, people and resources Implement and enforce Measure performance Review causes and improve Practical strategy components: maintain a convention-and-amendment register; assign each obligation to a responsible authority; enact or update laws; train surveyors and investigators; establish RO authorization and oversight; provide coastal/port services; ensure reporting to IMO; and review outcomes at management level. Where several ministries or agencies share functions, their interfaces must be documented so that an obligation is not lost between departments. 3. Instruments covered Paragraph 6 identifies six subject areas. The corresponding core instruments for the audit framework are shown below. Applicable protocols and mandatory codes incorporated through these instruments are considered within their relevant scope. [9,10] Subject Main convention framework Safety of life at sea SOLAS 1974, as amended, and applicable protocols. Prevention of pollution from MARPOL, including applicable annexes and protocols. ships Training, certification and STCW 1978, as amended, and the mandatory STCW Code provisions within watchkeeping audit scope. Load lines Load Lines 1966 and the 1988 Protocol, as applicable. Tonnage measurement Tonnage Convention 1969. Collision prevention COLREG 1972, as amended. Do not turn this into a list of every maritime convention. MLC is an ILO convention; UNCLOS supplies the broader jurisdictional framework but is not itself one of these six IMO subject instruments. Other treaties may impose additional duties without thereby becoming an extra item in this core audit list. 4. Common implementation requirements Under paragraphs 7-14, the State needs a functioning legal and administrative system. Key areas are jurisdiction; organization and authority; legislation and its publication; enforcement; survey, inspection, audit and certification; control of delegated bodies; investigations; and reporting to IMO and other Administrations. [9] Legislation must keep pace with amendments. An administration should know which version is effective and have a mechanism to incorporate changes on time. Qualified staff and resources are essential: legal powers without surveyors, investigators or data systems cannot deliver compliance. Records should demonstrate decisions, certificates, inspections, actions and communication. Deficiencies require corrective action directed at their causes, followed by checking effectiveness. 5. Flag State responsibilities - Part 2 Implementation: establish national requirements for ships entitled to fly the flag, survey and certification arrangements, seafarer competence controls and instructions necessary for consistent application. Verify that the ship is fit for its intended service and remains compliant during operation. Delegation to ROs: assess and authorize an organization within a defined scope through formal arrangements; communicate national requirements and interpretations; monitor its work and investigate performance concerns. Delegating a survey does not transfer the State's international responsibility. The RO Code, adopted by MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65), supports recognition, authorization and oversight. [9,47] Enforcement: inspect where needed, investigate non-compliance, require rectification and apply lawful sanctions. Respond to PSC deficiencies and detentions involving the fleet. Surveyors must be competent, properly authorized and supported with current instructions. Investigation: ensure casualty investigations are carried out as required, identify lessons and communicate the prescribed information. Evaluation: review whether the legislation, personnel, RO controls and enforcement actually improve fleet performance. 6. Performance indicators - paragraphs 42-44 The Code requires performance evaluation and gives indicators; it does not impose one universal numeric pass mark for every Flag State. Use consistent definitions and compare trends, fleet exposure and ship types. An increase in reports can reflect improved reporting rather than worsening safety. [9] Code-linked indicator What it helps reveal PSC detention rates and Whether certification and Flag oversight are preventing serious deficiencies. verified detentions relative to fleet size Flag inspection, survey Recurring defects, overdue surveys and the quality of follow-up. and control results Casualty and fleet-loss Underlying operational and structural risks; annual loss statistics in paragraph trends 43 exclude constructive total losses. Communication and Timely reporting, response to PSC interventions and reliable regulatory updates. information processes Very serious/serious Whether causes are identified and recommendations result in action. casualty investigations and lessons Competence or misconduct Weaknesses in training, examination, endorsement or enforcement. cases involving certificate holders Occupational accidents and Effectiveness of shipboard working practices and environmental controls. pollution violations Resources and Adequacy of administrative capability and use of enforcement powers. certificate/approval suspensions or withdrawals Illustrative management metrics, not prescribed formulas: detention percentage = detentions divided by inspections multiplied by 100; overdue corrective-action percentage = overdue actions divided by actions due multiplied by 100. Define the denominator and period. Combine outcome measures with process measures such as investigation completion and amendment implementation; one favourable number cannot establish overall compliance. 7. Coastal State responsibilities - Part 3 Paragraph 48 identifies radiocommunication, meteorological services and warnings, SAR, hydrographic services, ships' routeing, ship-reporting systems, VTS and aids to navigation, as applicable. The State should allocate duties, maintain service capability and ensure lawful, consistent implementation. [9] Paragraph 50 adds monitoring and data for trend analysis, timely response to pollution incidents, and cooperation with Flag and port States in casualty investigations. Under paragraph 51, performance is reviewed periodically. Practical examples include maintaining navigational warnings, checking SAR communication arrangements and analysing response failures. Coastal jurisdiction is exercised within applicable international law; proximity to the coast does not create unlimited enforcement powers. 8. Port State responsibilities - Part 4 Paragraph 56 highlights adequate reception facilities, PSC and a register of fuel-oil suppliers. The port must support compliance as well as inspect it: a ship cannot routinely deliver waste ashore if suitable facilities are absent. Supplier oversight supports MARPOL fuel-quality and documentation requirements. [9] PSC must be conducted by authorized, qualified officers, free of commercial conflicts, using applicable procedures. Apply no-more-favourable-treatment provisions where the instrument provides them, communicate deficiencies appropriately and avoid arbitrary intervention. Review the effectiveness of inspections and port services. The current general IMO Procedures for Port State Control, 2025, are A.1206(34); older editions should not be presented as the latest. [48] 9. Current audit and interpretation points A.1211(34) is the current IMSAS framework, replacing A.1067(28) from 30 June 2026. Its ICMM supports continuous information collection and risk-based audit planning. A.1070(28) remains the III Code; do not substitute the framework resolution for the audit standard. [10,11] For technical convention ambiguity, use the relevant IACS UI together with Flag instructions. UIs help interpret technical requirements; the III Code establishes State implementation responsibilities. An IACS UI cannot excuse a government from an obligation to investigate, report or maintain effective oversight. [26] 16-mark writing outline Objective, four parts and legal basis (2); paragraph 3 strategy (3); six instrument areas (2); paragraphs 42-44 indicators (3); Flag/coastal/port responsibilities (6). Draw the improvement cycle and emphasize State responsibility remains despite delegation. Source: MEO_ClassI_Section1_IMO_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 1 Oct 2026
