T01-Q06 · What are the key functions and components of GISIS (Global Integrated Shipping Information System of the IMO) and how does it facilitate data sharing among member states? Discuss the usefulness of this module for reporting/analysing compliant fuel oil information and reporting inadequacy of port reception facilities.
1. Definition and purpose GISIS means Global Integrated Shipping Information System. It is IMO's web-based collection of linked information modules, supporting information submitted under IMO instruments and facilitating access by Member States and other authorized users. It provides a structured route for reporting, retrieval and analysis; it is not one undifferentiated public database. [14] Why integration matters: common identifiers, particularly the IMO ship number and standardized port information, help connect reports about ships, casualties, services and compliance. Governments can use comparable information to identify recurring failures rather than act only on isolated complaints. Access rights differ between modules and datasets; sensitive data are not automatically visible to every user. 2. Main components and functions Module or information area Practical use Ship and company Identify the correct vessel/company and reduce confusion caused by name or particulars ownership changes. Marine casualties and Submit prescribed casualty information and investigation reports; support incidents safety analysis. Port reception facilities Locate facilities, identify waste streams and record alleged inadequacies and responses. MARPOL Annex VI Information on fuel availability, non-availability notifications, supplier issues and other Annex VI matters. Contact points Find official contacts for matters such as pollution reporting, PSC and casualty investigation. Recognized organizations, Support transparency about authorization and applicable administrative survey and certification information. information Member State audits Support audit planning, reporting, corrective actions and access to authorized audit information. Ship fuel-oil consumption Receive verified annual ship data through Administrations; support aggregate database / IMO DCS emissions analysis. National legislation, Share prescribed legal and operational information subject to access controls. maritime security and other modules Module names and access arrangements can evolve. MARPOL Annex VI fuel-availability information and the DCS fuel-consumption database serve different purposes even though both are associated with Annex VI. [14,49] 3. How information is shared The ship/operator gathers facts and sends them through the prescribed reporting route. The responsible State or authorized entity checks and enters data using the relevant module. Other authorized users can retrieve the information; the Secretariat and committees analyse trends and identify implementation needs. The reporting duty remains with the party specified by the convention. Having a GISIS account does not alter that allocation. Good data require a correct ship number, port, date, units, attachments and responsible contact. The submitting authority must correct inaccurate or outdated entries. A database record is useful evidence, but it does not by itself prove that a supplier's fuel meets every requirement or that a facility is currently available; confirm arrangements before arrival. 4. Compliant fuel-oil information - legal basis Under MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18.1, Parties take reasonable steps to promote availability of compliant fuel and inform IMO about availability in their ports and terminals. Regulation 18.2 addresses inability to obtain compliant fuel. Regulation 18.2.4 requires notification to the ship's Administration and the competent authority of the relevant destination port; 18.2.5 concerns a Party's notification to IMO of a ship presenting evidence of non-availability. Regulation 18.9.6 covers communication to IMO of fuel suppliers failing to meet regulations 14 or 18. [15,16] The Annex VI GISIS module enables patterns to be examined: repeated non-availability at a port, affected grades or suppliers, the frequency of reported problems and responses by authorities. This supports regulatory action and better voyage/bunker planning. Commercially expensive fuel is not, merely for that reason, unavailable compliant fuel. 5. FONAR: required evidence and correct responsibility chain FONAR = Fuel Oil Non-Availability Report. The standard format is in appendix 1 of MEPC.320(74). It records the voyage, planned bunkering, suppliers contacted, attempts to obtain compliant alternatives, reasons for failure, non-compliant fuel purchased if applicable, and the plan to obtain compliant fuel. [16] Chief Engineer establishes fuel facts Master informed; company assists procurement Notify Flag and destination competent authority Relevant State reports to IMO/GISIS Authority evaluates evidence and response Chief Engineer: verify ROB by tank, sulphur requirements for the route, machinery suitability, safe segregation and changeover arrangements. Preserve bunker enquiries, supplier refusals, timing, BDNs and relevant test evidence. Inform the Master immediately and assist the company in seeking compliant supplies. The Master/company follow the prescribed notification process; formal State-to-IMO reporting is not replaced by an informal email from the engine room. FONAR is not an exemption or automatic permission to burn non-compliant fuel. Under regulation 18.2, the authorities consider the circumstances and evidence when deciding the appropriate action. The ship is not required to deviate from its intended voyage or delay unduly solely to achieve compliance in the terms of that regulation, but reasonable procurement efforts and credible planning remain essential. Repeated unsupported reports invite closer scrutiny. [15,16] Non-availability versus quality complaint: non-availability means compliant fuel could not reasonably be obtained despite efforts. A delivered sample later found non-compliant is a separate quality/compliance issue requiring evidence, notification and corrective action. Do not use a FONAR to retrospectively erase a poor procurement decision or falsify a BDN. 6. Port reception facilities: purpose and legal basis Ports need facilities adequate for the ships using them without causing undue delay. Relevant provisions include MARPOL Annex I regulation 38, Annex II regulation 18, Annex IV regulation 12, Annex V regulation 8 and Annex VI regulation 17, within each provision's scope. GISIS helps users identify available facilities and helps States track inadequacies. [17,18] The database can identify facility/contact details and the categories of waste handled. The ship still checks quantity limits, booking requirements, connections, segregation, delivery timing and documentary arrangements with the port/agent. An entry on a website cannot guarantee a berth-side service on the required day. 7. Reporting alleged inadequacy - practical sequence 1. Plan and notify: the Chief Engineer provides expected sludge, bilge-water and other machinery-waste quantities, tank capacity, pump/connection details and delivery needs to the Master. The ship/company/agent gives the appropriate advance notification and obtains confirmation. 2. Establish the facts: if the facility refuses waste, lacks capacity, cannot receive the relevant category or causes unacceptable delay, record the port, terminal, dates, waste category and quantity, request made, response received and consequences. Preserve messages and receipts. A disputed charge alone should be explained factually rather than automatically labelled a treaty violation. 3. Report through the prescribed route: use appendix 1 of MEPC.1/Circ.834/Rev.1. The Master reports to the Flag Administration and, if possible, the port-State authorities. The Flag informs the port State and notifies IMO; the port State investigates and communicates its response through the appropriate arrangements. [18] 4. Maintain lawful containment: reassess retention capacity and arrange an alternative adequate facility with company support. Follow applicable discharge rules and emergency procedures. An inadequate shore facility does not automatically authorize an otherwise prohibited discharge. Record actual waste transfers accurately in the relevant record books and retain delivery receipts. 5. Close the feedback loop: the authority updates facility information, investigates recurring failures and implements corrective action. GISIS data can guide investment, enforcement and IMO policy. Reliable reporting helps other ships avoid the same problem. 16-mark writing outline Definition and access (2); modules and data sharing (4); regulation 18 and FONAR (5); reception-facility provisions and reporting chain (5). Two scoring sentences: FONAR is not an exemption; PRF inadequacy does not itself legalize discharge. Source: MEO_ClassI_Section1_IMO_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 1 Oct 2026

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_01_to_10.pdf

T01-Q07 · Describe the key elements and requirements of Goal-Based Ship Construction Standards (GBS) as outlined by IMO, for bulk carriers and oil tankers, emphasizing their significance in enhancing safety and environmental protection. Explain how GBS ensures ship safety and environmental protection, and discuss the implementation process considering the involvement of stakeholders (ship designers, shipbuilders, classification societies) and the role of verification and validation in ensuring compliance.
1. Principle and statutory basis Goal-Based Standards (GBS) state the safety and environmental outcomes to be achieved and the functions needed to achieve them. Detailed construction rules must then demonstrate conformity. This combines freedom to develop sound designs with an explicit, verifiable minimum outcome. GBS does not abolish detailed class rules; it sets the standard those rules must satisfy. [19] The statutory basis for bulk carriers and oil tankers is SOLAS II-1/3-10, introduced by MSC.290(87). The International GBS Standards are MSC.287(87). The current verification framework cited by IMO is MSC.454(100), which revised the earlier MSC.296(87) guidelines. [19-22] 2. Exact application: ship type, length and construction dates The regulation covers oil tankers of 150 m length and above and bulk carriers of 150 m and above constructed with a single deck, top-side tanks and hopper side tanks in cargo spaces, excluding ore carriers and combination carriers, meeting any relevant construction trigger below. [21] Trigger Date Building contract placed On or after 1 July 2016 No building contract: keel laid On or after 1 July 2017 or similar stage Delivery On or after 1 July 2020 The SOLAS amendment entered into force on 1 January 2012, but these are the later ship-application dates. Entry into force and newbuilding applicability are different. The delivery trigger can capture a ship even where its contract predates the first date. Use the regulatory definition of length and ship type; do not substitute an informal LOA test or describe this specific rule as applying to all ships. 3. The five-tier framework Tier I — Goals: safe and environmentally friendly ships Tier II — Functional requirements: what the design must achieve Tier III — Verification: rules demonstrated to conform to goals Tier IV — Rules and regulations: detailed design requirements Tier V — Industry practice and standards: implementation methods Tier What it answers Responsibility I - Goals What overall safety and environmental result IMO defines the high-level outcome. must be achieved? II - Functional What functions and performance are IMO defines the required functions. requirements necessary? III - Verification of Do the detailed rules adequately deliver Tiers I Independent IMO GBS verification conformity and II? process and MSC consideration. IV - Rules and What detailed design and construction Recognized classification societies regulations requirements must the ship satisfy? and/or Administrations. V - Industry How are design, production, operation and Industry standards, yard procedures practices and maintenance carried out consistently? and operating practices, where standards incorporated or applicable. Precision: MSC.287(87)'s Standards comprise Tiers I-III; the broader framework has five tiers. Saying “IMO directly writes every Tier IV scantling rule” misses the central distinction. [20] 4. Tier I goal explained A ship is to remain safe and environmentally sound throughout its specified design life when properly operated and maintained within its stated operating and environmental conditions, including intact and specified damaged conditions. Structural strength, integrity and stability reduce the risk of loss or pollution following structural failure. The goal also covers safe access, escape, inspection, maintenance and materials suitable for environmentally acceptable recycling. [20,21] The qualification about operation and maintenance is crucial. An approved design cannot compensate indefinitely for excessive corrosion, repeated overloading, damaged hatch covers or unauthorized structural alterations. GBS is a whole-life structural safety framework, not a lifetime warranty. 5. Tier II: all 15 functional requirements Requirement Explanation and practical significance II.1 Design life Not less than 25 years. This is the nominal design basis, not compulsory scrapping at 25 years or a guarantee of serviceability. II.2 Environmental Design for North Atlantic environmental conditions and relevant long-term conditions sea-state data for unrestricted navigation. Establishes a demanding common load basis. II.3 Structural strength Assess yielding, buckling, excessive deformation and ultimate strength, using relevant loading conditions, net scantlings and suitable safety margins. II.4 Fatigue life Design fatigue life must be at least the specified ship design life; repeated stress cycles must not lead to premature cracking. II.5 Residual strength Preserve sufficient strength in specified damaged conditions such as collision, grounding or flooding. Local damage must not necessarily cause immediate hull-girder failure. II.6 Corrosion protection Use coatings, corrosion additions and other suitable protection to preserve required net strength. Maintenance assumptions matter. II.7 Structural redundancy Local damage to a stiffening member should not immediately cause consequential collapse of the complete stiffened panel. II.8 Openings, closures and securing arrangements must maintain integrity under Watertight/weathertight intended service conditions. integrity II.9 Human element Apply ergonomic principles to access, working arrangements, inspection and maintenance. A structure must be safely maintainable. II.10 Design transparency Maintain controlled, traceable design information, including parameters that limit operation, while protecting legitimate intellectual property. II.11 Construction quality Control materials, assembly, alignment, joining/welding, surface preparation and coating application. Approved drawings alone cannot ensure build quality. II.12 Construction survey Establish a survey plan identifying scope and areas requiring special attention to verify that the as-built ship meets the standards. II.13 Survey and Design for effective inspection and maintenance, including attention to maintenance fatigue-sensitive or other critical areas. II.14 Structural Provide means of access for overall/close-up inspection and thickness accessibility measurement of internal structure. II.15 Recycling Select materials and construction arrangements that support environmentally acceptable recycling without compromising ship safety. Net scantling explained: the structure must retain the required load-carrying capability after the rule corrosion allowance is considered. Gross as-built thickness includes the relevant corrosion addition. Actual thickness measurements are compared with the applicable renewal criteria; the Chief Engineer must not invent a universal percentage wastage limit for every member. [20,24] 6. Implementation and stakeholder responsibilities Owner: defines intended service, operating envelope, maintenance strategy and procurement requirements; selects the Flag and class arrangements and ensures the approved limitations can be respected commercially. Designer: applies the accepted structural rules, demonstrates strength and fatigue performance, identifies critical details, specifies corrosion protection and provides accessible inspection arrangements. Numerical models must represent realistic loading and boundary conditions. Classification society/RO: develops and maintains rules, submits them to the GBS verification process, approves the ship's design and surveys construction within its class/statutory authority. The IACS Common Structural Rules for Bulk Carriers and Oil Tankers (CSR BC & OT) are the relevant common structural rule framework within their scope; use the applicable society's published edition and change history. IRS publishes its own rules and classification material through its official rules portal. [24,40] Shipbuilder: uses approved materials and procedures, controls welding and dimensional tolerances, records inspections and tests, resolves non-conformities and supplies accurate as-built documentation. Substitution of material or an unapproved detail can invalidate design assumptions. Flag Administration: recognizes and authorizes the RO where applicable, ensures use of conforming structural standards and retains statutory responsibility. IMO/MSC: considers the independent verification of the rule system; it does not personally survey every new ship. 7. Verification versus ship-specific validation Rule-level verification: the RO/Administration submits its rule package and supporting evidence. An independent GBS audit team assesses conformity with goals and functional requirements, identifies gaps and reviews corrective evidence. The report and recommendations go to MSC for consideration. Maintenance of conformity is necessary when rules change. [19,22] Ship-level verification: approved drawings, structural calculations, construction surveys, material certificates, welding/NDT records and required tests demonstrate that the actual ship follows the accepted rules. Engineering validation: calculations and modelling are checked against reliable methods, test evidence and service experience, as appropriate, to ensure the design assumptions represent real behaviour. This is an explanation of engineering assurance, not a separate universal “IMO GBS validation certificate”. Distinguish verification of rules from approval and survey of an individual ship. 8. Ship Construction File and Chief Engineer's role Under SOLAS II-1/3-10.4, a Ship Construction File (SCF) must explain how the functional requirements were applied. It is provided on delivery, retained onboard and/or ashore as allowed, and updated throughout the ship's life to reflect modifications. MSC.1/Circ.1343 provides SCF guidance. Relevant information includes design limitations, as-built structural information, material/coating details and areas requiring special attention, according to the prescribed file arrangements. [21,23] Chief Engineer's sequence: review relevant SCF and maintenance limitations; coordinate with the Master/Chief Officer on ballast tanks, structural condition and safe access; plan inspections and preserve records; report defects to the Master and superintendent; obtain class/Flag involvement and approval where required before structural repair or alteration; verify repairs and update documentation. The Master/Chief Officer normally lead cargo-loading and overall stability operations; the Chief Engineer supports machinery, ballast-system and assigned structural maintenance responsibilities under the SMS. Relevant UI: IACS UI SC190, Rev.2, November 2024, interprets SOLAS II-1/3-6 and the Technical Provisions for means of access. It can help resolve detailed access arrangements associated with Tier II.14. It is not a reinterpretation of all of II-1/3-10, and it must be used within its scope and applicable Flag instructions. [25,26] 16-mark writing outline Legal basis and exact applicability (3); five-tier diagram (3); functional requirements with 25 years, North Atlantic, fatigue, residual strength and corrosion emphasized (5); stakeholders and verification (3); SCF and in-service responsibilities (2). Source: MEO_ClassI_Section1_IMO_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 1 Oct 2026

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_01_to_10.pdf

T01-Q08 · What are the ongoing developments at IMO with respect to technical, operational and market-based measures to be invoked on board ships for combating greenhouse gas emissions from ships?
1. Answer in three layers: ambition, law and proposals IMO's approach combines technical efficiency, operational management and developing fuel-GHG/economic measures. The 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, MEPC.377(80) sets the policy direction; binding ship requirements arise from MARPOL Annex VI and applicable implementing provisions. An approved draft is not an adopted, in-force regulation. The status below was checked on 1 October 2026. [27,28,31-33] 2. 2023 Strategy: figures to reproduce accurately Objective Target or checkpoint Carbon intensity of At least 40% reduction in CO2 per transport work by 2030, compared international shipping with 2008, as an international-shipping average. Total annual GHG emissions - At least 20%, striving for 30% reduction, compared with 2008. 2030 Total annual GHG emissions - At least 70%, striving for 80% reduction, compared with 2008. 2040 Zero/near-zero GHG At least 5%, striving for 10% of energy used by international shipping technology, fuels and energy by 2030. Ultimate ambition Net-zero GHG emissions by or around, i.e. close to, 2050, taking account of different national circumstances. The Strategy promotes a just and equitable transition and assessment of impacts on States, especially small island developing States and least developed countries. These are sectoral ambitions; they are not a direct instruction that every individual vessel must cut its own absolute emissions by the same percentage. [27] 3. Existing technical measures EEDI - Energy Efficiency Design Index: a design-efficiency requirement for applicable new ships. MARPOL Annex VI regulations 22 and 24 address attained and required EEDI. It compares calculated CO2 output with transport capacity under standardized design conditions. Required performance depends on ship type, size and phase; one percentage does not apply to every ship. [28] EEXI - Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index: regulations 23 and 25 address attained and required EEXI for applicable ships, generally within specified types at 400 GT and above, subject to the regulation's application provisions. The regime applied from 2023, with verification at the relevant first survey on or after 1 January 2023. EEXI is a technical assessment, not an annual operational rating. [28,29] Engineering means of improvement: optimize hull and propeller, reduce resistance, use suitable energy-saving devices, improve propulsion efficiency, and apply engine-power limitation (EPL) or shaft-power limitation (ShaPoLi) where approved. A power limit must include approved arrangements for reserve power use and recording; it must not compromise emergency response or required manoeuvrability. Major changes may require recalculation and approval. Supporting technology: waste-heat recovery, variable-speed drives, efficient pumps/fans, improved auxiliary-load management, wind assistance, air lubrication and appropriate shore power can reduce fuel demand. Their effectiveness depends on ship design and service profile; do not claim a universal fuel-saving percentage. Alternative fuels require compatible storage, machinery, fire protection, training and approval, not merely a change of bunker specification. 4. Existing operational measures: SEEMP, DCS and CII Measure Provision and application What the ship must demonstrate SEEMP Part I Regulation 26; applicable ships 400 GT and A practical management approach above. to improving energy efficiency. SEEMP Part II / IMO Regulation 27; applicable ships 5,000 GT and Approved/documented DCS above. data-collection methodology and annual reporting for verification. SEEMP Part III / CII Regulations 26 and 28; specified ship types Required/attained CII, 5,000 GT and above. implementation plan, monitoring, self-evaluation and corrective action where triggered. CII - Carbon Intensity Indicator: annual operational performance is assessed against a required value and assigned A, B, C, D or E. A ship rated D for three consecutive years or E for one year must develop a corrective-action plan to achieve the required CII and revise the SEEMP for verification. State the application qualifications rather than saying every ship of every type must be CII-rated. [28,29] Formula - conceptual base before permitted corrections: Attained annual operational CII = annual CO2 mass / (ship capacity x annual distance). Capacity is DWT for many cargo ship types and GT for specified passenger/ro-ro types. The DWT-based expression is commonly called AER; the GT-based expression is cgDIST. CII is the regulatory indicator framework, not exclusively AER. EEOI uses actual cargo/transport work and is not the universal mandatory CII formula. Applicable correction factors and voyage adjustments must be taken from the relevant IMO guidelines. [28,50] 2026 clarification: MEPC.412(84) amends CII Guidelines G1 to retain supply-based capacity x total distance travelled for the individual ship's attained CII. Actual transport-work data collected under the enhanced DCS may be analysed during the review; it does not automatically replace the current denominator. [50] Onboard operational measures: coordinate speed and arrival with the Master/company/charterer; use weather routing and suitable trim; clean hull and propeller; maintain combustion and turbocharger performance; balance generators; reduce unnecessary steam, compressed-air and electrical demand; and monitor fuel consumption by operating mode. Safety, navigational judgment and the approved machinery operating envelope take priority. 5. Tightening CII and updated guidance Under MEPC.400(83), the reduction factors relative to the 2019 CII reference line are 13.625% for 2027, 16.250% for 2028, 18.875% for 2029 and 21.500% for 2030. The 2026 factor is 11%. These are not reductions against the Strategy's 2008 baseline. [30,31] The current IMO index also identifies MEPC.411(84) as the 2026 EEDI survey and certification guidelines, superseding MEPC.365(79) and its amendment, and MEPC.414(84) as updated guidance on measurement of engine CH4 and/or N2O emissions. MEPC.385(81) introduced enhanced DCS granularity, with associated implementation guidance. These developments strengthen data quality and assessment; they must not be confused with adoption of the Net-Zero Framework. [31] 6. Fuel life-cycle approach Well-to-wake = well-to-tank + tank-to-wake. Production, processing and transport emissions matter as well as onboard use. CO2, methane and nitrous oxide can change the total GHG result substantially. A fuel with no carbon at the point of use can still have a high upstream footprint; methane slip can undermine LNG's climate benefit. [34] The 2024 LCA Guidelines, MEPC.391(81) replaced MEPC.376(80). Traceable fuel pathways, credible emission factors, certification and measurement are essential to prevent shifting emissions outside the ship's exhaust boundary. The current CII and a life-cycle fuel-GHG metric measure different things; a lower CO2 CII alone does not prove the lowest well-to-wake GHG impact. 7. Economic/market-based measures and their purpose Economic measures aim to make emissions part of the cost of operation, improve the competitiveness of low-GHG options and potentially fund the transition. Common concepts include a levy or contribution per unit of emissions, emissions trading, and a fuel-GHG standard coupled to compliance units or payments. These mechanisms differ and should not all be called a simple bunker tax. IMO's draft Net-Zero Framework, approved at MEPC 83 in April 2025, combines a goal-based marine fuel-GHG intensity standard with an economic compliance mechanism. The approved draft envisaged progressively tighter annual fuel-GHG intensity requirements, surplus/remedial compliance units and an IMO fund supporting incentives and transition objectives. Describe these as draft design features, subject to the subsequent negotiations and adoption process. [30,51] Current status: the October 2025 extraordinary session was adjourned. MEPC 84 continued work toward convergence; ISWG-GHG 22, 1-4 September 2026, was still discussing concerns and proposed changes to the draft. MEPC 85 is scheduled for 30 November-3 December 2026, with resumption of the extraordinary session planned for 4 December, subject to MEPC 85 discussions. As at this document's check date, do not write that the Framework is already adopted or in force, or present the earlier 2027/2028 timetable as settled law. [32,33] Regional measures such as EU schemes are separate legal regimes; they do not establish that an equivalent global IMO charge is already mandatory. Exact draft prices and starting dates are omitted here because unresolved negotiations make them unsuitable as fixed current compliance requirements. 8. Implementation challenges and Chief Engineer's priorities Challenges: alternative-fuel availability and price, infrastructure, investment life, fuel safety, training, life-cycle verification, reliable emissions data and unequal effects on trades and States. Charterparty arrangements can separate the party paying for fuel from the party controlling the ship's design, making cooperation essential. Chief Engineer's hierarchy: establish the applicable statutory requirements and approved limits; assess machinery performance and data accuracy; inform the Master and company of gaps; propose technically justified operational or retrofit measures; secure required company, Flag and RO approvals; train the engine team and implement controls; monitor verified results and revise the SEEMP. Commercial speed and arrival commitments are coordinated through the Master/company, not unilaterally changed by the Chief Engineer. 16-mark writing outline Strategy targets (3); technical measures and regulations (4); operational measures and CII correction trigger (4); life-cycle and economic measures (3); accurate 2026 proposal status and CE implementation (2). The distinction binding now / guidance / draft is more valuable than reproducing an outdated future timetable. Source: MEO_ClassI_Section1_IMO_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 1 Oct 2026

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_01_to_10.pdf

T01-Q09 · MARPOL is considered one of the most flexible and efficient international environmental treaties currently in force. The adoption of a new amendment is enumerated in Article 16 of MARPOL, which establishes multiple ways for amending the convention depending on which part of MARPOL is being amended. Discuss the various ways in which MARPOL may be amended.
1. Why the amendment system makes MARPOL adaptable MARPOL Article 16 permits amendment through consideration by IMO or through a conference of Parties. It then distinguishes how different parts of the instrument are accepted and enter into force. Technical annexes can normally use tacit acceptance, enabling updates without waiting for every Party to send a positive acceptance. More fundamental treaty changes use express acceptance. [35] Three decisions must be separated: adoption of the amendment text; acceptance, either express or deemed; and entry into force after the specified interval. “MEPC approved a draft” is an earlier procedural event and is not any of these completed treaty effects. 2. Route A - Consideration by the Organization: Article 16(2) Proposal and circulation - 16(2)(a): a Party submits a proposed amendment to IMO. The Secretary-General circulates it to all IMO Members and all Parties at least six months before consideration. This gives governments time to examine technical, legal and economic effects. Consideration and participation - 16(2)(b)-(c): the proposal is submitted to the appropriate IMO body, normally MEPC for MARPOL. Parties may participate whether or not they are IMO Members. Participation and treaty voting rights must be distinguished from attendance by observers. Adoption - 16(2)(d): requires a two-thirds majority of Parties present and voting. It does not require two-thirds of all Parties worldwide to attend and vote in favour. Abstentions are not affirmative or negative votes in the ordinary present-and-voting calculation. Communication - 16(2)(e): the Secretary-General sends the adopted amendment to Parties for acceptance. The correct acceptance route then depends on the part of MARPOL being changed. 3. Acceptance route for each part Part being amended Article 16 provision Acceptance rule Articles of the 16(2)(f)(i) Express acceptance by two-thirds Convention of the Parties, whose combined fleets represent at least 50% of world merchant gross tonnage. Both conditions are required. An existing Annex 16(2)(f)(ii) Normally the tacit procedure in (f)(iii). The adopting body may instead decide to require the express two-thirds + 50% route. Appendix to an 16(2)(f)(iii) Deemed accepted after a period Annex fixed at adoption, not less than ten months, unless the prescribed objection threshold is met. Protocol I - incident 16(2)(f)(iv) Same procedures as Annex reporting amendments under (f)(ii) or (f)(iii). Protocol II - 16(2)(f)(v) Same express-acceptance arbitration procedure as amendments to Articles. A new Annex 16(5) Same adoption and entry-into-force procedures as an amendment to an Article. It is not automatically treated as an amendment to an existing technical Annex. Protocol warning: MARPOL's Protocol I and Protocol II are the incident-reporting and arbitration protocols attached to the Convention. They are not the separate 1978 and 1997 Protocols. The latter have their own provisions connecting their amendment arrangements with the MARPOL system. 4. Tacit acceptance: exact objection thresholds Under 16(2)(f)(iii), the amendment fails to achieve deemed acceptance if, within the specified period, objections are communicated by either: (a) Not less than one-third of the Parties; OR (b) Parties representing not less than 50% of world merchant gross tonnage. Either threshold is sufficient. Do not write “one-third and 50%” for blocking tacit acceptance. By contrast, the express acceptance test for Articles requires two-thirds and 50%. This change from AND to OR is an important scoring distinction. An individual Party may also notify that its express approval is necessary before an Annex amendment enters into force for it, under 16(2)(f)(ii). This individual safeguard is different from enough Parties objecting to prevent deemed acceptance generally. 5. Entry into force: Article 16(2)(g) Express-acceptance route - (g)(i): relevant amendments to Articles, Protocol II, or Protocol I/Annexes following the express route enter into force six months after acceptance, for Parties that have declared acceptance. Tacit route - (g)(ii): relevant Protocol I, Annex or appendix amendments enter into force six months after deemed acceptance, for Parties except those that made a permitted declaration of non-acceptance or required express approval under the applicable clause. Article 16: circulation and adoption through the prescribed route Articles / Protocol II / new Annex: express acceptance route Existing Annex / Protocol I: tacit route, or express if so decided Appendix to an Annex: tacit acceptance procedure Acceptance → entry into force: distinguish thresholds and dates Thus ten months + six months = a minimum 16-month period from adoption for the normal minimum tacit timetable, with the separate six-month advance circulation requirement before consideration. The adopting resolution may choose longer periods. Do not describe 16 months as an invariable timetable for every MARPOL amendment. 6. Route B - Conference of Parties: Article 16(3) Convening: on the request of a Party, supported by at least one-third of Parties, IMO shall convene a conference to consider amendments. Adoption: a conference amendment needs a two-thirds majority of Parties present and voting. The Secretary-General communicates it to the Parties for acceptance. Acceptance and entry into force: unless the conference decides otherwise, the procedures in 16(2)(f) and (g) apply. A conference is an alternative forum; it does not automatically eliminate the acceptance stage or make every amendment effective immediately. 7. Additional safeguards and application rules Optional Annexes - 16(4)(a): references to a Party for an Optional Annex amendment mean a Party bound by that Annex. Do not count States that are not bound by the relevant Optional Annex when applying that provision. Party declining an Annex amendment - 16(4)(b): treated as a non-Party only for application of that amendment, rather than automatically ceasing to be a Party to the entire Convention. Structural amendments - 16(6): unless expressly provided otherwise, an amendment relating to ship structure applies to ships with building contracts placed, or in their absence keels laid, on or after its entry-into-force date. Always read any specific override or phase-in in the adopted text. Consistency - 16(7): changes to a protocol or annex must concern its subject matter and remain consistent with the Convention's Articles. Depositary communications - 16(8)-(9): the Secretary-General informs Parties of amendments and effective dates; acceptances and objections are notified in writing and circulated. These records establish legal status, so a news headline cannot replace the resolution and depositary information. 8. Worked hypothetical example Assume 90 relevant Parties, of which 60 are present and vote. Adoption requires 40 affirmative votes. Later express acceptance would require 60 of all 90 Parties, together representing at least 50% of world merchant gross tonnage. A tacit amendment can be blocked by 30 Parties, or by fewer Parties whose fleets together meet the 50% tonnage threshold. These figures are illustrative, not current MARPOL membership statistics. If an amendment is deemed accepted on 1 January, the normal six-month interval makes 1 July the entry-into-force date, subject to the resolution and applicable declarations. The Chief Engineer then checks the ship-specific application date before arranging modification, certification or procedures. 16-mark writing outline Two routes and three legal stages (2); IMO circulation/voting (3); complete acceptance table (5); OR objection threshold and six-month entry interval (3); conference route and additional safeguards (3). Memorize 6 months circulation / two-thirds voting / minimum 10 months tacit acceptance / 6 months to entry without mixing their purposes. Source: MEO_ClassI_Section1_IMO_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 1 Oct 2026

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_01_to_10.pdf

T01-Q10 · Explain the objectives and importance of the IMO’s Convention on Facilitation of International Maritime Traffic (FAL Convention). Discuss recent amendments to the FAL Convention and analyze their impact on modern shipping operations, particularly digitalization and sustainability.
1. Purpose and importance The Convention on Facilitation of International Maritime Traffic, 1965 (FAL Convention) was adopted on 9 April 1965 and entered into force on 5 March 1967. It seeks to reduce unnecessary delay, promote cooperation between governments and harmonize formalities for the arrival, stay and departure of ships, persons and cargo. [36] Without a common framework, customs, immigration, health, security and port authorities can repeatedly request similar data in different formats. FAL reduces that burden while retaining legitimate controls. Faster, predictable clearance improves port use, voyage reliability and crew welfare; it is not permission to bypass customs, quarantine or security requirements. 2. Structure and legal character The Convention has 16 Articles and an Annex. The Annex covers general provisions; arrival/stay/departure of ships; arrival/departure of persons; stowaways; cargo and other articles; public health/quarantine; and miscellaneous matters. Its appendices identify required information and forms. [36,37] Standards establish internationally agreed measures considered necessary and practicable for facilitation. Recommended Practices describe measures considered desirable. Under Article VIII, a government unable to comply with a Standard, or adopting a different requirement, must notify the prescribed differences to IMO. Do not describe a Recommended Practice as having exactly the same legal character as a Standard, or assume notification of differences removes every other treaty obligation. Operational effect: authorities should request justified, standardized data and coordinate procedures. Shipowners must supply accurate information through the accepted system; digital submission does not excuse false declarations or shift all responsibility to the agent. 3. The seven familiar FAL declarations Form Declaration Purpose FAL 1 General Declaration Basic ship/voyage and arrival/departure particulars. FAL 2 Cargo Declaration Cargo information needed by authorities. FAL 3 Ship's Stores Declaration Stores carried for ship use. FAL 4 Crew's Effects Declaration Relevant personal effects of crew. FAL 5 Crew List Crew identity and associated information. FAL 6 Passenger List Passenger particulars. FAL 7 Dangerous Goods Manifest Information on dangerous goods onboard. These names remain useful for examination recall, but modern FAL implementation is based on electronic information exchange. The forms do not mean seven paper copies must routinely be handed in at every port. Appendix 2 concerns paper declarations in exceptional circumstances under Standard 2.11. Other permitted information includes security-related data, advance cargo information, waste-delivery notification and health information under the relevant legal requirements. [37,52] 4. Digitalization milestones already in force Electronic exchange from 9 April 2019: earlier amendments established electronic reporting as a mandatory element of facilitation. This must be distinguished from the later requirement for a single window. Emailing multiple forms separately to many departments does not necessarily deliver a coordinated single-window system. [36] FAL.14(46), adopted 13 May 2022, effective 1 January 2024: comprehensively revised the Annex and made the maritime single-window arrangements mandatory for public authorities. The exact Standards are important: [37] Standard Requirement and meaning 1.3bis Authorities establish, maintain and use electronic information-exchange systems. 1.3ter Publish requirements and allow an adequate transition; at least 12 months from publication of all functional/technical specifications for transition to a new system. Systems must be technologically neutral and compatible. 1.3quart Accommodate alternative means of supplying required information during the transition. 1.3quin Enable all information required under Standard 2.1 to be supplied electronically to a single window. Standard Requirement and meaning 1.3sext Coordinate transmission so information is supplied once and reused to the maximum extent possible. 1.6bis Use internationally agreed electronic-data standards. 1.8 and 1.8.2 Accept suitably authenticated electronic information/declarations under the stated conditions. Practical meaning: a maritime single window is a common electronic entry point through which required arrival, stay and departure information is submitted and made available to the responsible authorities. It is not a new ship statutory certificate. A port-community system may serve wider commercial logistics; interoperability is useful, but the terms are not automatically interchangeable. Ship / agent submits harmonized arrival, stay and departure data Maritime Single Window: single entry; reuse information Authorized authorities: port • customs • immigration • health Authorities process and return decisions through coordinated systems 5. Other amendments: public health, integrity and illicit activities The 2022 amendments incorporate lessons from COVID-19: maintain ship and port operations during a public-health emergency as far as possible; recognize relevant seafarers and port workers as key workers or equivalent; support necessary movement and continuity of supply chains; and coordinate public-health measures. They also address assessment and prevention of corruption associated with ship-port formalities. These provisions improve resilience and accountability as well as efficiency. [37] FAL.15(47), adopted 17 March 2023, effective 1 January 2025: amends Recommended Practice 7.11 to address combating illicit activities in national facilitation programmes. This is a targeted change to a Recommended Practice; do not call it a new mandatory shipboard anti-smuggling certificate. [53] 6. Latest 2026 developments: distinguish legal status Development Status checked on 1 October 2026 Vaccination and medical FAL 50 adopted FAL.19(50) amendments to Recommended Practice 6.24, care during international with entry into force scheduled for 1 January 2028. The amended provision public-health emergencies remains a Recommended Practice. Cybersecurity for maritime FAL 50 approved draft amendments. Planned for adoption at FAL 51 in single windows 2027, with expected entry into force 1 January 2029, subject to completion of the treaty process. Advance Passenger/Crew FAL 50 approved drafts for the same prospective adoption timetable; not Information (API) and current 2026 requirements merely because approved. Booking/Reservation Information (BRI) IMO Strategy on Maritime Approved at FAL 50 for further IMO consideration and eventual Assembly Digitalization adoption; a strategic policy initiative, not itself an in-force amendment to every ship requirement. IMO Compendium Updated at FAL 50 to support harmonized data and interoperability. It is a technical reference model, not a replacement for the Convention. These status distinctions follow IMO's FAL 50 meeting summary and digitalization announcements. The proposed 2029 dates must not be presented as unconditional current law. [38,39,41,54] 7. Effect on modern shipping operations Reduced duplication: common data definitions allow one verified item to serve several authorities, reducing re-keying, conflicting versions and correction work. A change to crew or arrival time can be transmitted consistently. Earlier decisions and better planning: pre-arrival data can allow authorities to assess risk before berthing. A vessel can receive coordinated clearance information and plan services more reliably. The benefit depends on authorities actually sharing data and avoiding parallel paper requirements. Improved risk-based control: standardized cargo, person and ship information helps identify cases needing attention without imposing the same burden on every compliant call. Facilitation and security reinforce each other when data are accurate and accessible to the right authority. Challenges: system outages, cyberattack, poor connectivity, incompatible formats, data quality, privacy, authentication and unequal digital capability. Authorities and operators need training, access control, audit trails, tested contingency arrangements and clear responsibilities. Single submission does not mean unrestricted access to personal or commercial data. 8. Sustainability: explain the mechanism, not an invented emission limit Digital formalities can reduce paper use and administrative travel, but the larger potential benefit is better port-call coordination. Reliable berth/service information can support a suitable arrival speed and reduce unnecessary waiting, auxiliary-engine running and associated emissions. Standardized waste notifications can improve reception-facility planning and reduce failed deliveries. [39] These are operational benefits inferred from improved information and coordination; FAL does not itself prescribe a universal percentage fuel saving or replace MARPOL's GHG requirements. Benefits require cooperation among port, terminal, pilotage, tug, ship and commercial stakeholders. A ship cannot safely slow down merely because a software system forecasts a later berth. 9. Chief Engineer's role and reporting hierarchy The Master retains overall responsibility for ship declarations and clearance, assisted by the company and authorized agent. The Chief Engineer supplies verified machinery-related information, relevant stores/bunker details, waste quantities and intended reception needs. Reconcile figures with tank soundings, logbooks and record books before submission. Notify the Master promptly of changes affecting arrival, services, pollution-prevention arrangements or technical readiness. Follow the approved company process for authenticated transmission and preserve acknowledgements. During a system failure, use the authority's accepted contingency route through the Master/agent; do not invent clearance or delete records. Implement relevant cyber controls under the SMS and train engine staff handling data. 16-mark writing outline Objectives, dates and structure (3); Standards versus Recommended Practices and forms (3); 2024 single-window requirements with 1.3quin/1.3sext (4); 2025/2026 developments with legal status (3); sustainability, challenges and CE contribution (3). Final exam distinction: 2019 electronic exchange; 2024 mandatory single-window arrangements; 2025 RP 7.11 change; 2026 adopted/approved developments with later prospective dates. Source: MEO_ClassI_Section1_IMO_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 1 Oct 2026

GHG, Decarbonisation & CII (10)
T02-Q01 · What is Decarbonisation in shipping? Briefly discuss the IMO decarbonisation ambitions and pathways with focus on short-term, mid-term and long-term goals. Explain the two short-term measures approved by IMO for implementation with their guidelines.
1. Meaning and purpose Decarbonisation in shipping means progressively reducing greenhouse-gas emissions from maritime transport, ultimately reaching net-zero GHG emissions. It combines lower energy demand, more efficient ships and operations, and a transition to fuels and energy with low or near-zero life-cycle emissions. The objective is broader than reducing visible smoke, sulphur or only the carbon dioxide leaving an exhaust funnel. Methane leakage and slip, nitrous oxide, and emissions from producing the fuel also matter. [1,26] Efficiency and decarbonisation are related but different. A ship may use less fuel per tonne-mile while its total annual emissions increase because it performs more voyages. Conversely, replacing fossil fuel with a verified low-GHG fuel can reduce life-cycle emissions even without changing the ship's hydrodynamic efficiency. The strategy therefore includes both intensity and absolute-emission ambitions. 2. Current IMO ambitions: write the baseline with every figure The 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, resolution MEPC.377(80), superseded the Initial Strategy of 2018. These are international-shipping sector ambitions, not identical percentage limits imposed on each individual ship. Their translation into ship obligations requires the relevant legal measures. [1] Item Current ambition or indicative checkpoint New ships Further improve energy efficiency, including strengthening design-efficiency requirements. CO2 intensity by 2030 Reduce CO2 per transport work by at least 40% compared with 2008, as an average across international shipping. Zero/near-zero energy by Such technologies, fuels and/or energy sources should represent at least 5%, 2030 striving for 10%, of energy used by international shipping. This is not a percentage of ships. Total annual GHG by 2030 Reduce by at least 20%, striving for 30%, compared with 2008. Total annual GHG by 2040 Reduce by at least 70%, striving for 80%, compared with 2008. Ultimate ambition Net-zero GHG emissions by or around, i.e. close to, 2050, taking different national circumstances into account. Why well-to-wake matters: moving emissions from the ship to a shore-based hydrogen or ammonia factory does not achieve genuine decarbonisation. The strategy uses a life-cycle perspective to avoid this transfer of emissions. [1,26] 3. Short-, mid- and long-term pathways Short-term measures: improve the efficiency of the existing fleet and new ships through design standards, EEXI, CII, SEEMP, data collection, speed optimization, maintenance and better voyage planning. In the 2023 Strategy, short-term measures are those finalized and agreed between 2018 and 2023; they continue operating after that period. Their title does not mean they expire in 2023. [1] Mid-term measures: develop a combined package with a technical fuel-GHG-intensity standard and an economic GHG-pricing element. Support fuel availability, infrastructure, training, research and an equitable transition. The original timetable envisaged agreement by 2025, but the Net-Zero Framework negotiations remained unfinished at the review date of this PDF. The timetable in an older strategy document must not be presented as evidence of adoption. [1,27,31] Long-term pathway: scale verified zero/near-zero-GHG energy, replace or convert unsuitable tonnage, expand global bunkering networks and deliver the 2040 and 2050 ambitions. Further measures beyond 2030 are to be developed as part of the evolving strategy, including its planned 2028 review. Technology selection depends on the trade, vessel, fuel pathway, cost and safe operation. [1] Reduce energy demand Improve technical and operating efficiency Replace high-GHG energy with verified low-GHG pathways Measure the complete life-cycle result 4. First short-term measure: EEXI The Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index is a technical efficiency measure. MARPOL Annex VI regulations 23 and 25 require an attained value and comparison against the applicable required value. Lower values indicate better technical CO2 efficiency. The general chapter threshold is 400 GT, but EEXI application depends on the specified ship category, propulsion provisions, capacity thresholds and exceptions; it is not a blanket numerical requirement for every ship above 400 GT. [2] Compliance principle: attained EEXI must not exceed required EEXI. The attained value is based on certified or accepted power, specific fuel consumption, fuel-to-CO2 conversion factors, capacity and reference speed, with applicable corrections. It is not obtained from the previous year's noon reports. A compliant attained EEDI may be accepted as attained EEXI in accordance with regulation 23. [2,3] Technical remedies include EPL or SHaPoLi, propeller improvement, verified energy-saving devices and approved machinery changes. The calculation and survey guidelines are MEPC.350(78) and MEPC.351(78), replacing the original MEPC.333(76) and MEPC.334(76). Power limitation follows MEPC.335(76), as amended by MEPC.375(80) and MEPC.390(81). [3-7] 5. Second short-term measure: operational CII The Carbon Intensity Indicator under regulation 28 measures the ship's annual operational CO2 intensity. It applies to the specified ship types of 5,000 GT and above, subject to chapter exclusions. It uses annual fuel/emissions and transport-work proxy data and produces a rating A, B, C, D or E. C is moderate; D inferior; E inferior to D. [2,8,11] The technical package is G1 calculation; G2 reference lines; G3 annual reduction factors; G4 ratings; G5 corrections/voyage adjustments. In 2026, G1 includes MEPC.412(84) and G3 includes MEPC.400(83). SEEMP Part III sets the ship's three-year implementation plan. A single E, or D in three consecutive years, triggers a verified corrective-action plan within the revised SEEMP. [8-16] EEXI controls the certified technical condition; CII measures annual operation. A ship can meet EEXI and still obtain a poor CII because of excessive fuel consumption, port waiting, adverse operating patterns or inadequate maintenance. It needs both a compliant technical baseline and effective operational management. 6. Dates, shipboard evidence and Chief Engineer's role MEPC.328(76) was adopted in June 2021; the revised Annex VI entered into force on 1 November 2022. EEXI/CII implementation began 1 January 2023. EEXI verification was linked to the first relevant annual, intermediate or renewal IAPP survey, or initial IEE survey, on/after that date. First-year 2023 operational CII was reported and rated in 2024. [2,43] As Chief Engineer, I would verify the EEXI Technical File and limitation settings, maintain reliable fuel and machinery data, monitor consumption against operating conditions, and train engineers on the approved system. The Master controls navigational safety and voyage decisions; the Chief Engineer advises on machinery limits; the company supplies resources and coordinates verification with the Flag Administration or authorized RO. Reserve power must remain available for legitimate safety needs under the approved procedure. [2,5-7] 16-mark writing outline Definition and distinction between efficiency and absolute reduction (2); current ambitions and baselines (4); pathways and legal status (3); EEXI (3); CII/SEEMP (3); Chief Engineer's implementation (1). These allocations are revision guidance, not an official marking scheme. Avoid: the superseded “50% GHG reduction by 2050” as the current ambition; 5% of ships instead of energy; treating EEXI as an annual fuel-consumption index; or describing the draft Net-Zero Framework as already mandatory. T02-Q02 · What are the key objectives and measures outlined in IMO’s revised strategy for the reduction of GHG emissions from ships? Discuss short-term, mid-term, and long-term goals and the potential impact on the maritime industry. Discuss the revised GHG strategy and amendments being considered under MARPOL Annex VI, including the new Chapter-V, to achieve the decarbonisation goal and transition to a Net Zero future in shipping.
1. Introduction and legal distinction The revised strategy is the 2023 IMO Strategy on Reduction of GHG Emissions from Ships, adopted by MEPC.377(80). It guides international shipping towards net-zero GHG emissions through efficiency, fuel transition, investment and equitable implementation. A strategy sets policy direction; a MARPOL amendment creates treaty obligations only after the required adoption, acceptance and entry-into-force process. [1,2] The question's reference to a new Chapter V concerns the proposed Net-Zero Framework in MARPOL Annex VI. As reviewed on 2 October 2026, the framework remains under negotiation. Do not write that the proposed chapter is already in force. [29-31] 2. Objectives and quantified ambitions The strategy seeks to contribute to global climate action, identify effective shipping-sector measures, support research and investment, and account for impacts on States. It recognizes shipping's role in international trade and the need for a just and equitable transition, particularly for small island developing States (SIDS) and least developed countries (LDCs). [1] Horizon Target to reproduce accurately 2030 intensity At least 40% reduction in CO2 per transport work versus 2008. 2030 energy transition At least 5%, striving for 10%, zero/near-zero-GHG technologies, fuels and/or energy sources, measured by energy used. 2030 absolute checkpoint At least 20%, striving for 30%, total annual GHG reduction versus 2008. 2040 absolute checkpoint At least 70%, striving for 80%, total annual GHG reduction versus 2008. 2050 ambition Net-zero by or around, i.e. close to, 2050. The words “at least” and “striving for” distinguish the minimum stated ambition from the higher aspiration. The 2008 strategy baseline is different from the 2019 CII reference line. Confusing these baselines changes the meaning of an answer. [1,10] 3. Existing short-term regulatory foundation EEDI under regulations 22 and 24 addresses design efficiency of applicable new ships. EEXI under regulations 23 and 25 establishes a technical-efficiency requirement for applicable existing ships. SEEMP, regulation 26, provides the management plan; DCS, regulation 27, supplies verified annual fuel data; and CII, regulation 28, gives an annual operating-intensity rating. Together they establish a cycle of technical compliance, measurement and improvement. [2] A technical measure is insufficient by itself. For example, fitting an approved power limiter establishes a lower certified power basis, but poor voyage planning and long port waits can still produce a poor annual CII. Similarly, a favourable CII does not establish that a fuel has a low upstream footprint. Each measure answers a different question. 4. Proposed mid-term package: two complementary elements Technical element - fuel GHG intensity: progressively reduce GHG emissions per unit of energy used by a ship, evaluated on a well-to-wake basis. This pushes the fuel supply and ship operation towards genuinely lower-emission energy pathways, including production emissions. It differs from CII, whose present regulatory metric is operational CO2 per capacity-distance. [1,26] Economic element - GHG pricing: place an economic cost on emissions or a compliance deficit and create incentives for lower-GHG alternatives. Revenue or rewards can support the energy transition, subject to the final scheme. A price signal helps reduce the gap between conventional fuel costs and cleaner alternatives. The detailed mechanism and allocation of funds remain matters for the negotiated legal text. [1,27-29] The proposed Chapter V framework: the 2025 approved draft combined GHG fuel-intensity requirements with economic compliance arrangements, including units associated with over-compliance/deficits and support for zero/near-zero-GHG energy. Explain this as a draft design, not as an existing worldwide carbon tax. Numerical prices and future compliance dates should not be quoted as settled obligations while adoption is unresolved. [29,31] 5. What happened to the adoption timetable? Stage Correct status MEPC 80, July 2023 Revised strategy adopted; policy timetable established. MEPC 81, March 2024 Possible outline of the Net-Zero Framework agreed for further development. MEPC 83, April 2025 Draft framework approved for the subsequent adoption process. Approval was not adoption. Extraordinary MEPC, October Adoption discussions adjourned. 2025 MEPC 84 and ISWG-GHG 22, Further consideration of concerns and proposed changes continued. 2026 Position at 2 October 2026 Negotiations ongoing. MEPC 85 is scheduled for 30 November-3 December 2026; resumption of the extraordinary session is scheduled for 4 December, subject to MEPC 85. These are planned meetings, not confirmed entry-into-force dates. This status is based on IMO's official meeting records and explanatory updates, labelled grey literature in the reference register. [27,29-31] 6. Long-term pathway and implications for industry Fleet investment: owners must evaluate whether to retrofit, change fuels or renew tonnage. Choices should consider remaining service life, cargo capacity lost to fuel tanks, maintenance, route, fuel supply, safety approval and whole-life cost. A nominally “fuel-ready” design must be checked against the actual work and investment required for conversion. Fuel supply and ports: new supply chains need verified production pathways, storage, safe transfer systems and competent personnel. Energy density affects endurance and bunker space. Alternative fuels can introduce toxicity, low-flashpoint, cryogenic or asphyxiation hazards. Safety assessment and crew competence must develop alongside environmental performance. [1,26] Commercial operation: compliance costs influence freight, charter negotiations and access to finance. Owners may fund the ship while charterers influence speed, cargo and route. Agreements need clear data sharing, operating limits and responsibility for compliance actions. A ship-specific business case should compare energy and emissions over the actual service profile rather than a single test point. Equity: remote economies may experience higher transport costs and have fewer alternative-fuel suppliers. Impact assessment, technical cooperation and access to finance are therefore central to implementation, rather than optional additions. The strategy expressly considers transport dependency, food security, geographic remoteness and socio-economic development. [1] 7. Chief Engineer's implementation priorities I would establish an accurate emissions and energy baseline, assess retrofit and fuel options with the superintendent, verify maker/Flag/RO requirements, and prepare training and maintenance changes. All changes affecting fuel systems, machinery safety or certification must follow the approved change-management process. I would give the Master clear machinery limits and keep company management informed of cost, reliability and data-quality risks. Shipboard priorities now are existing EEXI, CII, SEEMP and DCS compliance plus preparation for future measures. Keep draft requirements in a regulatory watch list and avoid treating an anticipated implementation date as a binding shipboard deadline. 16-mark writing outline Strategy/legal distinction (2); ambitions with baselines (4); existing short-term measures (3); proposed technical/economic package and Chapter V (3); current negotiating status (2); industry and CE implications (2). Examiner follow-up: “Does approval at MEPC make Chapter V mandatory?” No. Approval of the draft precedes adoption and the treaty acceptance/entry-into-force process. Source: MEO_ClassI_Section2_GHG_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 2 Oct 2026

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_11_to_20.pdf

T02-Q03 · IMO adopted amendments to MARPOL Annex VI in MEPC 76 as part of short-term GHG measures, introducing EEXI and operational CII. Discuss in detail the IMO guidelines for calculation, survey and certification of EEXI and calculation of CII.
1. Regulatory and guideline map MEPC.328(76) introduced the revised short-term framework. Always distinguish the mandatory MARPOL regulations from the guidelines used to calculate and verify compliance. [2] Subject Regulation / current supporting reference Attained and required EEXI Regulations 23 and 25; calculation MEPC.350(78). EEXI survey Regulation 5.4.7; MEPC.351(78). Power limitation MEPC.335(76) as amended by MEPC.375(80) and MEPC.390(81). Annual CII Regulation 28; G1 MEPC.352(78), amended by MEPC.412(84). CII reference line / reduction / G2 MEPC.353(78); G3 MEPC.338(76), amended by MEPC.400(83); G4 rating MEPC.354(78). Adjustments and management G5 MEPC.355(78); SEEMP guidelines MEPC.395(82), as amended, consolidated in MEPC.1/Circ.925. 2. Scope and timing EEXI applies to specified categories within Annex VI Chapter 4, generally 400 GT and above, subject to regulation 19 and the capacity thresholds in regulation 25. CII applies to specified categories of 5,000 GT and above, including bulk carriers, tankers, gas/LNG carriers, container ships, general and refrigerated cargo ships, combination carriers and specified passenger/ro-ro categories. Polar Code category A ships are excluded from regulations 23, 25 and 28 under regulation 19.3. Other propulsion and voyage exclusions must be checked against the actual ship. [2] EEXI verification was due at the first relevant annual, intermediate or renewal IAPP survey, or initial IEE survey, on or after 1 January 2023. It is normally a one-time technical verification, with further consideration where a major conversion or relevant change requires recalculation. Annual operational CII is a separate recurring obligation. [2,4] 3. EEXI calculation: understand numerator and denominator For a conventional diesel cargo ship, the following is an explanatory simplified expression, not a substitute for the full guideline equation: Attained EEXI ≈ [sum(P_ME × SFC_ME × C_F,ME) + P_AE × SFC_AE × C_F,AE - accepted efficiency credits] / [Capacity × V_ref × applicable correction factors]. The numerator represents a standardized CO2 emission rate in grams per hour. The denominator represents a standardized capacity-distance rate. With capacity in tonnes and speed in nautical miles per hour, the result is g CO2/(tonne·nautical mile). The full equation includes machinery-specific terms, shaft motor/generator treatment and prescribed correction factors. [3] Parameter Meaning and verification point P_ME Reference main-engine power. For a straightforward conventional installation without limitation, normally 75% MCR under the relevant calculation provisions. P_ME with overridable For the conventional case, use the lower of 83% of MCR_lim and 75% of EPL/SHaPoLi original MCR. Special LNG/electric propulsion provisions differ. SFC Specific fuel consumption in g/kWh at the appropriate calculation point. Use certified/accepted data; do not assume an unchanged SFC after limiting power. C_F Mass conversion factor from fuel to CO2. Select the prescribed factor for the fuel and applicable calculation method. Capacity The guideline-defined capacity for the ship type. It is not automatically today's cargo loaded. V_ref Reference speed at the calculation power and prescribed loading/sea conditions, supported by accepted speed-power evidence. It is not arbitrary service speed. Required EEXI = (1 - Y/100) × EEDI reference-line value. The reduction factor Y comes from regulation 25, table 3, according to ship type and size. It is not the annual CII reduction factor Z. A compliant attained EEDI may be used as attained EEXI as permitted by regulation 23. [2,3] 4. Worked illustrative EEXI calculation Assume a conventional bulk carrier, 50,000 DWT, original MCR 10,000 kW, approved limited power 7,000 kW, verified reference speed 12 knots, main-engine SFC 175 g/kWh, auxiliary reference power 400 kW, auxiliary SFC 200 g/kWh, and fuel factor 3.114. For teaching only, assume correction factors are unity and no efficiency credits. P_ME = min(0.83 × 7,000; 0.75 × 10,000) = 5,810 kW. CO2 rate = [(5,810 × 175) + (400 × 200)] × 3.114 = 3,415,276.5 g/h. Attained EEXI = 3,415,276.5 / (50,000 × 12) = 5.692 g CO2/(t·nm). For this bulk-carrier size, Y = 20% and the reference line is 961.79 × DWT^(-0.477). Thus required EEXI ≈ 0.8 × 961.79 × 50,000^(-0.477) ≈ 4.41 g CO2/(t·nm). The illustrative ship does not comply, because 5.692 > 4.41. Further approved improvements and recalculation are needed. The numerical inputs are hypothetical; they are not a ship approval. [2,3] Why this example matters: reducing power cannot be assessed by changing only the numerator. V_ref and the corresponding SFC must also be consistent with the new reference point. A very low limit may cause unacceptable operating restrictions even if the index improves. 5. EEXI Technical File and verification The file should identify the ship, machinery ratings, capacity, reference speed, fuel factors, SFC evidence, full calculation, corrections and any energy-saving devices. Include the speed-power curve and supporting trial/model/approved numerical or in-service measurement evidence, as appropriate. The verifier checks that the input data and methodology are acceptable and that the attained value meets the applicable required value. [3,4] Speed evidence is a frequent examination point. Existing ships may not possess modern EEDI trial records. The guidelines permit defined alternative approaches, including accepted estimated curves, approved in-service measurements or prescribed approximations. A new full-scale trial is not universally required, but an unsupported speed assumption is unacceptable. IACS Recommendations 172 Rev.2 and 173 assist implementation and numerical derivation of V_ref. [3,4,21,22] After any required modification, the Administration or authorized RO verifies the installed arrangement and associated documents. For EPL/SHaPoLi, check setting/sealing or access control, reserve-power availability, recording and the Onboard Management Manual (OMM). The International Energy Efficiency Certificate and its supplement record the verified position. EEXI should not be confused with an annual CII Statement of Compliance. [2,4-7] Establish applicability and required EEXI Compile approved technical inputs and calculate attained EEXI Implement any required approved modifications Flag or authorized RO verifies file and installation Maintain IEE documentation and approved onboard arrangements 6. CII calculation and rating procedure For the basic unadjusted calculation: M = sum(FC_j × C_F,j), converting fuel mass into grams of CO2. Attained annual operational CII = M / (Capacity × total annual distance). Under MEPC.412(84), the denominator remains a supply-based capacity-distance proxy. Use DWT for bulk, tanker, container, gas/LNG, general/refrigerated cargo and combination carriers; use GT for cruise passenger and the specified ro-ro categories. Total distance includes the under-way and not-under-way components as defined in the updated reporting guidance. Apply only eligible G5 factors and voyage adjustments, with evidence. [8,12,13,15] Required annual CII = (1 - Z/100) × CII reference line, with the ship-type coefficients in G2 and the annual Z value in G3. Compare attained/required CII using G4 ship-type-specific rating boundaries. A is major superior, B minor superior, C moderate, D minor inferior and E inferior performance. Do not use one set of percentage boundaries for every ship type. [9-11] Example: a 50,000-DWT bulk carrier emits 10,000 tonnes CO2 and travels 50,000 nm in the year. With no adjustments, CII = 10,000 × 1,000,000 / (50,000 × 50,000) = 4.00 g CO2/(dwt·nm). If the verified reference-line value is hypothetically 5.00, its 2026 required CII = 5.00 × 0.89 = 4.45. A rating still requires the applicable G4 boundary table; “below required” alone does not establish an A rating. 7. Annual reporting, corrective action and documents Report the previous calendar year's fuel data and attained CII within three months, normally 31 March, to the Administration/authorized organization. Verification and the Statement of Compliance (SoC) are due no later than five months from the beginning of the year, normally 31 May. The annual SoC remains valid through the first five months of the following year and must be retained onboard for at least five years. [2] E once or D three consecutive years: revise SEEMP Part III with a corrective-action plan and submit it for verification, preferably with the CII report and no later than one month after reporting the attained annual CII. Under the current IMO UI, a poor rating for year YYYY leads to plan verification in YYYY+1, targeting the required CII for YYYY+2. For example, an E for 2025 requires action planning/verification in 2026 targeting 2027. This does not justify postponing feasible improvements. [2,20] 16-mark writing outline Regulations and scope (2); EEXI equation/inputs (4); required value and example (2); technical file, survey and IEE certification (3); CII formula, reference and rating (3); deadlines/corrective action (2). Avoid: 75% of limited MCR as the universal EEXI power rule; confusing required EEXI with EEDI phase dates; replacing CII capacity with actual cargo without authority; or issuing yourself a rating from an invented boundary. T02-Q04 · IMO introduced EEXI requirements under MARPOL Annex VI Chapter IV for existing vessels from 2023. Explain the various design features adopted to comply with the present EEXI meeting Phase 2 (20–30% reduction).
1. Correct the question's premise before answering The reference to “EEXI meeting Phase 2 (20-30%)” is an oversimplification. EEDI has phased requirements for new ships; EEXI has its own ship-type/size reduction-factor table under MARPOL Annex VI regulation 25. Some EEXI reductions are 20-30%, but others are 5%, 15%, 35%, 45% or 50%, with interpolation for some smaller ships. An examiner expects the applicable table, not a universal percentage. [2] Example category EEXI reduction factor Y Bulk carrier or tanker, 20,000 20% to below 200,000 DWT Bulk carrier or tanker, 200,000 15% DWT and above Gas carrier, 15,000 DWT and 30% above LNG carrier, 10,000 DWT and 30% above Container ship, 40,000 to 30% below 80,000 DWT Container ship, 200,000 DWT 50% and above These selected examples show why ship type, size and statutory reference line must be established first. Smaller capacity thresholds, interpolation and exclusions are stated in the full regulation. If answering the EEDI variant of this question, use regulation 24 and the ship's applicable EEDI phase instead. [2] 2. Engineering principle: improve the complete ratio EEXI compares standardized CO2 emission rate with standardized transport capacity and reference speed. Measures can lower the power/fuel input, improve propulsion efficiency, change the certified fuel/engine combination, or provide a recognized energy-efficiency credit. Only improvements supported by the accepted calculation and verification method can be credited. [3,4] For a retrofit, first collect the existing EEXI/EEDI file, engine certificates, trial data, propeller information, electrical load table and actual operating profile. Establish the compliance gap and compare each option against capital cost, fuel saving, downtime, remaining life, operational restrictions and safety. A quoted percentage fuel saving is not automatically the same percentage improvement in attained EEXI. 3. Main technical measures, with benefits and limits Measure How it can improve efficiency Verification / practical limitation Engine Power Limits available engine output during ordinary Approved setting, OMM, access Limitation (EPL) operation, giving an accepted lower control and power reserve. Lower reference-power basis. power also changes reference speed and may affect schedule capability.
