A lower EEXI does not prove a lower well-to-wake footprint. The means of recognizing such improvements are governed by MEPC.350(78), MEPC.351(78) and the IMO guidance on innovative technologies and in-service performance measurements. IACS Recommendations 172 and 173 support calculation and verification. The table explains engineering mechanisms; actual savings are deliberately not invented. [3,4,21,22,32] Hull cleaning, propeller polishing and operational trim optimization normally improve real fuel consumption and CII. They should not be claimed automatically as a new permanent EEXI credit. Any altered technical value must be demonstrated through an accepted method and recorded in the verified file. 4. EPL/SHaPoLi: essential safety provisions The power reserve is retained for safety. It must be accessible by deliberate action of the Master or officer in charge of the navigational watch, under the approved arrangement. The Chief Engineer ensures the machinery and override procedure are understood and immediately supports the bridge. A commercial desire to recover time is not by itself a safety justification for using reserve power. [5-7] The OMM should explain limitation settings, operation, override, recording, reset and verification arrangements. The team must know the available restricted and unrestricted power and how these affect manoeuvring. Where applicable, the pilot card, wheelhouse poster and manoeuvring booklet must show both conditions. [7] Pre-emptive un-limiting differs from actually using reserve power. If an anticipated hazard leads to removal of the limit but reserve power is not used, record the event and power used in the required logs and reset promptly after the need passes. If reserve power is used, follow the applicable recording and reporting requirements in the amended guidance, including the reason and circumstances. [5-7] A poorly selected limit can leave the vessel unable to maintain schedule or unable to pass a restricted operating range satisfactorily. Safety evaluation must therefore include adverse-weather operation, manoeuvring, shafting limits, turbocharger/blower behaviour and minimum stable operating conditions. The approved safety reserve is not a substitute for sound selection of the normal limit. 5. Chief Engineer's retrofit sequence I would first inform and consult the Master and superintendent, identify the statutory gap and obtain maker/RO input before choosing equipment. I would evaluate practical access, maintenance, spares, reliability and compatibility with the existing plant. The company would authorize investment and arrange Flag/RO approvals within their respective powers. After installation, conduct the approved tests and verification, update technical files, machinery records and manoeuvring information, and train the watchkeepers. Monitor both actual fuel saving and reliability after return to service. A technically compliant modification is successful only if the ship can operate safely and economically within its intended trade. 16-mark writing outline Correct Phase 2 wording/applicability (2); EEXI improvement principle (2); six well-explained technical options (6); EPL/SHaPoLi safety and documentation (3); verification and CE implementation (3). Avoid: claiming all ships require a 20-30% reduction; adding unverified device savings; lowering power without updating V_ref; treating routine slow steaming as equivalent to an approved power-limitation installation. T02-Q05 · Explain the concept of CII rating and critically discuss the role of measuring metrics AER and EEOI. What measures are required to be taken by a vessel to improve its CII ratings? Discuss the concept of CII as adopted in MARPOL and the commercial impact of CII in shipping business, along with shortcomings of the current CII concept and remedial measures adopted by the industry.
1. Meaning and legal basis CII is the annual operational carbon-intensity framework under MARPOL Annex VI regulation 28. It compares a ship's attained annual CO2 intensity with a progressively more demanding required value, producing a rating from A to E. It applies to the specified ship types of 5,000 GT and above, subject to applicable exclusions. It is an annual operating indicator, distinct from the technical EEXI. [2] Lower attained CII means less counted CO2 per unit of the prescribed capacity-distance denominator. This helps identify efficiency trends, but its interpretation must account for the denominator and operating pattern. 2. Correct relationship between CII, AER and EEOI Metric Basic expression What it tells you AER: Annual Annual CO2 / (DWT × annual distance) A supply-based indicator: how Efficiency Ratio efficiently the ship's available deadweight capacity is moved. It does not measure cargo actually carried. GT-based CII / Annual CO2 / (GT × annual distance) The corresponding prescribed cgDIST form capacity-distance approach for cruise passenger and specified ro-ro ship categories. GT is a tonnage measure, not tonnes of cargo. EEOI: Energy CO2 over selected voyages / sum(actual A demand-based indicator: Efficiency cargo carried × voyage distance) emissions per actual transport work. Operational The appropriate cargo/service unit Indicator follows the EEOI guidance. CII is not universally identical to AER. AER describes the DWT-based form used for many cargo ships. The CII system also prescribes a GT-based form for other categories and applies rating rules, reference lines and allowed corrections. EEOI is not the mandatory CII denominator. [8,13,25] 2026 interpretation: MEPC.412(84) expressly retains capacity multiplied by total distance as the proxy for individual-ship regulatory CII. Actual transport work newly reported under enhanced DCS can inform review and analysis; it has not automatically replaced the CII denominator. [13] 3. Example showing why AER and EEOI differ Two identical 50,000-DWT ships each travel 10,000 nm and emit 2,000 tonnes CO2. Ship A carries 40,000 tonnes of cargo throughout; Ship B carries 20,000 tonnes, for this simplified example. AER of both = 2,000 × 1,000,000 / (50,000 × 10,000) = 4 g CO2/(dwt·nm). EEOI of A = 5 g CO2/(cargo tonne·nm); EEOI of B = 10 g CO2/(cargo tonne·nm). The same AER hides different utilization of cargo capacity. In practice the ships' fuel consumption may differ with loading, but the example deliberately holds it constant to isolate the denominator effect. For a multi-voyage EEOI, sum emissions and actual transport work consistently; ballast consumption cannot simply be discarded to improve the result. [25] 4. Required CII, tightening factors and ratings Required CII = (1 - Z/100) × 2019 CII reference-line value. The reference line and rating boundaries depend on ship type and size. The reductions in G3 are relative to the 2019 reference line, not consecutive percentage reductions from the preceding year's actual performance. [9-11] Year Z reduction 2023 / 2024 / 2025 / 2026 5% / 7% / 9% / 11% 2027 13.625% 2028 16.250% 2029 18.875% 2030 21.500% A: major superior; B: minor superior; C: moderate; D: minor inferior; E: inferior. The required value lies within the moderate rating region; rating is obtained from the applicable G4 boundaries. A ship can retain the same attained CII but receive a worse rating as the required value tightens. [10,11] 5. Measures to improve performance Voyage and speed optimization: agree an achievable speed with the Master and company, considering weather, safe navigation, machinery limitations and arrival windows. Avoid high-speed passage followed by unnecessary anchorage where a reliable just-in-time arrangement is possible. Safety-related route and speed decisions remain with the Master. Hull and propulsion condition: control fouling, maintain the propeller and evaluate practical trim/draft optimization. Compare performance at similar displacement, weather and speed so that apparent deterioration is not confused with different operating conditions. Machinery efficiency: monitor engine condition, injector performance, scavenge-air systems, heat exchangers and combustion balance. Optimize generator loading and stop genuinely unnecessary consumers while retaining required redundancy. Repair steam and compressed-air leaks and review heating/refrigeration demand. The objective is lower fuel use for the same useful service, rather than sacrificing essential equipment. Port and cargo operation: reduce avoidable waiting and auxiliary demand through coordination with the agent, terminal and company. Evaluate shore power where available and suitable. Cargo heating, refrigeration, discharge pumping and safety requirements must still be fulfilled. Any CII correction must meet G5 criteria; it is not a blanket exclusion of port fuel. [12,14] Fuel selection: use only the emission factors and biofuel treatment permitted by the applicable guidelines and supporting sustainability evidence. Do not assign zero CO2 to every fuel sold as “bio” or “green”. The current Annex VI document index lists MEPC.1/Circ.905/Rev.1; use the verified fuel-specific procedure through the company and verifier. [32] Monthly management: track actual and forecast annual CII, explain deviations, assign actions and verify completion. A monthly figure is a useful management forecast; the statutory rating is based on the verified annual dataset. Reconcile fuel meters, tank inventories and delivery records before relying on a favourable trend. 6. Critical limitations and remedies Limitation Explanation and practical response Available capacity rather AER can obscure utilization. Use EEOI/actual transport work as an additional than cargo moved management indicator, without altering the statutory formula. Port waiting and low Hotel and cargo-service emissions continue while distance may be small. annual distance Improve port coordination and use only authorized adjustments. Weather, trade and service Similar ships may face very different demand. Use normalized engineering differences analysis alongside CII when assessing machinery efficiency. Perverse distance Additional low-intensity sailing can mathematically dilute a ratio while incentive increasing total emissions. Track total CO2 and cargo service as well as CII; do not add unnecessary voyages. CO2 versus complete Methane slip or upstream emissions may not be reflected in the current CII climate impact number. Use verified WtW assessment for fuel decisions. Split incentives A charterer may control speed and employment while the owner provides equipment. Contractual cooperation and data sharing are essential. Data quality and Inaccurate meters or unsupported exclusions can distort ratings. Maintain corrections traceability and verifier-approved methods. The criticism should be balanced: a simple, auditable global indicator has practical value, but it should be supplemented by actual transport work, absolute emissions and ship-specific engineering analysis. The G1 amendments confirm that these issues remain under review. [13,25] 7. Commercial and compliance consequences Poor performance may affect charterer selection, employment flexibility, retrofit expenditure and asset value; these are commercial effects, not automatic MARPOL penalties of a fixed amount. A vessel may need to revise its operating profile or invest in equipment to remain competitive. Charter provisions should allocate cooperation duties without overriding the Master's safety authority. E for one year or D for three consecutive years requires a corrective-action plan in the revised SEEMP. Submit for verification preferably with the annual report, no later than one month after reporting the CII. Under the current IMO UI, a rating for year YYYY leads to verification in YYYY+1 and a plan targeting YYYY+2. A poor letter rating does not itself create an automatic worldwide trading ban, but failure to fulfil the associated MARPOL obligations is a compliance deficiency. [2,20] 16-mark writing outline Definition/applicability (2); formulas and AER/EEOI distinction (4); rating/reduction factors (2); improvement measures (3); limitations and remedies (3); commercial/corrective consequences (2). Memory line: EEXI = technical condition; CII = annual rating; AER = DWT-based proxy; EEOI = actual cargo work. Source: MEO_ClassI_Section2_GHG_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 2 Oct 2026

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_11_to_20.pdf

T02-Q06 · What are the main objectives and types of Market-Based Measures (MBMs) discussed during MEPC 81, and how do they contribute to reducing GHG emissions from international shipping? Describe implementation challenges and potential impacts on the shipping industry, and alignment with global climate goals.
1. Meaning and the correct historical context Market-based measures (MBMs) use economic incentives to change emissions-related behaviour. They make high-GHG operation more expensive, reward better performance, or allow trading of defined compliance instruments. Their purpose is to influence investment and operation through prices as well as technical rules. MEPC 81 was held in March 2024. It advanced an illustrative outline of an IMO Net-Zero Framework; it did not adopt a final worldwide levy or emissions-trading scheme. The 2023 Strategy called for a combined technical element and economic element. Your answer should distinguish the options discussed around MEPC 81 from the later framework negotiations. [1,27,28] 2. Objectives Close the fuel-price gap: verified low-GHG fuels often have different production and supply costs from conventional bunkers. A predictable emissions price improves their commercial attractiveness and gives suppliers a clearer demand signal. Reward real reductions: support improvements in vessel efficiency, operating practices and fuel pathways. Use reliable measurement and verification so that money follows genuine performance rather than misleading labels. Raise or mobilize transition finance: depending on scheme design, receipts can support research, infrastructure, fuel uptake, training and mitigation of adverse impacts on States. The eventual legal instrument determines which uses are allowed. Promote a fair global transition: reduce fragmented requirements, discourage avoidance and account for impacts on remote and vulnerable economies. The strategy calls for impact assessment, particularly for SIDS and LDCs, while respecting the applicable IMO framework. [1] 3. Types of economic mechanisms: explain how each works Mechanism Operation Main advantage and challenge Emissions levy / A defined charge is levied on an emissions Relatively clear price signal; contribution quantity, with the coverage and liable entity emissions outcome depends on the prescribed by the scheme. price, alternatives and response. Revenue governance and fair allocation are major issues. Feebate / reward Charges on higher-emission activity help Can reduce the price gap directly; arrangement finance rewards for qualifying requires robust eligibility and low/zero-emission performance. safeguards against rewarding fuels with hidden upstream emissions. GHG-intensity Ships must meet an intensity threshold; the Gives a performance requirement standard with scheme may permit transfers, banking or with flexibility; depends on reliable flexible compliance remedial payments for defined deficits. life-cycle values and precise rules for units and deficits. Cap-and-trade / A cap limits allowances; covered entities Controls covered emissions emissions trading surrender allowances and may trade them. quantity; allowance prices fluctuate and administration can be complex. The EU ETS is a regional example, not a global scheme adopted by MEPC 81. These are explanatory categories, not a claim that MEPC 81 adopted four parallel systems. At that meeting, the central IMO work was the basket combining a goal-based fuel standard and an emissions-pricing mechanism. A fuel standard by itself is a technical requirement; its associated transfer or payment arrangements are the economic/compliance component. [27,28,36] 4. Illustrative economic calculation Assume, solely for explanation, a charge of USD 100 per tonne CO2 on a fuel emitting 3.114 tonnes CO2 per tonne fuel. The direct charge would be 100 × 3.114 = USD 311.40 per tonne fuel, before any scope adjustment, exemptions or credits. Saving 1,000 tonnes of that fuel would avoid 3,114 tonnes CO2 and USD 311,400 of the illustrative charge. This is not an adopted IMO rate. It shows why a price on emissions can change a retrofit's payback and a fuel procurement decision. A real WtW-based scheme would require the prescribed life-cycle boundary, gases, factors and compliance rules, rather than this simplified combustion-only calculation. For a company investment decision, compare the annualized capital cost and additional maintenance/fuel costs with avoided energy and compliance costs. Test several fuel and carbon-price scenarios; do not claim that a retrofit is profitable solely because its initial quoted saving is high. 5. Implementation challenges and industry effects Measurement and verification: the regulator must define the ship/company responsible, covered voyages and emissions, fuel pathway evidence, default factors and acceptable actual values. It must prevent double counting and false sustainability claims. Robust DCS-type records are useful, but the existing CII dataset alone does not provide every upstream input required by life-cycle pricing. [2,26] Availability and safety: owners cannot instantly buy a fuel that is unavailable on their trade or unsafe for their machinery. New technology requires approved systems, trained personnel and bunkering infrastructure. An economic measure should be accompanied by technical cooperation and a credible supply transition. [1] Cost distribution: charges may be passed through freight or contracts, but the effect depends on market conditions and bargaining power. The owner, manager, charterer, cargo interest and fuel supplier may have different incentives. Written arrangements should explain data provision, purchasing decisions, cost allocation and cooperation on emissions reduction. Impacts on States: remote routes, low-value cargo and food-import dependence can make the same nominal charge more burdensome for some economies. Assess transport costs and development effects, and address disproportionately negative impacts through the agreed mechanisms. [1] Revenue governance and integrity: clear collection, accounting, allocation and audit rules are essential. Flexibility systems need safeguards against duplicate units, unverifiable transfers or using payment as a substitute for an effective transition. Compatibility with regional systems also matters so owners can identify the distinct legal obligations. Industry opportunities: a credible framework can stimulate shipyard retrofits, renewable-fuel production, port development, digital verification and skilled employment. Uncertainty about the final scheme may delay investment; clear implementation stages help companies plan. 6. Current position and Chief Engineer's contribution The MEPC 81 outline developed into the draft approved at MEPC 83, but adoption discussions in October 2025 were adjourned and negotiations continued in 2026. As at 2 October 2026, do not present a final global IMO MBM price as law. The latest reviewed IMO record is ISWG-GHG 22. [29-31] As Chief Engineer, I would provide accurate fuel and performance evidence, quantify realistic savings and maintenance implications, and advise the Master/company on machinery constraints. The company manages financial compliance and commercial contracts; the ship supplies verifiable data and implements approved operating measures. I would challenge an apparently cheap fuel option if its certification, safety compatibility or upstream emissions evidence were inadequate. 16-mark writing outline Definition and MEPC 81 status (2); objectives (3); economic mechanisms and technical/economic distinction (4); implementation and equity challenges (4); current status and CE role (3). Avoid: stating that MEPC 81 introduced a mandatory global carbon tax; equating CII rating with a trading allowance; or copying regional EU ETS rules into a purported global IMO scheme. Source: MEO_ClassI_Section2_GHG_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 2 Oct 2026

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_11_to_20.pdf

T02-Q07 · The SEEMP seeks to improve a ship’s energy efficiency through four steps: planning, implementation, monitoring, and self- evaluation and improvement. Describe with suitable examples the critical role of each component.
1. Purpose and regulatory framework The Ship Energy Efficiency Management Plan (SEEMP) is a ship-specific management tool required by MARPOL Annex VI regulation 26. It provides a structured method to improve energy efficiency and, where applicable, manage fuel-data reporting and annual carbon intensity. It may form part of the ship's Safety Management System. A generic manual kept only for inspection does not fulfil its operational purpose. [2] The current guidance is MEPC.395(82), amended by MEPC.401(83) and MEPC.413(84), with the consolidated text in MEPC.1/Circ.925. The 2024 guidelines replaced MEPC.346(78); an answer that names only the 2022 edition is incomplete. [14,15,32] 2. The three SEEMP parts Part Purpose and application Part I - Ship management Generally for ships 400 GT and above within Chapter 4 scope. Describes plan to improve energy efficiency measures, implementation, monitoring and review. efficiency Part II - Ship fuel oil For ships 5,000 GT and above subject to DCS. Sets out approved collection consumption methods, data handling and reporting processes. data-collection plan Part III - Ship operational For specified ship types 5,000 GT and above subject to CII. Includes carbon intensity plan calculation methodology, required CII for the next three years, implementation plan and self-evaluation; corrective actions where triggered. Part I's presence is checked under the relevant IEE survey provisions; it does not generally require the same content verification as Parts II and III. Parts II and III require Administration/authorized-RO verification or confirmation as applicable. Always apply regulation 19's exclusions and any additional Flag instructions. [2,16,23] 3. Step one: planning - establish what can actually be improved Establish a baseline. Collect consumption by fuel and consumer, shaft power/rpm, speed, draft, trim, weather, voyage/port time, cargo duties and relevant maintenance history. Separate the main causes of consumption so that the plan addresses controllable losses rather than arbitrary percentage targets. Select ship-specific measures. Examples are speed optimization, weather routing, hull/propeller maintenance, engine tuning, generator-load management, steam-system efficiency and improved cargo-heating control. Evaluate technical feasibility, cost, expected benefit, safety, human workload and contractual restrictions. Identify the shore support needed for each measure. [14] Set responsibility and milestones. State what will be done, by whom, by when and how success will be measured. Goals in Part I can be management targets; the mandatory required annual CII and Part III requirements remain distinct. For applicable ships, the three-year Part III plan must address future tightening rather than repeating the present year's target. [2,14] Illustrative plan: after confirming that a steam leak is responsible for avoidable auxiliary-boiler operation, schedule repair at the next safe opportunity, provide materials and an isolation plan, assign the Second Engineer, and measure comparable boiler fuel use before and after. The target should be supported by the actual baseline, not a claimed universal saving. 4. Step two: implementation - convert the plan into routine operation The Master coordinates the ship's overall implementation and approves operational arrangements affecting navigation and safety. The Chief Engineer organizes machinery measures, confirms limits and briefs engineers. The company/superintendent supplies resources, resolves commercial constraints and arranges necessary approval or verification. Clearly assigned duties prevent a plan from remaining a shore-office exercise. Integrate tasks into standing orders, PMS, voyage planning and the SMS as appropriate. Brief personnel on the purpose, sequence, measurement and safe limits of a measure. Record implementation dates, changes and difficulties. If a measure cannot be achieved, report the reason rather than silently marking it complete. [14] Example: optimize running generators using the actual load profile. Before stopping a set, check redundancy, expected load steps, blackout recovery, manoeuvring status and cargo requirements. Fuel saving must not defeat emergency readiness or required reliability. The approved operating configuration should be understandable to each watchkeeper. For capital improvements such as a VFD or waste-heat installation, the company must complete design review, authorization, installation and testing. The Chief Engineer cannot obtain a valid EEXI credit simply by adding the proposed saving to a spreadsheet. 5. Step three: monitoring - obtain reliable evidence Measure consistently and retain traceability. Use the approved Part II methods and documented instruments. Reconcile consumption against deliveries, opening/closing remaining-on-board quantities and relevant transfers, with correct density and units. Log meter faults and use the approved fallback method. A technically sophisticated dashboard is of little value if the underlying data is incomplete. [14] Monitor useful indicators at different levels: daily consumer performance, voyage energy use, total annual CO2, forecast CII and, where useful, EEOI. Compare like-for-like operating conditions. A higher daily consumption can be justified by heavier weather or more cargo service; a lower daily consumption may simply reflect fewer operating hours. Example: compare generator fuel consumption per kWh at similar load and conditions. If fuel per day decreases only because the ship spent fewer hours operating, this does not demonstrate improved generator efficiency. For the statutory report, however, all applicable fuel still belongs in the correct annual dataset. Updated data requirements: enhanced DCS under MEPC.385(81) includes more detailed consumption and transport-work information. MEPC.1/Circ.913 explains implementation over consistent calendar-year datasets. MEPC.413(84) updates distance reporting to cover total distance and its under-way/not-under-way components. These requirements must be reflected in the verified methodology. [15,18,19] 6. Step four: self-evaluation and improvement Review whether each measure was implemented and whether it delivered the expected result. Investigate differences using evidence: deteriorated machinery, changed trade, weather, instrumentation error, unrealistic assumptions or lack of shore cooperation. A missed target is a reason for root-cause analysis and revised action, not for altering the data. [14] Retain successful practices, revise ineffective measures, and incorporate findings into the next planning cycle. Update personnel training and resource requirements. If a CII corrective plan is required, include the necessary actions and obtain verification within the prescribed timeframe. Part III audits/verification follow MEPC.347(78); company self-evaluation is not a substitute for statutory verification. [2,16] Example: a propeller-cleaning programme produces less saving than expected. Review actual roughness/fouling, time since cleaning, draft and weather, measurement uncertainty and whether hull fouling dominates. Decide whether to change the cleaning interval or investigate other causes; do not conclude automatically that the propeller work failed. PLAN: baseline, target, responsibility and resources IMPLEMENT: approved actions and crew training MONITOR: reliable data and comparable performance EVALUATE: root cause, corrective action and revised plan 7. Linking all three parts and the Chief Engineer's records Part I says how to improve; Part II says how to measure/report; Part III says how to achieve the required CII. They must remain consistent. If an efficiency measure relies on a new sensor or a corrected operating boundary, update the relevant methods and plan through the required process. IACS Recommendation 175 Rev.1 explains this relationship; it is a recommendation, not a Unified Interpretation. [23] As Chief Engineer I would retain operating evidence, maintenance reports, training records, instrument verification/calibration records, anomaly investigations and correspondence with the company/verifier. I would present the Master with a concise monthly summary of the forecast outcome, machinery risks and decisions needed from shore. 16-mark writing outline Definition, regulation and three parts (3); planning with example (3); implementation and responsibilities (3); monitoring/data quality (3); evaluation/improvement with example (3); draw the cycle (1). Avoid: treating SEEMP as only a fuel-saving checklist; confusing voluntary management goals with required CII; or claiming that the Chief Engineer alone can authorize navigation, charter or certification changes. Source: MEO_ClassI_Section2_GHG_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 2 Oct 2026

T02-Q08 · The EU MRV regulation requires ship owners/operators to annually monitor, report and verify CO2 emissions for vessels >5000GT calling at EU ports. Explain differences between EU MRV and Data Collection/Reporting under MARPOL. Draft a fleet circular describing compliance procedures.
1. Correct the older wording in the examination question The original wording reflects the earlier CO2-only EU MRV system. EU MRV now covers CO2, CH4 and N2O, with the additional gases included from 2024. The main cargo/passenger threshold is 5,000 GT and above, not strictly greater than 5,000 GT. From 2025, scope also includes general cargo ships from 400 to below 5,000 GT and offshore ships of 400 GT and above, for the relevant covered activities. [34,36] Its legal foundation is Regulation (EU) 2015/757, as amended, including Regulation (EU) 2023/957. The reporting obligation applies by relevant EU/EEA port/voyage scope, irrespective of flag. An Indian-flagged ship may therefore have to comply with both EU MRV and IMO DCS. Current EU procedural details in this answer are checked against Commission/EMSA guidance, explicitly marked official grey literature in the references. [33-36] 2. Comparison of the two systems Aspect EU MRV IMO DCS under MARPOL Annex VI Purpose and basis Verified emissions and transport information Fuel-consumption reporting under under Regulation (EU) 2015/757, as amended; regulation 27, supporting IMO also supports EU ETS. analysis and operational CII where applicable. Geographical Relevant voyages to, from and between Annual global operation of ships coverage covered EU/EEA ports and applicable in-port within the MARPOL reporting scope. emissions. Apply the legal port-of-call It is not limited to EU voyages. definition and exclusions. Ship scope Main cargo/passenger category >=5,000 GT, >=5,000 GT, subject to Annex VI plus expanded general-cargo/offshore Chapter 4 application/exclusions. categories described above. What is monitored CO2, CH4, N2O and relevant fuel, voyage, Fuel by type, distance, hours and cargo and activity information. prescribed ship particulars; enhanced appendix IX data adds granularity and transport work. Monitoring Ship-specific MRV monitoring plan, Verified SEEMP Part II; Part III document assessed/approved through the applicable separately addresses CII for verifier/authority process. applicable categories. Verification Accredited MRV verifier; regulatory Flag Administration or duly administration rests with the relevant authorized organization. A class authorities. society must have the appropriate authorization for the role. Annual reporting Normally 31 March following the reporting Within three months after year; an administering authority can require an year-end, normally 31 March, to earlier permitted deadline. Administration/authorized organization. Onboard compliance Document of Compliance (DoC); ensure Statement of Compliance (SoC) document carriage by 30 June for applicable issued normally by 31 May; ships/activities. includes CII rating where applicable. Information Selected verified ship information is publicly IMO reporting/database access is disclosure disclosed. governed by MARPOL and the applicable data-access arrangements; it is not the same public ship-by-ship MRV system. Sources for the comparison: MARPOL regulations 5, 6, 9, 26-28 and amended appendix IX; current Commission/EMSA guidance. [2,18,19,33-36] 3. Do not confuse MRV with ETS MRV measures and verifies; ETS requires surrender of allowances for covered emissions. They interact but have different scope and compliance acts. Under EU ETS, the usual geographical coverage is 100% of emissions on intra-EU/EEA voyages and in covered ports, and 50% on covered extra-EU/EEA voyages, subject to specific provisions. Do not halve the monitored MRV voyage data simply because ETS has a 50% rule. [36] The ETS phase-in covers 40% of 2024 emissions, 70% of 2025 emissions and 100% of 2026 emissions onwards, with the corresponding surrender the following year. CH4 and N2O enter maritime ETS from 2026, although MRV has monitored them since 2024. For example, surrender in September 2026 concerns the relevant share of 2025 emissions. The percentage is not a GHG-reduction target. [36] FuelEU Maritime is another separate regime. Its fuel-energy-intensity compliance must not be substituted for either MRV reporting or IMO CII. A company may use a common data platform, but it must calculate each obligation according to its own boundaries. 4. IMO DCS changes relevant to the circular The older claim “DCS records no actual cargo/transport-work data” is no longer an adequate current comparison. MEPC.385(81) expanded appendix IX. MEPC.1/Circ.913 maintains consistent annual data granularity: for existing ships under the ordinary entry-into-force route, enhanced collection applies from 1 January 2026; an Administration's early-implementation route could start in 2025. Ships delivered on/after 1 August 2025 have a specific delivery-based provision. [18,19] IACS interpretation check: MPC131 originally addressed the transition but is marked withdrawn in December 2025 in the class register. Cite IMO Circ.913 as the current guidance rather than relying on a withdrawn UI. Enhanced reporting does not itself replace CII's capacity-distance denominator with actual cargo work. [13,19,24] 5. Model fleet circular - ready to reproduce in a written answer Company Technical Department - Fleet Circular ENV/2026/02 Date: 2 October 2026. To: All Masters, Chief Engineers and designated shore compliance staff. Subject: EU MRV and IMO DCS/CII - monitoring, reporting and verification. Status: Illustrative examination circular; company internal deadlines below are proposed controls, not additional statutory dates. 1. Applicability and documents. The shore compliance team shall maintain a ship-by-ship matrix identifying IMO DCS/CII, EU MRV and ETS obligations. Before relevant employment, the Master shall confirm that the current monitoring plan, verified SEEMP parts and applicable compliance documents are available. Changes of company, flag, machinery, fuel or trade shall be notified to the superintendent promptly for assessment and required plan updates. 2. Responsibilities. The Master is responsible for coordination and for complete voyage/port information. The Chief Engineer shall control fuel, engine, boiler and other energy-consumption records and report equipment/data defects. The Chief Officer/bridge team shall provide cargo, distance and activity records. Shore staff shall reconcile datasets, liaise with the accredited MRV verifier and Flag/authorized RO, and complete statutory submissions. Commercial staff shall supply relevant charter and port-call information. 3. Daily evidence. Record fuel by type and consumer as required by the approved plans, ROB, bunkering, applicable transfers, meter readings and relevant operating hours. Record voyage start/end, port events, distance and cargo/service information with consistent timestamps. Preserve BDNs, calibration evidence and any supporting emission or fuel-pathway documents. Do not omit consumption while waiting, manoeuvring or operating cargo services merely to improve the result. 4. Quality control. Reconcile fuel balances at agreed intervals and investigate unexplained differences. Record the start/end of sensor failures and apply the documented fallback method. Correct errors transparently with a retained audit trail; never overwrite raw evidence without explanation. Ship and shore shall reconcile common data while maintaining each scheme's separate reporting boundaries. 5. Reporting timetable. Submit the monthly package to shore by the fifth day of the following month and the final year-end package by 15 January; these are company targets. Shore shall complete verification in time for the applicable 31 March reporting deadlines, or any earlier lawful MRV deadline communicated by the authority. Arrange the IMO SoC normally by 31 May and ensure the EU MRV DoC is onboard by 30 June. ETS surrender is a separate company obligation, normally due 30 September for the preceding year. [2,35,36] 6. CII corrective action. Review the forecast at least monthly. If the verified rating is E, or D for three consecutive years, shore and vessel shall prepare the required corrective plan and obtain SEEMP verification within the MARPOL timeframe. The Chief Engineer shall identify machinery measures and limits; the Master/company shall coordinate voyage and commercial measures. Safety shall take priority over a desired rating. [2,20] 7. Record control and acknowledgement. Maintain current document versions and backups. Apply the approved retention schedule; retain IMO SoCs onboard for at least five years and BDNs for at least three years. The Master shall acknowledge this circular and confirm that relevant personnel have been briefed. Outstanding defects or uncertainties shall be escalated through the superintendent/compliance team. [2,14] Issued by: Technical Manager / designated company compliance function. 16-mark writing outline Correct scope/gases (2); structured MRV-DCS comparison (6); fleet circular with duties, data and deadlines (6); ETS distinction and updated DCS interpretation (2). Avoid: “CO2 only”, “all ships over 400 GT now under EU MRV”, a 30 April report deadline as current, exchanging DoC/SoC dates, or treating accreditation as automatically equivalent to Flag authorization. T02-Q09 · a. Explain Life Cycle Analysis (LCA) of fuels used in ship operations and its different stages. (b) Discuss Well-to-Wake (WtW) and Well-to-Tank (WtT) concepts employed in GHG emission calculation of fuels used in the maritime sector. Explain, with justification, which methodology would be feasible for the shipping industry.
1. Definition and current IMO reference Life Cycle Assessment (LCA) evaluates environmental impacts across a defined product or energy pathway. For IMO marine-fuel GHG assessment, it examines emissions from obtaining the feedstock through production and supply to onboard use. The current core reference is MEPC.391(81), the 2024 Guidelines on life cycle GHG intensity of marine fuels, which replaced MEPC.376(80). [26] The relevant gases are CO2, methane (CH4) and nitrous oxide (N2O). Results are commonly expressed as g CO2-equivalent per MJ of fuel/energy, using a stated calorific-value and accounting basis. This differs from a ship transport-efficiency index expressed in g CO2 per tonne-mile. [26] Set the boundary first. Fuel LCA under these guidelines is not a complete cradle-to-grave inventory of constructing, operating and recycling the entire ship. It also does not automatically include cargo VOCs, refrigerant leakage, SOx, particulate matter or every environmental impact. Sustainability aspects accompany the GHG assessment, but they should not be confused with the numerical fuel-GHG boundary. [26] 2. Stages and system boundaries Stage Activities / emissions included Feedstock extraction, Oil/gas extraction, biomass cultivation, waste recovery or other feedstock cultivation or recovery origin; relevant energy and material inputs and leakage. Conversion into fuel Refining, gas processing/liquefaction, hydrogen production, ammonia/methanol synthesis or biofuel production, according to the pathway. Transportation, storage Moving feedstock/fuel, terminal handling, storage and bunkering up to delivery and distribution into the ship's fuel tank. Onboard fuel use Fuel consumed for propulsion and auxiliary services; combustion CO2, relevant CH4/N2O and applicable fugitive/slip emissions within the defined boundary. Well-to-Tank (WtT) covers the upstream chain to the ship's tank. Tank-to-Wake (TtW) covers onboard use. Well-to-Wake (WtW) = WtT + TtW, provided both are calculated using compatible boundaries and energy units. [26] Feedstock extraction / cultivation / recovery Fuel production and conversion Transport, storage and bunkering: end of WtT Onboard use and emissions: TtW WtW combines the complete compatible pathway 3. Four assessment steps Goal and scope: define the comparison, fuel pathway, energy basis, gases, time horizon and boundaries. “LNG versus methanol” is incomplete without identifying production pathways and engine technologies. Inventory: collect the physical inputs and emissions at each stage. Include energy used in production and transport, relevant leakage and engine-specific emissions. Identify whether values are verified actual data or prescribed defaults and document their provenance. Impact calculation: convert the relevant gases to CO2-equivalent using the selected methodology and aggregate by the functional unit. For MEPC.391(81), the prescribed 100-year values are CO2 = 1, CH4 = 28 and N2O = 265. These are the values specified for that guideline; do not silently substitute a different scientific-report value or another scheme's factors. [26] Interpretation and review: compare results, identify dominant contributors, examine uncertainty and check whether conclusions change with different credible assumptions. Verify sustainability and chain-of-custody evidence before making a procurement or compliance claim. 4. Worked illustrative calculation Assume a fuel pathway has WtT = 18 g CO2e/MJ. Onboard use produces 60 g CO2/MJ, 0.10 g CH4/MJ, and 0.002 g N2O/MJ. Using the above GWP100 factors: TtW = 60 + (0.10 × 28) + (0.002 × 265) = 63.33 g CO2e/MJ. WtW = 18 + 63.33 = 81.33 g CO2e/MJ. These are hypothetical teaching inputs, not default values for a named fuel. The example shows that a small mass of a high-GWP gas can materially affect the outcome. It also shows why comparing only stack CO2 may change the ranking of fuels. For an illustrative blend using 30% of its energy from a pathway of 20 g CO2e/MJ and 70% from a pathway of 90 g CO2e/MJ, the simple compatible-boundary result is 0.30 × 20 + 0.70 × 90 = 69 g CO2e/MJ. This is an energy-weighted example; mass percentages cannot be substituted without accounting for different heating values and the applicable methodology. 5. Why fuel names alone are insufficient LNG: less combustion CO2 per unit of energy can be offset partly or substantially by upstream methane leakage and onboard methane slip. Engine type and operating condition matter; a result for one combustion technology cannot be copied to another. Hydrogen and ammonia: no carbon in the principal molecule does not establish zero life-cycle GHG emissions. Production electricity/feedstock can dominate the upstream result. Ammonia use also requires attention to N2O and pilot fuel; safety hazards remain separate from GHG accounting. Methanol: contains carbon and produces CO2 when burned. Fossil methanol, biomethanol and renewable synthetic methanol can have different upstream and carbon-source accounting. The label must be backed by a verified pathway. Biofuels: examine feedstock origin, production energy, land-use/sustainability conditions and certification. A fuel containing biogenic carbon is not automatically entitled to a zero value under every regulation. Avoid double counting the same environmental attribute. Shore electricity: removes or reduces onboard generator exhaust during use, but its WtW benefit depends on the electricity pathway and the accounting method. “No exhaust at berth” is not synonymous with “no upstream emissions”. These comparisons follow the pathway-based approach of the IMO LCA guidelines. [26] 6. Which methodology is feasible and appropriate? For choosing and regulating fuels towards genuine decarbonisation, WtW is the appropriate overall methodology. WtT alone omits onboard emissions; TtW alone can favour a fuel whose production causes high emissions ashore. WtW prevents shifting emissions between sectors and provides a more complete comparison. This is the approach recognized in the IMO Strategy. [1,26] Practical implementation should divide responsibility across the supply chain. Suppliers and accredited/recognized certification arrangements establish upstream data and traceability; the ship records fuel quantities, energy use and machinery-specific information; the verifier assesses evidence under the relevant scheme. Use permitted conservative defaults when valid actual data is unavailable rather than inventing an upstream number onboard. The Fuel Lifecycle Label (FLL) in the IMO guidelines organizes fuel type, feedstock/carbon origin, production route, emission factors, blend and sustainability information. It supports verification; it should not be described as an existing universal statutory ship certificate replacing the BDN. [26] A complete operational comparison also considers efficiency. Two fuels with the same g CO2e/MJ can produce different emissions per nautical mile if the propulsion systems have different conversion efficiencies. For procurement, compare the pathway intensity and the energy required to deliver the intended transport service. 7. Chief Engineer's application and limits I would request suitable fuel documentation through the company, check compatibility and approved operating modes with the maker, record quantities and any required emissions-related data, and report inconsistencies. Upstream calculations require supplier evidence; the Chief Engineer should not certify an unverified “green” claim. Actual CII and statutory reporting must continue using their prescribed methods, even when the company also calculates a WtW footprint. IMO continues developing the LCA framework. The current index includes newer CH4/N2O measurement guidance adopted in 2026. Such measurement guidance does not itself make the still-negotiated Net-Zero Framework legally operative. [31,32] 16-mark writing outline Definition/reference and boundary (2); WtT/TtW/WtW stages (4); calculation method/example (3); fuel-pathway examples (3); justified WtW recommendation and feasibility (3); CE application (1). Avoid: WtW = WtT only; zero-stack CO2 = zero GHG; treating every biofuel as carbon neutral; or comparing tonnes of unlike fuels without energy and efficiency context. Source: MEO_ClassI_Section2_GHG_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 2 Oct 2026

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_11_to_20.pdf

T02-Q10 · Reducing the speed of main propulsion engines is considered the simplest and most practical short-term solution for lowering GHG emissions from ships. What are the drawbacks and dangers of operating main propulsion two-stroke marine engines at low speeds for extended periods? How can these issues be resolved and the risks associated with low-speed operation reduced?
1. Why slowing down can save fuel For a displacement vessel over a suitable operating range, a useful engineering approximation is propulsive power proportional to speed cubed. Reducing speed to 80% can therefore reduce propulsion power to about 0.8 cubed = 0.512, or 51.2%, of the former value. For a fixed distance, passage time becomes 1/0.8 = 1.25 times as long; with unchanged SFC, propulsion energy becomes about 0.512 × 1.25 = 0.64, or 64%. [41] These are illustrative approximations, not a guaranteed 36% whole-voyage fuel saving. Weather, hull resistance, propeller matching, SFC, auxiliary loads, boiler firing and schedule effects alter the actual result. A reduction in rpm alone does not prove that the engine is operating efficiently. Low load is not inherently prohibited. MAN's SL2021-714 discusses sustained operation down to 5% load for the stated ME/ME-C and dual-fuel engine application with precautions. It is not permission to run every engine at 5%; the installed engine, fuel mode, maker's current instructions and approved operating envelope govern. [37,38] 2. Combustion, air supply and fouling risks At low load, a conventional turbocharger receives less exhaust energy. Inadequate scavenge air or poor matching can cause unstable combustion, smoke and deposits. Injection performance also depends on engine design: electronically controlled injection can retain capabilities that mechanically controlled engines may lose at very low rpm. Precautions: confirm satisfactory atomization, appropriate fuel treatment/viscosity and injector condition. Monitor cylinder pressure balance, exhaust-temperature spread and scavenge pressure. Use the maker's approved low-load tuning or air-management arrangement; do not make unauthorized fuel-timing or NOx-critical changes. Approved tuning must match the intended operating profile. [37,42] Auxiliary blowers may run for longer or repeatedly cycle near the changeover point. This raises electrical demand and maintenance exposure. Monitor motor current, bearing condition and flap behaviour; use the approved control mode and maintain available spares. For a multi-turbocharger plant, a maker-approved cut-out/rematching solution may help, but it imposes a defined permissible load range. Never improvise turbocharger isolation. [37] 3. Cold corrosion, lubrication and cylinder condition Cold corrosion occurs when acid-containing condensate forms on sufficiently cool liner surfaces and attacks the metal. Fuel sulphur, cylinder pressure, wall temperature and moisture affect the risk. A lower exhaust reading alone is not a reliable measure of the liner-wall condition. Monitor actual cylinder condition and retain the maker's cooling-temperature strategy. [40] Cylinder oil selection and feed rate must match fuel, engine and condition. Too little protection can promote wear; unnecessary oil can raise consumption and contribute to deposits. Do not prescribe one high BN or one g/kWh rate for every low-load engine. Use the approved lubricant and the maker's condition-based adjustment procedure. Current maker guidance: Everllence SL2025-776/NHN addresses drain-oil analysis for MC/MC-C and ME/ME-C engines. Evaluate iron, residual BN, the test method and possible system-oil dilution together. The letter normalizes measured iron to a reference feed rate; the same iron concentration can represent different absolute wear when oil delivery differs. Its routine laboratory interval is 1,500 running hours or three months, whichever comes first, with supplementary onboard monitoring and closer attention to abnormal findings. Apply the engine-specific instructions rather than treating these figures as a universal class rule. [44] Loss of ring freedom, blow-by, scuffing or worsening liner condition can develop if combustion and lubrication deteriorate. Review trends, inspect at safe opportunities and escalate abnormal findings promptly. A favourable fuel-consumption figure does not justify continuing damaging operation. 4. Scavenge and exhaust-system deposits and fire risk Deposits of oil and combustion products can accumulate in scavenge spaces and exhaust passages. They provide fuel for a fire if ignition conditions arise. Keep drains effective, inspect condition and use the approved cleaning procedure. Monitoring should include unusual scavenge/exhaust temperatures, smoke, pressure differences and evidence of blow-by. Exhaust-gas boiler/economizer risk: lower gas velocity reduces self-cleaning; deposits reduce heat transfer and can create soot-fire hazards. Unburned oil mixed with soot can make deposits more readily ignitable. Trend pressure drop and gas temperatures against a clean baseline at comparable load, maintain required circulation and inspect/clean according to the boiler maker's instructions. [39] Reduced exhaust energy also lowers steam production, potentially requiring extra oil-fired boiler operation. Include that fuel and blower/generator demand in the ship's net saving calculation. Observe the boiler's specified temperature limits to avoid condensation-related corrosion; do not invent a universal engine exhaust-temperature minimum from one boiler bulletin. [39] Avoid automatic daily “blow-through” advice. A heavily fouled exhaust system must not be exposed to a sudden load increase as an improvised cleaning method. Determine whether cleaning is required, coordinate with the Master, and follow the maker's gradual load-up procedure. MAN's letter specifically cautions that unnecessary frequent load-ups may harm cylinder condition. [37] 5. Further operating limitations Torsional vibration and barred speed ranges: check the approved shafting documentation. A desired economical speed is unacceptable if it requires continuous operation in a prohibited range. Passing through a barred range and sustained operation within it are different conditions. Match the selected service setting to the engine/propeller envelope. [41] Emissions-control effectiveness: very low load can reduce exhaust conditions needed by some SCR installations. Confirm the certified operating strategy and applicable NOx requirements; emissions-control equipment must not be bypassed merely to save fuel. Changes in fuel mode or tuning need the relevant approved arrangements. Navigation and readiness: low available thrust can affect response in adverse weather, restricted waters or traffic. The Master determines the navigational need; the Chief Engineer must state machinery limits and readiness for an increase in power. If EPL/SHaPoLi is fitted, maintain the approved safety-reserve procedure. [5-7] Commercial consequence: longer passage time may alter cargo schedules and require more ships to maintain a service. Assess total transport-service emissions and cost. A vessel-specific fuel saving does not automatically produce the same fleet-wide percentage reduction. 6. Chief Engineer's action plan: before, during and after Before prolonged low-load running: consult the Master and superintendent; establish the maker's permissible operating range, fuel mode, blower/turbocharger requirements, shafting restrictions and load-up procedure. Review the risk assessment and current cylinder/exhaust/boiler condition. Confirm measuring instruments, essential spares, lubrication instructions and emergency readiness. Request maker/RO input for proposed modifications. During operation: brief engineers and monitor deviations from the baseline. Keep clear records of load, rpm, fuel, scavenge pressure, individual exhaust temperatures, cylinder-pressure balance, blower behaviour, boiler pressure drop and relevant oil-analysis trends. Set escalation criteria in the standing orders and SMS according to approved limits. Allocate inspection and maintenance tasks without creating unsafe workload or fatigue. If deterioration appears: advise the Master immediately of any loss of propulsion reliability or required operating restriction. Investigate the cause, take maker/SMS-directed action and involve the company. Adjust or discontinue the low-load regime where necessary; do not postpone intervention merely to preserve a CII forecast. Before returning to higher load: confirm that the air/exhaust path, lubrication and cooling arrangements are ready, that any cut-out arrangement permits the intended load, and that the bridge agrees the change. Increase load progressively under the approved programme while watching cylinder and exhaust-system behaviour. Record anomalies and review the experience with the superintendent. Inspection safety update: Everllence SL2025-778/PRP requires at least two barriers for the specified cylinder-liner work/inspection arrangements and explains that lockout/tagout secures an isolation but is not itself a physical barrier. Use the vessel's applicable isolation/permit procedure, including starting-air and cooling-water hazards. This maker instruction is labelled technical grey literature in the register. [45] 7. Suggested monitoring record Group Record and interpret Operating condition Engine load/rpm, ship speed, draft, weather and fuel mode; compare like conditions. Combustion / air Cylinder pressure balance, exhaust-temperature spread, scavenge pressure and turbocharger/blower response. Cylinder protection Cylinder-oil grade and feed setting, drain-oil trends and inspection findings. Exhaust / steam Boiler pressure-drop/temperature trends, steam balance, cleaning and circulation condition. Safety / actions Approved restrictions, bridge communications, defects, maintenance and the result of corrective action. 16-mark writing outline Fuel-saving principle and limitations (2); combustion/air issues (3); corrosion/lubrication (3); deposits/boiler/fire risk (3); shafting/emissions/safety limits (2); CE procedure and monitoring (3). Avoid: claiming all two-strokes must remain above 40% load; a universal daily high-load cleaning run; indiscriminate increases in cylinder oil or cooling temperature; or operating outside approved vibration and emissions limits. Source: MEO_ClassI_Section2_GHG_10_Answers.pdf | Editorial review: 2 Oct 2026

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_11_to_20.pdf

Ship Energy Efficiency (10)
T03-Q01 · Optimization of ship dimensions is recognized as a means to improve energy efficiency. Discuss scope and impact of optimization of: (a) Ship size and capacity (b) Service speed (c) Principal dimensions.
Ship size and capacity • Larger ships can lower fuel and CO2 per tonne-mile through economies of scale: deadweight/cargo capacity increases faster than some resistance-producing dimensions; optimum is route- and draft-dependent. • Economies are limited by port depth, berth length, canal restrictions, demand, cargo utilization, loading time, structural weight and minimum safe power. • Compare laden/ballast fuel, port operations and transport work—not fuel per voyage alone. An oversized ship sailing partly loaded may perform worse on a tonne-mile basis. Service speed optimization • Calm-water shaft power approximates P proportional to V cubed over a limited operating range; slowing from V1 to V2 reduces instantaneous propulsion power roughly by (V2/V1)^3, but increases voyage duration and may raise auxiliary fuel per voyage. • Speed selection must include weather, currents, charter schedule, hull condition, main-engine low-load limitations and safe manoeuvring power. Propeller/engine efficiencies do not remain fixed. • Slow steaming may reduce fuel/tonne-mile but can increase soot, scavenge fouling, cold corrosion and auxiliary boiler operation without correct engine management. Principal dimensions • Length L: increases slenderness, often reducing wave-making resistance at a given displacement/speed; excessive L adds structural weight and bending concerns. • Beam B: affects wetted area, stability, propeller inflow and port limitations; wider hull can permit lower draught but may raise form resistance. • Draught T: influences displacement, wetted area, immersion and port access; optimum draught varies by load, trim and propeller submergence. • Block coefficient Cb = displacement volume/(L x B x T): fuller hulls carry greater cargo at low speed; finer hulls generally suit higher speeds at the cost of capacity. • L/B, B/T, prismatic coefficient, longitudinal centre of buoyancy and bulbous-bow design must be optimized together, using CFD/model tests and whole-voyage operating profile. Chief Engineer/management • Monitor kWh/tonne-mile, fuel/tonne-mile, speed-power trends, CII and weather-corrected performance; coordinate speed, trim and loading with master, charterer and superintendent. • Compare lifecycle economics including ship cost, emissions, propulsion margins and port compatibility. Reference • IMO GreenVoyage2050 Energy Efficiency Technologies Portal; MARPOL Annex VI Regulations 22–28 and SEEMP provisions (as applicable). Source note: Newly composed study answer, checked against the primary references listed at the back. Review ship-specific manufacturer data before applying design margins or technologies.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_21_to_30.pdf

T03-Q02 · Optimization of hull forms is recognized as a means to improve energy efficiency. Discuss scope and impact of: (i) Forebody optimization (ii) Aftbody Optimization (iii) Twin Skeg Design.
Forebody optimization • Bulbous bow alters bow-wave interference and can reduce wave resistance at designed speed/draught. A bulb designed for one waterline can be inefficient when operating consistently slower or at another draught. • Optimize stem shape, entrance angle, bulb size/vertical position, bow shoulders and wave-breaking behaviour; minimize added resistance in waves, not merely calm-water towing-tank resistance. • Evaluate bow slamming, green water, structural requirements, stability and loading condition. Aftbody optimization • Optimize stern run and wake field arriving at propeller; uniform inflow reduces blade loading fluctuations, cavitation, stern vibration and radiated underwater noise. • Design influences shaft inclination, propeller aperture, stern clearance, rudder interaction and appendage drag. Avoid flow separation and wake peaks. • Consider stern bulb, optimized transom, bilge vortices and full-scale performance; CFD must be validated by towing tank and sea trials. Twin-skeg design • Two skegs house/support separate shaft lines and influence the inflow to twin propellers, often considered for broad-beam/high-capacity vessels. • Advantages: possible propulsive efficiency through large, slower-turning propellers and favorable wake; redundancy with two propulsion lines; improved manoeuvrability with separate controls. • Disadvantages: extra wetted area and appendage drag, higher construction and maintenance cost, more complicated shafting/stern sealing; benefits highly hull-specific. Design decision • Assess delivered power P_D = effective towing power/(hull x relative-rotative x open-water propeller efficiencies), and compare both ballast and design draughts. • Consider ship safety and operability, EEDI/EEXI applicability and lifecycle fuel/emissions. Reference • IMO GreenVoyage2050 propulsion/hull improvement guidance; ITTC resistance and propulsion testing procedures. Source note: Newly composed study answer, checked against the primary references listed at the back. Review ship-specific manufacturer data before applying design margins or technologies.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_21_to_30.pdf

T03-Q03 · Improving efficiency of propellers is recognized as a growing means to improve energy efficiency. Discuss any three of the following high-efficiency propellers with merits and demerits: a) Ducted Propellers b) Kappel Propellers c) Contra-rotating Propellers d) Azimuth Propulsion.
(a) Ducted propeller / Kort nozzle • Propeller rotates inside hydrodynamically profiled annular duct; duct develops additional thrust at heavy loading/low advance speed. • Merits: higher bollard pull and low-speed efficiency; protected tips, suitable for tugs/trawlers/some slow heavy ships. • Demerits: nozzle drag at higher ship speed; extra surface, corrosion/fouling and risk of cavitation/erosion; not automatically efficient on fast merchant ships. (b) Kappel propeller • Tip geometry curves towards suction/pressure side to reduce tip vortex losses, optimize loading and improve propulsive efficiency. • Merits: potential lower fuel consumption, improved loading distribution and sometimes less cavitation. • Demerits: expensive precision manufacture and repair; benefits design-point dependent; special geometry complicates inspection. (c) Contra-rotating propellers (CRP) • Two propellers rotate oppositely on coaxial shafts or a pod/shaft combination; aft propeller recovers rotational energy from forward propeller wake. • Merits: reduced swirl loss, high efficiency under suitable loading, possibly improved cavitation behaviour. • Demerits: complex concentric shafts/bearings/seals/gearboxes, higher cost, maintenance and reliability challenges. (d) Azimuth propulsion • A steerable pod/thruster directs propeller thrust through 360 degrees; direct drive or geared arrangements. • Merits: strong manoeuvrability, improved low-speed handling, no conventional rudder, possible propulsive efficiency gains for selected hulls. • Demerits: electrical and pod losses, sealing and bearing issues, debris/grounding vulnerability, capital and service complexity. Selection and CE responsibilities • Compare efficiency across actual operating envelope rather than brochure savings; verify cavitation, vibrations, propeller immersion, reverse thrust, class approval and redundancy. • Inspect seal oil, bearings, drive trains, propeller surfaces, nozzles and pod alarms; trend fuel per transport work. Reference • IMO GreenVoyage2050 propulsion improving devices; ITTC propulsion trials and manufacturer design guidance. Exam comparison Device Principle / caution Ducted Nozzle adds thrust at low speed; drag at higher speeds Kappel Tip modification reduces vortex losses; complex manufacture CRP Recovers wake swirl; complex shafting Azimuth Excellent manoeuvring; costly pod maintenance Source note: Newly composed study answer, checked against the primary references listed at the back. Review ship-specific manufacturer data before applying design margins or technologies.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_21_to_30.pdf

T03-Q04 · Improvements in Rudder efficiency can considerably contribute to improving propulsion effectiveness. Discuss common rudder- efficiency improvement devices/techniques: (a) Rudder Thrust Fins (b) Asymmetric Rudders (c) Rudder Bulbs (d) Grim Vane Wheels.
Hydrodynamic principle • Rudder and stern appendages interact with rotating propeller slipstream. Properly designed energy-saving devices recover rotational kinetic energy, straighten flow or create useful axial force while retaining steerability. (a) Rudder thrust fins • Small fins fitted to rudder in the accelerated slipstream develop favorable lift with a forward thrust component and may reduce swirl loss. • Merits: retrofit potential, limited additional complexity; demers: added drag/structural loads, cavitation and susceptibility to damage; gain depends on angle/propeller wake. (b) Asymmetric rudder • Port/starboard foil shapes or twisted leading-edge sections tailored to asymmetric propeller inflow reduce incidence, separation and cavitation; improve rudder force and effective propulsive efficiency. • Merits: better use of wake, possible lower cavitation/noise; limitations: custom manufacture, off-design performance and steering-force balance. (c) Rudder bulb • Streamlined fairing near propeller hub/rudder leading edge fills low-pressure hub region, reducing hub vortex and energy loss; may incorporate end plates/fins. • Merits: potentially lower cavitation and resistance; limitations: stern geometry and propeller clearance critical, repair/inspection access needed. (d) Grim vane wheel — important classification correction • Free-rotating wheel installed behind the main propeller with inner turbine blades and outer propeller blades; recovers rotational energy of the wake. It is a POST-PROPELLER device, not strictly a rudder appendage. • Merits: possible recovery of wake swirl; limitations: large rotating appendage, mechanical support, debris/damage and vibration issues, limited modern adoption. Verification • Assess model tests/CFD, propeller/rudder clearance, cavitation, rudder torque and emergency steering, corrosion protection and structural/class requirements. • Quantify savings from corrected fuel-speed trials, not assumed fixed percentage. Avoid compromising manoeuvring or damage tolerance. Reference • IMO GreenVoyage2050; ITTC propulsion/rudder interaction studies. Exam comparison Device Principle / caution Rudder fins Recover slipstream energy; potential cavitation Asymmetric rudder Tailored to propeller swirl; ship-specific Rudder bulb Reduces hub vortex; clearance critical Grim wheel Free wheel behind propeller; not a rudder fitting Source note: Newly composed study answer, checked against the primary references listed at the back. Review ship-specific manufacturer data before applying design margins or technologies.

T03-Q05 · Recent stress on control of emissions from ships has led to development of devices aimed at improving propeller propulsion effectiveness. Discuss: a) Wake Equalizing Devices b) Pre-swirl Devices c) Post-swirl Devices d) High-efficiency Propellers.
Introduction • Propeller wake contains non-uniform axial velocity and rotational swirl; propulsion improving devices (PIDs) reduce losses before or after the propeller. All require ship-specific CFD/model testing and assessment of cavitation and safety. (a) Wake-equalizing devices (WED) • Wake-equalizing ducts and similar fins/ducts fitted upstream of propeller improve uneven inflow and reduce peak blade loading. • Advantages: may reduce vibration and cavitation and improve propulsion efficiency on full-form ships. • Disadvantages: duct drag, fouling, weld/fatigue concerns and poor results off design condition. (b) Pre-swirl devices • Pre-swirl fins/stators impose controlled swirl opposite the main propeller rotation to reduce net tangential kinetic-energy losses downstream. • Examples: pre-swirl stator vanes and asymmetric ducts; verify effect on manoeuvring and structural loads. (c) Post-swirl devices • Rudder bulbs with fins, propeller boss-cap fins (PBCF) and other downstream recovery devices reduce hub vortex or exploit the tangential momentum after propeller. • PBCF rotates WITH the propeller boss; fins are designed to alleviate hub-vortex losses. Rudder fins remain stationary relative to the rudder. (d) High-efficiency propellers • Optimized blade area/pitch and radial loading, skewed blades, large slow-turning propellers, tip-modified Kappel designs and contra-rotating systems can reduce losses. • Respect cavitation margin, propeller immersion, structural stress, clearances, torsional vibration and engine matching. Evaluation and CE role • Measure before/after corrected speed-power curves; account for changes in draught, trim, sea state, hull/propeller fouling and engine SFOC. • Maintain appendages and inspect cracks, erosion, misalignment, fouling, vibration and shaft bearings. Any modification needs class/flag approval where applicable. Reference • IMO GreenVoyage2050 Energy Efficiency Technologies Portal, propulsion improving devices. Exam comparison Device Principle / caution Wake equalizer More uniform upstream axial wake Pre-swirl Adds opposing swirl before propeller Post-swirl Recovers or reduces rotational losses after propeller Efficient propeller Optimizes blade loading, cavitation and pitch Source note: Newly composed study answer, checked against the primary references listed at the back. Review ship-specific manufacturer data before applying design margins or technologies.

T03-Q06 · Explain the significance of propeller curves. Enumerate the safety margins in relation to propeller operations. Using a typical engine power versus engine speed diagram and propeller demand curves, discuss the relation between maximum rated output of the propulsion plant and the power for which the propeller is designed.
1. Significance of propeller curves • For a fixed-pitch propeller under approximately constant wake/advance conditions, propeller torque Q rises roughly as n squared and delivered shaft power P = 2πnQ rises roughly as n cubed (propeller law). These are approximations, not universal over every speed/sea state. • Plot engine power versus rpm; a 'light-running' curve represents clean hull/favorable conditions; fouling, heavy weather and increased resistance can move the propeller to a HEAVIER curve requiring more torque/power at a given rpm. • The propeller demand curve must remain inside engine permitted load/torque and speed envelope, including air supply, exhaust temperature, barred speed and shaft limits. 2. Power points and margins • PD: propeller design point at specified ship design condition; SP: service propulsion point including sea margin; SMCR (or specified MCR): rated engine point selected with a power reserve and suitable light-running allowance. • Sea margin: extra propulsion power for wind, waves and ordinary in-service resistance; traditionally about 15% as a design first estimate but chosen for vessel/route (maker guidance notes about 10–30%). • Engine margin: allowance between sustained service power and SMCR for reliability/operational reserve, often around 10% traditionally; actual specified by design/engine maker. • Light-running RPM margin: designed allowance to prevent an excessively heavy-running propeller in fouled/hard-weather condition; maker-dependent, often a few percent. • Other checks: overload capacity and duration per maker, minimum safe power, cavitation, torsional vibration barred range, shaft/propeller strength and EEXI EPL/ShaPoLi limitations. 3. Illustrative calculation (not a mandatory design value) • If calm-water design propulsion power PD = 8,000 kW and design sea margin = 15%, required service point power ≈ 9,200 kW. • If engine margin = 10% of SMCR, then SMCR ≈ 9,200/0.90 = 10,222 kW. Engine matching also needs RPM/light-running margin and engine-layout checks. 4. Operational protection • Monitor main-engine load diagram, Pmax, scavenge pressure, exhaust temperatures, fuel index, turbocharger surge, thrust bearing and shaft vibration. • In heavy weather reduce RPM as necessary to avoid overload; never use the cubic law to infer safe rpm without maker envelope and propeller condition. Reference • Everllence/MAN basic principles of ship propulsion, 'Propulsion margins, including light running margin' (manufacturer guidance); SOLAS II-1/28; MARPOL Annex VI EEXI and EEDI as applicable. Illustrative engine power–RPM and propeller-demand curves Engine speed / RPM Engine power / kW Light running Heavy running Service point SMCR (illustrative) Schematic only: not a maker-approved engine layout diagram Source note: Newly composed study answer, checked against the primary references listed at the back. Review ship-specific manufacturer data before applying design margins or technologies.

T03-Q07 · Discuss what you understand by directional stability and manoeuvring, and how these attributes are provided in a ship, along with the engine power vs speed / propeller demand curve relationship.
1. Directional stability • A directionally stable ship tends to return toward a steady course after a small yaw disturbance with controls held fixed; dynamic stability is affected by hull, lateral planes, skegs, rudder, speed and loading. • Excessive directional stability may impede turning; insufficient stability leads to yawing, frequent steering and extra fuel consumption. 2. Manoeuvrability • Main qualities: course keeping, turning ability, initial turning response, stopping ability and yaw checking. • Rudder produces lift and yaw moment; steering effectiveness depends on flow speed, propeller slipstream, rudder area, aspect ratio and helm angle. • Twin rudders/twin screws, azimuth drives, thrusters and skegs alter manoeuvring balance. • IMO MSC.137(76) Standards for Ship Manoeuvrability specify assessment of turning, initial turning, yaw checking/course keeping and stopping for ships within scope; MSC/Circ.1053 provides explanatory notes. 3. Tests • Turning-circle: advance, transfer and tactical diameter; 10°/10° and 20°/20° zig-zag: initial turning/yaw checking; crash-stop: track reach; spiral/pull-out tests: directional stability as applicable. • Test under prescribed loading, water depth/weather and machinery condition; ship-specific manoeuvring booklet and wheelhouse poster support bridge team. 4. Engine and propeller-power relationships • Fixed-pitch approximate propeller law P ∝ n³; hull fouling or heavy seas produce heavier propeller demand at given RPM; ensure service and rated points lie inside maker's permitted engine envelope. • Maintain sea margin, engine margin, light-running allowance and emergency power; do not optimize propeller for pure efficiency at expense of safe astern manoeuvre and emergency stopping. Chief Engineer • Maintain steering gear, hydraulics, pumps, feedback, engine reversibility/astern air, propeller condition, shafting and bridge-control tests; support sea trials and manoeuvring records. Reference • IMO MSC.137(76), MSC/Circ.1053; SOLAS II-1 Regulations 28–29; engine maker propulsion guidance. Source note: Newly composed study answer, checked against the primary references listed at the back. Review ship-specific manufacturer data before applying design margins or technologies.
