T03-Q08 · Viscous (frictional) resistance is a major component of overall resistance. Explain various skin friction reduction methods: a) Hull Air Cavity Systems b) Hull Surface Texturing c) Micro bubbles.
Introduction • Frictional resistance acts tangentially over the wetted surface; it depends on Reynolds number, wetted area, hull roughness and boundary-layer conditions. Reduction of shear can lower required delivered power if device energy consumption is controlled. (a) Hull air cavity systems • Specially shaped recesses under relatively flat ship bottoms trap/maintain an air layer supplied by compressors, reducing direct water/steel contact. • Benefits: potentially lower skin-friction drag; drawbacks: compressors consume power, cavities lose stability in seas/heel, performance changes with draught and ballast condition; hull geometry/air leakage critical. (b) Hull surface texturing • Riblets/microgrooves aligned with local flow can reduce turbulent skin friction in a narrow design range by modifying near-wall flow structures. • Other concepts include shark-skin-inspired texture and superhydrophobic surfaces. Important: rough/random texture, coating damage or marine growth usually INCREASE resistance. • Limitations: durability, contamination, fouling, orientation, coatings and uneven benefit at full-scale Reynolds number. (c) Microbubble injection • Air injected along the underwater hull creates microbubbles or an air-water layer reducing effective local density/shear at wetted surface. • Components: air compressor/blower, distribution header, nozzles/injectors, controls and performance monitoring. • Benefits: possible fuel and emissions reduction; limitations: parasitic air-supply power, bubbles escaping, compressor maintenance, local hull geometry, draft/wave sensitivity. Comparison and economics • Air cavity requires purpose-designed geometry; microbubble systems may suit some retrofits. Textured coatings avoid blowers but must retain low roughness and performance over time. • Net power saving = propulsion-power reduction minus blower/compressor demand; compare across laden/ballast passages, speed and weather. Chief Engineer • Maintain compressor, filters, valves, delivery and control; check air flow, overpressure, vibrations and energy meter; monitor sea chest/structure for unintended effects. Reference • IMO GreenVoyage2050 Green Technology Portal: air lubrication and hull performance; ITTC frictional resistance methods. Source note: Newly composed study answer, checked against the primary references listed at the back. Review ship-specific manufacturer data before applying design margins or technologies.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_21_to_30.pdf

T03-Q09 · Hull & Propeller maintenance is gaining popularity with recent stress on energy efficiency. Write short notes on: (a) Hull Roughness Management (b) Condition Based Propeller Maintenance (c) Propeller Coating & Polishing.
(a) Hull roughness management • Distinguish micro-roughness (coating finish), macro-roughness (corrosion, dents, paint failure) and biofouling. Average Hull Roughness (AHR, micrometres) and underwater inspections can monitor deterioration. • Plan drydock preparation, controlled blasting, appropriate coatings, required dry-film thickness and low-roughness repairs; avoid excessive paint layers, overspray and poor curing. • Compare normalized speed-power/fuel trends at the same draught, trim and weather; monitor engine power for a given speed and carbon intensity. (b) Condition-based propeller maintenance • Record baseline propeller blade profile/surface, pitch (where relevant), cavitation damage, shaft vibration, fuel index and torque/RPM versus speed. • Inspect/polish when performance deterioration, vibration or diving inspection indicates fouling, scratches, tip damage, bent blades or erosion; use OEM/class limits and ensure diver safety. • Detect and rectify cavitation and bearing/misalignment causes rather than polish indefinitely; avoid excessive blade material removal or altered geometry. (c) Propeller coatings and polishing • Smooth polished blade surfaces reduce roughness and recover propulsive efficiency when fouled/rough; specialist coatings may reduce biofouling/cavitation erosion but must be appropriate for the blade material and operating stresses. • Inspect coating adhesion, damage, galvanic effects, cavitation erosion; do not indiscriminately apply hull antifouling paint to propellers. • Prepare a maintenance plan: inspection baseline → identify increased fuel power → planned diving/drydock → clean/polish/repair → performance verification → update PMS. Environmental/operational safeguards • In-water cleaning must follow local biosecurity/environmental restrictions, debris capture requirements where applicable and approved dive permits; avoid release of toxic coating residues. Reference • IMO GreenVoyage2050 hull cleaning/propeller polishing; 2023 IMO Biofouling Guidelines MEPC.378(80). Source note: Newly composed study answer, checked against the primary references listed at the back. Review ship-specific manufacturer data before applying design margins or technologies.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_21_to_30.pdf

T03-Q10 · With reference to biofouling of ships’ hulls: (a) Discuss consequences of biofouling on ship performance and the environment (b) New approaches to reduce biofouling (c) How a ship-specific Bio Fouling Management Plan can minimize biofouling (d) State the entries in the Bio Fouling Record Book.
(a) Consequences of biofouling • Biofouling = organisms accumulating on immersed hull and niche areas; added roughness increases frictional resistance, fuel consumption, CO2 emissions and may worsen operational CII/AER. • Heavy fouling can cause propeller cavitation, vibration, reduced service speed and increased maintenance; fouling in sea chests/intakes obstructs cooling. • Transport of invasive aquatic species threatens marine biodiversity, fisheries and coastal ecology. IMO biofouling guidance addresses transfer risk independently of ballast-water management. (b) New approaches • TBT-free self-polishing copolymers, biocide-free silicone/foul-release systems, advanced low-adhesion surfaces, hull monitoring and targeted in-water cleaning. • Risk-based cleaning schedules, robotic inspection, capture-equipped hull cleaning where permitted, digital speed–power performance alerts and treatment of niche areas. • All paints must meet applicable AFS Convention restrictions (organotin biocides, cybutryne); evaluate environmental effects of cleaning and coating systems. (c) Ship-specific Biofouling Management Plan (BFMP) • 2023 IMO Guidelines MEPC.378(80) recommend a BFMP: ship details; responsible officers; anti-fouling system specification/expected life; locations of niche areas; inspection/cleaning schedule and methods; maintenance and contingencies for extended idle periods, coating damage or unusual operating profiles. • Monitor biofouling risk factors such as idle time, speed, temperature/salinity region, coating condition and operational profile. Define triggers for inspection, cleaning, review and reporting. • Guidelines are VOLUNTARY globally; port/coastal States may separately impose biosecurity rules. A BFMP is not a universal MARPOL statutory certificate. (d) Biofouling Record Book (BFRB) • Part I: record anti-fouling system application/repair, hull and niche-area inspections (dates, location, results), maintenance/cleaning methods, coating or organism removal, waste handling and follow-up measures. • Part II: monitor heightened biofouling risk parameters and corresponding contingency actions; record date, coded items, observed conditions, responses and responsible officer signatures; master signs completed pages. • Record specific drydock activities, inspection results, temporary idleness, cleaning contractors and any reference evidence as applicable to IMO template; maintain accessible records over ship's life as recommended. Chief Engineer • Maintain sea chests, strainers, cooling intakes, shafting and propeller; coordinate with master and superintendent; inspect energy-use trends and prepare maintenance records. Reference • IMO Resolution MEPC.378(80) (2023 Biofouling Guidelines), including Appendices on BFMP/BFRB; AFS Convention 2001 as amended. Source note: Newly composed study answer, checked against the primary references listed at the back. Review ship-specific manufacturer data before applying design margins or technologies. Source list and regulatory distinctions https://greenvoyage2050.imo.org/energy-efficiency-technologies-information-portal/ https://www.imo.org/en/ourwork/environment/pages/biofouling.aspx https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/KnowledgeCentre/IndexofIMOResolutions/MEPCDocuments/MEPC.378%2880%29.pdf https://www.imo.org/en/ourwork/environment/pages/improving%20the%20energy%20efficiency%20of%20ships.aspx https://www.man-es.com/docs/default-source/document-sync/basic-principles-of-ship-propulsion-eng.pdf https://www.imo.org/en/ourwork/safety/pages/ship-design-and-stability.aspx Scope cautions • IMO 2023 Biofouling Guidelines are recommendations; they do not themselves create a universal mandatory Biofouling Certificate. • Propeller power law and sea/engine/light-running margins are approximate design tools; use the engine maker layout and vessel-specific speed-power curves. • Fuel-saving percentages from vendors are not guaranteed; use corrected sea trials and lifecycle assessment. • A technical device may be commercially available yet inappropriate for a ship’s hull and operating profile.

Marine Insurance & General Average (17)
T04-Q01 · Explain the principle of ‘Uberrimae Fidei’ in Marine Insurance. What disclosures must the insured make? Which circumstances need not be disclosed? What happens if disclosures are not made at conclusion of contract?
Principle: utmost good faith (uberrimae fidei) • Marine insurance is based on utmost good faith by BOTH assured and insurer; Marine Insurance Act, 1963 Section 19. Failure permits the other party to avoid the contract according to applicable law. • Section 20: before the insurance contract is concluded, assured must disclose every material circumstance known or which ought to be known in ordinary business. Material = would influence the judgment of a prudent insurer on accepting risk or fixing premium (Section 20(2)). • Section 21: an agent effecting insurance must disclose relevant material facts known, or which ought to be known, by the agent as well as those the assured must disclose. Material facts: practical examples • Ship's age, hull/engine condition, class/flag, detentions and major unrepaired defects; voyage/trading area, cargo/packing, loading or towage risks; prior serious losses and unusual operations. • Known safety-critical generator defects, deficient steering gear, extensive corrosion, special survey overdue, dangerous cargo or intended high-risk route may be material. Disclose facts accurately, without concealment or half-truths. Circumstances not requiring disclosure absent enquiry (Section 20(3)) • Circumstances that diminish the risk; those known or presumed known to insurer (public/common maritime knowledge); circumstances as to which information is waived by insurer; matters superfluous to disclose because of an express or implied warranty. • Ordinary business judgment determines materiality; whether fact is undisclosed does not automatically prove materiality. If disclosure is omitted • Insurer may avoid the contract for material non-disclosure; fraudulent misrepresentation may entail further consequences, subject to facts and applicable law. Section 22 requires material representations to be true; distinguish representations from warranties. Chief Engineer procedure • Report defects and class/statutory limitations through master, superintendent and DPA; preserve PMS records, survey reports, machinery failure history and voyage-relevant restrictions. • Never sign inaccurate technical declarations. Maintain traceable defect/corrective-action chronology; promptly notify material changes through assured/broker. Legal references • Marine Insurance Act 1963 Sections 19-22; policy terms and current applicable case law. Principle applies at contract formation; post-contract duties depend on contract and law. Source references: https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/1520/5/A1963-11.pdf ; https://comitemaritime.org/work/york-antwerp-rules-yar/ ; https://www.imo.org/ ; policy/club wording must be checked for the ship.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_31_to_40.pdf

T04-Q02 · What are the perils of the sea covered in a Marine Insurance contract? What can an insured do to cover a peril other than an insured peril of the sea? Enumerate the perils covered but where the onus of proof of “due diligence” is on insurers.
Definition of perils of the seas • Marine Insurance Act 1963 Section 2(e): fortuitous accidents or casualties of the seas; excludes ordinary action of winds and waves. Marine peril is accidental, not inevitable wear, gradual deterioration or ordinary weathering. • Typical examples: unexpected heavy-weather sea ingress damaging cargo, grounding, collision, striking a submerged object, accidental loss overboard—subject to policy wording, proximate cause and exclusions. Named perils and extent of cover • A hull policy may insure 'perils of the seas, rivers, lakes or other navigable waters', fire/explosion, violent theft by persons outside ship, jettison, piracy, collision/contact, earthquake/volcanic eruption/lightning and specified accidents, according to the attached Institute hull clauses. • War, strikes, terrorism, pollution and machinery breakdown are not automatically all covered under the basic perils-of-seas grant; obtain appropriate war/strikes clauses, P&I, loss of hire, cargo all-risks clauses, or tailored endorsements. • Cargo Institute Cargo Clauses (A)/(B)/(C) grant DIFFERENT extents of cover; Clause A is all-risks subject to exclusions, not a guarantee against every loss. Inchmaree/additional-perils clause • Institute Time Clauses - Hulls (1983) clause 6.2 extends cover for specified losses caused by: accidents in loading/discharging/shifting cargo or fuel; bursting of boilers/breakage of shafts or latent defects in machinery/hull; negligence of master, officers, crew or pilots; negligence of repairers/charterers, subject to the precise wording and due-diligence proviso. • Due diligence proviso: loss must not result from want of due diligence by assured, owners or managers; master/crew not automatically treated as owners merely because they own shares. Contract versions differ; do not call all routine wear an insured peril. • The statement that 'onus is on insurers' is wording-/jurisdiction-dependent; under standard hull clause practice insurers generally must establish the relevant exception once assured establishes insured loss, but evidentiary burdens must be verified against actual clause and governing law. CE evidence and precautions • Identify sudden fortuitous event, timing, casualty photographs, alarm/PMS records; notify underwriters/P&I and surveyor promptly; do not dismantle critical evidence without authorization except for safety; mitigate further loss. References • Marine Insurance Act 1963 Sections 2(e), 55; Institute Time Clauses Hulls 1/10/83 clause 6 (exact edition to be checked); Institute Cargo Clauses as attached. Source references: https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/1520/5/A1963-11.pdf ; https://comitemaritime.org/work/york-antwerp-rules-yar/ ; https://www.imo.org/ ; policy/club wording must be checked for the ship.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_31_to_40.pdf

T04-Q03 · Explain the principle of ‘Causa Proxima’ in Marine Insurance. An auxiliary generator suffered damage due to low LO pressure from water contamination — what actions/documents would you prepare for the insurance surveyor?
Causa proxima / proximate cause • Section 55(1), Marine Insurance Act 1963: insurer liable for loss proximately caused by peril insured against, unless policy otherwise provides; not liable for loss not proximately so caused. • Proximate = dominant, efficient and operative cause in the causal chain, not necessarily the event closest in time. Determine insured peril, exclusion and intervening causes using facts. Generator damage case: water-contaminated lubricating oil • Sequence: water ingress/contamination -> reduced effective lubrication, bearing film breakdown -> low LO pressure alarm/trip or sustained operation -> bearing overheating/seizure and crankshaft damage. • Root source of water: leaking LO cooler, contaminated bunkered oil, seawater ingress, jacket-water leakage, maintenance failure or another source—investigate rather than assume. • Low LO pressure is a symptom/intermediate link; identify the dominant insured cause. Mechanical damage due only to inherent wear or defective maintenance may be excluded; sudden accidental ingress/covered negligence may be insured subject to wording. Immediate Chief Engineer actions 1. Stop/secure generator, transfer electrical load safely, restore power with standby set; protect crew and prevent fire. 2. Isolate contaminated oil and possible cooler/water source; stop further damage and alert master/DPA/company/insurer. 3. Take labeled retained LO, water and damaged-component samples using agreed chain of custody; preserve original parts. 4. Examine LO cooler hydrotest, water content/Karl Fischer lab analysis, filters, sump condition, pressure transmitter and bearing clearances; use maker/class support. Insurance-surveyor documentary pack • Date/time incident narrative; engine-room logbook and alarms/trends (LO pressure, temperature, load), trip history, generator running hours; defect and maintenance records, LO analysis and bunkering/LO supply records. • Cause investigation with photos, oil and water samples, failed cooler pressure-test results, crankshaft/bearing measurements, service reports, maker recommendations, repair quotations, invoices, off-hire costs where separately covered; damaged spares inventory. • Class notification, surveyor attendance, repairs/workshop tests, approvals and sign-off; maintain truthful chronology and avoid admissions without factual support. Claim sequence • Notify -> protect/sue and labour -> survey -> establish proximate insured peril -> assess exclusions/deductible -> repair evidence and costs -> insurer adjustment; settle only under policy terms. Reference • Marine Insurance Act 1963 Sections 55, 64 and applicable hull-clause additional perils and exclusions. Source references: https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/1520/5/A1963-11.pdf ; https://comitemaritime.org/work/york-antwerp-rules-yar/ ; https://www.imo.org/ ; policy/club wording must be checked for the ship.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_31_to_40.pdf

T04-Q04 · State the different types of marine insurance policies that could be taken by owners, shippers or other related parties. Explain salient liabilities and exclusions for an insurer in each case. Name agencies in India dealing with marine insurance. Discuss the principles of Marine Insurance with suitable examples of each.
Types of marine policies and cover • Hull and Machinery (H&M): insured physical vessel/machinery damage caused by covered perils; exclusions usually wear, ordinary corrosion, gradual deterioration, wilful misconduct, war/strikes unless endorsed; deductibles apply. • Protection and Indemnity (P&I): member mutual third-party liabilities—crew injury/death, cargo liabilities, covered pollution, wreck removal, fines/legal costs as Club Rules allow; not simply duplicate H&M. • Cargo insurance: seller/buyer or cargo-interest policy under Institute Cargo Clauses A/B/C; excludes inherent vice, normal leakage/wear, insufficient packing as specified, delay and insolvency provisions subject to wording. • Freight/disbursements/increased-value: insures specifically declared freight, anticipated earnings or added ship value according to separate terms. • War risk/strikes: specified war, mines, terrorism/strikes exposures through endorsements or separate policy; standard marine policies often exclude war risks. • Loss of Hire: indemnifies insured loss of earnings following covered casualty after waiting period, subject to policy terms. • Open/floating, voyage, time, mixed, valued and unvalued policies describe HOW insurance attaches, not a separate peril by themselves. Principles with examples 1. Utmost good faith (S19): disclose major main-engine defect before placement. 2. Insurable interest (S7): owner has interest in vessel; cargo buyer interest depends on sale terms/title/risk. 3. Indemnity: compensation limited to covered financial loss (subject to agreed-value exceptions/policy). 4. Proximate cause (S55): insured storm damage versus excluded ordinary corrosion. 5. Subrogation (S79): after indemnifying, insurer may pursue responsible third party to extent allowed. 6. Contribution (S80): double insurance shares covered loss, not double profit. 7. Warranties (Ss35-40): express/implied policy obligations; breach consequences depend on governing law and terms. 8. Loss minimization (S78): assured takes reasonable steps to avert/minimize insured loss; sue-and-labour expenses may be recoverable. Indian industry/authorities • General insurers licensed by IRDAI (e.g., New India Assurance, United India, Oriental Insurance, National Insurance, private general insurers); marine brokers/intermediaries; P&I Clubs and correspondents. IRDAI regulates insurance; it does not itself underwrite claims. Exam note • Always identify insured interest, peril, coverage, exclusion, deductible, notification and insurance period; the policy wording and applicable law govern. References • Marine Insurance Act 1963 Sections 7,19,20,35-40,55,78-80; IRDAI; actual H&M/P&I/Institute Cargo Clauses. Source references: https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/1520/5/A1963-11.pdf ; https://comitemaritime.org/work/york-antwerp-rules-yar/ ; https://www.imo.org/ ; policy/club wording must be checked for the ship.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_31_to_40.pdf

T04-Q05 · What are the types of losses in marine insurance? Define General Average Act as per York Antwerp Rules 1994. State circumstances under which General Average can be declared. Differentiate express and implied warranties, with an example of each with reference to a hull and machinery policy.
Types of losses (Marine Insurance Act 1963) • Total loss: actual total loss (ATL, S57) where subject destroyed, ceases to be thing insured or irretrievably lost; constructive total loss (CTL, S60) where unavoidable ATL or cost of recovery/repair exceeds relevant value under statutory/policy test; notice of abandonment usually necessary for CTL (S62). • Partial loss: particular average (PA)—partial loss from insured peril borne by individual interest, not GA; general average (GA)—deliberate extraordinary sacrifice/expenditure for common safety shared among interests. • Particular charges and salvage charges are different concepts; distinguish from GA contributions. General Average Act — York–Antwerp Rules 1994, Rule A • Extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure intentionally and reasonably made/incurred for common safety to preserve from peril property involved in a common maritime adventure. • Requirements: actual common peril, deliberate and voluntary measure, extraordinary nature, reasonable action, common safety, direct causation and property saved/continuation of adventure. • Rule Paramount: no allowance unless reasonably made; Rule C excludes indirect losses/delay and environmental damage claims as GA. Examples of GA • Jettison of properly carried cargo, deliberate flooding to extinguish fire, voluntary stranding for common safety, qualifying salvage remuneration, port-of-refuge extraordinary expenses under rule-specific tests, damage to machinery when refloating as Rule VII permits. • Ordinary engine repairs, routine bunkering, cargo delay/demurrage and negligent maintenance are not themselves GA merely because voyage was delayed. Express versus implied warranties • Express: stated in policy or incorporated document, e.g., 'warranted trading only within specified geographical limits' / class-maintained warranty, subject to actual wording. • Implied (Marine Insurance Act S41): legality of insured adventure and conduct lawfully; historically a voyage-policy seaworthiness warranty arises at commencement (S41 as printed Act sections should be checked: voyage seaworthiness is S41, legality S43). Distinguish warranties from mere representations. • In H&M policy, class and trading limits are typically express conditions; applicability of statutory implied warranties varies for time versus voyage policies. Chief Engineer case • Record reason for GA act, peril, master decision and costs; preserve tank logs, machinery data, survey reports and invoices; notify H&M and P&I, GA adjuster, ship and cargo interests. References • Marine Insurance Act 1963 Ss56-64,66-67,35-43; YAR 1994 Rule A, Paramount, C and numbered rules. Contract may incorporate YAR 2016 rather than 1994. Source references: https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/1520/5/A1963-11.pdf ; https://comitemaritime.org/work/york-antwerp-rules-yar/ ; https://www.imo.org/ ; policy/club wording must be checked for the ship.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_31_to_40.pdf

T04-Q06 · “General average is based on certain essential features” — Discuss the statement. A vessel ran aground; main engine used ahead/astern to shake it loose, burning bearings due to excessive load — can this be included in general average? Justify.
Essential features of General Average • York–Antwerp Rules 1994 Rule A: common maritime adventure in peril; extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure; intentional and reasonable; solely for common safety; direct consequence; contribution by saved interests. • Rule Paramount demands reasonable sacrifice/expenditure. Rule C restricts allowable direct consequences and excludes delay losses and environmental damage liabilities. Grounding + main-engine bearing damage from efforts to refloat • YAR 1994 Rule VII specifically permits damage to machinery/boilers when a SHIP ASHORE AND IN PERIL endeavours to refloat for common safety, if damage arose from actual intention to refloat at risk of such damage. • If grounded vessel deliberately operated ahead/astern to free the ship and bearings burnt because of extraordinary loading, the loss MAY be allowable as GA when facts establish common peril, intention, reasonableness, direct causal damage and supporting evidence. • Important exclusion: if the ship is AFLOAT, damage caused by working propelling machinery/boilers is NEVER allowed under Rule VII, even if propulsion was used during emergency. • Normal machinery defects, pre-existing bearing wear, inadequate lubrication or reckless/unreasonable actions may defeat claim; no automatic cover simply because grounding occurred. Procedure and evidence 1. Master and CE assess grounding, stability, hull stress and danger; record deliberate refloating decision and reason. 2. Preserve manoeuvring orders, RPM/shaft power, LO pressure/temperature, bearing alarms, engine logs, VDR data, groundings and chart positions. 3. Arrange independent class/H&M survey and maker measurements; establish pre-existing condition versus additional refloating damage. 4. Document repair costs, salvage expenses, cargo/cargo values and all expenditures; notify GA adjuster and P&I/H&M promptly. 5. GA adjuster applies CONTRACTUALLY INCORPORATED York–Antwerp version (1994 if specified), determines allowances and collects GA security. Numerical example • Reasonable qualifying bearing-repair expenditure = USD 200,000; if contributory values ship USD 6m, cargo USD 4m, total USD 10m, ship's share 60%=USD 120,000 and cargo 40%=USD 80,000 (simplified, no other adjustments). References • YAR 1994 Rule A, Paramount, C and Rule VII; Marine Insurance Act 1963 Sections 66,67. YAR 2016 Rule VII retains core grounded/afloat distinction. Source references: https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/1520/5/A1963-11.pdf ; https://comitemaritime.org/work/york-antwerp-rules-yar/ ; https://www.imo.org/ ; policy/club wording must be checked for the ship.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_31_to_40.pdf

T04-Q07 · Explain the concepts of ‘Particular Average’ and ‘General Average’. What are the circumstances when a general average can be declared? Who are average adjusters and what is their role?
Particular Average (PA) • Partial loss to one insured interest caused by an insured peril; borne initially by that interest, recoverable from its insurer subject to coverage, insured value, deductible and exclusions. • Example: storm cracks a vessel's hatch cover, damaging only ship property without deliberate common-safety sacrifice. General Average (GA) • Extraordinary and intentional reasonable sacrifice/expenditure made for COMMON SAFETY from common peril, shared by ship, cargo and other contributory interests (YAR Rule A). • Example: jettisoning cargo to restore stability; intentionally flooding a hold to extinguish fire; salvage or refuge costs if the contract's rules permit. PA vs GA • PA: fortuitous loss, one interest, usually insurance indemnity; GA: voluntary common-safety act, direct reasonable loss, shared among interests; coverage of GA contributions depends on each party's policy. Conditions for GA declaration 1. Actual common peril threatening the adventure; ship/cargo interests jointly exposed. 2. Extraordinary sacrifice/expenditure, not routine operational expense. 3. Deliberate reasonable action for common safety and preservation. 4. Direct causal loss or expense under York–Antwerp Rules and Rule Paramount. 5. Common maritime adventure property saved or benefits enabling contribution. • Master/owner may declare GA; declaration is not conclusive that individual claims qualify: adjuster determines allowances according to contractual YAR edition. Average adjuster duties • Independent specialist appointed often by owner: gather casualty facts, identify YAR applicable, distinguish PA/GA, value ship and cargo at termination, calculate GA allowances and contributory values, issue adjustment, prepare GA security/cash deposits/guarantees and claims reconciliation. • GA contribution formula: interest's contributory value / all contributory values x total allowable GA expenditure; adjust for sacrifices, deposits, commissions and contractual rules. Documents / CE role • GA declaration, casualty and master's logs, cargo manifest, bills of lading, valuation, salvage contracts, survey reports, machinery logs, invoices; CE preserves technical causation, safe response, expenditure details and accurate reports. References • Marine Insurance Act 1963 Sections 66-67; YAR 1994 Rule A, Paramount, C, XXI as applicable; YAR 2016 if incorporated. Source references: https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/1520/5/A1963-11.pdf ; https://comitemaritime.org/work/york-antwerp-rules-yar/ ; https://www.imo.org/ ; policy/club wording must be checked for the ship.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_31_to_40.pdf

T04-Q08 · With reference to Marine Insurance, write short notes on: (a) Port of Refuge (b) Particular Average and General Average (c) Total Loss and Constructive Loss.
(a) Port of Refuge • A port/safe place entered after casualty to avert danger to life, vessel or cargo and enable inspection, repair, discharge or assistance; requires assessment of safety, environmental risks, shelter, berth/port facilities, weather and permission. • Under YAR 1994 Rule X, expenses of entering or leaving a port/place of refuge may be GA if entry is necessary for common safety by reason of casualty/sacrifice/extraordinary circumstance, subject to exact rule; Rule XI may allow certain wages/maintenance during qualifying detention and treatment; Rule F substituted expenses possible. • Port-of-refuge decisions do not automatically make all port charges GA; directness, reasonableness and rule exclusions matter. (b) Particular Average and General Average • Particular Average: accidental partial loss to single interest due to insured peril; e.g., sea-water damage to vessel's electrical distribution panel. Owner claims on H&M if covered. • General Average: intentional reasonable extraordinary sacrifice/expenditure for common safety of common adventure, contributing interests share; e.g., cargo jettison to avoid capsizing; salvage or tug costs as allowable. • YAR Rule A and Rule Paramount. GA contribution shares depend on actual contributory values and allowances; not equally split per container or cargo party. (c) Total Loss and Constructive Total Loss • Actual Total Loss (ATL) S57: physical destruction, no longer thing insured, or irrevocable deprivation; notice of abandonment unnecessary. • Constructive Total Loss (CTL) S60: total loss effectively unavoidable or reasonable recovery/repair cost greater than relevant repaired/recovered value, subject to policy; generally requires timely notice of abandonment S62 unless exception. • Example: ship insured value USD 10m, repaired value USD 7m, necessary repairs USD 9m: potentially CTL under statutory repair-cost versus repaired value test, subject to applicable policy wording. • Owner must not automatically declare CTL based solely on 'repair cost > insured value' without checking actual policy and law. Chief Engineer actions • Prepare refuge machinery condition, risk assessment, pollution report, repair list, work permits, class survey, repair quotes, expenditure and cargo discharge records; notify master, flag, company and insurers through established channels. References • Marine Insurance Act 1963 Ss56-62; YAR 1994 Rules A, X, XI, F. Source references: https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/1520/5/A1963-11.pdf ; https://comitemaritime.org/work/york-antwerp-rules-yar/ ; https://www.imo.org/ ; policy/club wording must be checked for the ship.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_31_to_40.pdf

T04-Q09 · What is ‘Running Down Clause’ and ‘General Average Expenses’? How are claims under these clauses treated by H&M and P&I insurers? A vessel sustained damage identified only during drydocking a year later — admissibility and claim valuation.
Running Down Clause (RDC) • H&M collision-liability extension, often traditional 'three-fourths collision liability' clause in Institute Time Clauses Hulls 1983: insurer indemnifies specified 3/4 of covered legal liability for damage to another vessel/property, subject to insured-value ceiling, terms, exclusions and sister-ship/cross-liability provisions. • Remaining 1/4 collision liability frequently falls to P&I Club under Club Rules; many modern hull wordings provide 4/4 RDC—never state 3/4 H&M + 1/4 P&I as universal. • Traditional RDC often excludes crew injury/death, cargo under carriage, pollution, wreck removal, contract liabilities, etc.; P&I may cover those subject to Rules. General Average expenses • Allowable extraordinary sacrifice/expenditure for common safety: qualifying salvage, jettison sacrifice, port-of-refuge expense, refloating machinery damage under YAR Rule VII. H&M usually covers ship's admissible GA sacrifice/contribution according to clause; P&I can provide cargo GA-related liabilities where conditions met—not duplicate a normal H&M GA claim. Collision numerical example • Ship A 40% at fault, Ship B 60%; A's liability to B property damage USD 1m -> covered three-fourths H&M USD 750k, one-fourth P&I USD 250k if policies match; P&I pollution and crew claims separately assessed. • Actual insured liability depends on court/settlement, fault shares, deductible, exclusions and policy terms. Damage first discovered one year later in dry dock • Late discovery is not automatically barred; claimant must establish loss occurred during insured period, proximate insured cause, timely notice 'as soon as reasonably practicable' under policy, and preservation of evidence. Insurer may challenge late notice, prejudice, pre-existing defect or wear and tear. • Obtain contemporaneous log evidence, previous/next class surveys, dated photographs, tank/hull condition and metallurgical evidence, yard repair quotation, surveyor's causal report, repair receipts, claim correspondence. • Valuation: reasonable covered cost of repairs + permitted related expenses; apply deductible, betterment/new-for-old provisions, apportionment between insured and uninsured damage and any policy limits. Costs incurred a year later are not automatically paid at current prices without review. Chief Engineer duties • Notify master/company/broker/P&I/H&M promptly on discovery, avoid speculative cause, preserve damaged components, arrange surveyor attendance BEFORE irreversible repairs where practicable, protect safety. References • Institute Time Clauses Hulls 1/10/83 3/4 Collision Liability clause; relevant H&M and P&I Rules; Marine Insurance Act 1963 S55, Ss66-67,78. Source references: https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/1520/5/A1963-11.pdf ; https://comitemaritime.org/work/york-antwerp-rules-yar/ ; https://www.imo.org/ ; policy/club wording must be checked for the ship.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_31_to_40.pdf

T04-Q10 · What are the principles of modern salvage law? What is General Average? Explain with context to General Average: i. Entitlement ii. Artificial iii. Adjustment iv. Contestation.
Principles of modern salvage law • International Convention on Salvage 1989: voluntary professional assistance to property in danger in navigable waters; reward reflects useful results and Article 13 criteria; Article 12 'no cure, no pay' is the normal reward principle, subject to Article 14 special compensation and contract/SCOPIC arrangements. • Article 8: salvor uses due care, prevents/minimizes environmental damage, seeks assistance and cooperates; owner/master cooperate, exercise due care and accept redelivery when reasonably requested. • Article 13 assessment includes salved value, environmental skill/efforts, success, peril, salvor skill, time/expenses/losses, risk of liability, promptness, equipment availability/readiness; reward excluding interest and recoverable costs cannot exceed salved value. • Article 14 special compensation may apply in specified environmental-threat cases; SCOPIC is optional LOF contractual machinery, not an automatic treaty entitlement. General Average • YAR 1994 Rule A: intentional reasonable extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure for common safety from common peril involving property in maritime adventure; contributing interests share cost. (i) Entitlement • GA allowance depends on proof of Rule A, Paramount, directness Rule C and numbered Rules; carrier/owner may declare, but adjuster determines compensable amounts; insurer reimburses respective insured's qualifying GA sacrifice/contribution subject to contract. (ii) 'Artificial' General Average • Not a defined YAR statutory category. Industry/exam usage often refers to expenses deemed GA by numbered or substituted-expense Rules, despite not being an original physical sacrifice; e.g. Rule F substituted expense allowable up to GA cost thereby avoided. Do NOT present invented automatic entitlement. (iii) Adjustment • Average adjuster reviews evidence, allocates allowable expenses, determines contributory ship/cargo/freight values, requests GA guarantees/cash security, issues signed adjustment. GA rate = total allowable GA / total contributory value; each interest pays proportional share subject to adjustments. (iv) Contestation • Ship/cargo insurer or cargo interest may challenge: absence of common peril, unreasonable expenditure, loss not direct, faulty valuations, inadmissible pollution/delay expenses, defective notice/security, contractual edition or time limits; dispute resolved by contract, arbitration or competent court. Flow • Marine peril -> master orders reasonable extraordinary common-safety act -> save property -> preserve evidence -> declare GA and collect security -> adjuster applies incorporated YAR -> allocate contributions -> insurance settlement/dispute resolution. Chief Engineer • Document orders, causation, technical damage, work permits, ballast/transfer and repair logs, survey reports, salvage assistance, expenses; notify owner/insurers and cooperate with adjuster. References • Salvage Convention 1989 Articles 8,12-14,20; YAR 1994 A, Paramount, C, F and VII; Marine Insurance Act 1963 Sections 66-67. Source references: https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/1520/5/A1963-11.pdf ; https://comitemaritime.org/work/york-antwerp-rules-yar/ ; https://www.imo.org/ ; policy/club wording must be checked for the ship.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_31_to_40.pdf

T04-Q11 · What are P&I clubs? Describe how P&I clubs collect funds from ship-owners. What risks are covered under ‘protection’ and ‘indemnity’? How do P&I clubs handle claims / minimum requirements for averages?
Question bank: 2021-07, 2021-08, 2021-12, 2022-03, 2023-11 Q9, 2024-06 Q7, 2025-02 Q7, 2025-04 Q8 Definition, club structure and funding • Protection and Indemnity (P&I) Clubs are mutual, generally not-for-profit marine liability insurers owned by their shipowner/charterer members. Their cover is governed by the Club Rules and certificate of entry; clubs are not statutory compensation funds. • Members contribute advance/estimated calls (often payable in instalments), supplementary or additional calls for a deficit, release calls upon terminating entry to close future open-year liabilities; club investment income also supports claims. Some clubs price entries on fixed premium, especially charterers. • Calls are assessed by risk: ship type, entered GT, trade, claims record, deductibles, crew, management, regulatory compliance. Supplementary calls are not a fixed percentage applicable to every Club or year. • International Group (IG) pooling combines participating clubs' major liabilities and buys market/captive reinsurance; club retention and pool/reinsurance should not be conflated with member's deductible. Protection risks and indemnity risks • Historically 'protection' included crew and passenger injury/death, collision liability outside hull cover, damage to third-party property; 'indemnity' included cargo liabilities and related legal liabilities. In modern club rules the categories overlap: do NOT draw an absolute boundary. • Common covered risks (subject to Rules): crew illness/death/repatriation, passenger liabilities, cargo loss or damage due to legal liability, specified pollution response/compensation, wreck removal, 1/4 RDC if H&M covers 3/4, fixed/floating objects, life salvage, certain fines and legal expenses, diversion/stowaway expenses. • Typically excluded or requiring separate cover: damage to entered vessel (H&M), loss of earnings, ordinary contractual penalties, deliberate misconduct, war risks unless specifically covered, prohibited/illegal trading. Claims handling and adjustment 1. Inform master, Club/appointed correspondent promptly, preserve evidence, follow pollution/casualty reporting duties and mitigate loss. 2. Club appoints surveyor/lawyer/correspondent, checks causation, legal liability, insurance terms and limits; obtains security (letter of undertaking) where appropriate. 3. Claims documents: incident report, logs/VDR, crew statements, cargo papers, photographs, bills, medical and expense invoices, class reports, communications, protest. 4. Pay/settle according to Club Rules; 'pay to be paid' often applies subject to exceptions, law and club rules. Club may advance or guarantee claims. 5. In General Average, hull/cargo interests generally provide GA security and contribute under York-Antwerp Rules; Club may assist for covered liabilities but does not automatically pay all GA contributions. Averages are established by an average adjuster; no universal 'minimum requirement for averages' exists. IG reinsurance update (2026/27) • As published by the International Group: individual Club retention USD 10m; pool USD 10m–100m; GXL USD 2.25bn excess USD 100m (to USD 2.35bn); collective overspill USD 1bn excess USD 2.35bn (to USD 3.35bn). Actual claim limits and exclusions are determined by programme and Club Rules. CE duties and sources • Preserve ER log, ORB, fuel/cargo plans, maintenance and alarm history, pollution records; coordinate with master, DPA, surveyor and correspondent; do not admit liability or sign inaccurate statements. • References: Club Rules; International Group 2026/27 Pool/GXL (igpandi.org/reinsurance/); York-Antwerp Rules 2016.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_41_to_50.pdf

T04-Q12 · Define P&I insurance and H&M insurance, outlining main differences in coverage and purpose. Discuss typical risks covered by each with examples of incidents.
Question bank: 2024-11 Q6 Definition and primary difference • Hull & Machinery (H&M): first-party property insurance covering insured physical hull, machinery and specified associated vessel interests against named/covered marine perils, subject to deductibles and exclusions. • Protection & Indemnity (P&I): primarily third-party legal liability insurance through a mutual Club, for incidents involving persons, cargo, pollution, wreck and other listed liabilities according to Club Rules. Comparison • Insured object: H&M = vessel/property; P&I = member's legal liabilities and specified expenses. • Typical insurer: H&M commercial underwriter(s); P&I mutual Club/association. Both can use commercial reinsurance. • Pricing: H&M premium; P&I calls/estimated premiums, possible supplementary/release calls or fixed premium. • Claims: H&M repairs, actual/constructive total loss, some salvage and GA; P&I crew, cargo, pollution, wreck, third-party property and covered collision portion. • Limit: H&M sum insured/clauses; P&I limits vary by risk/Club, with special limits for pollution and other exposures. • Typical exclusions: H&M wear/corrosion, ordinary machinery deterioration and most third-party risks; P&I damage to own vessel, lost earnings, fines/contractual liabilities outside rules, certain war risks. Examples 1. Sudden grounding damages shell plate and propeller: H&M ordinarily assesses covered physical damage; P&I may respond to cargo, pollution and wreck liability. 2. Engine fire damages turbocharger: H&M if insured peril and exclusions/due diligence satisfied; crew injury liability may be P&I. 3. Tank overflow pollutes coastal waters: P&I handles qualifying statutory cleanup and third-party liabilities; own tank repairs may be H&M. 4. Collision: a typical older H&M 3/4 RDC plus P&I remaining 1/4 arrangement is policy-dependent; some H&M policies cover 4/4 collision liability. CE procedure • Save life, contain risk, preserve machinery evidence, notify Master/Company/Class/insurers, collect alarm/PMS/history, segregate samples, meet surveys; don't assume insurance automatically follows any malfunction. • References: Marine Insurance Act 1963 (ss. 55,66,78); actual hull terms and Club Rules.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_41_to_50.pdf

T04-Q13 · Why does a ship require Marine Insurance cover? Explain Hull Claims and Cargo Claims related with Marine Insurance. State related documents/information required from the ship, highlighting validity.
Question bank: 2022-12 Q6 Why cover is required • Shipping involves valuable property, cargo, accidents, collision, breakdown, crew injuries, pollution and claims by third parties. H&M, P&I, cargo and war risks transfer insured financial exposures and help maintain continuity and creditor/charterparty obligations. • Marine insurance is not a universal statutory obligation for every possible risk: some conventions require insurance/financial security, e.g. CLC 1992 for qualifying tanker oil cargo pollution (more than 2,000 tonnes of persistent oil cargo), Bunker Convention 2001 for ships over 1,000 GT, Nairobi wreck removal for ships >=300 GT where applicable. Hull claims • Covered peril causes damage to hull/machinery, e.g. collision, fire, grounding, sudden casualty; extent depends on hull policy and additional-perils clauses. • Immediate steps: mitigate loss, prevent secondary damage; promptly notify broker/underwriter/class; appoint surveyor, obtain repair estimates; retain damaged components and failed-part analysis; meet policy notice/approval requirements. • Damage/repair claim requires evidence of occurrence, insured interest, proximate cause, extent and expenditure. Deductible and exclusions assessed by insurer. Cargo claims • Cargo owner/insurer may claim against carrying ship for loss/wet damage/contamination/shortage; legal carrier liability depends on applicable contract, Hague/Hague-Visby/Hamburg/COGSA etc. Cargo insurance protects cargo interest, whereas P&I may cover carrier's legal liability. • Preserve bills of lading and mate's receipts, manifest, loading/discharge tally, stowage/lashing plans, reefer temperature logs, sea/weather reports, survey, notices of damage, protest and photographs. • Do not confuse shipowner's P&I claim defense with the cargo owner's first-party cargo policy claim. Documents from vessel and validity • Insurance certificate/entry and endorsements valid on incident date; H&M policy schedule, P&I certificate of entry, financial security certificates when applicable; class/registry/statutory certification as relevant. • Engine log/ORB/bridge log, VDR, AIS/ECDIS, planned-maintenance records, machinery alarms, bunker documents, drawings, photographs, crew statements, surveyor findings, invoices, quotations, loss-prevention records. • Check vessel name/IMO number, voyage, ship/insured values, insured period, trading warranties, deductibles, notification conditions; certificate must be valid at time of loss, not merely at later claim submission. CE role and legal sources • Accurate factual narrative, secure samples, evidence chain, no tampering; assist survey/class and owners, reduce further loss. • References: Marine Insurance Act 1963 ss. 7,19-22,55,66,78-80; Club Rules; IMO CLC/Bunker requirements.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_41_to_50.pdf

T04-Q14 · What is the New Jason Clause and how does it protect shipowners in cases of general average? Explain the 3/4ths Collision Clause in maritime insurance and its implications for insurer and shipowner.
Question bank: 2024-07 Q8 New Jason Clause: purpose • US general-average practice: traditional Jason Clause and the modern New Jason Clause address GA contributions even where the casualty was caused by ship/crew negligence, insofar as the carrier would not be legally responsible for that negligence under the applicable contract and cargo law. • Incorporated into bill of lading/contract of carriage; protects the owner's right to contributions from cargo for allowable GA expenditure in situations such as a navigational error where the carrier retains a relevant statutory defense. • Clause does NOT excuse failure to exercise due diligence to make the ship seaworthy at commencement where the law imposes this obligation, nor make all losses GA; must still satisfy Rule A/Rule Paramount under incorporated York–Antwerp Rules. • Illustration: vessel grounds through negligent navigation; master reasonably hires salvors to save cargo and vessel; where carrier has legal immunity for navigation error under governing cargo regime, New Jason Clause may permit cargo contribution to GA notwithstanding that error. 3/4 Running Down (Collision) Clause • Under traditional Institute Time Clauses Hulls, insurers indemnify three-fourths of covered liability incurred by assured to another ship/property following collision, subject to conditions, exclusions and agreed insured value; shipowner remains liable for remaining one-fourth (often insured via P&I). • This is a CONTRACTUAL division between insurers, not an automatic collision-fault percentage between ships; if two vessels are 40/60 negligent, apportion fault first, calculate recoverable collision liability, then apply policy's RDC cover. • Often includes 'cross liabilities' provision: each shipowner considered separately liable to the other unless one/both vessels' liabilities limited by law, subject to exact clause. • Classic exclusions under RDC can include damage to fixed/floating objects (unless covered separately), personal injury and cargo on insured ship, pollution and wreck removal; club or other covers may apply. Numerical illustration • Insured ship liable USD 800,000 to other vessel after contributory-fault apportionment. With a responding traditional 3/4 RDC and no deductible/limit complications: H&M covers USD 600,000; shipowner's residual USD 200,000 may be insured under P&I Rules. • A modern H&M 4/4 collision clause changes this division. Always inspect actual wording. CE role • Document manoeuvring orders, ship speeds, engine response, machinery and steering records; notify master, insurer and P&I; preserve VDR/alarm logs and defect records. • References: York–Antwerp Rules 2016; Marine Insurance Act 1963 s.66; applicable bills of lading and Institute Time Clauses Hulls.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_41_to_50.pdf

T04-Q15 · Compare and discuss the basic features of CLC’92 and Bunker Convention 2001 with reference to type of ships, oil & nature of pollution damages covered, limits of liability and geographical coverage.
Question bank: 2023-02 Q9 Core distinction • CLC 1992: strict liability of registered tanker owner for pollution damage caused by persistent oil carried as cargo, including tanker's qualifying bunker pollution; covers tankers laden and certain ballast voyages when residues of such oil remain. • Bunker Convention 2001: strict liability for pollution damage from oil used/intended to operate or propel a ship, and residues thereof; applies to most seagoing ships, including tankers where a spill is NOT covered by CLC. No double recovery under both conventions for the same CLC-covered pollution damage. Comparison • Ship: CLC applies to seagoing vessels built/adapted to carry oil in bulk as cargo (with conditions for combination ships); Bunker Convention covers seagoing vessels of any type within treaty scope. • Oil: CLC 'oil' is persistent hydrocarbon mineral oil as cargo or related tanker bunker; Bunker includes hydrocarbon mineral oils used for propulsion/operation, including fuel and lubricating oil, whether persistent or not. • Damage: contamination loss/damage, preventive measures and resulting loss, reasonable reinstatement costs and economic loss where legally compensable; pure ecological damages limited as treaty defines. • Geography: territory incl territorial sea and EEZ/equivalent area up to 200 nm of a Party; preventive measures wherever taken to prevent/minimize pollution within these areas. • Responsibility: CLC registered owner strictly liable (with limited exceptions); Bunker shipowner includes registered owner, bareboat charterer, manager, operator (joint/several where multiple liable). • Insurance: CLC compulsory security where ship carries >2,000 tonnes oil in bulk as cargo; Bunker compulsory insurance ships >1,000 GT, according to treaty. Liability limits • CLC 1992 amended 2000: up to 5,000 GT = 4.51m SDR; 5,000–140,000 GT = 4.51m + 631 SDR for each GT >5,000; >140,000 GT max 89.77m SDR. Registered owner's right to limit lost for personal intentional/reckless-with-knowledge conduct per treaty. • Bunker Convention has NO stand-alone scale of limits: owner may use applicable national/international limitation (e.g. LLMC 1996 as amended), but special limits/exclusions depend on jurisdiction and conventions. • No standalone international Bunker Fund. CLC may be supplemented by 1992 IOPC Fund and supplementary fund in States party to relevant instruments; separate from the owner's CLC limitation. Exceptions and evidence • Limited defenses: war/hostilities or exceptional inevitable irresistible natural phenomenon; wholly intentional third-party act; government negligence maintaining navigation aids (within wording). Burden matters. Ordinary crew negligence does NOT remove strict liability. • CE: identify source of spilled oil (cargo/ballast residues/bunker), tank soundings and samples, SOPEP, ORB, BDN and manifold records; immediately notify authority and Club. • References: IMO CLC 1992 page; IMO Bunker Convention 2001; LLMC 1996/2012.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_41_to_50.pdf

T04-Q16 · With respect to the geo-political/war situations ongoing in the Persian Gulf: (a) Explain War Risk cover in Marine Insurance and the key aspects included in this cover (b) Describe measures which insurers and P&I clubs take to protect themselves from high claims during war situations.
Question bank: 2026-03 Q3 Meaning and scope of war risks • War risks are generally excluded from ordinary H&M/P&I unless specially reinsured/endorsed. Marine war policies may cover war, civil war, revolution, hostilities, mines/torpedoes, warlike weapon effects, capture/seizure, and specified terrorism/strikes per wordings. • War risk H&M protects physical vessel/property; war P&I protects legal liabilities arising from insured war events, subject to club's automatic limits and optional excess covers. Detention, loss of hire, blocking/trapping may need distinct extensions; sanctions restrictions can prevent payment. • Geographic exclusions and listed areas change over time; Persian Gulf/Strait of Hormuz trading must be assessed on the current club/war-underwriter notice, not assumed automatically prohibited or covered. Shipowner measures 1. Obtain specific war-risks quotation and check listed-area notification, additional premium, cancellation clause and territorial scope. 2. Verify insurance certificates, P&I war cover limits and exclusions; obtain charterer allocation of additional premium and deviation costs. 3. Perform company voyage risk assessment: missile/drone risk, mines, piracy/security and local conflict; apply BMP guidance and ISPS/SMS procedures where appropriate. 4. Carry out crew briefing, bridge watches, contact authorities as directed, maintain citadel/communications where appropriate; never defeat sanctions restrictions. How insurers/P&I Clubs manage accumulation • Identify excluded/listed areas; require advance notice and pay additional premium or impose limits for entering high-risk areas. • Purchase facultative and treaty reinsurance, IG pool/GXL and specialist war reinsurance; maintain catastrophe modelling and accumulate exposures by region. • Set deductibles, retention, territorial exclusions, annual aggregates, cancellation notice periods and sanctions clauses; control claims, surveys and recoveries. • Require compliance with safety advisories, ship security plans and risk mitigation as conditions where written. Current reinsurance update • IG 2026/27 individual Club retention USD 10m, Pool to USD 100m; GXL to USD 2.35bn, Collective Overspill additional USD 1bn to USD 3.35bn. These are GENERAL Group reinsurance figures, not proof that any single war claim has identical cover. Chief Engineer • Check vital/critical spares, standby generation, fire containment, emergency steering, emergency communications and crew drill; preserve evidence and report war-damage/pollution as required. • References: Club Rules and war exclusions, International Group 2026/27 programme, actual H&M war clauses and JWC listed-area circulars.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_41_to_50.pdf

T04-Q17 · A VLCC suffered hull damage following a tank explosion and sank after a few days while undergoing salvage operations in the territorial sea of a coastal state. Discuss ship-owner’s protections with reference to (a) marine insurance and (b) liabilities under international conventions.
Question bank: 2026-01 Q4, 2026-04 Q4 Facts and issue • VLCC has tank explosion, hull damage, then sinks during salvage within coastal State territorial sea. Multiple risks arise: own-hull loss, salvage remuneration, cargo oil and bunker pollution, claims for wreck removal and third-party damage. • Investigate initial cause (tank atmosphere, static, hot work, inert-gas failure, ignition source) and whether due diligence/warranties affect insurance; do not presume culpability from explosion alone. (a) Marine insurance protection • H&M: repairs/actual total loss if covered peril; a vessel irretrievably sunk is usually ATL under Marine Insurance Act s.57, subject to policy and cause. Before sinking, CTL may arise where cost to recover/repair exceeds policy/statutory test; proper notice of abandonment may be necessary. • H&M salvage charges/GA (if covered), sue-and-labour expenses to minimize insured loss, collision risks where relevant; separate loss-of-hire if purchased; exclusions for wear, wilful misconduct and certain war risks. • P&I: crew/death, cargo third-party liability (subject to conventions and carriage law), pollution cleanup, wreck removal if legally liable, fines and legal expenses, as provided by rules; CLC certificates/security backed by insurer where applicable. • Cargo underwriters insure oil cargo interests and can seek recovery against liable parties subject to applicable conventions/contract. (b) International legal liabilities • CLC 1992 if persistent cargo oil pollution from qualifying tanker within territorial sea: registered owner strict liability, limited treaty defenses; compulsory insurance where carriage >2,000 tonnes persistent oil cargo, direct action against insurer, CLC limitation scales by GT (max 89.77m SDR). Pollution cleanup/preventive measures may be claimed; IOPC Fund may supplement where State party. • Bunker Convention applies only to bunker pollution not within the CLC's pollution-damage definition; do not apply both for same spill. It has no own limits; applicable LLMC/other law matters. • Nairobi Wreck Removal 2007 primarily EEZ but coastal State may elect territorial application by treaty declaration; evaluate whether coastal State has extended it. National port/territorial laws could independently mandate wreck removal. • LLMC limitation of relevant maritime claims under applicable law, subject to exclusions; CLC compensation is governed by CLC, not automatically aggregated into LLMC fund. • Salvage Convention 1989 Article 13 reward, 14 special compensation for environment where applicable; LOF/SCOPIC may alter contractual remuneration. CE operational sequence 1. Save life, fight fire, isolate ignition, assess inert-gas/tank conditions, pollution response, alert Master/coastal State/SOPEP. 2. Preserve explosion investigation evidence, cargo plans, tank atmosphere logs, inert-gas readings, hot-work permits, alarms, VDR and ER records. 3. Support salvors: stability, bunker inventory, structural plans, safe fuel transfer; preserve invoices and communication. 4. Notify Company, flag, class, H&M, P&I, CLC insurer and coastal authorities; record subsequent sinking timing. • References: IMO CLC, Bunker, Nairobi, LLMC and Salvage conventions; Marine Insurance Act 1963 ss. 55,57,60,62,78.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_41_to_50.pdf

Salvage, Liens & LLMC (9)
T05-Q01 · A grounded ship has been re-floated and towed by a professional salvor to a place of safety. As per the Salvage Convention the salvor has a right to award. List criteria under Article 13 for determining the amount of award. State duties of the salvor and shipowner/master. Assuming LOF-2000 was agreed, discuss when the Convention applies and when the LOF applies if a dispute arises.
Question bank: 2022-08 Q3, 2022-12 Q4, 2023-07 Q1, 2024-08 Q7 Salvage Convention 1989, Article 13: 10 award criteria (not necessarily equal weight) 1. Salved value of ship and other property. 2. Skill and efforts of salvors preventing/minimizing environmental damage. 3. Measure of success obtained by salvor. 4. Nature and degree of danger. 5. Skill/effort saving ship, other property and life. 6. Time used and expenses/losses incurred by salvors. 7. Risk of liability and other risks run by salvors/equipment. 8. Promptness of services. 9. Availability/use of vessels or other salvage equipment. 10. State of readiness/efficiency of salvor's equipment and value thereof. • Article 13(3): total salvage reward (excluding interest and recoverable legal costs) shall not exceed salved value of ship and other property. Life salvage is not an independently chargeable reward (Article 16), though life-saving services may affect distribution/reward. Salvor duties (Article 8(1)) • Exercise due care; exercise due care to prevent/minimize environmental damage; seek help from other salvors when reasonably required; accept intervention of other salvors when reasonably requested by owner/master unless unreasonable (without prejudice to award). Owner/master duties (Article 8(2)) • Cooperate fully during salvage; exercise due care to prevent/minimize environmental damage; accept redelivery of salved property when reasonably requested by salvor. LOF-2000 vs Convention • The International Salvage Convention 1989 provides the legal background and Articles 8, 13, 14, among others. LOF-2000 is a private written salvage contract setting 'no cure - no pay', standard obligations, choice of arbitration at Lloyd's and agreed procedures; contract provisions govern to extent permitted by Convention/applicable law. • If the parties sign LOF-2000, a remuneration dispute is ordinarily resolved according to its arbitration provisions; tribunal applies contractual rules and relevant Salvage Convention provisions. Contract date does not justify assuming LOF-2024 terms. • SCOPIC applies ONLY if the particular LOF incorporates and SCOPIC is invoked per its conditions; it is not automatically in every LOF. CE records • Grounding/refloating timeline, tug(s) and personnel deployed, towage logs, machinery availability, hazards, bunker/cargo quantities, pollution avoided, salved values, communication, security and cost documentation. • References: Salvage Convention 1989 arts. 8,13,14,16; LOF-2000 agreed text; Lloyd's SCOPIC.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_41_to_50.pdf

T05-Q02 · Explain the primary objectives of the International Convention on Salvage, 1989. How does it balance interests of salvors and vessel owners? Discuss significance of Articles 13 & 14 — reward criteria and ‘Special Compensation’ for preventing environmental damage.
Question bank: 2024-11 Q5 Objectives of 1989 International Salvage Convention • Encourage prompt voluntary salvage of ships/property and protection of marine environment; harmonize rights and duties, remuneration and dispute resolution. • Preserve classic 'no cure no pay' property salvage while allowing environmental compensation to reduce incentives to abandon low-value but pollution-threatening casualties. Key provisions • Article 6: parties may make salvage contracts; certain mandatory Convention obligations continue. • Article 8: duties of salvor and owner/master (due care, cooperation, environment). • Article 12: reward requires useful result, subject to Article 14 special compensation; pure life salvage not independently rewarded under Article 16. • Article 13: 10 criteria: salved value; environmental protection skill; success; danger; skill saving persons/property; time/expenses/losses; liability/other risks; promptness; equipment availability/use; readiness/efficiency/value. Reward paid by ship/property interests proportionate to respective salved values unless national law differs. • Article 13(3): award cannot exceed salved property value exclusive of interest/legal costs. Article 14 special compensation • Salvor conducts salvage of a vessel that by itself/cargo threatens environmental damage but Article 13 reward is less than Article 14 special compensation assessed under article: salvor eligible for expenses (fair rate for personnel/equipment as defined in Art.14(3)); increases up to 30% and in justified cases up to 100% of expenses if salvor prevents/minimizes environmental damage, subject to article's requirements. • Article 14 special compensation is payable by vessel owner only to extent compensation exceeds Article 13 reward; not cargo's rateable contribution in same way as Art.13 property award. Distinguish special compensation from a reward for pollution actually caused. • Article 14 liability may be reduced/removed if negligence of salvor prevents or reduces success. SCOPIC • A contractual alternative in LOF when incorporated and invoked; tariff-based remuneration plus 25% uplift, with the adjustment/discount to Art.13 award laid down in SCOPIC. SCOPIC is not automatically part of the Convention and does not apply without contractual incorporation/invocation. Balancing interests • Salvors: financially motivated by award and environmental compensation even when hull value small. • Owners/cargo: remuneration measured by benefit, risk, services and capped by salved property (Art.13); owner must cooperate, can challenge award under contractual/treaty procedures. • Public: reduced navigation hazard, lower environmental risk; early salvage intervention. Example • Damaged tanker with cargo value USD 1m but oil spill risk; prompt prevention might justify special compensation even if Art.13 reward inadequate, subject to Art.14/SCOPIC prerequisites. • References: IMO Salvage Convention 1989 arts. 6,8,12-14; Lloyd's SCOPIC documentation.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_41_to_50.pdf

T05-Q03 · State the difference between Maritime salvage and contractual salvage, with examples. Who is responsible for payment of award as per the salvage convention? Which form is popularly used for contractual salvage and how is the award calculated per this form?
Question bank: 2023-02 Q4 Distinguish maritime salvage from contractual salvage • Maritime/pure salvage: voluntary assistance to marine property in real peril without pre-existing contractual obligation; entitlement follows applicable salvage law when useful result is obtained. Ordinary contractual towing before danger arises is generally not salvage. • Contractual salvage: services rendered under an agreement concluded before/during services, e.g. Lloyd's Open Form (LOF), fixing conditions, arbitral forum and/or remuneration; original legal duty or service contract may affect whether additional services are salvage. • A pre-existing duty to perform the same service without additional danger generally does not automatically earn salvage, but extraordinary voluntary services beyond duty might qualify. Examples 1. Fishing vessel voluntarily helps grounded freighter and successfully saves property, no advance agreement: possible pure salvage. 2. Professional salvor agrees on LOF to refloat stranded tanker: contractual salvage, usually 'no cure no pay' for property, with Art.14/SCOPIC where applicable. 3. Tug contracted for routine safe-harbour tow: ordinary towage not salvage. If unexpected danger arises and work changes radically, may be salvage subject to facts/agreement. Who pays Article 13 salvage reward? • Under Salvage Convention 1989 Article 13(2), payment is made by all ship/property interests in proportion to their respective salved values (subject to alternative national rule whereby a party pays with right of contribution). Owner of salved ship and owners of salved cargo bear their shares; not automatically 100% from shipowner. • Article 14 special compensation instead payable by owner of vessel only to the extent it exceeds Article 13 reward. • Life salvage is not a direct separate charge under Article 16, although life savers may share in property award in the circumstances specified. Lloyd's Open Form (LOF) • Most familiar standard salvage agreement; LOF-2024 is a current available form, but exam questions and existing contracts may specify LOF-2000. Principle: 'no cure, no pay' subject to Art.14 and any incorporated/invoked SCOPIC clause. • Award is NOT fixed as an arbitrary 10%/20% of salved value: arbitrator determines reasonable remuneration using 10 Article 13 factors, limited by salved value (except separate special compensation), with security, interest/costs as provided by contract. • Disputes proceed by Lloyd's salvage arbitration under signed LOF, with appeal provisions according to current/agreed terms. Award procedure flow • Peril -> voluntary salvage or sign LOF -> services/useful result -> determine salved values and Art.13 criteria -> give security -> Lloyd's arbitration/settlement -> allocate shares among saved interests; if environmental clause applies calculate Art.14/SCOPIC independently under its rules. CE roles • Preserve casualty and salvage logs, fuel/ballast inventories, engine status, contribution to successful refloating, operation hazards and factual reports; cooperate with salvor and surveyors. • References: 1989 Salvage Convention arts. 6,8,12-14,16,20; Lloyd's LOF and SCOPIC. Core source links https://www.igpandi.org/reinsurance/ https://www.imo.org/en/about/conventions/pages/international-convention-on-civil-liability-for-oil-pollution-damage-%28clc%29.aspx https://www.imo.org/en/about/conventions/pages/convention-on-limitation-of-liability-for-maritime-claims-%28llmc%29.aspx https://www.lloyds.com/market-resources/salvage-arbitration-branch/scopic https://www.imo.org/en/about/conventions/pages/international-convention-on-salvage.aspx

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_41_to_50.pdf

T05-Q04 · What is SCOPIC? How does it solve the difficulties of salvors? In case of a collision between two ships on high seas, which state has the right & responsibility for: i) taking disciplinary action against crew/master ii) cancelling COC of crew members iii) payment of compensation? Justify with the salient points of the applicable convention.
Catalogue references: 2023-01 Q4, 2025-06 Q4 A. SCOPIC: definition and necessity • SCOPIC = Special Compensation P&I Club Clause, a contractual clause incorporated by agreement into a Lloyd's Open Form (LOF) salvage contract and invoked by notice by the salvor. It is not an independent IMO convention. • Under Salvage Convention 1989 Article 13, the traditional successful salvage award reflects salved value and listed criteria. Article 14 gives limited special compensation for environmentally threatening casualties, but assessing 'expenses', uplift, and compensation caused disputes (notably after the Nagasaki Spirit litigation). • SCOPIC addresses uncertainty using published tariffs for personnel, equipment and tugs, with specified uplift; it gives a clearer remuneration mechanism when salvage has little prospect of earning adequate Article 13 reward. An initial security of USD 3 million is normally to be provided by the owner/insurer upon invocation under the clause, subject to contract wording. • SCOPIC remuneration operates independently of a strict requirement for proven environmental threat; the salvor receives the relevant tariff-based amount if it exceeds the Article 13 award, with contractual reductions/adjustments under the wording. The shipowner/P&I side obtains oversight through a Special Casualty Representative (SCR); SCOPIC usually replaces Article 14 compensation where incorporated and invoked. • Flow: LOF signed -> salvor invokes SCOPIC -> owner supplies security -> salvage and SCR monitoring -> tariff-based assessment -> compare Article 13 award -> apply contractual payment/discount rules. Exact LOF/SCOPIC edition controls. B. High-seas collision: penal jurisdiction and certificates (UNCLOS Art. 97) • Article 97(1): penal or disciplinary proceedings against master/crew for high-seas navigation incident may be instituted ONLY by the ship's flag State or the State of nationality of the person involved. Do not claim the coastal State may automatically prosecute merely because one ship is registered there. • Article 97(2): ONLY the State that issued a master's/crew member's competency certificate or licence can withdraw it through due legal process, even if the holder has another nationality. • Article 97(3): no arrest/detention of the ship, even for investigation, may be ordered by authorities other than the flag State (for high-seas collision context). C. Civil compensation • Article 97 does NOT allocate civil compensation. Owners/claimants pursue appropriate admiralty courts/arbitration under applicable private international law, collision-fault rules (e.g. 1910 Collision Convention) and limitation rules (LLMC where applicable). • Salvors, collision parties and pollution claimants may be subject to different regimes; pollution may attract CLC/Bunker Convention rather than ordinary collision liability. CE operational response • Preserve bridge and ER logs, engine orders, VDR and alarms; report casualty, protect life/environment, cooperate with flag safety investigation; never confuse criminal certificate jurisdiction with civil claims. Sources • UNCLOS Article 97 (un.org/depts/los/convention_agreements/texts/unclos/part7.htm); IMO 1989 Salvage Convention; LOF and SCOPIC clause issued by Lloyd's.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_51_to_60.pdf

T05-Q05 · What is Maritime Lien? How does this lien travel with property? Explain the associated terms ‘In Rem’ and ‘In personam’. Which convention deals with maritime lien? Give the order of settlement of various debtor’s claims after legal action per this convention.
Catalogue references: 2022-12 Q9, 2023-04 Q5, 2023-08 Q2, 2024-04 Q3, 2025-09 Q4, 2025-12 Q9 Definition: maritime lien • A maritime lien is a privileged claim against the ship arising by operation of law from a specified maritime event, enforceable against the vessel in admiralty proceedings without necessarily depending on a registered charge. It attaches to the vessel itself and ordinarily follows it through a voluntary change of ownership, registration or flag. • India: Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017, Sections 5, 8–10. International comparator: International Convention on Maritime Liens and Mortgages, 1993; the 1993 Convention does not replace the governing Indian statute in Indian proceedings. In rem vs in personam • Action IN REM: proceeding directed against ship/property as the res; vessel may be arrested as security if statutory conditions are satisfied (2017 Act s.5). • Action IN PERSONAM: personal claim against liable owner/other person, subject to jurisdiction and restrictions (ss.6–7). Arrest does not itself prove liability. Lien travels with property: limits • 2017 Act s.9(2): lien continues despite change of ownership, registration or flag; usually expires after one year, with TWO years for qualifying seafarer wage/employment liens, subject to qualifying arrest/seizure and forced-sale provisions. • Judicial sale by the admiralty High Court conveys title free of the specified encumbrances under s.8; claims attach to proceeds and are distributed under priority law. Section 9(1): INTER SE order of maritime liens (highest first) 1. Crew/master/officer wages, repatriation and social insurance contributions. 2. Death or personal injury directly connected with vessel operation. 3. Salvage reward including special compensation. 4. Port, canal and waterway dues, pilotage and other statutory vessel dues. 5. Tortious loss/damage from vessel operation excluding damage to carried cargo/containers. • Some oil/HNS compensation or nuclear claims are excluded from maritime lien by s.9(4); do not present every cargo claim as a maritime lien. Section 10: broader claims priority • General ranking: (1) maritime liens; (2) registered mortgages and similar charges; (3) all other claims. Section 9 ranks different LIENS, while Section 10 ranks CATEGORIES of claims; judicial sale and procedural expenses may be treated under applicable law/court orders.
