PSC, Flag State & Classification (26)
T07-Q01 · With reference to Port State Control inspection: What are ‘clear grounds’ and ‘ISM related deficiencies’ for a PSCO to conduct a more detailed inspection? List five deficiencies that may lead to detention, their causes and preventive action. Identify scenarios leading to issuance of PR17 by RO.
A. PSC framework and clear grounds - Port State Control (PSC) verifies foreign ships, crew, equipment and operations against applicable conventions. Legal basis: SOLAS I/19 and XI-1/4; MARPOL article 5 (and annex provisions); STCW I/4; MLC Title 5; Load Lines article 21; applicable anti-fouling/BWM controls. Latest IMO guidance at October 2026: A.1206(34), Procedures for PSC, 2025, superseding A.1185(33). - Clear grounds = evidence indicating ship, its equipment or crew do not substantially correspond to valid certificates or master/crew are unfamiliar with essential procedures, justifying detailed inspection. Examples: missing/invalid certificates, unsafe operation of emergency gear, serious structural deterioration, obvious pollution, crew unable to demonstrate essential drill or equipment, inconsistent SMS records and repeated deficiencies. - ISM-related deficiency means objective evidence that SMS implementation is ineffective (e.g., unrepaired repeated critical defect, absent risk assessment for hazardous operation, false familiarization/maintenance records, inconsistent incident reporting). A serious deficiency is not automatically a major ISM NC unless criteria are met. B. Five likely detention deficiencies: cause -> prevention 1. Inoperative emergency generator: defective starting system/untested standby -> routine auto-start testing, PMS and spares. 2. Fire main or fixed fire system ineffective: corrosion/isolations -> functional tests, inspection, competent repairs. 3. Steering gear unsafe: hydraulic leak/control fault -> tests before departure, isolation control, planned inspection. 4. Severe watertight integrity or stability/loading issue: open doors/overload/structural damage -> loading review, closing checks, class survey. 5. Unsafe crew familiarity and inadequate emergency response: weak training/fatigue -> STCW competence checks, realistic drills, SMS audit. C. IACS PR17 reporting/RO action - PR17 is an IACS Procedural Requirement about reporting/handling PSC detentions and follow-up; it is not a detention 'certificate' issued automatically to every ship. PSC detention involving class/statutory defects or possible RO responsibility prompts review of detention report, RO attendance when required, survey of deficiencies, root-cause/fleet actions, reports to relevant parties and quality review. Use currently applicable IACS PR17 text, flag authorization and RO instructions for exact triggers and deadlines. CE flow and references - Prepare truthful documents -> accompany PSCO -> demonstrate equipment -> record finding -> immediate risk control -> corrective action/root cause -> class/flag where needed -> verification -> prevention. IMO A.1206(34); ISM 9–10; IACS PR17.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_71_to_84.pdf

T07-Q02 · During a PSC inspection, the PSCO desired a more detailed inspection. What are ‘clear grounds’ for this, with examples? What is the difference between ‘corrective action’ and ‘preventive action’?
A. Why more detailed inspection occurs - Under IMO PSC Procedures A.1206(34), PSCO conducts initial checks of documentation and general ship condition; clear grounds justify more detailed inspection against applicable conventions. Evidence includes invalid documents, important differences from certificate particulars, damaged/unsafe machinery, poor maintenance, serious pollution or unsafe crew performance. - Examples: (1) emergency fire pump fails demonstrated start; (2) generator unable to support essential safety loads; (3) steering gear steering from bridge/standby transfer defective; (4) lifeboat launching arrangement unusable; (5) significant oil leak/illegal bypass; (6) crew cannot demonstrate firefighting or enclosed-space procedure; (7) incorrect safe-manning/qualification documents; (8) repeated SMS nonconformities, falsified records. - A PSCO's clear grounds must be objective and related to applicable instrument; a mere preference is not itself a legal standard. Expanded examination may include tests or operational drills within safe limits. B. Corrective vs preventive action - CORRECTION: fix detected problem immediately (replace leaking gasket). CORRECTIVE ACTION: remove cause of an existing defect (replace unsuitable gasket grade, correct torque specifications and revise installation training). PREVENTIVE ACTION: address potential defect before it occurs (review same gaskets fleetwide and improve procurement checks). Preventive steps can be part of action to prevent recurrence; do not claim 'replace gasket' alone resolves root cause. C. Response algorithm - Notify Master/CE -> identify objective evidence -> ensure safe control -> demonstrate documents/operation -> record PSC deficiency code -> root-cause analysis -> repair/test -> request PSCO verification -> notify flag/RO/DPA as required -> fleet learning and SMS audit. References - SOLAS I/19, XI-1/4; MARPOL article 5; STCW I/4; IMO Resolution A.1206(34); ISM 9,10,12.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_71_to_84.pdf

T07-Q03 · With reference to Port State Control, enumerate: (i) Regional cooperation/agreements (ii) Future of port State control (iii) Is PSC an effective tool for ship safety?
A. Regional PSC cooperation - Regional MoUs/agreements: Paris MoU (Europe/North Atlantic), Tokyo MoU (Asia-Pacific), Indian Ocean MoU, Mediterranean MoU, Black Sea MoU, Caribbean MoU, Abuja MoU (West/Central Africa), Riyadh MoU (Gulf region), Viña del Mar Agreement (Latin America); United States Coast Guard operates a distinct PSC regime. Membership and coverage should be checked rather than assumed identical. - Main mechanisms: harmonized inspection guidance, database sharing, ship risk targeting, coordinated concentrated inspection campaigns (CIC), detention notices, flag/RO performance monitoring and training of PSCOs. B. Future PSC - Greater use of risk-based profiles and inspection histories; targeting of aging ships and weak company/flag performance; digital verification of certificates/records; scrutiny of BWMS, Annex VI, SEEMP/CII, cybersecurity and alternative fuels as legally applicable; remote data can support targeting but should not replace competent physical examination where required. - Challenges: inconsistent enforcement, digital document fraud, rising workload, differing regional rules, training needs, risk of undue delay and disproportionate reliance on detention statistics. C. Effectiveness - PSC is a valuable second line of defence when flag State/class oversight fails. Detentions and deficiencies encourage correction and can improve compliance. However, a short port inspection is a sampling process; hidden defects, false records, bribery, limited expertise and differing national practice mean PSC cannot by itself eliminate substandard ships. - Success should be measured through corrected deficiencies, repeat-detention trends, incident prevention and fair consistent enforcement, not detention count alone. CE approach - Maintain real readiness not paper appearance; check emergency gear, documentation, SMS implementation, drills, pollution control and critical spares; address trends before port arrival. References - IMO PSC overview and A.1206(34); regional MoU official publications; SOLAS XI-1/4.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_71_to_84.pdf

T07-Q04 · What provisions are made under PSC towards: (i) Certificates issued by non-party states (ii) Inspection of ships below convention size (iii) No more favorable treatment.
A. No more favourable treatment (NMFT) - Principle: ships flying flag of a non-party to a convention should not gain more favourable treatment than ships of parties when visiting ports of contracting States, subject to the particular convention's wording and national implementing law. The PSCO may require equivalent evidence of compliance, even if conventional certificates cannot be demanded in an identical legal manner. B. Certificates of non-party States - For convention certificates issued by non-party administrations, PSCO checks whether documents and ship conditions offer equivalent compliance as required by the relevant instrument, rather than automatically treating the certificate as equivalent to a Party-issued one; examine equipment, manning and operations as necessary. - SOLAS I/19 and XI-1/4, MARPOL article 5(4), STCW I/4, and MLC standard A5.2.1 (with NMFT concept in MLC article V(7)) provide applicable control frameworks; treaty-specific scope matters. C. Ships below convention size - If vessel is below size threshold, do not impose all large-ship requirements automatically. Apply relevant national law, applicable convention clauses and IMO PSC guidance for non-convention-size ships; use professional judgement, check essential safety, pollution prevention, manning and local rules. Document the legal basis for any action; the smaller ship does not automatically escape port State control. D. Examples - A sub-500 GT vessel not covered by a particular SOLAS certificate requirement may still be controlled under applicable domestic safety standards. A non-party flag ship entering a MARPOL party port may be checked for equivalent pollution prevention and cannot obtain a free pass merely because its State is not party. CE examination conclusion - Always distinguish treaty coverage from domestic control authority; enforce NMFT without inventing a missing certificate obligation. IMO PSC Resolution A.1206(34), relevant annexes; MARPOL art 5(4); MLC art V(7).

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_71_to_84.pdf

T07-Q05 · Explain the concept of a Port State Control with focus on the legal framework. What rights to appeal are available to the Chief Engineer/Company if the ship has been detained for deficiencies considered unreasonable and unfair?
A. PSC legal basis and procedure - PSC is a port State's verification of foreign ships for compliance with applicable international instruments and implementing national law; it supplements flag oversight. SOLAS I/19 & XI-1/4, MARPOL article 5, Load Lines art 21, STCW I/4, MLC Title 5, BWM/AFS convention inspection clauses, as applicable. IMO procedures: A.1206(34) (2025). - FLOW: PSCO authorized arrival/selection -> certificate and general inspection -> clear grounds? -> detailed inspection/operational verification -> record deficiencies -> identify detention-worthy hazards -> notify master/flag/RO -> repairs and verification -> release or authorized conditions. B. Unreasonable or unfair detention and appeal 1. Obtain written detention decision, cited legislation, deficiency list, grounds, responsible authority and appeal instructions; make contemporaneous accurate record. 2. Inform Master, owner, DPA, P&I, flag and RO; do not obstruct official duties. 3. Provide objective evidence: valid certificates, test reports, class condition, photos, witness logs and OEM requirements; seek reinspection and correction of factual errors. 4. Use applicable national port State administrative/judicial appeal mechanism within its deadline. 5. Under IMO A.1206(34), company/representative has right of appeal; filing appeal does NOT itself suspend detention. 6. Separately consider claim for undue detention/delay under applicable treaty/domestic law, proving wrongfulness, loss and causation; compensation is not automatic. - Chief Engineer has no universal personal right to release a detained ship; normally owner/company or authorized representative files the appeal under local law. C. CE duties - Rectify any immediate safety issues even while challenging disputed findings; follow safe repair process, seek surveyor/PSCO verification, preserve records; avoid retaliatory or false records. D. Oral caution - PSC appeal and owner complaint to Paris MoU review panel are distinct pathways where the relevant regional MoU provides additional review; regional review does not automatically replace national statutory appeal. References - IMO A.1206(34); SOLAS I/19, XI-1/4; applicable national PSC law and regional MoU appeal rules.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_71_to_84.pdf

T07-Q06 · The concept of New Inspection Regime (NIR) was introduced first by Paris MoU. Explain the salient features of NIR. Enumerate the pros & cons of NIR implementation and how owners/managers should respond.
A. Paris MoU New Inspection Regime (NIR) - Introduced 1 Jan 2011; risk-based ship selection allocates inspection resources according to ship risk profile (SRP) and prior findings. HRS high-risk, SRS standard-risk, LRS low-risk; factors include ship type/age, flag performance, recognized organization, company performance and inspection/detention history. - Current Paris MoU ship selection intervals: HRS 5–6 months, SRS 10–12 months, LRS 24–36 months. Priority I: inspection required due to elapsed window or overriding factors; Priority II: inspection may be carried out within window or due to unexpected factors. Additional inspections for incidents, reports or other triggering factors; an inspection normally resets the inspection window. B. Advantages - Targets higher-risk ships; improves allocation of PSCO resources; rewards consistent management; encourages timely rectification, transparency and fleet safety culture; reduces unnecessary inspection of reliably maintained low-risk vessels. C. Disadvantages and limitations - Risk of inaccurate/incomplete inspection data or rapid improvement/deterioration between inspections; high-risk label can magnify commercial impact; cross-region profile variation; expensive preparation and uneven inspection practice; longer intervals may miss newly developing hazards on LRS ships. D. Owner/manager response 1. Monitor SRP, flag/RO/company scores and prior deficiencies. 2. Address fleetwide root causes; ensure crew competence and credible SMS records. 3. Carry out realistic pre-arrival equipment tests and document evidence. 4. Respond transparently to PSC findings, with completion verification. 5. Invest in predictive critical-maintenance, spare support and drills. 6. Appeal factually incorrect detention/profile information via established process. CE quick sketch - Ship profile -> HRS / SRS / LRS -> inspection time window -> Priority I / Priority II + overriding/unexpected factors -> inspection -> findings and refreshed risk/interval. References - Official Paris MoU Ship Selection Scheme; IMO A.1206(34); SOLAS XI-1/4.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_71_to_84.pdf

T07-Q07 · State the obligations of a port State to exercise control on shipping in addition to control by flag states. Comment on whether the Risk- based approach or Concentrated Inspection Campaigns by PSC MOUs have helped eliminate sub-standard ships.
A. Port State obligations and authority - Flag State retains primary duty for compliance; port State exercises complementary inspection/control of foreign ships in its ports under its laws and applicable conventions. Basic sources: SOLAS I/19/XI-1/4; MARPOL art 5 and 6 as applicable; STCW I/4; MLC Title 5; Load Line art 21; UNCLOS arts 218–220 for specified pollution enforcement. - Port State must follow proper legal grounds, qualified PSCO procedures, non-discriminatory application/NMFT where mandated, proportionate action, notification of flag/RO/other authorities, fair appeal, efforts to avoid undue detention/delay and accurate records. B. Risk-based approach - Risk targeting uses age, type, flag/RO/company performance and past deficiencies to prioritise inspection; Paris MoU NIR categories and windows HRS 5–6, SRS 10–12, LRS 24–36 months. It does not prove a ship compliant: sampling cannot replace continuous flag/company obligations. C. Concentrated Inspection Campaigns (CIC) - Time-limited, jointly coordinated campaigns target one specific theme (e.g. STCW watchkeeping, firefighting, MARPOL). Benefits: consistent attention, trend data, seafarer preparation and identification of systematic deficiencies. Limitations: narrow focus, seasonal effect, temporary 'inspection readiness', inconsistent resources and no assurance against unrelated defects. D. Have substandard ships been eliminated? - No. Evidence-based view: PSC has improved detection and deterrence; deficiencies and repeat offenders still occur. Inspectors can only sample system and operations; enforcement and oversight quality vary. No defensible claim that risk-based PSC/CIC has eliminated all substandard shipping. E. CE actions - Fleet trend review -> risk review -> readiness and realistic drills -> corrective action -> independent verification -> update SMS/PMS and management review. References - IMO A.1206(34); official regional MoU CIC and risk-targeting data; ISM 9,10,12.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_71_to_84.pdf

T07-Q08 · What is a Recognized Organization (RO)? What are the salient features of the R.O. Code? How do Administrations monitor R.O.s? Enumerate the structure of IACS and explain the difference between UI, UR, PR — and their significance to ship owners/operators.
A. Recognized Organization (RO) definition - RO = an organization assessed and authorized by a flag Administration to carry out defined statutory surveys, inspections and certification on its behalf. Often a classification society, but class membership and RO authorization are separate; State retains overall treaty responsibility. B. RO Code — sources and structure - IMO Code for Recognized Organizations under MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65), mandatory through relevant SOLAS and MARPOL provisions. Part 1 General; Part 2 Recognition and Authorization (mandatory); Part 3 Oversight of ROs (mandatory). Relevant appendix covers quality management and certification provisions. Apply mandatory wording and current amendments. - Approval checks: adequate staff, technical competence, survey procedures, independence/impartiality, ethical controls, resources worldwide, quality management, records, survey reporting, liability/authority arrangements and formal written agreement defining authorized services. C. Administration oversight - Audit RO quality and survey operations; review performance indicators, complaints, casualty and PSC detention trends, certificate/survey files; witness shipboard surveys and perform vertical audits / additional inspections; review changes in key personnel and subcontracting; require corrective action, suspend or withdraw authorization if needed. Frequency depends on RO Code requirements and flag's risk/oversight plan; avoid asserting every check is annual without specific legal source. D. IACS organization and resolutions - IACS = association of member classification societies with Council, technical and policy work groups, secretariat and QSCS. It is not itself a class society and does not issue ship class certificates. - UR (Unified Requirements): minimum technical rules for incorporation by members, which may set stricter rules. UI (Unified Interpretations): harmonized interpretations of IMO instruments where wording is ambiguous/left to Administration, used subject to flag's definitive interpretations. PR (Procedural Requirements): common procedural duties of members, including survey reporting and quality processes. UIs are NOT themselves new IMO convention regulations. E. Why it matters - Owner gains more consistent technical standards, comparable class rules, and coordinated statutory compliance; retains ISM, maintenance and overall compliance duties. RO oversight does not extinguish flag responsibility. References - IMO RO Code MSC.349(92)/MEPC.237(65); IACS official UR, UI and PR publications; SOLAS XI-1/1 and applicable RO Code mandatory references.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_71_to_84.pdf

T07-Q09 · According to the RO Code, what are the mandatory requirements/procedures a flag state must follow to approve an RO? Describe oversight mechanisms to ensure continued compliance of an approved RO.
A. Legal authority and scope - A flag Administration may authorize an RO to perform defined statutory services consistent with SOLAS XI-1/1 and the IMO Code for Recognized Organizations (RO Code), MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65), with applicable treaty implementing requirements. Flag remains responsible for effectiveness of delegated functions. B. Approval process / mandatory checks 1. Determine authorized scope (survey, inspection, certification, plan approval and administrative functions) and legal competence to delegate. 2. Assess RO technical resources, competent surveyors, training, operating procedures, geographical capability, equipment and impartiality. 3. Review quality management system (independent audits, internal audits, document control, NC handling, continuous improvement), transparency and data management. 4. Inspect RO performance, sample vessel reports and check certification decision process. 5. Check conflict-of-interest policies, independent technical review and professional integrity. 6. Conclude formal written agreement stating delegated authority, reporting lines, access rights, supervision, exchange of information, performance measures, liability and withdrawal terms. 7. Notify IMO/shipowners and other authorities as applicable and maintain current authorization details. C. Oversight after approval - Risk-based programme with regular management audits, office audits, vertical contract or vessel surveys (trace ship from initial request through plan review, attendance, findings, follow-up, certificate issuance), attendance on additional ship inspections, PSC/class-condition trend analysis, complaints, accident feedback and corrective action verification. - Observe surveyor training and consistency, certification integrity and subcontracted services. Require finding closure; restrict, suspend or withdraw authorization where severe failures continue. Admin must resource competent oversight rather than outsource oversight itself wholly to the RO. D. Example vertical audit - Select ship -> review authorization/service request -> inspect survey plan and surveyor qualification -> witness onboard equipment/record checks -> compare report findings with certificates and rule requirements -> verify follow-up and closure -> document audit conclusion. E. CE relevance - Assist survey transparently; maintain class/statutory records, demonstrate emergency machinery, rectify defects and report class-related failures to flag/RO as applicable. No survey may be falsified or backdated. References - IMO RO Code, MSC.349(92)/MEPC.237(65), Parts 1–3; III Code; relevant flag authorization instructions.

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_71_to_84.pdf

T07-Q10 · Explain the role of a Classification society acting as an RO in carrying out statutory surveys. What is the criteria for approval of an RO by the Administration? Can statutory and class surveys be done simultaneously? State limitations of the RO and list statutory services undertaken on behalf of the Administration.
A. Class versus RO - Class society certifies compliance with its hull, structural, machinery and electrical class rules. When separately appointed as a Recognized Organization (RO), it performs statutory surveys on behalf of a flag Administration under its written authorization. Class certificate is not equivalent to every statutory certificate, and the Administration retains ultimate statutory responsibility. B. Criteria for RO approval - RO Code: competence/expertise, survey procedures and technical rules, sufficient skilled personnel and service presence, independence/impartiality, professional integrity, quality management, certification and records control, effective reporting, communication with Administration, and arrangements to ensure performance. Formal agreement defines exact authority and limits. C. Surveys undertaken - SOLAS construction, safety equipment/radio, load line, MARPOL I/II/IV/V/VI, tonnage, BWM, AFS, ISM audits, ISPS verification, MLC inspection, polar/other instruments may be delegated where the flag has authorized that particular service. Do not assume every RO has authorization for all instruments or that specific certificates are always delegated. - Newbuilding: plan approval, material tests, structural surveys, machinery and electrical inspection, sea trials and statutory safety checks. In service: annual/intermediate/renewal/additional statutory inspections, surveys after damage and surveys following major modifications. D. Can class and statutory surveys be concurrent? - YES, commonly planned together under Harmonized System of Survey and Certification (HSSC), subject to independent application of each survey's scope, time window, acceptance criteria, documentation and authority. A successful class survey alone does not prove statutory survey completion; separate endorsements/certificates are required. E. Limits of RO authority - RO cannot exceed delegated functions or waive convention requirements without flag approval; cannot unilaterally grant dispensations, interpretations reserved to flag or exemptions outside mandate; statutory detentions and criminal enforcement rest with legally designated authorities. Serious survey findings must be reported to flag/port and appropriate measures taken as authorized. Conflicts and quality problems trigger oversight. F. CE readiness - Maintain up-to-date survey status/class conditions, certificates, PMS records, critical-spare register, machinery/emergency-generator/steering tests and ISM deficiency records. Prepare crew and tests, demonstrate safety performance and close deficiencies with evidence. Exam flow - Flag authorization -> RO survey/inspection -> deficiency/action -> independent verification -> valid certificate/endorsement -> flag monitoring and quality oversight. References - IMO RO Code MSC.349(92)/MEPC.237(65); SOLAS XI-1/1 and HSSC; relevant MARPOL Annex survey rules; IACS UR/UI/PR. Official online references IMO PSC 2025 — https://www.imo.org/en/ourwork/iiis/pages/port%20state%20control.aspx Paris MoU NIR — https://parismou.org/inspection-regime/ IMO FSA — https://www.imo.org/en/ourwork/safety/pages/formalsafetyassessment.aspx IMO fatigue — https://www.imo.org/en/ourwork/humanelement/pages/seafarers-hours-of-work-and-rest.aspx IACS UR — https://iacs.org.uk/resolutions/unified-requirements IACS UI — https://iacs.org.uk/resolutions/unified-interpretations IACS PR — https://iacs.org.uk/resolutions/procedural-requirements

Source: MEO_Class_I_Verified_Questions_71_to_84.pdf

T07-Q11 · Discuss the role of Classification societies in structural, mechanical and electrical rule formation/implementation during design, construction and maintenance. Explain why compliance with Class rules is now mentioned in SOLAS Ch.II-1. Explain the difference and relevance between annual and periodical surveys toward harmonized survey and certification system.
QUESTION 01 (a) | 10 MARKS | Original Q8(a) Role of Classification Societies in structural, mechanical and electrical rule-making and implementation. Why SOLAS Chapter II-1 refers to Class Rules. Introduction A Classification Society is an independent technical organization that develops, publishes and maintains rules for ship structure, machinery and electrical installations, and verifies compliance through design review, construction survey and in-service surveys. Classification is a technical assessment, not a guarantee that a vessel is safe in all circumstances. [R1] A. Rule formation: areas covered Rule discipline Examples of technical requirements Hull / structural Hull-girder strength, scantlings, buckling and fatigue, corrosion additions, materials, welding, watertight integrity and structural surveys. Mechanical Propulsion engines, shafting, boilers, pressure vessels, piping, steering gear, auxiliary machinery, automation and protective devices. Electrical Generators, switchboards, emergency supply, cables, distribution, protection, short-circuit capability and automation interfaces. Class rules are updated in response to operating experience, casualties, research, technology, SOLAS changes and harmonized IACS resolutions. Relevant IACS products include UR, UI, PR and CSR (explained in Q3). [R1, R2] B. Application during ship life cycle 1. Design: Check ship drawings, scantling and strength calculations; approve machinery arrangement, propulsion/shafting, pressure systems and electrical distribution/protection; verify the submitted designs against applicable class/statutory requirements. 2. Construction: Survey approved materials, welding/NDT and fabrication; witness pressure and operational tests, machinery installation, electrical tests, alarm/protection trials and relevant sea trials. 3. Entry into class: After satisfactory compliance, assign class notation and issue classification documentation, with any outstanding items controlled under society rules. 4. In service: Carry out class annual, intermediate, docking and special/renewal surveys, machinery surveys and damage/repair surveys. Verify repairs, approved modifications and ongoing rule compliance. 5. Deficiencies: Issue relevant conditions/recommendations, specify rectification and re-survey, and suspend/withdraw class where class rules so require. C. SOLAS statutory link - important answer SOLAS Chapter II-1, Regulation 3-1 states that ships shall be designed, constructed and maintained in compliance with structural, mechanical and electrical requirements of a classification society recognized by the flag Administration under SOLAS XI-1/1, or applicable Administration national standards providing equivalent safety. [R3] • Technical depth: Convention provisions are mainly broad safety outcomes; detailed class rules supply engineering calculations, testing and acceptance criteria. • Lifecycle safety: The requirement addresses not only newbuild design and construction but continued maintenance in service. • Uniformity and verification: Recognized technical rules promote consistency, with inspection by competent organizations and flag control. • Legal distinction: SOLAS II-1/3-1 makes the referenced technical standard a statutory requirement; this does not turn every class survey or notation into a separate SOLAS certificate. EXAMINER NOTE Write SOLAS II-1/3-1, not II-1/3-10. Regulation 3-10 concerns goal-based ship construction standards and is a different provision. Also state the equivalent national-standard alternative. Primary references: [R1] IACS FAQ; [R2] IACS Resolutions; [R3] SOLAS II-1/3-1; [R4] IMO Recognized Organizations. QUESTION 01 (b) | 6 MARKS | Original Q8(b) Difference and relevance of annual versus periodical survey under HSSC. Introduction The Harmonized System of Survey and Certification (HSSC) coordinates survey windows, certificate validity and endorsements under SOLAS, MARPOL, Load Lines and relevant codes. The current IMO survey guidance is Resolution A.1207(34), HSSC 2025, adopted 3 December 2025. [R5] Particular Annual survey Periodical survey Purpose General inspection to verify continuing Certificate-specific inspection/tests to satisfactory maintenance of ship, machinery demonstrate that relevant equipment complies and equipment. and is fit for service. Survey window Within 3 months before/after applicable Within 3 months before/after anniversary certificate anniversary. prescribed for the certificate. Frequency Each anniversary when required by that Safety Equipment: 2nd OR 3rd anniversary; certificate. Safety Radio: EACH anniversary. Scope Certificate examination, visual condition and Relevant equipment examination, functional necessary tests. tests and examination of specified records/manuals. Certificate action Certificate endorsed as applicable. Certificate endorsed; may replace annual survey for Safety Equipment. Exact SOLAS applications Certificate Anniversary and type Cargo Ship Safety Equipment - SOLAS I/8 Annual survey (normally 1st and 4th); periodical survey at the 2nd OR 3rd anniversary, replacing an annual survey. Cargo Ship Safety Radio - SOLAS I/9 Periodical survey at every annual anniversary, ±3 months. Cargo Ship Safety Construction - SOLAS I/10 Annual survey plus intermediate survey at 2nd OR 3rd anniversary, replacing an annual survey. IOPP / Load Line IOPP: annual plus intermediate at 2nd or 3rd. Load Line: annual. Do not incorrectly call all intermediate surveys periodical surveys. Relevance • Combines surveys into coordinated attendance and minimizes unnecessary duplication/off-hire. • Establishes predictable anniversary windows and certificate endorsements. • Supports continuous ship safety while permitting common survey and certification cycles. EXAMINER NOTE Periodical survey ≠ five-year renewal. The Cargo Ship Safety Equipment periodical is at the second or third anniversary; Radio periodical is at each anniversary. [R5, R6] Primary references: [R5] IMO A.1207(34), sections 4.2-4.5; [R6] SOLAS I/8, I/9 and I/10. T07-Q12 · Briefly explain the role of IACS, its function and the scope of classification. As per IACS, explain Unified Requirements and Procedural Requirements with examples. Explain assignment, maintenance, suspension and withdrawal of class.
QUESTION 03 | 8-16 MARKS | Previously asked IACS structure / making procedure Enumerate the structure of IACS; distinguish UR, UI and PR; explain their preparation and significance for shipowners/operators. IACS structure and functions IACS (International Association of Classification Societies) is an association of member classification societies. It develops common technical resolutions and quality arrangements. IACS itself does not class ships or issue individual ship class certificates. • Council: highest governing body responsible for overall association policy. • General Policy Group (GPG): develops and coordinates technical policy and approves applicable resolutions. • Technical Panels / expert working groups: prepare, review and maintain resolutions across hull, machinery, electrical and other domains. • Quality and Secretariat arrangements: support QSCS, coordination, communications and records. UR versus UI versus PR Type Meaning and use Example UR - Unified Minimum technical class requirement; subject to member ratification Hull strength, machinery, Requirement and incorporation in applicable society rules. Members may set stricter electrical installation or survey rules. requirements. UI - Unified Uniform interpretation of unclear IMO Convention provisions; applied Interpretation of SOLAS, Load Interpretation when flag has not issued definite instructions. Does not supersede the Line or MARPOL technical Administration. wording. PR - Procedural Common procedure for IACS members to implement in their own PR1B (double/dual class); PR17 Requirement practices. (reporting ISM-related deficiencies). Typical formation/implementation sequence Issue identified (IMO change, technology or operational problem) → technical panel drafts a proposal → review and consultation among members → GPG approval under IACS procedures → implementation through society rules/practices with applicable effective dates → periodic revision. For UR, the member governing body ratifies and normally implements approved applicable requirements in its rules within the IACS-prescribed period. UI is circulated as appropriate to IMO/Administrations, and definite flag instructions govern. PR is incorporated into society procedures in the agreed implementation period. [R2, R7] Significance to owner/operator • Reduces divergent baseline technical requirements and improves survey predictability. • Assists acceptance by charterers, insurers and industry. • Supports common interpretations of international statutory regulations. • Permits owners to anticipate technical updates, survey requirements, retrofits and investment. Primary references: [R1] IACS FAQ; [R2] IACS Resolution descriptions; [R7] IACS Unified Interpretations. Official reference list and authoritative links [R1] IACS - Frequently Asked Questions, classification, UR and CSR [R2] IACS - Resolutions, UR / UI / PR [R3] SOLAS II-1, Regulation 3-1 (text as reproduced by IMO Rules) [R4] IMO - Recognized Organizations, framework and authorization [R5] IMO Resolution A.1207(34) - HSSC Survey Guidelines 2025 [R6] SOLAS Chapter I, Regulations 8, 9, 10 (HSSC survey schedules) [R7] IACS - Unified Interpretations [R8] IMO MSC.349(92) - RO Code full adopted text [R9] DG Shipping - Comprehensive Safety Management Report 2025, RO oversight section [R10] IACS PR1B - Double and Dual Class, version status [R11] IACS PR17 - Reporting deficiencies affecting ISM implementation [R12] IMO - Port State Control / Procedures A.1206(34), 2025 [R13] IMO - Ship construction and ESP overview Preparation standard: Short direct introductions, subpart-by-subpart response, comparison tables for distinctions, numbered procedures and explicit IMO regulation numbers. Source status reviewed 27 October 2026.

T07-Q13 · What is the purpose of annual surveys? Give a list of items examined during an annual survey. Explain ‘condition of class’ and its impact on a ship’s commercial vs technical aspects.
QUESTION 07 | 6-8 MARKS | Previous class / survey / PMS question Distinguish classification surveys, statutory surveys and maintenance; explain how PMS records and Conditions of Class fit in. Activity Authority and purpose Examples Class survey Classification society rules; retain class notation, Annual hull/machinery, docking, special assess hull/machinery/electrical fitness. survey, shaft/boiler surveys as applicable. Statutory survey Convention and flag law; performed by flag or SOLAS Safety Construction, MARPOL IOPP, authorized RO. Load Line, safety equipment. PMS / routine maintenance Owner/operator procedures and ISM section 10; Clearances, calibration, filter service, machinery kept effective between surveys. generator tests and defect records. Role of PMS • Plan and record jobs, condition checks, calibrations, repairs, testing and spares. • Provide evidence to attending class/statutory surveyors. • Routine PMS completion does not automatically earn classification survey credit. Credit is only available under approved survey arrangements and subject to society rules and surveyor verification. Conditions of Class (CoC) A Condition of Class is an outstanding requirement placed by the society for a deficiency or required work, with a stated action/due date under its rules. Non-compliance may lead to restrictions, suspension or withdrawal of class. The Master/Chief Engineer should notify the company, plan permanent repair and obtain required re-survey and formal closure. Primary references: [R1] IACS FAQ; [R3] SOLAS II-1/3-1; society-specific classification rules. T07-Q14 · Differentiate between annual, intermediate, renewal, damage and repair surveys. What are the purposes of each survey onboard? Enlist all statutory certificates carried onboard, issuing authority, and the IMO Convention under which issued.
QUESTION 08 | 6-8 MARKS | Survey types and HSSC oral follow-up Enumerate HSSC survey types and show the harmonized five-year survey/certificate cycle. HSSC survey Meaning / timing Initial Before putting the ship into service or before first issue of relevant statutory certificate. Annual Applicable certificate anniversary ±3 months; general continuing-maintenance verification. Intermediate Normally 2nd OR 3rd anniversary ±3 months for specified certificates, replacing one annual survey. Periodical Safety Equipment 2nd OR 3rd; Safety Radio EACH anniversary; scope specific to that certificate. Renewal Before certificate renewal, normally at intervals not exceeding five years, subject to Convention exceptions. Bottom inspection Cargo ship normally at least two inspections of outside bottom within any five-year period; max interval generally 36 months, subject to applicable provisions. Additional After important repairs/renewals or where otherwise prescribed. Five-year certificate example (Safety Equipment) Initial Year 1 Year 2 Year 3 Year 4 Expiry Issue Annual Annual OR periodical Periodical OR annual Annual Renewal before expiry Periodical survey is performed at one of Year 2 and Year 3, with a normal annual survey at the other. The certificate anniversary date is a defined statutory date; survey window is not recalculated by simply adding one year to the actual last survey attendance date. [R5, R6] Example: anniversary is 15 June Normal annual or prescribed periodical window is 15 March through 15 September for the relevant anniversary. For the 2nd-or-3rd survey, choose the relevant year and apply the ±3-month window. The certificate and endorsements remain subject to the survey rules and any exceptional extensions. Primary references: [R5] A.1207(34), 4.1-4.8 and appendices; [R6] SOLAS Ch I. QUESTION 08 | 6-8 MARKS | Survey types and HSSC oral follow-up Enumerate HSSC survey types and show the harmonized five-year survey/certificate cycle. HSSC survey Meaning / timing Initial Before putting the ship into service or before first issue of relevant statutory certificate. Annual Applicable certificate anniversary ±3 months; general continuing-maintenance verification. Intermediate Normally 2nd OR 3rd anniversary ±3 months for specified certificates, replacing one annual survey. Periodical Safety Equipment 2nd OR 3rd; Safety Radio EACH anniversary; scope specific to that certificate. Renewal Before certificate renewal, normally at intervals not exceeding five years, subject to Convention exceptions. Bottom inspection Cargo ship normally at least two inspections of outside bottom within any five-year period; max interval generally 36 months, subject to applicable provisions. Additional After important repairs/renewals or where otherwise prescribed. Five-year certificate example (Safety Equipment) Initial Year 1 Year 2 Year 3 Year 4 Expiry Issue Annual Annual OR periodical Periodical OR annual Annual Renewal before expiry Periodical survey is performed at one of Year 2 and Year 3, with a normal annual survey at the other. The certificate anniversary date is a defined statutory date; survey window is not recalculated by simply adding one year to the actual last survey attendance date. [R5, R6] Example: anniversary is 15 June Normal annual or prescribed periodical window is 15 March through 15 September for the relevant anniversary. For the 2nd-or-3rd survey, choose the relevant year and apply the ±3-month window. The certificate and endorsements remain subject to the survey rules and any exceptional extensions. T07-Q18 · What are the requirements for ship identification number marking and the Continuous Synopsis Record (CSR) under SOLAS Chapter XI-1? What is the Enhanced Survey Program (ESP) under SOLAS and which vessels are subject to it? Explain key components/objectives of ESP.
QUESTION 10 | 6 MARKS | Related structural/class survey question What are ESP and Common Structural Rules (CSR), and how do they connect with classification? ESP: The 2011 ESP Code, as amended, contains enhanced survey requirements for relevant bulk carriers and oil tankers, with detailed planning, close-up surveys, thickness measurements and structural condition assessment. It is a mandatory SOLAS XI-1/2 framework as applicable; class/RO may carry out authorized surveys. CSR: IACS Common Structural Rules specify comprehensive minimum hull structural rules for covered bulk carriers and oil tankers, implemented by IACS member societies. CSR concerns structural design/construction, while ESP mainly concerns continued structural condition assessment of ships in service. [R1, R13] • Hull checks: coating condition, cracking, deformation, corrosion and gauging results. • Substantial corrosion: defined/assessed according to applicable ESP/Class criteria; calls for closer measurement, reassessment and repair as needed. • Chief Engineer: support ballast/cargo tank preparation, safe access, isolation, permits and equipment readiness; ensure survey/repair findings are reported and closed. Primary references: [R1] IACS CSR explanation; [R13] IMO ESP Code overview. Official reference list and authoritative links [R1] IACS - Frequently Asked Questions, classification, UR and CSR [R2] IACS - Resolutions, UR / UI / PR [R3] SOLAS II-1, Regulation 3-1 (text as reproduced by IMO Rules) [R4] IMO - Recognized Organizations, framework and authorization [R5] IMO Resolution A.1207(34) - HSSC Survey Guidelines 2025 [R6] SOLAS Chapter I, Regulations 8, 9, 10 (HSSC survey schedules) [R7] IACS - Unified Interpretations [R8] IMO MSC.349(92) - RO Code full adopted text [R9] DG Shipping - Comprehensive Safety Management Report 2025, RO oversight section [R10] IACS PR1B - Double and Dual Class, version status [R11] IACS PR17 - Reporting deficiencies affecting ISM implementation [R12] IMO - Port State Control / Procedures A.1206(34), 2025 [R13] IMO - Ship construction and ESP overview Preparation standard: Short direct introductions, subpart-by-subpart response, comparison tables for distinctions, numbered procedures and explicit IMO regulation numbers. T07-Q21 · Define substantial corrosion in the context of ship structure and classification requirements. What criteria are used to assess substantial corrosion during inspections? Discuss the steps taken to address substantial corrosion during a renewal survey, including inspection techniques, repair methods, and the role of the classification society in ensuring compliance.
DG SHIPPING / MMD INDIA | MEO CLASS I - ENGINEERING MANAGEMENT July 2025 - Q7: Substantial Corrosion Question: Define substantial corrosion in the context of ship structure and classification requirements. What criteria are used to assess substantial corrosion during inspections? Discuss the steps taken to address substantial corrosion during a renewal survey, including inspection techniques, repair methods, and the role of the classification society in ensuring compliance. Answer for written examination: Corrected and reorganised from the four-page handwritten notes. References are numbered and updated to October 2026. 1. Meaning and applicable regulations Substantial corrosion is a corrosion condition in which assessment of the corrosion pattern indicates wastage exceeding 75% of the allowable corrosion margin, but still within the acceptable limit. This is not 75% loss of the original plate thickness. [2, 3] For a ship built to the IACS Common Structural Rules (CSR), the measured thickness lies in the interval from the renewal thickness, tren, up to tren + 0.5 mm. The renewal thickness is the minimum permissible thickness; steel measured below tren requires renewal/approved corrective action under the applicable class rules. [2, 3] Statutory/class basis: SOLAS regulation XI-1/2 makes the 2011 ESP Code mandatory for applicable bulk carriers and oil tankers (normally 500 GT and above). Apply the current ESP Code, applicable IACS UR Z10-series, class rules and approved renewal criteria. [1-3] Simple numerical illustration If original thickness = 12.0 mm and minimum permissible thickness = 10.0 mm, allowable wastage = 2.0 mm. At an actual measured thickness of 10.3 mm, wastage is 1.7 mm or 85% of the allowable margin: it is substantial corrosion but is not automatically beyond the renewal limit. At 9.8 mm, the renewal limit has been exceeded. This is an illustration; actual scantling limits are decided from the ship-specific class criteria. 2. Criteria used by the surveyor to assess corrosion 1. Visual and coating condition: inspect ballast tanks, cargo holds/tanks, shell plating, deck, bottom structures, longitudinals, frames, stiffeners, bulkheads, welds and joints for scale, rust, coating failure, pitting, grooving, edge corrosion, cracks, buckling and deformation. POOR coating means general coating breakdown over 20% or more of the area, or hard scale over 10% or more. This prompts further examination; it does not by itself establish substantial corrosion. [2] 2. Thickness measurement (TM): use calibrated ultrasonic gauging and compare the actual measured thickness with the as-built/original thickness and the applicable allowable or renewal thickness. Evaluate the extent, severity and distribution of corrosion, including local pitting and grooving. [2, 3] 3. Survey/history evidence: review earlier TM reports, coating condition, repair history, structural plans, class conditions and locations known to experience rapid wastage. Record suspect areas (locations already showing substantial corrosion or considered by the surveyor prone to rapid wastage) and critical structural areas. [2] 4. Structural significance: determine whether reduced scantlings, fractures, buckling or distortion compromise hull-girder/local strength, or watertight/weathertight integrity. Such defects may require prompt repair even apart from the nominal thickness threshold. [2] Verified answer | Original handwritten scan: 4 pages | Updated Oct 2026 Page 1 3. Procedure during a renewal (special) survey A. Before starting the survey - preparation and safety 1. The owner and classification society prepare/agreed a written survey programme using previous survey and TM records; identify suspect/critical areas and agree the scope of overall survey, close-up survey, TM and tank testing. Conduct the survey-planning meeting with the attending surveyor and TM firm. [2] 2. Arrange tank cleaning, sufficient lighting, safe access (scaffolds/staging, ladders or other accepted means), ventilation, safe enclosed-space entry, atmosphere testing, permit to work, rescue arrangements and communication. Any remotely operated tool is used only where accepted under the applicable survey arrangements. [2] B. Examination and gauging 3. Perform the overall survey to determine general hull condition, followed by close-up surveys of required and suspect/critical areas. Close-up survey means details are within close visual range, normally within reach of hand. [2] 4. Have the qualified, certified thickness-measurement firm carry out calibrated UT gauging simultaneously with close-up surveys where required, under the control/witness of the class/Administration surveyor. Mark or sketch measurement positions on plans so the findings are traceable. [2, 4] 5. Compare each relevant measurement with the approved acceptance/renewal criteria; calculate percentage wastage where appropriate; distinguish general corrosion, pitting, edge and grooving corrosion. Where substantial corrosion is detected, extend the close-up examination and TM to the extent prescribed by the ESP Code/class rules before the survey is credited complete. [2, 3] 6. If cracks or suspected structural defects are found, apply further NDT (e.g. magnetic-particle, dye-penetrant or ultrasonic flaw detection) as directed by the surveyor. Report all unacceptable findings immediately. [2] 4. Repair methods, monitoring and acceptance Condition found Correct action Steel below approved renewal limit; Carry out prompt, thorough permanent repair to the surveyor's unacceptable fracture, buckling, satisfaction, usually by cropping and renewing plating, stiffeners or structural grooving or strength/watertight members. Use accepted material, approved repair details/welding procedures, defect suitable NDT and required tests. [2, 3] Substantial corrosion, but thickness Area is identified, documented and kept under enhanced examination / remains within acceptance limits and thickness monitoring. Corrosion control may include cleaning and restoring no unsafe structural defect an approved hard protective coating; the class surveyor decides permitted follow-up. Coating alone is not a universal substitute for steel renewal. [2, 3] Isolated defect where permanent A temporary repair may be accepted only in the limited circumstances repair is not immediately practicable provided by applicable class rules, with a condition of class and specified deadline; unsafe service must not continue. [2] CSR bulk-carrier qualification: For identified substantial-corrosion areas in certain CSR bulk-carrier arrangements, prescribed annual monitoring may be by maintaining an approved coating in GOOD condition or by annual thickness measurements. This ship-type-specific provision should not be applied as a general exemption to all tankers. [3] 5. Role of classification society / authorised RO 1. Survey planning: agree the renewal-survey programme; study previous findings and plans; select suspect/critical locations and approve/require further examination as necessary. Verified answer | Original handwritten scan: 4 pages | Updated Oct 2026 Page 2 2. Independent verification: attend the survey, control/witness gauging as required, check calibration and the qualifications/certification of the TM firm, request additional readings and evaluate remaining scantlings. [2, 4] 3. Repair acceptance: apply class and ESP acceptance criteria, approve the repair approach, inspect completed steelwork and tests, and require corrective action or impose class conditions where permitted. [2] 4. Reporting and follow-up: review the signed TM report and countersign it, record corrosion/repairs/suspect areas in the survey records and Condition Evaluation Report / Executive Hull Summary; schedule future checks and credit the survey only when applicable requirements are met. [2] 5. Flag-state role: when undertaking statutory surveys as a recognised organisation, act within the flag Administration's authorisation, including applicable reporting and certification requirements. 6. Chief Engineer / ship-management responsibilities • Notify the Master, superintendent/owner and class surveyor promptly of significant corrosion, cracking or buckling; protect affected spaces and equipment as necessary and assess operational risks. • Supply historical reports, original thickness/scantling drawings, corrosion-control records and a safe, clean, gas-tested, accessible survey space; coordinate the gauging/repair contractors and safety permits. • Implement class-approved steelwork and coating repairs, preserve photographs/sketches/TM readings, verify test results and close out corrective actions in the planned-maintenance/SMS records. • Monitor identified suspect locations at the intervals imposed by the survey programme or class; never conceal defects with coating before their extent and remaining thickness are established. 7. Important corrections made to the handwritten answer Original shorthand / potential Correct exam wording error "75% corrosion" or "75% loss of plate" More than 75% of allowable corrosion margin, not 75% loss of nominal steel thickness. "Poor coating = substantial corrosion" POOR coating (20% breakdown or 10% hard scale) is a separate indicator; confirm steel wastage by TM. "Any substantial corrosion needs Steel below renewal/acceptance limit, or structurally unsafe defects, needs immediate steel renewal" approved repair. Substantial but acceptable wastage triggers additional examination and future monitoring. "Apply epoxy instead of permanent Epoxy coating may control corrosion in eligible and accepted cases; it does repair" not restore lost steel scantling. "All coating-restored areas may skip Exceptions depend on ship type, CSR applicability, class/ESP provisions and annual TM" acceptance of coating condition; not a general permission. "Argonaut TM" software is prescribed No particular commercial software name is imposed by the cited provisions. Use a traceable, approved TM report format. 8. Current regulatory update - 1 January 2026 Resolution MSC.553(108) amended the 2011 ESP Code procedures concerning approval/certification of firms engaged in hull thickness measurement, including auditing by the Administration to verify that the firm is properly organised, managed and competent. The amendment entered into force on 1 January 2026. For MEO Class I answers, mention the Administration/authorised RO framework rather than describing these firms as informal/private gauging companies. [1, 4, 5] Verified answer | Original handwritten scan: 4 pages | Updated Oct 2026 Page 3 9. Conclusion - examiner-friendly Substantial corrosion is an early warning stage before the renewal limit is exceeded. The surveyor assesses it by overall and close-up survey, calibrated ultrasonic thickness measurements, coating assessment and previous survey history. A renewal survey therefore requires planned access and survey, extended measurements in affected areas, approved steel repair whenever limits or integrity demand it, corrosion prevention and future monitoring, all verified and recorded by class / the Administration. 10. Quick revision - 20-25 minute exam writing plan Suggested sequence (not an official marking scheme): 3 lines of definition and regulatory basis; 4 clear inspection criteria; 6 numbered renewal-survey actions; separate steel repair versus accepted monitoring; 4 specific duties of class and a one-line conclusion. Reading from gauge / survey Correct interpretation and action More than 75% of allowable margin Record substantial corrosion if still within accepted scantling limits; used (general definition) expand measurements and apply required follow-up. CSR: tren to tren + 0.5 mm Substantial-corrosion range; confirm extent and class-prescribed survey/repair or monitoring action. Below tren, or otherwise unsafe structural Class-approved repair/renewal and tests before service in accordance condition with class requirements. Memory chain: PLAN - ACCESS - INSPECT - GAUGE - COMPARE - EXTEND - REPAIR/MONITOR - RECORD - FOLLOW UP. This follows the action verbs in the question and covers the Chief Engineer and class responsibilities. References (primary rules and official current IMO material, accessed October 2026) [1] IMO - 2011 ESP Code; SOLAS XI-1/2, with January 2026 supplement. [2] IACS UR Z10.4 (Hull Surveys of Double-Hull Oil Tankers): definitions, repairs, special/annual surveys, gauging and reporting. [3] IACS UR Z10.2 (Hull Surveys of Bulk Carriers): extent of gauging and CSR coating/annual survey qualification. [4] IMO Resolution MSC.553(108), adopted 23 May 2024, effective 1 January 2026. [5] IMO - Shipping rules in force from 1 January 2026 (official overview). [6] IACS - Common Structural Rules for Bulk Carriers and Oil Tankers. Scope note: This is a corrected and expanded typed answer based on the supplied handwritten four-page scan, not a line-for-line certified transcription. Use the ship-specific ESP annex, class rules and flag instructions when deciding particular survey or repair requirements. T07-Q22 · a. Explain the requirements for the classification of a ship, including the role of classification societies in ensuring compliance with safety and construction standards. (b) Discuss the scenario where a ship is classed by two classification societies — how the process is managed and who is responsible for conducting statutory surveys and audits in such cases.
QUESTION 06 | 6-8 MARKS | Earlier dual-class oral question What is dual class? Why is it arranged, what is the procedure, and how long does it normally last? Dual class Double class Two class societies with written agreement sharing agreed Two societies each class the vessel independently; each survey work; each society maintains its own class decision and conducts its own complete survey programme. certificate. Formal bilateral agreement for existing ship; newbuild may No reliance on a survey-sharing arrangement; duplicate involve trilateral agreement including yard. workload/cost generally greater. Reasons • Charterer/financier/insurer preferences or requirements; owner fleet policy. • Specialist technical services, regional access or newbuilding arrangements. • Commercial and technical flexibility where acceptable to both societies. Procedure 1. Owner applies to the second society, discloses existing class status, outstanding conditions and survey history. 2. Societies exchange necessary technical and survey information under IACS PR1B and agree responsibilities. 3. For an existing ship, enter the written bilateral survey-sharing agreement (or appropriate newbuild arrangement). 4. Complete additional plan checks or surveys needed to satisfy the incoming society; close outstanding requirements. 5. Each society assigns/maintains class and issues its own certificate; owner maintains compliance with both. Duration: No IMO-wide or IACS PR1B fixed one-month or one-year duration for the dual-class relationship. It lasts while both societies maintain the arrangement and each ship complies. A PR1B one-month communication deadline is a procedural deadline, not the maximum dual-class duration. [R10] EXAMINER NOTE As at Oct 2026, IACS PR1B Rev.8 (Nov 2024) is current; Rev.9 is indicated for 1 Jan 2027 entry into force. [R10] Primary references: [R10] IACS PR1B status and procedure. Maintenance & Machinery Surveys (5)
T17-Q01 · What are the essential elements of preventive maintenance on- board ships? Analyse the link between statutory and classification survey of ship machinery/equipment with respect to routine maintenance and how it is effectively merged in the ship’s SMS under the ISM Code.
QUESTION 07 | 6-8 MARKS | Previous class / survey / PMS question Distinguish classification surveys, statutory surveys and maintenance; explain how PMS records and Conditions of Class fit in. Activity Authority and purpose Examples Class survey Classification society rules; retain class notation, Annual hull/machinery, docking, special assess hull/machinery/electrical fitness. survey, shaft/boiler surveys as applicable. Statutory survey Convention and flag law; performed by flag or SOLAS Safety Construction, MARPOL IOPP, authorized RO. Load Line, safety equipment. PMS / routine maintenance Owner/operator procedures and ISM section 10; Clearances, calibration, filter service, machinery kept effective between surveys. generator tests and defect records. Role of PMS • Plan and record jobs, condition checks, calibrations, repairs, testing and spares. • Provide evidence to attending class/statutory surveyors. • Routine PMS completion does not automatically earn classification survey credit. Credit is only available under approved survey arrangements and subject to society rules and surveyor verification. Conditions of Class (CoC) A Condition of Class is an outstanding requirement placed by the society for a deficiency or required work, with a stated action/due date under its rules. Non-compliance may lead to restrictions, suspension or withdrawal of class. The Master/Chief Engineer should notify the company, plan permanent repair and obtain required re-survey and formal closure. Primary references: [R1] IACS FAQ; [R3] SOLAS II-1/3-1; society-specific classification rules. Official reference list and authoritative links [R1] IACS - Frequently Asked Questions, classification, UR and CSR [R2] IACS - Resolutions, UR / UI / PR [R3] SOLAS II-1, Regulation 3-1 (text as reproduced by IMO Rules) [R4] IMO - Recognized Organizations, framework and authorization [R5] IMO Resolution A.1207(34) - HSSC Survey Guidelines 2025 [R6] SOLAS Chapter I, Regulations 8, 9, 10 (HSSC survey schedules) [R7] IACS - Unified Interpretations [R8] IMO MSC.349(92) - RO Code full adopted text [R9] DG Shipping - Comprehensive Safety Management Report 2025, RO oversight section [R10] IACS PR1B - Double and Dual Class, version status [R11] IACS PR17 - Reporting deficiencies affecting ISM implementation [R12] IMO - Port State Control / Procedures A.1206(34), 2025 [R13] IMO - Ship construction and ESP overview Preparation standard: Short direct introductions, subpart-by-subpart response, comparison tables for distinctions, numbered procedures and explicit IMO regulation numbers. Source status reviewed 27 October 2026.
