Bunkering & MARPOL (7)
O01-Q01 · PSC detects 0.52% sulphur in fuel oil. What will you do as Chief Engineer?
From September oral question collection: p. 33; similar scenarios pp. 2, 26 • First clarify the sample: MARPOL delivered sample, in-use sample or onboard sample. Also establish whether the vessel uses an Administration-approved equivalent arrangement such as an EGCS. • Legal limit: MARPOL Annex VI regulation 14: ordinarily 0.50% m/m outside SOx ECAs; 0.10% m/m in applicable SOx ECAs. A reading of 0.52% does not by itself mean the fuel is authorized for use. • Verification: Appendix VI Part 1 for official delivered sample: valid mean greater than 0.50% does not meet the requirement. Part 2 for official in-use/onboard samples: a verification margin applies; for 0.50%, the published test margin is 0.53% (not a change to the 0.50% limit). • Immediate CE response: Cooperate with PSCO; retain sample identity, seals, BDN, bunker logs, tank soundings, changeover entries and laboratory reports; advise Master, company/DPA and flag Administration. • Prevent further exposure: Where non-compliance is established, arrange compliant fuel/changeover consistent with machinery safety and PSCO directions; segregate suspect fuel; do not conceal, discharge or transfer it without approved arrangements. • Root cause and close-out: Check supplier specification, sampling chain, tank contamination/mixing and tests. Raise nonconformity, supplier protest/claim via company, corrective actions, and record evidence for PSC clearance. Cross-question: Is 0.53% the legal limit? No. It is the Appendix VI Part 2 verification margin for official in-use/onboard sampling, not a purchase or bunkering target.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_01_Fuel_MARPOL.pdf

O01-Q02 · Explain repeatability and reproducibility in sulphur testing.
From September oral question collection: pp. 2, 7, 19, 33 • Repeatability (r): closeness of independent results under the same test method, laboratory, operator/equipment and short-time conditions. Appendix VI requires two subsamples to satisfy repeatability before averaging. • Reproducibility (R): closeness under changed conditions, commonly different laboratories/operators/equipment, using the same standardized method. • Official verification: Delivered sample Part 1 compares mean X to the regulatory limit; in-use/onboard Part 2 includes a margin linked to 0.59R and the prescribed table (0.53% for the 0.50% case). • Practical CE role: Do not calculate compliance by applying an arbitrary tolerance to a BDN. Preserve sealed samples and request the prescribed competent-authority process.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_01_Fuel_MARPOL.pdf

O01-Q03 · You receive 0.55% sulphur fuel. What action will you take?
From September oral question collection: p. 3; related p. 7 • Before bunkering: Review ordering specification, BDN declared sulphur, sulphur limit at route/port, sample plan and compatibility; reject a declared 0.55% supply for ordinary non-EGCS operation. • After delivery or lab result: Inform Master, company, flag and relevant authorities; isolate/segregate tank and secure evidence; maintain safe compliant fuel supply. Request supplier investigation and company-led replacement/debunkering arrangements. • Do not treat FONAR as exemption: It concerns demonstrated unavailability of compliant fuel despite best efforts, not acceptance of an off-spec delivery. • Record: BDN, sample numbers, quantity, tank, dates, protests, communications, operational mitigation, NC/CAPA.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_01_Fuel_MARPOL.pdf

O01-Q04 · What are the fuel oil sample types, and what is the 0.53% rule?
From September oral question collection: pp. 6, 7, 18, 33 • MARPOL delivered sample: representative sample taken during bunkering (regulation 18.8.1); official delivered-sample sulphur verification under Appendix VI Part 1. • In-use sample: fuel being consumed in the service system; regulation 14.8 and designated sampling points under regulation 14.10. • Onboard sample: fuel carried or intended for use onboard, typically sampled from an appropriate tank; regulation 14.8. • Commercial sample: independently retained bunker/ship/supplier sample for contractual quality disputes; not automatically identical to the official MARPOL sample. • Exam trap: Appendix VI Part 2 table accepts an officially verified in-use/onboard mean up to 0.53% for the global 0.50% case, subject to prescribed sampling/test steps; Part 1 delivered-sample table does not.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_01_Fuel_MARPOL.pdf

O01-Q05 · You join a vessel and find high cat fines in bunker fuel. What actions?
From September oral question collection: pp. 18, 29–31 • Risk: Aluminium and silicon catalyst particles cause severe abrasive wear of fuel pumps, injectors, piston rings and liners. ISO 8217 is a fuel-quality specification; use the correct edition and grade stipulated in the bunker contract. • Initial control: Verify independent lab result and tank identity; hold or segregate suspect bunker; brief Master/company; request supplier claim and further sampling where needed. • Machinery precautions: Maximize settling time/temperature within fuel properties, optimize purifier throughput and temperature per maker, check separator condition, reduce throughput where necessary, monitor drains/sludge and filters, and obtain a post-treatment sample as close to engine inlet as practicable. • Monitoring: Observe filter differential pressure, fuel pump/injector abnormalities, exhaust temperatures, scavenge drain and liner/piston wear indications; maintain corrective action records. • Decision: Do not feed engine if safe fuel quality cannot be assured; seek manufacturer/company guidance and arrange alternative fuel. No universal inlet cat-fines guarantee can be inferred solely from a bunker delivery result.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_01_Fuel_MARPOL.pdf

O01-Q06 · Bunker checklist is missing and an external auditor raises a major nonconformity. Your action as CE?
From September oral question collection: pp. 26, 35 • Immediately: Notify Master and company/DPA. Do not retrospectively fabricate or backdate a checklist. Preserve existing bunker records and confirm whether the operation was safely completed. • Containment: Check BDN, ORB Part I entries if applicable, tank soundings, bunker sample records, transfer and spill precautions, toolbox talk and permit records; identify actual missing controls. • Corrective action: Raise SMS nonconformity, conduct root-cause analysis (procedure/training/supervision/document control), verify controls and implement training and revised checklist/check points. • Audit close-out: Send objective evidence to company/auditor within prescribed process and timescale. The auditor having left the vessel does not end the corrective-action obligation. • System follow-up: Consider fleetwide preventive action and effectiveness check at next internal audit. Explain why this was a major NC and whether it indicates systemic SMS failure.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_01_Fuel_MARPOL.pdf

O01-Q07 · IMO 2020: What complications can a Chief Engineer face with 0.50% fuel?
From September oral question collection: p. 1; related pp. 2, 18 • Compatibility/stability: Different VLSFO blends may precipitate asphaltenes causing sludge and purifier/filter blockage. Keep grades segregated and test compatibility before mixing. • Viscosity/lubricity: Lower viscosity can cause leakage and inadequate pump lubrication; observe equipment inlet viscosity/temperature maker limits. • Cold-flow: Waxing, cloud/pour point behavior and filter blockage if storage/transfer temperatures are inadequate. • Ignition/combustion and thermal stability: Poor combustion or deposits can occur with off-spec blends; monitor engine parameters. • High cat fines: Need optimized purification and engine-inlet sampling. • Changeover: Account for fuel temperature and viscosity changes, tank capacity, ECA entry records, segregation, fire safety and equipment maker guidance. • Documents: BDN and retained samples under Annex VI regulation 18; fuel changeover log and approved EGCS documentation where relevant. Regulatory and primary reference reading IMO: IMO 2020 sulphur overview — https://www.imo.org/en/mediacentre/hottopics/pages/sulphur-2020.aspx IMO: MEPC.320(74), 2019 Guidelines on consistent implementation of the 0.50% sulphur limit — https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/KnowledgeCentre/IndexofIMOResolutions/MEPCDocuments/MEPC.320%2874%29.pdf MARPOL Annex VI Appendix VI, delivered/in-use/onboard verification text — https://www.imorules.com/GUID-43BB88E5-D8E4-4C9A-86CE-0CCFF1F974B0.html DG Shipping India, Merchant Shipping Notice 16 of 2023, implementation of MARPOL Annex VI — https://www.dgshipping.gov.in/Writ eReadData/News/202311140526524377526MSNotice16of2023onImplementationofMARPOLAnnexVI-combined.pdf IRS Technical Circular 006/2022 (Appendix VI sample verification) — https://www.irclass.org/media/5889/technical-circular-no006.pdf

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_01_Fuel_MARPOL.pdf

Class, RO & ESP (10)
O02-Q01 · What is the role of a classification society? Why is class needed?
Source: September 2026 oral question compilation, pp. 5, 23, 33. • An independent technical organization publishes and applies rules for hull structure, machinery, electrical installations and essential systems. It reviews plans, surveys construction, conducts periodic surveys and assigns/maintains class notations. • Class establishes conformity with society rules; it is not itself a statutory flag-State certificate. Flag may separately authorize the society as an RO to undertake statutory work. • CE checks classification status, machinery survey schedule, outstanding conditions, approved alterations, survey reports and maintenance records. Class status supports safety, insurance, chartering and commercial acceptability. Examiner cross-question: Does class guarantee seaworthiness? No. The owner, master and responsible officers continue to have duties to operate and maintain a seaworthy vessel. Regulatory basis: SOLAS II-1/3-1; XI-1/1; SOLAS I/6; society classification rules.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_02_Class_RO_ESP_Surveys.pdf

O02-Q02 · Differentiate classification society, RO and RSO; explain RO Code parts
Source: September 2026 oral question compilation, pp. 12, 15, 23, 33. • Classification society: technical rulemaking and ship classification; RO: an organization recognized/authorized by a flag State for specified statutory surveys and certification; RSO: organization authorized for certain SOLAS XI-2/ISPS security verification functions. • RO Code Part 1: general provisions; Part 2: recognition and authorization requirements for organizations; Part 3: oversight of recognized organizations by flag States. • Flag State remains ultimately responsible for compliance even when it delegates surveys; authorization scope and agreement must be checked. CE must not assume every class society is authorized by every flag for every certificate. Examiner cross-question: What is VCA? Vertical Contract Audit: review of a specific RO contract/order, including witnessing relevant survey, audit or plan approval activity. Regulatory basis: RO Code, MSC.349(92), MEPC.237(65); IMO III Code para. 20; SOLAS XI-1/1.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_02_Class_RO_ESP_Surveys.pdf

O02-Q03 · Difference between Condition of Class (CoC) and Memoranda; does Memoranda have a time limit?
Source: September 2026 oral question compilation, pp. 2, 9, 14, 23. • Condition of Class is a requirement imposed by the society, commonly linked to defect/repair or an outstanding rule item, requiring satisfactory action by a due date. Overdue conditions can lead to class suspension. • Memoranda are entries recording information or recommendations for attention, subject to the society’s defined category. Do not automatically equate every memorandum to a mandatory timed CoC. • Check actual wording and due date in the vessel class status; an item described as memorandum can still require action if society specifies it. Notify company, prepare corrective plan, seek class acceptance and close out with evidence. Examiner cross-question: Can CE extend a CoC? No unilateral extension. Society must agree according to applicable rules, with flag involvement where statutory matters are affected. Regulatory basis: IACS PR1C and the ship’s classification-society rules/status report.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_02_Class_RO_ESP_Surveys.pdf

O02-Q04 · When is class automatically suspended and when withdrawn?
Source: September 2026 oral question compilation, pp. 2, 6, 9, 33. • Automatic suspension commonly follows overdue class renewal/special survey, overdue annual/intermediate surveys after permissible windows, or other prescribed overdue class requirements, subject to the society’s precise rules. • Suspension can also be imposed following class decision for serious damage, failure to report damage, non-compliance with conditions, or refusal to permit surveys. • Withdrawal is a final class-status decision, for example owner request, scrapping, serious unresolved non-compliance or prolonged suspension. A common IACS PR1C threshold is withdrawal after six months of suspension for overdue surveys/conditions, subject to stated exceptions. • Reinstatement requires the society to verify outstanding survey/repair/conditions; notify flag and insurers and ensure valid statutory certificates before trade. Examiner cross-question: Difference between withdrawal and cancellation? Terminology differs among societies; explain actual legal/class status shown by society rather than inventing a universal distinction. Regulatory basis: IACS PR1C; society Rules for Classification, Suspension and Withdrawal.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_02_Class_RO_ESP_Surveys.pdf

O02-Q05 · As CE, owner directs ship to sail with special survey due or overdue: actions?
Source: September 2026 oral question compilation, p. 19. • Check due date, permissible survey window, actual status and relevant certificate validity. Inform master and DPA/company immediately in writing; check class online status and survey report. • Arrange attendance by class, defects rectification, thickness measurements and testing. If survey cannot be completed, request a written, rule-compliant arrangement from class and any necessary flag authorization; do not self-authorize extension. • Conduct risk assessment for machinery/hull limitations, document voyage and safety implications, and advise master against departure if vessel lacks valid class/statutory certification or is unsafe. Obtain written clearance and update PMS and class survey file. Examiner cross-question: Can force majeure allow a voyage? Certain class rules provide narrowly constrained arrangements subject to class checks, direct routing and documented conditions; not an automatic entitlement. Regulatory basis: IACS PR1C; ship classification rules; SOLAS I survey/certification obligations.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_02_Class_RO_ESP_Surveys.pdf

O02-Q06 · Explain ESP Code, applicability and how CE supports its survey
Source: September 2026 oral question compilation, pp. 10–13, 16, 20, 29–31. • 2011 ESP Code (adopted A.1049(27)) is mandatory under SOLAS XI-1/2 for applicable bulk carriers and oil tankers. Its requirements supplement the scope/depth of annual, intermediate and renewal hull surveys. • Key elements: ship-specific survey programme, survey planning meeting, thickness measurement, overall and close-up inspections, corrosion evaluation, access and safe entry, reporting and evaluation. • CE prepares ballast tanks, pumping/ventilation, atmosphere monitoring, lighting, safe access and relevant records; coordinates with master/chief officer and surveyor. Ensure a qualified thickness-measurement firm and approved measuring arrangements as required. • Preserve survey report file including structure, coating condition, suspect areas, substantial corrosion, repairs and historical thickness measurements. Examiner cross-question: What is substantial corrosion? Wastage exceeding 75% of allowable diminution but remaining within acceptable limits; verify relevant ESP definition/structural acceptance data. Regulatory basis: SOLAS XI-1/2; 2011 ESP Code; IACS Z10 series.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_02_Class_RO_ESP_Surveys.pdf

O02-Q07 · What is close-up survey? Can drones or robots be used?
Source: September 2026 oral question compilation, pp. 13, 16, 17, 22, 30. • Close-up survey means a survey where structural details are within close visual inspection range of the surveyor, normally within reach of hand. • Remote inspection techniques such as drones/ROVs may be considered where allowed by the currently applicable ESP amendments, flag/RO/class procedures, technology capability and surveyor acceptance. Do not claim drones automatically replace every close-up examination. • Survey plan must ensure adequate image quality, access to critical details, safe atmosphere, communication, defect follow-up, thickness measurement and direct examination where required. Examiner cross-question: Will a drone measure plate thickness? A camera alone cannot; compatible qualified NDT/UT equipment and approved procedures are needed. Regulatory basis: 2011 ESP Code; applicable amendments; IACS UR Z-series and surveyor instructions.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_02_Class_RO_ESP_Surveys.pdf

O02-Q08 · Differentiate annual, intermediate, renewal/special, and docking surveys
Source: September 2026 oral question compilation, pp. 6, 11, 23, 32, 36. • Annual survey: general examination of accessible systems and condition at each anniversary, within the relevant permitted window. • Intermediate survey: more detailed checks scheduled within the five-year certification/class cycle, normally centered on second/third annual window, depending on the governing certificate and rules. • Renewal/special survey: comprehensive examination, internal examinations, structural thickness gauging and machinery tests as applicable; normally a five-year class cycle. • Docking/bottom survey: checks underwater hull, sea chests, valves, propeller, rudder, stern gear, anodes/ICCP, hull coatings and appendages; dry-dock or accepted in-water survey depends on ship type, age, flag and rules. Examiner cross-question: Are class and statutory annual survey the same? No; often coordinated, but differing sources of authority, scope and certificates. Regulatory basis: SOLAS I/7, I/8, I/10; HSSC guidance; classification society survey rules.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_02_Class_RO_ESP_Surveys.pdf

O02-Q09 · What is in-water survey, tail-shaft monitoring (TMON), and implications for dry docking?
Source: September 2026 oral question compilation, pp. 12, 25, 32. • IWS is an authorized underwater examination in place of a docking bottom survey in eligible circumstances, using approved divers/ROVs and class procedures; it is not universally available to all vessel types. • TMON is a society machinery notation/survey arrangement based on approved shaft/sterntube-bearing condition monitoring and records, facilitating survey interval arrangements when permitted; it does not remove safety duties. • CE produces shaft bearing temperature trends, oil analysis, stern-tube leakage reports, propeller/shaft vibration records, seal maintenance and manufacturer limits, and confirms the exact assigned notation and conditions. Examiner cross-question: Does TMON automatically exempt shaft withdrawal or docking? No; survey credit/alternatives are conditional on society rules, condition and flag requirements. Regulatory basis: Class society shaft surveys, TMON notation and bottom-survey rules; SOLAS I/10 where applicable.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_02_Class_RO_ESP_Surveys.pdf

O02-Q10 · What will CE check before ship is flooded out of dry dock?
Source: September 2026 oral question compilation, pp. 14, 19, 26, 36. • Conduct documented dock departure meeting with master, yard and docking master; verify all personnel/tools removed and underwater work signed off. • Confirm sea chest strainers, blanks, spool pieces, overboard valves, hull penetrations and sea connections reassembled, pressure-tested and correctly lined up; bilge/tank alarms operational. • Confirm propeller and shaft clear, rudder movement and limits, stern-tube seals and lube oil, thrusters, ICCP/anodes, external sensors, echo sounder/speed log fittings; check tank plugs, drain plugs and manholes. • Check cooling water available before engine and generators, emergency power/steering/fire systems and relevant permits; inspect for leaks progressively during flooding, keep dewatering ready and record clearances. • Reconcile class/flag survey endorsements, dry-dock repairs, coating and AFS records; take over yard completion reports and update certificates as applicable. Examiner cross-question: Which items are not yet safe to run before flooding? Equipment requiring sea-water cooling or immersion must not be started without correct seawater availability and OEM instructions. Regulatory basis: Company dry-dock SMS; classification survey rules; SOLAS II-1 systems as applicable. Insurance & Liability (12)
O03-Q01 · State the fundamental principles of marine insurance.
Asked in September oral source: pp. 2, 10, 12; principles of marine insurance. • Insurable interest: assured must have a legally recognizable interest in the marine adventure or subject matter; see Marine Insurance Act, 1963, sections 7-8. • Utmost good faith: disclosure and representations are governed by applicable law and policy terms; material non-disclosure can affect cover. • Indemnity: insurer compensates insured loss to the extent of agreed policy cover, not an opportunity for profit. • Proximate cause: effective dominant cause of loss governs insured-peril assessment (section 55). • Subrogation: after indemnifying the assured, insurer may exercise the assured’s recovery rights (section 79). • Contribution: multiple policies covering the same interest and risk share indemnity appropriately (section 80). • Duty to minimise loss: assured and agents must take reasonable measures to avert or minimise loss (section 78). Likely cross-question: Is a marine policy always a contract of indemnity? Generally yes, subject to valued policy and terms. Authority: India Marine Insurance Act 1963 ss. 7, 17-20, 55, 78-80.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_03_Insurance_Liability.pdf

O03-Q02 · Explain subrogation. What are its types?
Asked in September oral source: p. 2 and p. 11; types of subrogation and principle. • After payment of an insured loss, insurer steps into the assured’s rights against the responsible third party, to the extent indemnified; prevents double recovery. • Total loss: under section 79(1), insurer may take the assured’s interest in remaining subject matter and associated rights. • Partial loss: under section 79(2), insurer acquires no title to remaining subject matter but may pursue recovery rights to the extent it indemnified the assured. • Classification often taught: equitable/legal subrogation and contractual subrogation (by express terms); the statutory Marine Insurance Act distinction is total versus partial loss. • Example: H&M insurer pays for collision damage and pursues negligent other vessel in the assured’s name as legally appropriate. Likely cross-question: Does paying a partial claim transfer ownership of the vessel? No. Authority: Marine Insurance Act 1963 s.79.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_03_Insurance_Liability.pdf

O03-Q03 · Differentiate Hull & Machinery (H&M) and P&I insurance.
Asked in September oral source: pp. 9, 15, 27; running-down and P&I. • H&M: typically indemnifies physical loss/damage to insured hull, machinery and insured interests; also collision liability as defined by policy. • P&I: mutual third-party liability cover including crew injury/death, pollution, cargo liabilities, wreck removal, fines where permitted, and collision liabilities according to club rules. • Standard historical 3/4 running-down arrangement: H&M pays covered 3/4 collision liability and P&I commonly responds for the remaining 1/4; modern H&M cover may include 4/4, so examine actual clauses. • Chief Engineer: promptly notify Master, company and correspondents; preserve oil records, machinery logs, photos and event evidence; do not admit liability. Likely cross-question: Is one quarter collision liability automatically paid by every P&I Club? No: subject to club entry and policy terms. Authority: International Group of P&I Clubs, About the Group; Institute Time Clauses / individual cover wording.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_03_Insurance_Liability.pdf

O03-Q04 · Explain running-down clause with an example.
Asked in September oral source: pp. 2, 9; running-down clause. • Running-down clause (RDC) is a hull policy collision liability provision for damage to another ship/property following collision, according to its wording. • Illustration: vessel A found 40% responsible; recoverable collision damages claimed by vessel B are USD 1,000,000. A’s apportioned liability = USD 400,000. • If covered on a conventional 3/4 RDC basis, H&M share = USD 300,000 and uncovered 1/4 = USD 100,000 may fall to P&I subject to entry, terms, limits and exclusions. • If H&M has 4/4 RDC, the split is different. Do not say that all collisions are covered by 3/4 automatically. Likely cross-question: Who covers own ship damage? Generally H&M, subject to terms and deductible. Authority: Policy-specific; International Group cover overview.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_03_Insurance_Liability.pdf

O03-Q05 · What is vicarious liability? Give a marine example.
Asked in September oral source: pp. 2, 9, 13; vicarious liability. • An employer/principal may be legally responsible for a wrongful act of its employee/agent committed within the course or scope of employment, even without personal wrongdoing. • Marine example: an engineer negligently transfers fuel and causes pollution while carrying out assigned duties; shipowner/employer may incur liability under applicable legal regime. • Distinguish vicarious liability from strict liability: strict liability attaches without need to establish negligence subject to statutory exceptions; vicarious liability attributes another person’s actionable conduct. • For CLC pollution, owner liability is established under a treaty strict-liability regime, not merely through common-law vicarious liability. Likely cross-question: Will insurance always cover wilful or criminal acts? No; policy terms and law govern. Authority: General legal doctrine; CLC 1992 article III.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_03_Insurance_Liability.pdf

O03-Q06 · Explain LLMC 1976/1996 and updated limits.
Asked in September oral source: pp. 13, 21; LLMC and liability limitation. • LLMC permits shipowners, salvors and other entitled persons (Article 1) to limit qualifying maritime claims (Article 2), subject to exclusions (Article 3) and loss-of-right-to-limit test (Article 4). • Article 4: limitation is lost if personal act/omission was committed intentionally to cause the loss, or recklessly with knowledge such loss would probably result. • 2015 effective amendments to 1996 Protocol - personal injury/death: up to 2,000 GT = 3.02 million SDR; additional per GT: 2,001-30,000 = 1,208 SDR; 30,001-70,000 = 906 SDR; above 70,000 = 604 SDR. • Other/property claims: up to 2,000 GT = 1.51 million SDR; additional per GT: 2,001-30,000 = 604 SDR; 30,001-70,000 = 453 SDR; above 70,000 = 302 SDR. • Exam trap: establishing a limitation fund under Article 11 is possible, not universally mandatory to invoke limitation; no LLMC international compensation fund comparable to IOPC Funds. • Applicable limits depend on relevant State treaty participation, reservations and implementing legislation. Likely cross-question: Are oil pollution claims subject to CLC also limited under LLMC? Claims within CLC are excluded by LLMC Article 3(b). Authority: IMO LLMC Convention and 2012 amendments effective 8 June 2015.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_03_Insurance_Liability.pdf

O03-Q07 · Explain CLC 1992: coverage, strict liability and exceptions.
Asked in September oral source: pp. 21, 22, 35; CLC and Bunker comparison. • 1992 CLC governs qualifying persistent oil pollution from a ship constructed/adapted to carry oil in bulk as cargo, including relevant laden/unladen tanker situations; pollution damage in territory/territorial sea/EEZ of a State Party and preventive measures. • Strict liability: registered owner liable irrespective of negligence (Article III), with defences including war/hostilities, exceptional inevitable irresistible natural phenomenon, wholly intentional third-party act, and wholly negligent/wrongful navigational-aid authority action. • Limitation: up to 5,000 GT = 4.51 million SDR; 5,000-140,000 GT = 4.51m + 631 SDR/GT above 5,000; 140,000 GT and above = 89.77m SDR (post-2000 amendments). • Insurance: ships carrying over 2,000 tonnes persistent oil in bulk as cargo must maintain compulsory insurance; claimants may directly sue insurer. • Time bar: action within 3 years of damage and in no case later than 6 years after incident (Article VIII). Likely cross-question: If crude tanker bunkers spill while in ballast? CLC may apply to qualifying tanker bunker spill; do not automatically apply Bunker Convention. Authority: IMO CLC; IOPC Funds 1992 CLC.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_03_Insurance_Liability.pdf

O03-Q08 · Compare CLC and Bunker Convention 2001.
Asked in September oral source: pp. 21, 22, 35. • CLC: qualifying persistent tanker cargo oil and related tanker bunkers; owner strict liability, CLC-specific limits and IOPC Fund regime (where applicable). • Bunker 2001: pollution by bunker oil used or intended for ship operation, including fuel residues, where CLC does not apply to the damage; strict liability of shipowner as defined by the Convention (Article 3). • Bunker insurance: registered owner of ship over 1,000 GT maintains financial security (Article 7); under 1,000 GT may still be liable, although compulsory cover threshold not met. • Bunker Convention does not establish its own fixed compensation limits or an IOPC-style international fund: Article 6 preserves applicable national/international limitation rights. • Both: cover specified territorial/EEZ pollution damage and preventive measures, permit direct insurer action under the respective provisions. Likely cross-question: If bulk carrier bunker oil spills, which convention? Bunker Convention where in force; not CLC. Authority: IMO Bunkers Convention; LEG.1/Circ.13.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_03_Insurance_Liability.pdf

O03-Q09 · Explain IOPC Funds and Supplementary Fund.
Asked in September oral source: p. 21; CLC, Bunker, Athens and well-to-wake discussion. • Tier 1: tanker owner and insurer under 1992 CLC. • Tier 2: 1992 Fund provides additional compensation in qualifying circumstances where the shipowner’s compensation is inadequate/unavailable; combined ceiling 203 million SDR per incident inclusive of CLC payments. • Tier 3: in participating Supplementary Fund States, total combined compensation ceiling 750 million SDR per incident. • Funds are principally financed by contributions from receivers of contributing oil in member States, not by an LLMC assessment. Likely cross-question: Does every CLC State automatically have Supplementary Fund cover? No; treaty participation matters. Authority: IOPC Funds 1992 Fund Convention and Supplementary Fund Protocol.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_03_Insurance_Liability.pdf

O03-Q10 · What are Protection and Indemnity (P&I) Clubs?
Asked in September oral source: pp. 4, 21; meaning of P and I, coverage. • Protection: historically liabilities such as injury, collision and property damage; Indemnity: historically cargo-related liabilities, though current club cover is integrated. • Clubs generally provide mutual third-party liability insurance to members with calls/terms governed by club rules. • Typical risks: crew and passenger liabilities, cargo damage, oil pollution, removal of wreck, certain fines, collision liability, stowaways and legal defence/assistance where covered. • Chief Engineer: preserve objective records, notify company, comply with emergency pollution plans and follow club correspondent instructions through the Master/company. • Calls: estimated/advance calls, supplementary calls and release calls may apply depending on club structure. Likely cross-question: Can P&I cover damage to own main engine? Normally H&M, not routine P&I. Authority: International Group of P&I Clubs - About the Group.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_03_Insurance_Liability.pdf

O03-Q11 · Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention: owner liabilities and cover.
Asked in September oral source: pp. 18, 24; Nairobi Convention and wreck. • 2007 Convention, in force 14 April 2015, addresses hazardous wrecks in the EEZ or equivalent area of States Parties; extension into territorial waters depends on State declaration/implementation. • Articles 5-9: report, locate, warn/mark and remove hazards, with affected State and registered owner duties. • Article 10: registered owner normally responsible for costs of locating, marking and removing hazard, with narrow defences. • Article 12: insurance/financial security for registered ships 300 GT and above; certificate and direct action against insurer. • For casualty involving oil pollution, coordinate with CLC/Bunker and Article 11 exclusions to avoid double recovery. Likely cross-question: Can any floating lost container be a wreck? Yes, potentially under defined conditions following a maritime casualty. Authority: IMO Nairobi Convention 2007, Articles 1, 5-12.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_03_Insurance_Liability.pdf

O03-Q12 · Facultative insurance, general average and particular average.
Asked in September oral source: pp. 7, 13 and 2; facultative insurance, GA/PA, port of refuge. • Facultative insurance/reinsurance: individual risk accepted by a reinsurer case-by-case, unlike automatic treaty reinsurance for qualifying portfolio risks. • General average: voluntary and reasonable extraordinary sacrifice/expenditure made for common safety in a maritime adventure; losses shared among contributing interests as adjusted, often applying York-Antwerp Rules if incorporated. • Particular average: partial loss borne by the affected interest, subject to applicable insurance indemnity; not automatically shared among all. • Port of refuge: ship seeks shelter for safety; certain extraordinary expenses may form part of GA if legal/contractual requirements are met, not every port cost automatically qualifies. • CE evidence: engine room logs, repairs, consumption, emergency actions, invoices, spare usage and dated reports. Likely cross-question: Is salvage always general average? Not automatically; depends on applicable adjustment rules and circumstances. Authority: Marine Insurance Act 1963 ss. 64-66; relevant York-Antwerp Rules where contractually incorporated. ISM, ISPS & MLC (11)
O04-Q01 · ISM Code: when introduced, structure and application
Original question / theme: p. 3: "ISM which date how many chapters"; p. 27: how many chapters (7 and 3). 1. IMO adopted the ISM Code under resolution A.741(18) in 1993. SOLAS Chapter IX makes the Code mandatory; initial implementation began on 1 July 1998 for specified ship types, followed by 1 July 2002 for other covered cargo ships/mobile offshore drilling units. 2. The Code has Part A: Implementation (sections 1-12), and Part B: Certification and Verification (sections 13-16). Within Part A, 1-7 contain general management / operational duties, and 8-12 cover emergency preparedness, reports, maintenance, documentation and verification. Do NOT answer that ISM has only 7 chapters. 3. Applicable ship types under SOLAS IX/2 include passenger ships and cargo ships 500 GT or over on international voyages, and MODUs 500 GT or over, subject to exclusions. DOC is issued to company; SMC is issued to ship. Examiner follow-up: Who is a DPA? Designated person(s) ashore with access to highest level of management (ISM section 4). Regulatory authority: SOLAS IX/2 and IX/3; ISM Code sections 1-16; IMO A.741(18).

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_04_ISM_ISPS_MLC_Audits.pdf

O04-Q02 · ISM vs ISO, and how an SMS is implemented
Original question / theme: pp. 17, 25, 29: "ISM vs ISO" and "safety culture vs safety management". 1. ISM: mandatory international ship-safety/pollution-prevention framework through SOLAS IX, audited for DOC/SMC. ISO 9001 / 14001 / 45001: voluntary management-system standards except where individually required by a contract or other scheme. 2. As CE: understand the safety/environmental protection policy; identify engine-room hazards and legal requirements; put standing orders, maintenance, permits, drills, emergency plans, critical equipment and reporting into ship-specific SMS procedures. 3. Follow the Plan-Do-Check-Act cycle: risk assessment and objectives; implement training/procedures; collect evidence through checks/audits; correct root causes; review effectiveness and update SMS. Examiner follow-up: Does ISO certification replace an SMC? No. Regulatory authority: SOLAS IX; ISM Code sections 1, 6-12; relevant ISO standard requirements.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_04_ISM_ISPS_MLC_Audits.pdf

O04-Q03 · Types of ISM audits and audit versus inspection
Original question / theme: pp. 3, 6, 17, 25, 29: "Types of audit"; "Audit and Inspection difference". 1. Internal audit: conducted for company/SMS compliance by personnel independent of the audited activity, where practicable. External certification: initial, intermediate, renewal, additional, and interim verification as applicable to DOC/SMC. 2. DOC is valid up to 5 years subject to annual verification within 3 months before/after anniversary. SMC is valid up to 5 years with at least one intermediate verification; when only one, between the second and third anniversary dates. 3. Inspection checks equipment, condition or actual compliance at a point in time; audit systematically examines SMS processes, records and implementation against criteria. PSC is not the company’s internal ISM audit. Examiner follow-up: How often internal audit? ISM section 12.1 requires internal audits at intervals not exceeding 12 months; exceptional extension up to 3 months under the amended text subject to stated conditions. Regulatory authority: ISM Code sections 12 and 13; IMO resolution A.1188(33).

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_04_ISM_ISPS_MLC_Audits.pdf

O04-Q04 · Major NC: bunker checklist missing; external auditor has left
Original question / theme: pp. 26, 35, 38: missing bunker checklist; major NC, CE actions. 1. Immediately notify Master and DPA/company; retrieve audit report and precise finding, and verify whether the missing record represents isolated document loss or a failure of bunker safety controls. Do not retrospectively fabricate a checklist. 2. Apply containment: suspend bunkering until company-approved controls and pre-transfer checklist are in place; verify bunker plan, manifold readiness, communications, emergency stop, scuppers, spill kits, tank soundings and transfers. 3. Investigate root cause (procedure, personnel competence, document management); take corrective/preventive action, retrain and retain objective evidence. Inform the auditor/RO and request required follow-up / additional verification under agreed process. 4. A major non-conformity is an identifiable deviation posing a serious threat to safety/environment and needing immediate corrective action, or lacking effective/systematic ISM implementation. Certification consequences are for the certifying authority to decide; no CE self-closure. Examiner follow-up: Can you sail after a major NC? Only if the competent flag/RO process permits and the deficiency and certification status are addressed; do not assume a fixed grace period. Regulatory authority: ISM Code sections 9, 10, 12, 13; A.1188(33) paras 4.14-4.15; MSC/Circ.1059-MEPC/Circ.401.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_04_ISM_ISPS_MLC_Audits.pdf

O04-Q05 · What is the Master’s review? How often?
Original question / theme: p. 27 image: "Masters review". 1. ISM section 5.1.5 requires the Master to review the SMS and report deficiencies to shore-based management. It is a mechanism for identifying weak procedures, resources, crew competence and changes needed. 2. Check audits, NCs, near misses, incidents, drills, maintenance overdue items, crew fatigue, management of change and effectiveness of previous actions; send documented findings to company for review/action. 3. The Code does not itself prescribe a universal numerical frequency for Master’s review; ship-specific SMS/company instructions set the interval. Distinguish it from company management review under section 12.2. Examiner follow-up: If examiner asks "six-monthly?" Answer: only if company SMS requires six-monthly. Regulatory authority: ISM Code sections 5.1.5 and 12.2.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_04_ISM_ISPS_MLC_Audits.pdf

O04-Q06 · ISPS part of ISM? ISPS certificates and security levels
Original question / theme: pp. 15, 17, 22: "Is ISPS part of ISM" and ISM/ISPS certificates. 1. No. ISPS Code is under SOLAS XI-2, not the ISM Code. Both are required where applicable and may be integrated in company management arrangements. 2. ISPS Code Part A is mandatory, Part B guidance. The Ship Security Plan is based on Ship Security Assessment, controlled by SSO/CSO; ship carries International Ship Security Certificate (ISSC) or Interim ISSC as applicable. 3. Level 1 normal protective measures; Level 2 additional measures for heightened risk; Level 3 further specific measures when a security incident is probable or imminent. Ships must observe applicable higher port security level. Examiner follow-up: Can ISPS Level 2 ship visit Level 1 port? Yes, coordinate the differing measures; Declaration of Security may be required based on applicable provisions and risk. Regulatory authority: SOLAS XI-2/3, XI-2/4, XI-2/8; ISPS Code Part A sections 7, 9, 11-12, 19.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_04_ISM_ISPS_MLC_Audits.pdf

O04-Q07 · ISM and ISPS certificates: correct names and validity
Original question / theme: p. 15 image: "Certificates under ISM Code and ISPS". 1. ISM: DOC issued to the Company (up to 5 years, annual verification); SMC to ship (up to 5 years, intermediate verification). Interim DOC ordinarily up to 12 months and Interim SMC up to 6 months, with limited extension where applicable. 2. ISPS: ISSC up to 5 years with at least one intermediate verification; Interim ISSC up to 6 months under Part A section 19.4, not repeatedly issued merely to avoid full compliance. 3. MLC is separate: Maritime Labour Certificate normally up to 5 years; interim MLC certificate up to 6 months subject to conditions. Examiner follow-up: Does the DPA issue the SMC? No; the Administration or authorized RO conducts verification and issues it. Regulatory authority: ISM Code section 13; ISPS Code section 19; MLC Standard A5.1.3.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_04_ISM_ISPS_MLC_Audits.pdf

O04-Q08 · Implementing MLC on delivery of a new vessel as Chief Engineer
Original question / theme: p. 3: "How will you implement MLC on a new ship as a CE". 1. Confirm flag implementation, company SEA/CBA procedures, crew minimum age/medical fitness, certificates, qualification, recruitment and repatriation arrangements. Review the applicable DMLC Part I; company prepares Part II describing practical compliance. 2. Inspect accommodation, noise, vibration, lighting, potable water, galley refrigeration, hot water, sanitary fittings, laundry, ventilation/AC and recreational facilities; rectify defects before delivery. 3. Establish hours-of-work/rest records, safe manning, wages/leave procedures, food and catering checks, medical care, occupational safety, accident reporting, onboard complaint procedures and financial security documentation. 4. Coordinate interim MLC inspection for new ship, demonstrate compliant procedures and Master familiarity; arrange full certification before interim expires. Examiner follow-up: Who creates DMLC Part I versus Part II? Part I is drawn up by competent flag authority; Part II by shipowner, certified by authority/RO. Regulatory authority: MLC Reg. 5.1.3, Standards A5.1.3 and A4.3; Titles 1-5.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_04_ISM_ISPS_MLC_Audits.pdf

O04-Q09 · DMLC Parts I and II; Interim MLC certificate
Original question / theme: pp. 11-12: "DMLC 1&2 Content in part 2"; "MLC Article 5", interim audit. 1. DMLC Part I identifies national requirements implementing MLC, exemptions and equivalencies; Part II details shipowner measures for ongoing compliance, responsibilities, records, inspections and corrective actions. 2. Certificate ordinarily valid up to 5 years with intermediate inspection between 2nd and 3rd anniversary. Interim certificate available on new delivery, flag change or new shipowner assuming responsibility, maximum 6 months. No DMLC is required during interim validity. 3. Appendix A5-I has evolved through amendments: older notes cite 14 areas, while ILO consolidated material includes 16 inspection areas after additions including financial security. Use the applicable current flag and consolidated MLC version; do not memorise 14 as an unchanging count. Examiner follow-up: Is a second interim certificate available on expiry? Not simply to continue the same initial period; full verification is expected. Regulatory authority: MLC Regulation 5.1.3; Standard A5.1.3; Appendix A5-I; ILO FAQ.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_04_ISM_ISPS_MLC_Audits.pdf

O04-Q10 · MLC Article V: what does it require?
Original question / theme: p. 12: "MLC ARTICLE 5". 1. Article V is Implementation and enforcement responsibilities: each Member implements/enforces applicable requirements for ships and seafarers under its jurisdiction, exercising effective flag-state jurisdiction/control. 2. It also addresses effective regulation of recruitment/placement, measures concerning vessels entering ports, and ensuring that ships of non-ratifying States do not receive more favourable treatment than those of ratifying States. 3. Distinguish Article V from Title 5: Title 5 addresses compliance/enforcement including flag and port responsibilities. Examiner follow-up: What is the no-more-favourable-treatment principle? Non-party-flag ships should not gain advantage simply through non-ratification. Regulatory authority: MLC 2006 Article V; Title 5.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_04_ISM_ISPS_MLC_Audits.pdf

O04-Q11 · Training a junior engineer and implementing safety culture
Original question / theme: p. 12: "How to Training of junior engineer"; p. 27: safety culture / Masters review. 1. Conduct induction, assess competence and limitations; explain SMS, alarm/emergency arrangements, lockout/tagout, permit-to-work, enclosed-space, bunkering, ORB and recordkeeping; verify understanding on the equipment. 2. Use supervised OJT with task-specific risk assessment, demonstrations, toolbox talks, competence sign-off and feedback. Allocate work based on assessed competence and always provide supervision for critical tasks. 3. Promote reporting of near misses and hazards without concealment, close corrective actions and evaluate whether training reduced repeat errors. Examiner follow-up: What ISM provision supports this? Sections 6 (resources/personnel), 7 (operations), 8 (emergency preparedness) and 9 (reports/analysis). Regulatory authority: ISM Code sections 6-9; MLC Regulation 4.3 (health and safety). Official references for independent verification IMO ISM Code A.741(18): https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/KnowledgeCentre/IndexofIMOResolutions/AssemblyDocuments/A.741%2818%29.pdf IMO Administration Guidelines A.1188(33): https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/KnowledgeCentre/IndexofIMOResolutions/AssemblyDocuments/A.1188%2833%29.pdf IMO SOLAS XI-2 / ISPS overview: https://www.imo.org/en/ourwork/security/pages/solas-xi-2%20isps%20code.aspx ILO MLC convention consolidated: https://normlex.ilo.org/dyn/nrmlx_en/f?p=NORMLEXPUB:91:0:::NO::P91_SECTION:MLCA_AMEND_A5 ILO MLC FAQ: https://www.ilo.org/sites/default/files/wcmsp5/groups/public/%40ed_norm/%40normes/documents/publication/wcms_765083.pdf

Fuels & GHG (14)
O05-Q01 · What are the short-term, mid-term and long-term IMO GHG measures?
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 13, 18, 19, 32 Short-term (already adopted): technical EEXI requirements, operational CII rating and enhanced SEEMP framework under MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4; EEXI and annual CII implementation began in 2023 for applicable ships. Mid-term: proposed global fuel GHG-intensity standard plus economic/pricing mechanism under the IMO Net-Zero Framework. These were under negotiation, not binding on 29 October 2026. Long-term: 2023 IMO GHG Strategy vision of net-zero GHG emissions from international shipping by or around 2050. Indicative checkpoints: at least 20% (striving for 30%) cut in total annual GHG emissions by 2030, and at least 70% (striving for 80%) by 2040, against 2008. Likely examiner follow-up: Are the 2030 and 2040 checkpoints legally enforceable obligations for an individual ship? No: they are strategy-wide indicative checkpoints; ship-specific obligations arise from adopted regulations. Regulation / reference: IMO MEPC.377(80); MARPOL Annex VI ch. 4; IMO MEPC 84 meeting summary.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q02 · What is the IMO Net-Zero Framework, GFI and market-based measures (MBM)?
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 3-4, 7, 18, 23, 29 NZF: a package of proposed amendments to MARPOL Annex VI including a global GHG fuel-intensity measure and economic compliance mechanism. The idea is to incentivize increasingly low-GHG fuels, but legal adoption and entry into force are separate stages. GFI: life-cycle greenhouse-gas emissions divided by fuel energy used: gCO2-equivalent/MJ, on a well-to-wake basis. Include CO2, CH4 and N2O through appropriate global-warming-potential factors. MBM: market-based measures use an economic signal (e.g. levies, credit trading or contributions) to encourage emissions reduction. Proposed compliance units and contributions must not be confused with existing CII A-E ratings. Likely examiner follow-up: What happens if a ship arrives in an Indian port today? Comply with currently effective MARPOL Annex VI and national port requirements; a draft IMO NZF alone creates no IMO GFI payment liability. Regulation / reference: IMO Net-Zero Framework FAQ; MEPC 83 and 84 meeting reports; IMO 2024 LCA Guidelines.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q03 · Explain EEDI, its phases, and difference between EEDI and EEXI.
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 11, 25, 26, 32 EEDI: Energy Efficiency Design Index - design-based efficiency index for qualifying new ships, expressed in gCO2/tonne-nautical mile, with ship-type/size-dependent reference line and reduction phases. Required EEDI = reference line x (1 - prescribed reduction factor). Phases: Phase 0 (2013-2014), phase 1 (from 2015), phase 2 (from 2020) and phase 3 (typically from 2025, with earlier application for certain ship types). Never give one universal phase 3 start date for every ship type. EEXI: Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index - largely a one-time technical efficiency requirement for applicable existing ships, first verified at the first applicable IAPP annual/intermediate/renewal survey on or after 1 January 2023. EPL or ShaPoLi may be used for compliance. Likely examiner follow-up: Does EEXI automatically equal measured actual fuel performance? No. EEDI/EEXI are standardized technical indices, unlike operational CII. Regulation / reference: MARPOL Annex VI regs 20-25 (check current consolidated numbering); MEPC.350(78) and subsequent revisions; MEPC.411(84) EEDI survey guidance.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q04 · Explain CII rating and Chief Engineer actions on an E rating.
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 13, 22, 23 CII: attained annual operational carbon-intensity indicator calculated using actual operational data, compared with a required annual CII. Applicable ship types generally 5,000 GT and above under MARPOL Annex VI regulation 28. Ratings: A (major superior), B (minor superior), C (moderate), D (minor inferior), E (inferior). E for one year, or D for three consecutive years, triggers a corrective-action plan in SEEMP Part III, with verification per the regulation. CE actions: verify fuel-consumption data and distance/cargo-capacity inputs; review hull/propeller condition, engine tuning, trim, speed and hotel loads; investigate wrong readings; agree realistic actions with Master/company; implement, monitor, and document actions in SEEMP. Likely examiner follow-up: Is AER identical to EEOI? No. For commonly used CII AER, denominator uses capacity (e.g. DWT) x distance; EEOI uses actual cargo carried x distance. Regulation / reference: MARPOL Annex VI regs 27-28; MEPC.352(78), MEPC.354(78), and amendments to CII guidelines.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q05 · What is SEEMP Part I, II and III?
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 1, 3, 10, 22 Part I: ship-specific plan for continuously improving operational energy efficiency - planning, implementation, monitoring and self-evaluation. Part II: IMO fuel-oil-consumption Data Collection System (DCS) methodology: collection of fuel consumption, distance, hours under way and specified particulars (subject to applicable threshold, typically 5,000 GT). Part III: ship-specific operational carbon-intensity plan: calculation methodology, required CII, three-year implementation plan, self-evaluation/improvement measures and corrective action if E for one year or D for three consecutive years. Likely examiner follow-up: Who checks it? Flag Administration or authorized RO as relevant, according to MARPOL Annex VI and verification guidelines. Regulation / reference: MARPOL Annex VI regs 26-28; MEPC.395(82), MEPC.413(84).

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q06 · What is well-to-wake, well-to-tank and tank-to-wake?
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 13, 21, 22 Well-to-tank (WtT): upstream extraction/feedstock production, processing, transport and fuel delivery emissions. Tank-to-wake (TtW): emissions during shipboard use, including combustion and relevant methane slip or other GHG effects. Well-to-wake (WtW): WtT + TtW on a comparable energy basis; captures emissions shifted upstream, including for hydrogen, ammonia and synthetic fuels. Fuel labels such as green, blue and grey depend on production pathway, not merely what comes out of the exhaust. Likely examiner follow-up: Can ammonia have zero CO2 at the exhaust but non-zero WtW GHG? Yes: production emissions, and potentially N2O emissions in use, matter. Regulation / reference: IMO 2024 Life Cycle GHG Intensity of Marine Fuels Guidelines (LCA).

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q07 · Alternative marine fuels: advantages and disadvantages.
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 3, 7, 18, 26, 35-36 LNG: mature marine technology, very low SOx/PM and potentially lower TtW CO2 than oil; challenges are cryogenic storage, methane slip, hazardous zones, tank volume and upstream methane leakage. Methanol: liquid at ambient conditions and easier handling than cryogenic LNG, but low flashpoint, toxicity, lower volumetric energy density and dependence on low-carbon production pathway. Ammonia: no carbon in fuel molecule; major toxicity, corrosive/material-compatibility concerns, ignition/combustion challenges, possible NOx/N2O, fuel-containment and emergency-release controls. Hydrogen: possible low lifecycle GHG with suitable production; very low volumetric density, high leakage/flammability concerns, difficult storage and challenging bunkering. Biofuels / renewable drop-in blends: possible use in existing engines subject to maker/class/flag approval and fuel specifications; assess stability, microbiological issues, compatibility and sustainability certification. Likely examiner follow-up: What must be approved before converting a ship? Class/flag-approved fuel design, risk assessment, engine maker acceptance, statutory certification, SMS training and bunkering procedures. Regulation / reference: SOLAS II-1/55 and II-1/56 as relevant; IGF Code; MSC.1/Circ.1621; MSC.1/Circ.1687; IMO alternative fuels safety guidance.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q08 · As Chief Engineer, how will you convert a conventional ship to methanol fuel?
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 32-33 1. Feasibility: owner/manager/flag/class and maker study: engine conversion suitability, endurance, storage space, economics and lifecycle fuel sourcing. 2. Design approval: HAZID/HAZOP, fuel tank and piping arrangements, segregation, double-wall piping/ventilation, spill containment, leak detection, firefighting, hazardous zones and emergency shutdowns. 3. Execution: approved dry-dock work, materials testing, installation, pressure/leak tests, controls/instrumentation and engine conversion by maker-approved methods. 4. Commissioning: fuel changeover tests, alarm/ESD logic, emergency drills, documentation, revised stability and fire plans, updated SMS, crew training, trials and statutory/class endorsements. Likely examiner follow-up: What distinguishes methanol from HFO? Low flash point and toxicity require purpose-designed safety barriers; do not assume the existing HFO system is suitable. Regulation / reference: MSC.1/Circ.1621 (interim methyl/ethyl alcohol guidelines); applicable IGF/alternative-design and flag/class approval provisions.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q09 · Ammonia as fuel: technical issues, storage, and green/blue/grey ammonia.
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 7, 26, 35-36 Main risks: severe toxicity, corrosive effects and materials compatibility, ammonia gas detection, ventilation, emergency shutdown, personnel protection, limited ignition stability, NOx/N2O management and safe bunkering. Storage: anhydrous ammonia can be refrigerated near -33 C at atmospheric pressure, or stored in pressurized systems at higher temperatures; exact conditions depend on approved tank design. State the design condition rather than claiming one universal storage pressure. Colours: grey: generally fossil production without substantial carbon capture; blue: fossil-based with carbon capture; green: renewable electricity-derived hydrogen and nitrogen, subject to certified supply chain. These are descriptive labels, not automatic proof of lifecycle emissions. Likely examiner follow-up: Which specific IMO guidance? MSC.1/Circ.1687, interim guidelines for safety of ships using ammonia as fuel (2025). Regulation / reference: MSC.1/Circ.1687; relevant class rules and flag approvals; STCW.7/Circ.27 training guidelines (2026).

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q10 · Explain hydrogen fuel cells and possible marine applications.
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 7, 27-28, 30 A fuel cell converts hydrogen and oxygen electrochemically to electrical power; main products of hydrogen fuel cell operation are water and heat, but total lifecycle emissions depend on hydrogen production. Advantages: high electrical efficiency at suitable loads, quiet operation, modularity and potentially very low TtW emissions. Challenges: storage, hydrogen leak detection, ventilation, ignition prevention, high-voltage isolation, bunkering, safety certification and cost. CE responsibility: preserve redundancy; test gas detectors, isolation valves, ventilation and emergency stops; maintain electrical protection and battery/power-management integration; follow approved HAZOP and maker procedures. Likely examiner follow-up: Is every hydrogen system zero carbon? No; production, transportation and electricity sources determine WtW performance. Regulation / reference: IMO fuel-cell interim guidelines MSC.1/Circ.1647; IMO hydrogen alternative-fuel guidance.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q11 · What are EPL, ShaPoLi and the power reserve override?
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 2, 25, 35 EPL: Engine Power Limitation limits available engine output; ShaPoLi: Shaft Power Limitation caps deliverable shaft power. These can support required EEXI compliance. An approved power reserve may be used for defined safety circumstances. Following override, Master and CE must comply with the approved Onboard Management Manual: record reason, time, position, power and duration; inform the Administration/RO as required; restore and secure the limitation, and make requisite records and reports. Do not imply the CE can routinely override simply to improve schedule or commercial speed. Likely examiner follow-up: What is the authority? The approval documents/OMM and flag/RO reporting requirements, not the CE acting alone. Regulation / reference: MEPC.335(76), as amended, shaft/engine power limitation guideline; MARPOL Annex VI EEXI regs.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q12 · What is derating, variable compression ratio, and engine low-load operation?
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 3, 32, 35 Derating: selecting or modifying an engine operating rating below its maximum design capability, within maker-approved limits, potentially improving fuel economy at intended load. Match turbocharging, injection, cooling and torsional vibration characteristics. Variable compression ratio: changing effective engine compression ratio through an approved mechanical/control strategy to optimize performance across conditions; specific mechanisms are maker-dependent. Low load: risks may include poor combustion, cold corrosion, fouling and insufficient exhaust temperature; follow maker minimum continuous load, auxiliary blower and cylinder lubrication guidance. Do not state a universal minimum RPM. Likely examiner follow-up: Can derating be used to bypass NOx certification? No. Any NOx-relevant changes must remain within approved EIAPP/technical-file and certification arrangements. Regulation / reference: NOx Technical Code 2008; maker technical documentation; MARPOL Annex VI regs 13, 20-25.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q13 · What are the advantages of an attained good ESI score, and what is green shipping?
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 29, 30 ESI: Environmental Ship Index is a voluntary incentive/benchmarking scheme used by participating ports and stakeholders; it is not equivalent to a statutory IMO CII rating. Benefits can include port incentives where offered and performance recognition. Green shipping: reducing ship lifecycle environmental impacts through efficient ship design and operation, low-emission fuels, clean ballast/bilge handling, reduced underwater noise, responsible coatings and ship recycling. Before claiming a port discount, confirm the particular port tariff and ESI scheme eligibility. Likely examiner follow-up: Is a high ESI score a substitute for MARPOL certification? No. Regulation / reference: IMO MARPOL obligations; ESI program rules (separate voluntary scheme).

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_05_Alternative_Fuels_GHG_EEXI_CII.pdf

O05-Q14 · You are CE: what is the step-by-step plan for alternative-fuel bunkering?
Source: September 2026 oral-question collection, PDF page(s) 18, 26, 30, 32 Before: maker/flag/class-approved operating envelope, fuel compatibility and quality certificates, ship-to-shore checklist, quantity/temperature/pressure limits, communication test, gas detection, ESD link, exclusion zones, PPE, firefighting and muster readiness. During: controlled initial flow, monitor transfer rate and tank pressure/temperature, cargo/fuel tank fill levels, leaks, ventilation, gas alarms and common ESD functions; maintain effective ship-shore communication. After: safe isolation/purge/drain according to fuel-specific system, close-out documents, retained samples where applicable, tank soundings/measurement, incident/near-miss reporting and complete bunkering log/BDN requirements as applicable. Likely examiner follow-up: How do you handle leak or unexpected pressure? Stop transfer, initiate approved ESD, isolate affected area and muster per ship emergency plan. Regulation / reference: IGF Code and applicable fuel-specific IMO interim guidelines; MARPOL Annex VI reg 18 for relevant fuel-oil documentation. EXAMINER TRAPS / FINAL RECAP • NZF draft approval is NOT adoption or entry into force. • 2023 IMO GHG Strategy indicative checkpoints are fleet-wide policy targets, not the same as ship CII limits. • EEDI/EEXI are technical indices; CII is operational and calculated annually. • CII E for one year / D for three consecutive years: corrective action plan in SEEMP Part III. • LNG is not automatically zero-GHG: check methane slip and well-to-wake footprint. • A methanol or ammonia conversion demands flag/class approval and fuel-specific risk controls. Dates and future implementation plans must be rechecked immediately before the oral exam.

SOLAS & Emergencies (11)
O06-Q01 · AMSA inspection: emergency generator does not start. What will you do as Chief Engineer?
Question source: Page 2; emergency generator fails to start during AMSA inspection. • Immediately: Acknowledge deficiency; inform Master and attending PSCO; determine whether alternate emergency power is available and take compensatory safety measures. Do not bypass protective trips or disguise the defect. • Diagnose systematically: local/remote-auto mode, starting batteries or air supply, chargers, fuel level and priming, emergency stop, alarms, interlocks, control fuses, cooling/lubrication and breaker position. • Rectify and demonstrate: test local and automatic start following simulated main power failure, changeover to emergency switchboard, supply of required consumers, alarms and endurance as approved. • Management: log defect and test result; arrange technicians/spares; notify Company/DPA and Flag/Class when required; obtain PSCO acceptance before any departure with an unresolved essential deficiency. Regulatory reference: SOLAS II-1/42 or 43 (as appropriate to ship type), II-1/44; SOLAS I/19 PSC inspection. Examiner cross-question: Which emergency consumers must be supplied? What is the required duration on your vessel?

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_06_SOLAS_Emergencies_CE_Actions.pdf

O06-Q02 · Sunday in a Chinese port: emergency fire pump is inoperative, no spare available.
Question source: Page 35; ship in China, emergency fire pump not working. • Safety: Inform Master immediately; stop risky operations; verify main fire pumps, isolation valves, hydrants and fixed systems; impose enhanced fire watch and temporary precautions approved by Master. • Technical: Identify fault (priming, suction, fuel/drive, starter, power, pressure, relief valve, sea chest), isolate and repair if safe; seek local emergency repair/vendor/part transfer. • Reporting: Alert Company/DPA; coordinate with Flag Administration, class/RO and port State for instructions and any conditions or statutory dispensation. Never presume a temporary workaround restores compliance. • Evidence: record defect, risk assessment, alternative measures, communications and post-repair functional test including pressure/flow at hydrants. Regulatory reference: SOLAS II-2/10.2 fire main and fire pumps; SOLAS I/11 condition after damage/defect. Examiner cross-question: May the vessel sail on Monday with only main fire pumps functioning? Not without appropriate authority acceptance and safe compliance plan.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_06_SOLAS_Emergencies_CE_Actions.pdf

O06-Q03 · Starboard lifeboat engine not working: Chief Engineer actions.
Question source: Page 28; lifeboat engine non-operational. • Inform Master; make lifeboat unavailable for launching unless required in immediate emergency; do not risk personnel during testing. • Check fuel, battery/charger, starter, seawater circuit, lubricating oil, cooling, throttle/stop controls, gearbox and starting arrangements per manufacturer. • Raise defect and arrange approved servicing/repair; verify running ahead/astern where fitted, cooling and readiness. Record in planned maintenance and drill records. • If inability to restore promptly, consult Flag/Class and port State, update muster arrangements and ensure no sailing on an unauthorized deficiency. Regulatory reference: SOLAS III/20 operational readiness, maintenance and inspections; III/19 drills; MSC.402(96) where service/repair within its scope. Examiner cross-question: Who may conduct annual thorough examination and operational tests? Certified personnel under MSC.402(96).

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_06_SOLAS_Emergencies_CE_Actions.pdf

O06-Q04 · Enclosed-space entry and rescue drill: five essential elements and frequency.
Question source: Pages 35-36; asks five things to record and frequency. • Frequency: Crew with enclosed-space entry or rescue responsibilities must drill at least once every two months. • Five required drill elements: (1) PPE checking/use, (2) communications checking/use, (3) atmospheric-testing instruments, (4) rescue equipment/procedures, (5) first aid and resuscitation. • Record: date/time/place, crew participation, scenario, all five elements performed, equipment checks, lessons, deficiencies and corrective action; keep records as required by SOLAS and SMS. • Practical: Entry permit, hazard/risk assessment, isolation, ventilation, oxygen/flammable/toxic monitoring, standby attendant, rescue plan and suitable BA. Do not attempt impulsive unprotected rescue. Regulatory reference: SOLAS III/19.3.3 and III/19.3.6; III/19.5 drill records; IMO MSC.581(110) revised enclosed-space recommendations. Examiner cross-question: Is a rescue drill every two months required for everybody? Specifically crew assigned entry/rescue responsibilities.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_06_SOLAS_Emergencies_CE_Actions.pdf

O06-Q05 · Two-foot hull crack above waterline while vessel is discharging cargo.
Question source: Page 26; hull crack during discharge. • Stop or adjust affected cargo operation with Master and Chief Officer; establish position, length, crack orientation, deformation, leakage and proximity to tanks/supports. • Confirm stability, loading sequence, hull stress/shear force and bending moment on loading computer. Reduce local loads only under approved stability/strength plan; avoid sudden ballast changes. • Isolate hazardous spaces, cordon area, monitor crack growth and water ingress; inspect adjacent structure safely, with no unauthorized hot work. • Notify Master, terminal, Company, Flag/Class and port authority as circumstances require. Arrange class survey and approved permanent/temporary repair; retain images, logs and readings. • Do not sail until ship is judged safe and any imposed survey or certificate requirements are satisfied. Regulatory reference: SOLAS I/11; ISM Code 8, 9 and 10; Load Line structural integrity and applicable class hull rules. Examiner cross-question: If crack is above waterline, can you ignore it? No: stresses and progressive fracture may threaten structural integrity.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_06_SOLAS_Emergencies_CE_Actions.pdf

O06-Q06 · A hole is found in ballast tank near/above waterline: your actions.
Question source: Pages 35-36; hole in ballast tank reported. • Confirm location, size, tank contents, whether open to sea, gas hazards, cross-flooding and contamination. Alert Master and suspend associated ballast transfer. • Secure safe access: enclosed-space procedures, isolate lines, test atmosphere, ventilate and assess safe manning; do not send crew inside unprotected. • Protect buoyancy and strength; monitor soundings/draft, trim, list and shear/bending loads. Plan temporary containment only with class/Flag approval as relevant. • Notify Company, class/RO, Flag and port authorities as applicable; arrange permanent steel repair and survey; test integrity after repair and update records. Regulatory reference: SOLAS I/11 and II-1 subdivision/damage stability (ship-specific); ISM Code 8-10; class structural rules. Examiner cross-question: Who authorizes a welded insert repair? Repair scheme requires appropriate class/RO approval and inspection.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_06_SOLAS_Emergencies_CE_Actions.pdf

O06-Q07 · Second Engineer reports a hole in bilge holding tank: what will you do?
Question source: Page 35; bilge holding tank perforation. • Cease use of affected tank, isolate valves/transfers, secure bilges against overflow and control any leakage using clean containment. • Check what escaped, where it accumulated and whether pollution occurred; retain bilge/OOW/ORB records accurately; never discharge oily residues illegally. • Assess thickness/corrosion and adjacent boundaries with safe entry permit. Arrange approved repair and pressure/leak test. • Inform Master, Company/DPA and Flag/Class where condition affects safety or statutory arrangements. Notify relevant coastal authority for actual pollution as required. Regulatory reference: MARPOL Annex I (bilge control and Oil Record Book); SOLAS I/11; ISM Code 9-10. Examiner cross-question: Can you transfer bilge tank contents overboard? Only via an approved compliant route, never as a shortcut.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_06_SOLAS_Emergencies_CE_Actions.pdf

O06-Q08 · PSC finds OWS 15-ppm monitor erratic and threatens detention.
Question source: Page 26; 15-ppm monitor unstable. • Accept the finding; stop overboard discharge, close/secure discharge valve, retain bilges in holding tanks, consider shore reception. • Check analyzer sample supply, air bubbles, water quality, sample lines, sensor condition, calibration/zero, alarms, automatic stopping device and maintenance record; avoid manipulating readings. • Call maker/authorized technician and restore stable alarm/automatic stop response; demonstrate function to PSCO and preserve results. • Notify Master, DPA, Class/Flag as required; document deviation, corrective and preventive actions. Never demand release before PSCO is satisfied. Regulatory reference: MARPOL Annex I regulation 14; MEPC.107(49) performance specifications, as applicable to equipment; IMO PSC procedures. Examiner cross-question: Can overboard discharge continue while sensor is unreliable? No.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_06_SOLAS_Emergencies_CE_Actions.pdf

O06-Q09 · Chief Engineer is asked to sail when special survey is overdue.
Question source: Page 19; special survey due and company asks to sail. • Advise Master/company that overdue survey may cause class suspension and invalidate relevant statutory certificates; check class status and exact survey window. • Obtain written instructions from Flag and class/RO; request authorized extension only where the governing rules allow and justified circumstances exist. • Arrange survey at berth/port, dry dock or approved alternative; maintain safe vessel condition and records. Do not certify compliance yourself. • If class or essential statutory certificate is invalid, ship must not depart solely on a commercial instruction. Regulatory reference: SOLAS I/11, I/12 and I/14; IACS/class society rules on overdue surveys and suspension. Examiner cross-question: Does class itself extend a statutory certificate? Only under delegated Flag authority as applicable.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_06_SOLAS_Emergencies_CE_Actions.pdf

O06-Q10 · New SOLAS lifting appliance requirements from 1 January 2026.
Question source: Page 33 and 35; asks new 2026 regulation. • Effective: 1 January 2026 under SOLAS II-1/3-13, introduced by MSC.532(107). • For new lifting appliances: rules on design, construction, installation, testing and thorough examination before first use; document and mark SWL. • For existing lifting appliances: test and thorough examination and SWL marking/documentation by the first applicable renewal survey on/after 1 January 2026, according to flag guidance. • Keep records, register, maintenance plan and inspection of loose gear; inoperative lifting appliance must be identified, isolated and not used. • Indian-flag detail: DG Shipping Merchant Shipping Notice 02 of 2026 addresses implementation, exceptions for specified sub-1000 kg appliances, and registers. Regulatory reference: SOLAS II-1/3-13; MSC.532(107); MSC.1/Circ.1662/1663; DGS MS Notice 02/2026. Examiner cross-question: Are all cranes below 1000 kg wholly exempt? No; certain provisions differ, but marking, documentary SWL and SMS inspection controls remain.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_06_SOLAS_Emergencies_CE_Actions.pdf

O06-Q11 · Drydock: six-hour final preparations before dock entry.
Question source: Page 26; vessel waits six hours for drydock. • Hold pre-docking meeting with Master, Chief Officer, dock master, shipyard and CE; verify approved docking plan, trim/draft, stability, ballast and communication. • Confirm overboard/sea-inlet inventory; isolate or tag applicable sea valves, sewage, bilge, coolers, ICCP and sonar/speed log as required by plan. • Secure loose equipment; prepare main engine/shafting/propeller and thruster arrangements, fuel/services, shore connection, fire protection and emergency response. • Clear tanks/permit confined-space work as appropriate; keep ballast sequence under one control and monitor soundings throughout. • Check dock blocks align with ship plan, sensitive protrusions clear; confirm final readiness with dock master before pumping down. Regulatory reference: Approved ship docking plan and stability booklet; ISM Code 7-8; class/yard-specific docking procedures. Examiner cross-question: Why keep one ballast-control authority? To prevent unexpected loads and instability while taking the blocks.
