UNCLOS & Commercial (14)
O07-Q01 · What is innocent passage? On what grounds may it be denied?
September question bank: p. 2. • UNCLOS Article 17 grants ships of all States the right of innocent passage through the territorial sea. Article 18 requires passage to be continuous and expeditious, but permits stopping for ordinary navigation, force majeure, distress or rescue. • Article 19: passage is innocent only if not prejudicial to peace, good order or security. Examples of non-innocent activities: weapons exercises, intelligence collection, wilful serious pollution, fishing, research/survey or interfering with coastal communications. • Article 21 allows conforming coastal laws for navigation, pollution and similar matters. Article 25 permits necessary protective steps against passage that is not innocent; Article 25(3) permits published, non-discriminatory, temporary suspension in specified areas when essential for security. • Do not say a coastal State may refuse innocent passage arbitrarily; Article 24 prohibits improper hampering and discrimination. Authority: UNCLOS Articles 17-19, 21, 24-25 (UN text). Examiner cross-question: Is a distress stop a breach? No, if justified under Article 18(2).

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q02 · Innocent passage versus transit passage; Strait of Hormuz scenario
September question bank: p. 12. • Innocent passage applies in the territorial sea (UNCLOS Part II). Transit passage applies to qualifying straits used for international navigation between one part of the high seas/EEZ and another (Part III, Articles 37-38). • Transit passage is continuous and expeditious navigation and overflight, and is not to be impeded (Art. 38); ships must comply with Art. 39 duties. Coastal States may adopt limited laws under Art. 42 but may not hamper or suspend transit passage (Art. 44). • The precise legal position in a particular strait also depends on geography, treaty participation and applicable international law; do not infer permission to disregard navigational security advisories. • Chief Engineer: maintain propulsion and emergency plant readiness, fuel reserve and contingency plans, implement approved company/security measures, and promptly report machinery defects to Master. Authority: UNCLOS Articles 37-44. Examiner cross-question: Can a coastal State suspend transit passage? Article 44 says no.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q03 · Explain bill of lading and mate’s receipt
September question bank: p. 12. • Bill of Lading (B/L) has three functions: receipt of goods as described; evidence of the carriage contract; and, in transferable form, a document of title. • Mate’s receipt is issued by ship/agent acknowledging goods received and their apparent order and condition. It provides a basis for issuing the B/L but is not ordinarily itself a negotiable document of title. • Record qualifications accurately: number, packaging, marks, apparent damage and dates. Do not issue a clean B/L where the condition actually noted is defective without appropriate legal/owner guidance. • Straight B/L names consignee; order B/L transfers by endorsement and delivery; bearer B/L transfers by delivery. Delivery must follow the governing B/L terms and lawful instructions. Authority: Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 2025, Schedule Arts III(3)-(4); standard carriage practice. Examiner cross-question: What is a clean B/L? One without adverse qualification about apparent order and condition.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q04 · What is a stale bill of lading?
September question bank: p. 10. • A stale B/L is presented later than the time or document-presentation period required under the sale or documentary credit arrangements; delay may cause bank refusal or commercial difficulty. • It is not a specific SOLAS statutory category. Assess the terms of the relevant letter of credit and banking rules, including UCP 600 if incorporated. • Chief Engineer’s relevance: accurate bunker/cargo operations records and prompt delivery of ship particulars to Master; commercial documentation is principally dealt with by Master/agent/owner. Authority: ICC UCP 600 (where incorporated); relevant sale/credit terms. Examiner cross-question: Does stale automatically mean fraudulent? No.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q05 · Explain CIF, FOB and CFR; who receives an insurance claim?
September question bank: p. 1. • Under Incoterms 2020 FOB (Free on Board), seller delivers goods on board the nominated vessel; risk passes to buyer when loaded on board. Buyer generally contracts main carriage. • Under CFR (Cost and Freight), seller pays freight to named destination port, but risk passes to buyer when goods are loaded on board at shipment port. Seller has no Incoterms obligation to procure cargo insurance. • Under CIF (Cost, Insurance and Freight), seller pays carriage and obtains agreed cargo insurance cover for the buyer’s benefit; risk nevertheless generally passes when loaded on board at shipment port. • For damage before discharge, an insurance claim is paid to the person validly entitled under the policy/certificate and proof of loss, often buyer/assignee - not automatically the seller merely because the seller arranged insurance. • These trade terms allocate seller/buyer obligations; they do not themselves determine the shipowner’s cargo liability. Authority: ICC Incoterms 2020 official guidance. Examiner cross-question: Can CIF risk pass at loading even though seller pays carriage to destination? Yes.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q06 · Demurrage versus off-hire
September question bank: p. 30. • Demurrage: agreed liquidated amount under a voyage charter for delay beyond allowed laytime in loading/discharging, subject to charterparty exceptions and terms. • Off-hire: under a time charter, charterer may suspend hire for specified events depriving use of the vessel (e.g. machinery breakdown) according to the exact off-hire clause. • Chief Engineer must accurately document machinery stoppages, cause, duration, work done, spares and repairs; immediately advise Master and company; avoid speculative admissions of charterparty liability. Authority: Charterparty clauses and applicable governing law; not a universal IMO regulation. Examiner cross-question: Is every machinery failure automatically off-hire? No, wording and causal effect matter.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q07 · Explain demise clause, proximity clause and Himalaya clause
September question bank: p. 17 and 34. • Demise/identity-of-carrier clause attempts to identify the contractual carrier under a chartered ship B/L. Its legal effectiveness depends on the B/L, signatures and applicable law. • A proximity clause may authorize diversion or discharge at an alternative reasonably safe/near port on contractually defined contingencies. Always examine exact wording. • Himalaya clause seeks to extend contractual defences/limitations to servants, agents or independent contractors such as stevedores, subject to applicable law and clause wording. • Do not treat any of these clauses as an automatic override of mandatory cargo-carriage legislation. Authority: B/L/charterparty contract law; Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 2025 where applicable. Examiner cross-question: Does Himalaya clause protect every contractor automatically? No.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q08 · What is cargo liquefaction and what should the ship do?
September question bank: p. 2 and 35. • Cargo liquefaction is loss of shear strength in susceptible moisture-bearing solid bulk cargo under cyclic ship motion, permitting cargo shift and potential stability loss. • IMSBC Code Group A cargoes are liable to liquefy; shipper provides proper cargo declaration, moisture content and Transportable Moisture Limit (TML) certificate as applicable. • Chief Engineer ensures ballast, bilge, pumping, power and monitoring are reliable, supports Master/Chief Officer in cargo safety, and reports abnormal soundings/list/structural issues. • Never load Group A cargo with moisture content exceeding TML, subject to specific Code provisions. Stop and escalate doubtful cargo documentation or wet cargo observations. Authority: SOLAS VI/2; mandatory IMSBC Code, sections 4, 7 and relevant cargo schedules. Examiner cross-question: Difference between TML and moisture content? TML is safe carriage limit; MC is actual tested water content.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q09 · VGM: what are the two methods?
September question bank: p. 34. • Verified Gross Mass is obtained by (Method 1) weighing the packed container using calibrated and certified equipment, or (Method 2) weighing all packages, cargo, pallets, dunnage and securing materials, then adding container tare using an approved certified method. • Shipper must provide signed VGM in shipping document sufficiently in advance for stowage planning; without VGM the packed container shall not be loaded. • Chief Engineer may support reliable shipboard equipment where relevant but responsibility for VGM rests on shipper, not Chief Engineer. Authority: SOLAS VI/2 paragraphs 4-6; IMO MSC.1/Circ.1475. Examiner cross-question: Is shipper mass declaration without verification enough? No.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q10 · Explain FAL Convention and Maritime Single Window
September question bank: p. 33. • FAL Convention simplifies formalities, documentary requirements and procedures for the arrival, stay and departure of ships in international traffic. • Electronic exchange of required declarations became mandatory in 2019; Maritime Single Window became mandatory from 1 January 2024, through resolution FAL.14(46). • Single Window means required data can be submitted electronically to an integrated entry point, allowing reuse across relevant public authorities. • Chief Engineer provides timely accurate fuel, waste, equipment and pollution-related information needed for clearance and reception facilities; collaborate with Master/agent. Authority: IMO FAL Convention official page, resolution FAL.14(46). Examiner cross-question: Does the MSW remove the duty to provide required information? No - it streamlines submission.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q11 · Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 2025: what changed in India?
September question bank: p. 12 and 34. • India enacted the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 2025 (Act 19 of 2025), and it came into force on 10 September 2025 under S.O. 4082(E). • Section 3 gives effect to the applicable rules in the Schedule in relation to carriage by sea from an Indian port to another port in or outside India; the Schedule contains carrier duties, liabilities and rights. • Under Schedule Article III(6), notice of apparent cargo damage is due before or at removal; concealed damage ordinarily within three days. Suit ordinarily within one year of delivery/due delivery, subject to allowed extensions and the statutory provision for a further court-allowed period up to three months. • Do not call the new Indian statute the 1925 Act, or assume every international cargo dispute has the same governing law. Authority: India Code Act 19 of 2025; DGS Gazette commencement S.O.4082(E). Examiner cross-question: Is three-day notice the lawsuit limitation? No - separate from one-year suit period.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q12 · UNCLOS: flag State duties and nationality of ship
September question bank: p. 12. • Article 91: every State fixes conditions for granting nationality, registration and right to fly its flag; there must be a genuine link between State and ship. • Article 92: ship generally sails under the flag of one State and, save exceptional cases provided by treaty or convention, is subject to its exclusive jurisdiction on the high seas. • Article 94: flag State must effectively exercise jurisdiction and control over administrative, technical and social matters including register, safety, seaworthiness, manning, training and investigations as required. • Chief Engineer complies with flag requirements, maintains machinery and statutory certification, informs Master/company of defects and participates in mandatory reporting and investigations. Authority: UNCLOS Articles 91-94. Examiner cross-question: Does class society replace flag State responsibility? No.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q13 · What are a port of refuge and general average?
September question bank: p. 2. • Port of refuge: port/place sought to safeguard ship, crew, cargo or environment during serious emergency, generally decided by Master/owner with relevant coastal/port authorities. • General Average: extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure intentionally and reasonably made for the common safety in a common maritime adventure, apportioned among contributing interests under the applicable contract/rules. • Particular Average: partial loss falling on a particular insured interest, rather than shared across the venture as GA. • Chief Engineer: document casualty timeline, repair requisitions, fuel consumption, machinery condition and costs; preserve evidence for claims and adjusters. Authority: York-Antwerp Rules if incorporated; insurance/adjustment contract law. Examiner cross-question: Is every dry-dock bill GA? No - only qualifying extraordinary expenditure.

Source: MEO_Class_I_September_Oral_Batch_07_UNCLOS_Cargo_Commercial.pdf

O07-Q14 · Unseaworthy versus unsafe ship
September question bank: p. 13 and 33. • Unseaworthiness concerns whether the vessel is reasonably fit for the contemplated voyage and cargo, including hull, machinery, manning and equipment. • Unsafe ship is a statutory/regulatory safety concept and may cover hazards to persons or environment even if a particular issue is not framed as contractual seaworthiness. The applicable Indian statutory definition and enforcement criteria must be consulted. • As CE: identify defect; record and report to Master/company/DPA; perform risk assessment; make safe; seek class/flag/port approval where needed; do not operate or conceal a condition jeopardizing safety. Authority: Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 2025 Schedule Art III(1) on due diligence; applicable Indian Merchant Shipping law and SOLAS/ISM. Examiner cross-question: Does a valid certificate guarantee a ship is safe? No. REFERENCE LINKS (AUTHORITATIVE FIRST) United Nations - UNCLOS: https://www.un.org/depts/los/convention_agreements/texts/unclos/unclos_e.pdf IMO - FAL Convention: https://www.imo.org/en/about/conventions/pages/convention-on-facilitation-of-international-maritime-traffic-%28fal%29.aspx IMO - VGM: https://www.imo.org/en/ourwork/safety/pages/verification-of-the-gross-mass.aspx ICC - Incoterms: https://iccwbo.org/business-solutions/incoterms-rules/ India Code - Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 2025: https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/21917/1/A2025-19.pdf DGS - Act commencement notification: https://www.dgshipping.gov.in/WriteReadData/News/202509110346292845419CarriageofGoods bySeaAct2025-NotificationtoputActintoforcewef100925.pdf Exam caution: Non-statutory expressions such as stale B/L, demise clause, proximity clause and charterparty off-hire depend on the actual governing commercial contract. Do not manufacture an IMO regulation for them.

Survey · Classification (1)
T07-Q21 · Define substantial corrosion in the context of ship structure and classification requirements. What criteria are used to assess substantial corrosion during inspections? Discuss the steps taken to address substantial corrosion during a renewal survey, including inspection techniques, repair methods, and the role of the classification society in ensuring compliance.
DG SHIPPING / MMD INDIA | MEO CLASS I - ENGINEERING MANAGEMENT July 2025 - Q7: Substantial Corrosion Question: Define substantial corrosion in the context of ship structure and classification requirements. What criteria are used to assess substantial corrosion during inspections? Discuss the steps taken to address substantial corrosion during a renewal survey, including inspection techniques, repair methods, and the role of the classification society in ensuring compliance. Answer for written examination: Corrected and reorganised from the four-page handwritten notes. References are numbered and updated to October 2026. 1. Meaning and applicable regulations Substantial corrosion is a corrosion condition in which assessment of the corrosion pattern indicates wastage exceeding 75% of the allowable corrosion margin, but still within the acceptable limit. This is not 75% loss of the original plate thickness. [2, 3] For a ship built to the IACS Common Structural Rules (CSR), the measured thickness lies in the interval from the renewal thickness, tren, up to tren + 0.5 mm. The renewal thickness is the minimum permissible thickness; steel measured below tren requires renewal/approved corrective action under the applicable class rules. [2, 3] Statutory/class basis: SOLAS regulation XI-1/2 makes the 2011 ESP Code mandatory for applicable bulk carriers and oil tankers (normally 500 GT and above). Apply the current ESP Code, applicable IACS UR Z10-series, class rules and approved renewal criteria. [1-3] Simple numerical illustration If original thickness = 12.0 mm and minimum permissible thickness = 10.0 mm, allowable wastage = 2.0 mm. At an actual measured thickness of 10.3 mm, wastage is 1.7 mm or 85% of the allowable margin: it is substantial corrosion but is not automatically beyond the renewal limit. At 9.8 mm, the renewal limit has been exceeded. This is an illustration; actual scantling limits are decided from the ship-specific class criteria. 2. Criteria used by the surveyor to assess corrosion 1. Visual and coating condition: inspect ballast tanks, cargo holds/tanks, shell plating, deck, bottom structures, longitudinals, frames, stiffeners, bulkheads, welds and joints for scale, rust, coating failure, pitting, grooving, edge corrosion, cracks, buckling and deformation. POOR coating means general coating breakdown over 20% or more of the area, or hard scale over 10% or more. This prompts further examination; it does not by itself establish substantial corrosion. [2] 2. Thickness measurement (TM): use calibrated ultrasonic gauging and compare the actual measured thickness with the as-built/original thickness and the applicable allowable or renewal thickness. Evaluate the extent, severity and distribution of corrosion, including local pitting and grooving. [2, 3] 3. Survey/history evidence: review earlier TM reports, coating condition, repair history, structural plans, class conditions and locations known to experience rapid wastage. Record suspect areas (locations already showing substantial corrosion or considered by the surveyor prone to rapid wastage) and critical structural areas. [2] 4. Structural significance: determine whether reduced scantlings, fractures, buckling or distortion compromise hull-girder/local strength, or watertight/weathertight integrity. Such defects may require prompt repair even apart from the nominal thickness threshold. [2] Verified answer | Original handwritten scan: 4 pages | Updated Oct 2026 Page 1 3. Procedure during a renewal (special) survey A. Before starting the survey - preparation and safety 1. The owner and classification society prepare/agreed a written survey programme using previous survey and TM records; identify suspect/critical areas and agree the scope of overall survey, close-up survey, TM and tank testing. Conduct the survey-planning meeting with the attending surveyor and TM firm. [2] 2. Arrange tank cleaning, sufficient lighting, safe access (scaffolds/staging, ladders or other accepted means), ventilation, safe enclosed-space entry, atmosphere testing, permit to work, rescue arrangements and communication. Any remotely operated tool is used only where accepted under the applicable survey arrangements. [2] B. Examination and gauging 3. Perform the overall survey to determine general hull condition, followed by close-up surveys of required and suspect/critical areas. Close-up survey means details are within close visual range, normally within reach of hand. [2] 4. Have the qualified, certified thickness-measurement firm carry out calibrated UT gauging simultaneously with close-up surveys where required, under the control/witness of the class/Administration surveyor. Mark or sketch measurement positions on plans so the findings are traceable. [2, 4] 5. Compare each relevant measurement with the approved acceptance/renewal criteria; calculate percentage wastage where appropriate; distinguish general corrosion, pitting, edge and grooving corrosion. Where substantial corrosion is detected, extend the close-up examination and TM to the extent prescribed by the ESP Code/class rules before the survey is credited complete. [2, 3] 6. If cracks or suspected structural defects are found, apply further NDT (e.g. magnetic-particle, dye-penetrant or ultrasonic flaw detection) as directed by the surveyor. Report all unacceptable findings immediately. [2] 4. Repair methods, monitoring and acceptance Condition found Correct action Steel below approved renewal limit; Carry out prompt, thorough permanent repair to the surveyor's unacceptable fracture, buckling, satisfaction, usually by cropping and renewing plating, stiffeners or structural grooving or strength/watertight members. Use accepted material, approved repair details/welding procedures, defect suitable NDT and required tests. [2, 3] Substantial corrosion, but thickness Area is identified, documented and kept under enhanced examination / remains within acceptance limits and thickness monitoring. Corrosion control may include cleaning and restoring no unsafe structural defect an approved hard protective coating; the class surveyor decides permitted follow-up. Coating alone is not a universal substitute for steel renewal. [2, 3] Isolated defect where permanent A temporary repair may be accepted only in the limited circumstances repair is not immediately practicable provided by applicable class rules, with a condition of class and specified deadline; unsafe service must not continue. [2] CSR bulk-carrier qualification: For identified substantial-corrosion areas in certain CSR bulk-carrier arrangements, prescribed annual monitoring may be by maintaining an approved coating in GOOD condition or by annual thickness measurements. This ship-type-specific provision should not be applied as a general exemption to all tankers. [3] 5. Role of classification society / authorised RO 1. Survey planning: agree the renewal-survey programme; study previous findings and plans; select suspect/critical locations and approve/require further examination as necessary. Verified answer | Original handwritten scan: 4 pages | Updated Oct 2026 Page 2 2. Independent verification: attend the survey, control/witness gauging as required, check calibration and the qualifications/certification of the TM firm, request additional readings and evaluate remaining scantlings. [2, 4] 3. Repair acceptance: apply class and ESP acceptance criteria, approve the repair approach, inspect completed steelwork and tests, and require corrective action or impose class conditions where permitted. [2] 4. Reporting and follow-up: review the signed TM report and countersign it, record corrosion/repairs/suspect areas in the survey records and Condition Evaluation Report / Executive Hull Summary; schedule future checks and credit the survey only when applicable requirements are met. [2] 5. Flag-state role: when undertaking statutory surveys as a recognised organisation, act within the flag Administration's authorisation, including applicable reporting and certification requirements. 6. Chief Engineer / ship-management responsibilities • Notify the Master, superintendent/owner and class surveyor promptly of significant corrosion, cracking or buckling; protect affected spaces and equipment as necessary and assess operational risks. • Supply historical reports, original thickness/scantling drawings, corrosion-control records and a safe, clean, gas-tested, accessible survey space; coordinate the gauging/repair contractors and safety permits. • Implement class-approved steelwork and coating repairs, preserve photographs/sketches/TM readings, verify test results and close out corrective actions in the planned-maintenance/SMS records. • Monitor identified suspect locations at the intervals imposed by the survey programme or class; never conceal defects with coating before their extent and remaining thickness are established. 7. Important corrections made to the handwritten answer Original shorthand / potential Correct exam wording error "75% corrosion" or "75% loss of plate" More than 75% of allowable corrosion margin, not 75% loss of nominal steel thickness. "Poor coating = substantial corrosion" POOR coating (20% breakdown or 10% hard scale) is a separate indicator; confirm steel wastage by TM. "Any substantial corrosion needs Steel below renewal/acceptance limit, or structurally unsafe defects, needs immediate steel renewal" approved repair. Substantial but acceptable wastage triggers additional examination and future monitoring. "Apply epoxy instead of permanent Epoxy coating may control corrosion in eligible and accepted cases; it does repair" not restore lost steel scantling. "All coating-restored areas may skip Exceptions depend on ship type, CSR applicability, class/ESP provisions and annual TM" acceptance of coating condition; not a general permission. "Argonaut TM" software is prescribed No particular commercial software name is imposed by the cited provisions. Use a traceable, approved TM report format. 8. Current regulatory update - 1 January 2026 Resolution MSC.553(108) amended the 2011 ESP Code procedures concerning approval/certification of firms engaged in hull thickness measurement, including auditing by the Administration to verify that the firm is properly organised, managed and competent. The amendment entered into force on 1 January 2026. For MEO Class I answers, mention the Administration/authorised RO framework rather than describing these firms as informal/private gauging companies. [1, 4, 5] Verified answer | Original handwritten scan: 4 pages | Updated Oct 2026 Page 3 9. Conclusion - examiner-friendly Substantial corrosion is an early warning stage before the renewal limit is exceeded. The surveyor assesses it by overall and close-up survey, calibrated ultrasonic thickness measurements, coating assessment and previous survey history. A renewal survey therefore requires planned access and survey, extended measurements in affected areas, approved steel repair whenever limits or integrity demand it, corrosion prevention and future monitoring, all verified and recorded by class / the Administration. 10. Quick revision - 20-25 minute exam writing plan Suggested sequence (not an official marking scheme): 3 lines of definition and regulatory basis; 4 clear inspection criteria; 6 numbered renewal-survey actions; separate steel repair versus accepted monitoring; 4 specific duties of class and a one-line conclusion. Reading from gauge / survey Correct interpretation and action More than 75% of allowable margin Record substantial corrosion if still within accepted scantling limits; used (general definition) expand measurements and apply required follow-up. CSR: tren to tren + 0.5 mm Substantial-corrosion range; confirm extent and class-prescribed survey/repair or monitoring action. Below tren, or otherwise unsafe structural Class-approved repair/renewal and tests before service in accordance condition with class requirements. Memory chain: PLAN - ACCESS - INSPECT - GAUGE - COMPARE - EXTEND - REPAIR/MONITOR - RECORD - FOLLOW UP. This follows the action verbs in the question and covers the Chief Engineer and class responsibilities. References (primary rules and official current IMO material, accessed October 2026) [1] IMO - 2011 ESP Code; SOLAS XI-1/2, with January 2026 supplement. [2] IACS UR Z10.4 (Hull Surveys of Double-Hull Oil Tankers): definitions, repairs, special/annual surveys, gauging and reporting. [3] IACS UR Z10.2 (Hull Surveys of Bulk Carriers): extent of gauging and CSR coating/annual survey qualification. [4] IMO Resolution MSC.553(108), adopted 23 May 2024, effective 1 January 2026. [5] IMO - Shipping rules in force from 1 January 2026 (official overview). [6] IACS - Common Structural Rules for Bulk Carriers and Oil Tankers. Scope note: This is a corrected and expanded typed answer based on the supplied handwritten four-page scan, not a line-for-line certified transcription. Use the ship-specific ESP annex, class rules and flag instructions when deciding particular survey or repair requirements. Verified answer | Original handwritten scan: 4 pages | Updated Oct 2026 Page 4

Marine Insurance (1)
INSURANCE-MASTER-2026 · Marine insurance master chapter: 16 question families (GA, H&M, P&I, losses, claims)
MEO CLASS I - ENGINEERING MANAGEMENT MARINE INSURANCE - MASTER CHAPTER Past-paper question families | Marine Insurance Act, 1963 | York-Antwerp Rules 2016 | H&M; / P&I; | Chief Engineer claims practice Scope. This chapter consolidates the recurring Marine Insurance questions seen in recent Indian MEO Class I Engineering Management papers into one exam-ready reference. It deliberately goes deeper into General Average and the Marine Insurance Act, 1963, because these are repeatedly examined and frequently answered too generically. Primary authority hierarchy: Marine Insurance Act, 1963 (India Code); York-Antwerp Rules 2016 with the 2022 Rule XXI technical amendment (CMI); DG Shipping current competency syllabus; Institute Hull Clauses concepts and P&I;/claims guidance where relevant. MASTER QUESTION MAP 1. Purpose, nature and types of marine insurance 2. Insurable interest and indemnity 3. Utmost good faith / disclosure / misrepresentation 4. Warranties, deviation and delay 5. Proximate cause (causa proxima) and perils insured 6. Particular Average, partial loss and salvage charges 7. General Average - Marine Insurance Act s.66 + York-Antwerp Rules 8. GA machinery-damage/refloating scenario and Port of Refuge 9. Actual Total Loss, Missing Ship and Constructive Total Loss 10. Sue & Labour, subrogation, contribution and under-insurance 11. H&M; insurance and practical Chief Engineer claim procedure 12. Delayed discovery / unrepaired damage 13. Running Down / collision liability and P&I; overlap 14. New Jason Clause and GA security 15. P&I; versus H&M; and integrated casualty response 16. Major casualty scenario + rapid revision MEO Class I - Marine Insurance Master Chapter | 2026 Page 1 1. PURPOSE, NATURE & TYPES OF MARINE INSURANCE Past-paper family: Why is marine insurance required? State different marine insurance policies and explain their salient liabilities/exclusions. Marine insurance transfers defined maritime financial risks from the assured to an insurer in consideration of premium. It does not remove the owner's statutory or contractual responsibility; it finances covered consequences subject to policy terms, exclusions, deductibles, limits and warranties. Marine Insurance Act, 1963: the Act codifies Indian marine-insurance law. For exam answers, use the Act to support principles and loss classification, then use the actual policy/Institute Clauses for what is contractually insured. Cover Primary interest / examples Hull & Machinery Physical loss/damage to ship and machinery from insured perils; specified collision liability depending on hull wording. Cargo Cargo owner's insurable interest in goods; commonly written under Institute Cargo Clauses or equivalent terms. P&I; Specified third-party/legal liabilities and associated costs: crew, cargo liability, pollution, wreck, collision/FFO etc., subject to Club Rules. War Risks War/hostilities, mines, capture/seizure and related excluded war perils under separate terms. Loss of Hire Loss of earning capacity/hire during insured off-hire period following qualifying physical damage, subject to waiting period/limits. Freight / Disbursements / Separate financial interests exposed to marine casualty, subject to policy wording. Increased Value Builders' Risks Construction, launching, trials and delivery risks for a newbuilding. Policy forms: The DG Shipping syllabus specifically expects voyage policy, time policy and floating policy. A valued policy agrees the insured value; an unvalued policy leaves insurable value to be established under the Act. 2. INSURABLE INTEREST & PRINCIPLE OF INDEMNITY Past-paper family: Explain principles of marine insurance / insurable interest. Insurable interest. The assured must stand in a legal or equitable relationship to the marine adventure such that he benefits from its safety/due arrival or is prejudiced by its loss, damage, detention or liability. Shipowner, mortgagee, cargo owner, freight interest and liability exposure can each create distinct insurable interests. Indemnity. Marine insurance is fundamentally compensatory: the assured should recover the insured measure of loss, not make a profit from casualty. Valuation, policy limits, deductibles, under-insurance, contribution and subrogation implement this principle. Exam link: A single casualty may affect several separately insured interests: hull damage, cargo damage, freight, GA contribution, loss of hire and third-party liability. Do not call them one insurance claim. 3. UTMOST GOOD FAITH, DISCLOSURE & MISREPRESENTATION Past-paper family: Explain Uberrimae Fidei. What must be disclosed? What need not be disclosed? Consequence of non-disclosure? MEO Class I - Marine Insurance Master Chapter | 2026 Page 2 Traditional marine insurance law imposes a high duty of good faith. Material facts are those capable of influencing a prudent insurer's judgment in fixing premium or deciding whether/how to take the risk. Examples include casualty/claims history, trading pattern, material defects, class status and unusual risk information known to the assured. Exam method: define material circumstance -> state duty before contract/renewal/variation -> distinguish representation from concealment -> explain that the legal remedy depends on governing law and policy. Avoid writing the outdated universal formula 'any non-disclosure automatically voids the policy from inception' without qualification. Information normally not requiring separate disclosure includes matters already known or presumed known to the insurer, matters waived, and matters rendered unnecessary by an express/implied warranty, subject to the governing statutory/policy framework. 4. WARRANTIES, DEVIATION & DELAY Past-paper family: How many types of warranties are there? Give examples with H&M.; Explain deviation. Warranties: traditional classification is express and implied. Express warranties are written/ incorporated contractual promises; implied marine warranties historically include legality and, in voyage-policy contexts, seaworthiness concepts. Modern policy wording and governing law may alter consequences, so quote the policy where a practical claim is involved. Deviation: an unjustified departure from the contemplated voyage can affect cover. The Act separately addresses deviation, change of voyage and delay. Excused deviations include circumstances authorised by policy/law, safety necessity, saving human life, obtaining medical aid, and other legally recognised causes. Once the excuse ends, voyage should be resumed with reasonable dispatch. MEO point: distinguish navigational deviation under marine insurance from 'reasonable deviation' under carriage-of-goods law; they arise under different legal frameworks. 5. PROXIMATE CAUSE (CAUSA PROXIMA) & PERILS Past-paper family: Explain causa proxima. What perils of the sea are covered? How can excluded/additional perils be insured? Proximate cause means the dominant, effective cause of the loss, not necessarily the event nearest in time. The claim analysis asks whether the proximate cause is an insured peril, an excluded peril, or an uninsured cause. Perils of the seas means fortuitous accidents/casualties of the sea, not the ordinary action of winds and waves. Hull clauses typically enumerate insured perils and exclusions. Machinery damage may be covered under specified clauses only if the policy requirements and due-diligence conditions are met. Example: Water contaminates generator lubricating oil, LO pressure falls and bearings fail. Do not stop at 'machinery damage'. Establish the chain: source of water -> contamination -> pressure loss -> alarm/protective response -> bearing failure -> whether insured peril/latent defect/negligence exclusion/due diligence wording applies. Additional perils may be insured by endorsement/additional premium or broader Institute clause wording. War/strike risks are commonly separated from ordinary marine perils. 6. PARTICULAR AVERAGE, PARTIAL LOSS & SALVAGE CHARGES Past-paper family: Explain Particular Average and General Average; distinguish them. MEO Class I - Marine Insurance Master Chapter | 2026 Page 3 Marine Insurance Act s.64: a Particular Average loss is a partial loss of the subject matter insured caused by an insured peril and which is not a General Average loss. It is borne initially by the owner of the damaged interest and claimed from that interest's insurer if covered. Examples: collision damage to insured hull; seawater damage to a particular cargo parcel; machinery damage from an insured peril where there was no common-safety GA act. Marine Insurance Act s.65: salvage charges are charges recoverable under maritime law by a salvor independently of contract. The Act distinguishes these from expenses incurred by the assured/agents to avert loss (Sue & Labour) and from GA treatment. Core distinction: PA = accidental partial loss affecting a particular insured interest. GA = extraordinary intentional sacrifice/expenditure reasonably made for common safety in a common maritime adventure, followed by contribution among saved interests. MEO Class I - Marine Insurance Master Chapter | 2026 Page 4 7. GENERAL AVERAGE - SECTION 66 + YORK-ANTWERP RULES Past-paper family: Define General Average. State circumstances for GA, role of Average Adjuster, entitlement/contribution and treatment by insurers. Marine Insurance Act, 1963 - Section 66 is the statutory insurance anchor. S.66(1): a GA loss is caused by or directly consequential on a GA act. It includes a GA expenditure as well as a GA sacrifice. S.66(2): there is a GA act where an extraordinary sacrifice or expenditure is voluntarily and reasonably made/incurred in time of peril for the purpose of preserving the property imperilled in the common adventure. S.66(3): where there is GA loss, the party on whom it falls is entitled, subject to legal conditions, to a rateable contribution from the other interests; this is the general average contribution. S.66(4): where the assured incurs GA expenditure, he may recover from the insurer in respect of the proportion falling on him; for GA sacrifice he may recover the whole loss from his insurer without first enforcing contribution against others, subject to policy/Act. S.66(5)-(6): the insurer's liability for GA contribution is linked to insured value versus contributory value; under-insurance can leave part of the contribution for the assured. Five elements to write for a GA act: • Common maritime adventure: ship, cargo and usually freight/other contributing interests are exposed together. • Real peril: danger must be substantial; certainty of destruction is not required, but mere ordinary voyage expense is insufficient. • Extraordinary act: beyond ordinary operational expenditure. • Voluntary and intentional sacrifice/expenditure: deliberate common-safety decision, not accidental damage itself. • Reasonable and for common safety: the act must be reasonably directed at preserving the imperilled property. YAR Rule A: expresses the classic GA test. Rule Paramount: no sacrifice/expenditure is allowed unless reasonably made/incurred. Rule B: extends treatment to certain tug/tow common-peril situations. Rule C: only losses/damages/expenses that are the direct consequence of the GA act are allowed; environmental damage and certain consequential losses are excluded. Rule D: rights to GA contribution are not defeated merely because the event resulted from fault, but remedies/defences concerning that fault remain. Rule E: party claiming in GA bears burden of showing allowance. Rule F: substituted expenses may be allowed up to the GA expense avoided. Rule G: adjustment is based on values at termination of adventure, subject to the Rules. Average Adjuster: gathers GA security and evidence, determines allowances, values sacrifices, establishes contributory values, applies YAR/contract/law, calculates each interest's rateable contribution and issues the GA adjustment. The adjuster is not the person who 'declares' the casualty; owner/master commonly initiates GA handling and appoints an adjuster. GA SECURITY & SETTLEMENT CHAIN • Casualty creates common peril -> Master/owner takes common-safety action. • Owner/insurers consider GA and appoint Average Adjuster. MEO Class I - Marine Insurance Master Chapter | 2026 Page 5 • Cargo interests provide GA Bond; cargo insurers commonly provide GA Guarantee. Cash deposit may be requested where security is inadequate. • Ship/cargo documents, invoices, survey evidence and values are collected. • Adjuster decides GA allowances under incorporated YAR and determines contributory values. • GA percentage/contributions are calculated and final adjustment issued. • Insurers indemnify their assureds according to policy and Marine Insurance Act treatment. Important: 'Declaring GA' does not make an expense allowable. Every item must independently satisfy the applicable GA rules. 8. RULE VII MACHINERY DAMAGE + PORT OF REFUGE Repeated scenario: Ship grounds; main engine is repeatedly worked ahead/astern to refloat; bearings/engine are damaged. Is it GA? YAR Rule VII - exact exam logic: If the ship is ashore and in a position of peril, machinery/boiler damage caused while endeavouring to refloat is allowed in GA when it is shown that machinery was worked with the actual intention of floating the ship for common safety at the risk of such damage. If the ship is afloat, loss/damage from working propulsion machinery/boilers is not allowed as GA under Rule VII. Therefore: Grounded + common peril + intentional refloating effort + machinery worked at recognised risk + causal machinery damage = potentially allowable GA. Ordinary breakdown while afloat = not Rule VII GA. Rule VIII: extra costs of lightening an ashore ship, lighter hire/reshipping and consequential property loss can be GA where conditions are met. Port of Refuge - YAR Rule X: where entry/return to a port/place is necessary for common safety because of accident, sacrifice or extraordinary circumstances, expenses of entering and corresponding departure with original cargo may be GA. If repairs cannot be done there and a second refuge is necessary, specified removal/towage/temporary repair costs may also qualify. YAR Rule XI: deals with wages/maintenance and other expenses at a port of refuge in specified circumstances. Do not write 'all Port of Refuge expenses are GA'. Each expense must satisfy Rule X/XI or another allowance. 9. TOTAL LOSS, MISSING SHIP & CONSTRUCTIVE TOTAL LOSS Past-paper family: Explain Total Loss and Constructive Total Loss. What is abandonment? S.56: loss may be total or partial; total loss may be Actual Total Loss (ATL) or Constructive Total Loss (CTL). S.57 ATL: broadly arises where subject matter is destroyed, ceases to be a thing of the kind insured, or the assured is irretrievably deprived of it. Formal notice of abandonment is unnecessary for ATL. S.58 Missing ship: where ship is missing and no news is received after a reasonable time, ATL may be presumed. S.60 CTL: exists where subject matter is reasonably abandoned because ATL appears unavoidable or preservation/recovery/repair would exceed the relevant value test under the Act. For ship damage, compare cost of recovery/repair against value as prescribed; policy wording can modify practical treatment. Notice of abandonment: CTL traditionally requires timely notice where the assured elects to abandon to insurer, unless notice is excused. Do not confuse physical abandonment of a casualty with the legal MEO Class I - Marine Insurance Master Chapter | 2026 Page 6 insurance notice. Exam example: A badly damaged vessel that still physically exists is not automatically CTL. Establish recovery/repair economics and statutory/policy test. 10. SUE & LABOUR, SUBROGATION, CONTRIBUTION & UNDER-INSURANCE S.78 Sue and Labour. Where the policy contains a suing and labouring clause, engagement is supplementary to the insurance contract. Reasonable expenses incurred to avert/minimise a covered loss may be recoverable under the clause. GA losses/contributions and salvage charges as defined by the Act are not recoverable under Sue & Labour. The assured/agents have a duty to take reasonable loss-minimisation measures. S.79 Subrogation. After paying an insured loss, insurer steps into the assured's rights/remedies to the extent provided by the Act: after total loss, insurer may take over remaining interest and rights; after partial loss, insurer does not acquire title to remaining property but is subrogated to rights/remedies to the extent indemnified. S.80 Contribution. In double insurance, insurers contribute rateably; an insurer paying more than its proportion can recover contribution from co-insurers. S.81 Under-insurance. Where insured amount is below insurable value/policy valuation, assured is treated as own insurer for uninsured balance. This is highly relevant when calculating recovery of GA contribution. 11. H&M; CLAIM - CHIEF ENGINEER'S PRACTICAL PROCEDURE Past-paper family: Machinery casualty occurs. As Chief Engineer, what actions and documents are required for marine insurance surveyor? • Make machinery/ship safe; isolate damaged equipment; prevent consequential loss and pollution. • Inform Master/company immediately; notify Class/Flag/authorities where required and insurer/broker as directed. • Preserve evidence. Do not discard failed bearings, filters, seals, oil samples, electronic data or alarm histories before survey/authorisation. • Record exact chronology in Engine Log Book and defect/casualty reports: loads, pressures, temperatures, alarms, trips, actions and communications. • Secure PMS history, last overhaul/measurement records, maker recommendations, class survey records and relevant work permits. • Collect LO/FO/cooling-water analysis, purifier records, tank soundings, contamination tests and laboratory reports. • Photograph damage before dismantling and during opening-up; identify parts and measurements. • Prepare preliminary RCA without prematurely admitting negligence; distinguish immediate cause, root cause and contributing factors. • Obtain maker/service reports, repair specification, quotations, invoices, spares/labour records and off-hire/delay records where relevant. • Co-operate with insurer's surveyor, Class and P&I;/H&M; representatives; agree scope of damage and preserve recovery rights against manufacturers/repairers/third parties. MEO Class I - Marine Insurance Master Chapter | 2026 Page 7 Claim file: policy/certificate details; casualty statement; logs; photos; survey reports; class recommendations; repair bills; spares invoices; crew overtime where claimable; tow/salvage invoices; GA documents if relevant; correspondence; third-party evidence; proof of payment. Golden rule: mitigate the loss but preserve evidence. Insurance investigation is causation-driven; a beautifully repaired machine with destroyed evidence can make the claim harder to prove. MEO Class I - Marine Insurance Master Chapter | 2026 Page 8 12. DELAYED DISCOVERY & UNREPAIRED DAMAGE Past-paper family: Damage from an insured incident is discovered at later drydock. Is it admissible? If not repaired, can owner claim? Do not answer by calendar alone. Establish: casualty date/policy period; when damage could reasonably have been discovered; prompt-notice obligations; causal link between insured peril and later-found damage; whether deterioration occurred after casualty; survey evidence; limitation/time-bar provisions; and policy wording. Unrepaired damage: Some hull forms provide a measure for unrepaired damage, often linked to reasonable depreciation in market value caused by insured damage at policy expiry, with restrictions and no recovery where subsequent total loss intervenes, depending on clause wording. Therefore 'Class does not require repair' does not automatically mean either zero claim or full repair-cost claim. 13. RUNNING DOWN / COLLISION LIABILITY & P&I; OVERLAP Past-paper family: Explain Running Down Clause / 3/4 Collision Clause and treatment under H&M; and P&I.; The Running Down/Collision Liability Clause is the hull policy's liability cover for collision with another vessel. Traditional English Institute Time Clauses are commonly taught with three-fourths collision liability under hull insurance, historically leaving a residual portion for P&I.; This is not universal: modern policies may insure four-fourths or use other structures. Exam sequence: identify collision liability -> read actual hull collision clause -> identify exclusions (e.g. certain pollution, wreck, cargo/person liabilities depending on wording) -> test residual liability against P&I; Club Rules -> apply deductible/limits. Do not write: 'H&M; always pays 3/4 and P&I; always pays 1/4.' State that this is the traditional clause structure and actual allocation depends on policy/entry. 14. NEW JASON CLAUSE + GENERAL AVERAGE SECURITY Past-paper family: What is New Jason Clause and how does it protect shipowner in GA? The New Jason Clause is principally associated with contracts exposed to US cargo law. It preserves the carrier/shipowner's contractual right to GA contribution from cargo where the casualty resulted from negligence or other cause for which the carrier is legally exempt from cargo liability, provided the clause conditions are satisfied. It should be linked in the answer to GA contribution, not described as an insurance policy. GA Bond: undertaking by cargo interest to pay contribution when adjustment is completed. GA Guarantee: commonly given by cargo insurer as security for its assured's contribution. Cash deposit: may be required where satisfactory guarantee is unavailable. Average Adjuster: calculates allowance/contribution; does not act as insurer and does not decide criminal/civil fault. 15. P&I; vs H&M; + INTEGRATED CASUALTY Point H&M; P&I; Primary subject Own ship/machinery physical interest + Itemised legal liabilities/costs arising from specified collision liability. entered ship. Typical claims Grounding/fire/collision damage to own hull; Crew, cargo liability, pollution, wreck removal, insured machinery casualty. FFO, specified collision liabilities. MEO Class I - Marine Insurance Master Chapter | 2026 Page 9 Point H&M; P&I; GA Ship's GA sacrifice/contribution commonly Not ordinary primary GA insurance; may recoverable under hull policy subject to terms. respond only where Club Rules expressly cover a related liability/unrecoverable exposure. Integrated casualty example: tanker grounds, hull breached, oil spills, tug salvage undertaken, cargo sacrificed and wreck removal later ordered. One casualty may generate H&M; hull damage, salvage/GA, cargo claims, pollution P&I;, wreck-removal P&I;, collision liability and statutory compulsory-insurance issues. Analyse each head separately. 16. MAJOR CASUALTY MODEL + 16-MARK ANSWER BLUEPRINTS Scenario: VLCC suffers explosion/hull damage and later sinks during salvage. Discuss protection under marine insurance and international liability regimes. • H&M;: insured physical damage; causation/peril; repair vs total/CTL; sue & labour/salvage treatment; policy exclusions. • Salvage/GA: determine whether salvage award/SCOPIC, GA sacrifice/expenditure and YAR allowances apply; do not double count. • P&I;: third-party injury, cargo liability, pollution, wreck removal and other itemised liabilities subject to Club Rules. • Oil pollution: identify whether tanker persistent-oil cargo brings CLC/Fund regime into play; bunker pollution regime applies according to scope and exclusions. • Wreck: Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention may create registered-owner liability/compulsory insurance where applicable. • Evidence: casualty investigation, Class, Flag/coastal reports and insurer surveys are distinct processes; preserve evidence and avoid prejudicing recovery. 16-MARK MASTER STRUCTURES Principles question: Definition -> insurable interest -> utmost good faith -> indemnity -> proximate cause -> subrogation -> contribution -> warranties -> examples. Perils question: Define peril/proximate cause -> insured hull perils -> exclusions -> due diligence -> additional perils/endorsement -> machinery example. GA question: S.66 definition/elements -> YAR A-G -> sacrifice/expenditure -> contribution -> adjuster/security -> insurer treatment -> scenario. ATL/CTL question: S.56 -> S.57 ATL -> S.58 missing ship -> S.60 CTL -> abandonment -> examples -> insurer/subrogation. CE claim question: Safety/mitigation -> notify -> preserve evidence -> logs/PMS/samples -> survey/Class -> RCA -> repair/cost records -> claim/recovery. Collision question: RDC definition -> hull wording -> traditional 3/4 structure qualification -> excluded heads -> P&I; residual -> example. January 2024 · Model Answers (9)
2024-JAN-Q01 · Uberrimae Fidei & Marine Insurance
16 MARKS Q1. Uberrimae fidei Explain the principle of “Uberrimae Fidei” in Marine Insurance. What disclosures must the insured make? Which circumstances need not be disclosed? What happens if disclosures are not made at conclusion of contract? 1. Meaning and legal basis Uberrimae fidei means utmost good faith. Marine insurance depends on truthful disclosure because the assured normally knows more about the risk than the insurer. Under section 19, Marine Insurance Act, 1963, this duty applies to both parties; failure permits the other party to avoid the contract. 2. Disclosure by the assured - section 20 Before concluding the contract, disclose every material circumstance known to the assured. This includes circumstances the assured ought to know in the ordinary course of business. Materiality concerns whether the fact would influence a prudent insurer in fixing the premium or deciding whether to accept the risk. Typical disclosures: ship identity, age, type and value; ownership and management; class status and outstanding conditions; machinery defects and unrepaired damage; casualty and claims history; intended trade, voyage and trading limits; hazardous cargoes or unusual operations; and relevant previous refusals or special insurance terms. Their materiality depends on the particular risk. 3. Circumstances not requiring disclosure - section 20(3) In the absence of inquiry: (a) facts diminishing the risk; (b) facts known or presumed known to the insurer, including common notoriety/knowledge and matters an insurer ought to know in ordinary business; (c) information waived by the insurer; and (d) facts rendered superfluous by an express or implied warranty. These exceptions do not justify an untrue answer to a specific question. 4. Agent, representations and conclusion - sections 21-23 Section 21: the placing agent must disclose material facts known to the agent, or which the agent ought to know, and relevant facts the assured must disclose, subject to the statutory exception. Section 22: material representations must be true; statements of fact must be substantially correct, while expectation or belief must be held in good faith. Section 23: the contract is concluded when the insurer accepts the proposal, whether or not the policy has yet been issued. 5. Consequence and Chief Engineer’s action Material non-disclosure or misrepresentation allows the insurer to avoid the contract; it is not merely a reason to increase premium and does not require an intentional lie in every case. Example: concealing a known crankshaft crack may materially affect underwriting. The Chief Engineer must accurately report defects, survey recommendations and repairs to the Master/company, preserve records and support the owner’s disclosure. The Chief Engineer does not independently negotiate cover unless authorized. Examiner focus: Utmost good faith; prudent-insurer test; four exceptions; sections 19-23; avoidance and practical disclosure. References: [1] Marine Insurance Act, 1963, sections 19-23.

Source: Jan2024_question_and_answer.pdf

2024-JAN-Q02 · FAL Convention & Maritime Facilitation
6 + 4 + 6 MARKS Q2. FAL Convention (a) Explain the main provisions and objectives of the FAL Convention. (6) (b) Explain its effect on efficiency and security. (4) (c) Discuss international cooperation and standardization under the Convention. (6) Question wording reconstructed from the earlier topic description. (a) Objective and principal provisions The Convention on Facilitation of International Maritime Traffic, 1965 reduces unnecessary formalities and delays associated with the arrival, stay and departure of ships on international voyages. It promotes consistent procedures for ships, cargo, passengers and crew while preserving lawful government controls. Its Annex contains Standards and Recommended Practices. Standards promote uniform requirements; Recommended Practices identify desirable measures for easier clearance. Differences from Standards are addressed through the Convention’s notification arrangements; the two categories should not be described as legally identical. The traditional seven FAL declarations are: 1 General Declaration; 2 Cargo Declaration; 3 Ship’s Stores Declaration; 4 Crew’s Effects Declaration; 5 Crew List; 6 Passenger List; and 7 Dangerous Goods Manifest. Their information is now exchanged electronically as applicable; they are not seven certificates. Other information may be required under applicable instruments. (b) Efficiency and security Efficiency: standardized data avoid repeated entry, reduce errors, allow pre-arrival processing and shorten clearance time. Better coordination between Customs, immigration, health and port authorities reduces avoidable waiting and administrative cost. Security: accurate pre-arrival information supports targeting of suspicious cargo and persons. Access control, authentication, secure transmission and an audit trail protect information. Facilitation supports lawful screening; it does not exempt the ship from SOLAS XI-2/ISPS obligations or border controls. (c) Cooperation, standardization and digital exchange Contracting Governments coordinate their agencies and cooperate with shipping companies, agents and ports. Common data meanings and electronic message formats allow different systems to exchange information. The IMO Compendium on Facilitation and Electronic Business supports this interoperability. Technical assistance and training help administrations implement compatible processes. Dates to remember: mandatory electronic exchange began on 8 April 2019. Amendments under FAL.14(46), effective 1 January 2024, made the maritime single-window approach mandatory under the Convention. Information is submitted through a common entry point and shared among relevant authorities, minimizing duplicate requests. A single window is a coordinated data system, not simply a scanned paper form sent by email. Chief Engineer’s practical contribution Provide accurate machinery, bunker, stores and waste-related information requested through the Master/company/agent. Reconcile figures with shipboard records, report errors promptly and use authorized systems. The Master/agent normally coordinates clearance; the Chief Engineer supports the technical information and readiness of machinery for the declared port schedule. Examiner focus: Objective; seven declarations; Standards versus Recommended Practices; efficiency/security; 2019 versus 2024 dates. References: [2]-[4] IMO FAL Convention and maritime single-window guidance.

Source: Jan2024_question_and_answer.pdf

2024-JAN-Q03 · CII, AER and EEOI
16 MARKS Q3. CII, AER and EEOI Explain the concept of CII rating and critically discuss AER and EEOI. What measures improve CII? Discuss CII under MARPOL, its commercial impact, shortcomings and industry remedies. Consolidated question-bank wording. 1. CII and mandatory response Carbon Intensity Indicator measures annual operational CO2 emissions relative to capacity-distance. MARPOL Annex VI, regulation 28 applies to specified ship types of 5,000 GT and above, subject to applicability provisions. Ratings are A major superior, B minor superior, C moderate, D minor inferior and E inferior. Operational data collection began in 2023; the first rating followed in 2024. An E in one year or D for three consecutive years requires a corrective-action plan within a revised SEEMP Part III, verified by the Administration/RO. Regulations 26, 27 and 28 connect SEEMP, fuel-consumption reporting and CII. Required CII becomes progressively more stringent; A-E boundaries depend on ship type and applicable rating guidelines. 2. Metrics and worked example AER = annual CO2 mass / (DWT × annual distance). EEOI = total CO2 mass / sum of (actual cargo mass × voyage distance). Use grams CO2, tonnes and nautical miles for g CO2/(t·nm). CO2 mass = sum of fuel mass × the applicable fuel carbon-conversion factor. For designated ship types, statutory CII uses GT-based cgDIST instead of DWT-based AER. Example, ignoring regulatory corrections: CO2 = 10,000 t = 1010 g; DWT = 50,000 t; distance = 40,000 nm. AER = 1010/(50,000 × 40,000) = 5.0 g CO2/(t·nm). If actual annual transport work is 1.2 × 109 tonne-nm, EEOI = 1010/(1.2 × 109) = 8.33 g CO2/(t·nm). Neither number alone determines an A-E rating. 3. Critical comparison and commercial effects AER is simpler to verify but uses carrying capacity, not actual cargo: ballast distance can improve its denominator. EEOI reflects actual transport work but depends on cargo data and utilization, often controlled by charterers. It is a voluntary operational indicator, not the universal statutory CII metric. Poor ratings may affect charterer selection, speed commitments, retrofit spending and asset attractiveness; they are not automatically a worldwide trading ban. 4. Improvement measures and limitations Optimize speed safely, trim and voyage planning; improve hull/propeller condition; maintain injection, combustion and turbocharging; reduce steam/air leaks and auxiliary loads; consider justified energy-saving retrofits. Coordinate arrival times with charterers/ports. The Chief Engineer validates fuel data, trends consumption and implements machinery measures with the Master/company. Port waiting, weather, short voyages and cargo-handling energy can distort comparisons. Use only permitted correction factors/voyage adjustments, improve data quality, agree charterparty responsibilities and examine EEOI alongside CII. Safety takes priority over a rating. Later-update note: use G1 MEPC.352(78) as amended, including MEPC.412(84); do not apply the January 2024 guideline edition uncritically to later reporting. Examiner focus: Scope; correct denominators/units; D/E trigger; practical improvement; balanced commercial criticism. References: [5]-[7] MARPOL VI/26-28; CII G1; EEOI MEPC.1/Circ.684.

Source: Jan2024_question_and_answer.pdf

2024-JAN-Q04 · Maintenance, Surveys and ISM
6 + 10 MARKS Q4. Maintenance, surveys and ISM (a) What are the essential elements of preventive maintenance onboard ships? (6) (b) Analyse the link between statutory and classification surveys of machinery/equipment and routine maintenance, and how they are effectively merged in the ship’s SMS under the ISM Code. (10) (a) Essential elements of preventive maintenance Preventive maintenance is planned work at suitable time/running-hour intervals, with condition information where appropriate, intended to reduce failures and preserve safe operation. It requires: 1. An equipment register with maker’s instructions, drawings and history. 2. Criticality assessment and realistic calendar/running-hour schedules. 3. Defined job scope, acceptance limits, responsible personnel and competent supervision. 4. Spares, tools, calibrated instruments and sufficient manpower. 5. Risk assessment, permit to work, isolation/lock-out and safe access. 6. Inspection, measurements, functional testing, records and review of recurring defects. (b) Statutory survey, class survey and routine maintenance Statutory surveys verify convention/national requirements, e.g. SOLAS safety systems and MARPOL pollution-prevention equipment, by the Administration or an authorized RO. Class surveys verify compliance with the society’s rules and conditions of class. Routine maintenance continuously preserves the condition between these independent verification events. A completed PMS job does not automatically constitute survey credit. An approved machinery/PMS survey arrangement may permit defined credit under class rules, with the required records and verification. The Chief Engineer cannot independently extend a statutory certificate or endorse a survey without applicable authorization. ISM Code section 10 - the central link 10.1: procedures maintain the ship against applicable rules/regulations and additional company requirements. 10.2: arrange inspections at appropriate intervals; report non-conformities with possible causes; take corrective action; maintain records. 10.3: identify equipment whose sudden failure creates hazards and promote reliability, including regular tests of standby arrangements and systems not in continuous use. 10.4: integrate these inspections and measures into the operational maintenance routine. SMS definition, ISM 1.1.4: “a structured and documented system enabling Company personnel to implement effectively the Company safety and environmental protection policy”. The ISM Code is mandatory through SOLAS chapter IX. Practical integration by the Chief Engineer Maintain one controlled schedule mapping each equipment item to its PMS job, class/statutory due date, certificate and required surveyor attendance. Review overdue jobs and defects with the superintendent; obtain resources and approvals before expiry. Retain measured clearances, calibration reports, test results and spare-part traceability. Close defects only after satisfactory testing and record verification. Example: plan emergency-generator maintenance and blackout/automatic-start testing before the relevant survey. Arrange safe testing with the Master; document faults, repair and retest. Link serious/recurrent defects to ISM section 9 reporting, section 11 document control and section 12 verification/review. Survey preparation becomes part of continuous maintenance, rather than a last-minute repair campaign. Examiner focus: Six maintenance elements; distinguish three functions; all four ISM section 10 clauses; survey-credit limits. References: [8] ISM Code, sections 1, 9-12; SOLAS IX.

Source: Jan2024_question_and_answer.pdf

2024-JAN-Q05 · IACS, UI/UR/PR and RO Code
8 + 8 MARKS Q5. IACS and the RO Code (a) Enumerate the structure of IACS and explain UI, UR and PR and their significance to owners/operators. (8) (b) What is a Recognized Organization? State the salient features of the RO Code and how Administrations monitor ROs. (8) (a) IACS structure and purpose The International Association of Classification Societies promotes consistency in classification standards and contributes technical expertise to IMO. It is an association of class societies; it does not itself class or issue certificates to individual ships. Council: governing policy and strategy. General Policy Group: coordinates technical work and resolutions. Panels, expert groups and project teams: develop specialist requirements. Quality bodies and the Secretariat: support quality-system oversight, coordination and administration. Document Meaning and practical significance UR Common minimum technical class requirements incorporated into members’ Unified Requirement rules/practices within applicable implementation arrangements. A member may require a higher standard. UI A common interpretation of an IMO provision where clarification is needed. Applied in Unified Interpretation statutory work subject to the authorizing flag Administration’s instructions; it does not amend the Convention. PR Common procedures for member societies, such as transfer of class and handling Procedural defined survey/certification matters. These govern how class work is performed. Requirement Owners must check the applicable rule edition, implementation date, ship/build-date scope and flag instructions. Consistent requirements improve planning for design, survey and repair and reduce conflicting technical expectations. (b) Recognized Organization and RO Code An RO is an organization assessed against the Code and authorized by a flag State to perform specified statutory certification and services on its behalf (RO Code, part 2, paragraph 1.1; meaning paraphrased). A class society’s class function and its delegated statutory function are distinct. The Code was adopted by MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65), taking effect on 1 January 2015. Parts 1 and 2 are mandatory under the relevant treaty amendments; part 3 provides recommendatory oversight guidance. It covers competence, independence, impartiality, integrity, technical resources, trained staff, documented quality management, certification procedures and corrective action. Administration’s authorization and monitoring Establish a formal authorization agreement defining scope, powers, reporting and limitations. Review competence and quality systems; audit offices and witness shipboard work; examine survey reports, detentions and complaints; require corrective action and follow-up. Restrict or withdraw authorization when justified. Delegation does not transfer the flag State’s responsibility. The Chief Engineer verifies survey scope and provides truthful records, access and demonstrations. Examiner focus: IACS hierarchy; UR/UI/PR distinction; recognition versus classification; Code parts; continuing flag oversight. References: [9]-[12] IACS resolutions/procedures; RO Code; SOLAS XI-1/1.

Source: Jan2024_question_and_answer.pdf

2024-JAN-Q06 · Marine Tribology and Lubricating Oil
16 MARKS Q6. Marine tribology and oil analysis Explain the latest developments in Marine “Tribology”. Discuss the inferences you may draw from a main-engine lubricating-oil analysis report, with the proposed corrective action in each case. 1. Meaning and developments Tribology is the study of friction, wear and lubrication between surfaces in relative motion. Developments include improved surface finishes/texturing and wear-resistant coatings; suitable synthetic and environmentally acceptable lubricants; electronically controlled cylinder lubrication; improved additive chemistry; and condition monitoring using online water, viscosity and wear-debris sensors. Laboratory ICP spectroscopy, FTIR, particle analysis and ferrography help distinguish contamination, oil deterioration and wear. Use maker-approved products; sensors supplement laboratory results. 2. Take a representative sample first Sample warm, circulating oil at a consistent designated point after flushing the sampling line. Use a clean bottle and record oil grade, sample point, running hours, oil hours, top-ups and load. Compare with fresh oil, earlier trends and the specific maker/lubricant limits; one universal rejection value is unsafe. Finding / inference Corrective action Viscosity falls: fuel dilution or wrong Find fuel-pump/injector leakage or mixing error. Repair, lower-viscosity oil; low flashpoint supports fuel assess oil safety and renew/blend only as approved; dilution. verify by repeat sample. Viscosity rises: oxidation, soot/insolubles, Check temperatures, combustion, filtration and purifier overheating or wrong oil. performance; remove contamination and renew degraded oil as required. Water; sodium/chloride; glycol: investigate Pressure-test cooler, check seals and cooling-water seawater/freshwater/coolant ingress; markers system. Eliminate leak; purify if suitable or renew oil, need context. particularly persistent emulsion/glycol contamination. Iron, copper, lead, tin or aluminium rising: Correlate with filters/debris, pressure, temperatures and possible wear, depending on component vibration. Locate bearing/liner or other source; reduce metallurgy. load/stop safely if evidence indicates serious damage. BN falls / AN rises: loss of alkalinity and/or acid Investigate combustion, blow-by and overheating; check formation; oxidation trends matter. oil suitability. Correct cause and replenish/renew under supplier and maker guidance. Insolubles / particle count rises: soot, dirt, Inspect filters and separators, contamination entry and wear or ineffective cleaning. combustion; clean the system, confirm cleanliness and resample. 3. Critical distinction and Chief Engineer’s decision Crosshead system oil is not cylinder drain oil. High system-oil BN can indicate cylinder-oil ingress through the stuffing box. Cylinder scrape-down analysis uses residual BN and iron to assess acid neutralization, corrosion and feed rate. In trunk-piston engines, the circulating oil also receives combustion contamination. Spectroscopy may miss large wear particles: inspect filters and use debris analysis. Investigate, correct and verify; do not dilute an alarming report by topping up. Examiner focus: Developments; representative sampling; paired inference/action; maker-specific limits; correct oil-system distinction. References: [13]-[14] MAN Energy Solutions lubrication and oil-monitoring guidance.
