2024-JAN-Q07 · Vetting and Emerging Technology
8 + 8 MARKS Q7. Vetting and new technology (a) Discuss vetting inspections, their significance in the maritime oil trade and contribution to continuous improvement and safety culture. (8) (b) Discuss emerging trends and advancements, including technology and data analytics. (8) (a) Concept and commercial significance Vetting is a charterer’s or other commercial user’s risk-assessment process for determining whether a vessel/operator is acceptable for intended employment. It considers inspection reports, operator performance and other relevant information. An inspection supplies evidence; the receiving company makes its own acceptance decision. In oil trading, an incident can cause major pollution, injury, terminal damage and business interruption. Vetting therefore assesses operational condition and competence beyond possession of valid certificates. Unacceptable vetting can mean lost employment despite valid statutory/class certificates. Inspection scope and Chief Engineer’s preparation Typical areas include certificates and records, navigation, cargo/ballast systems, inert gas, mooring, pollution prevention, machinery reliability, emergency systems, permits, crew competence and practical execution of procedures. Inspectors compare hardware, procedures and actual human performance. As Chief Engineer, review previous observations and recurring defects; verify PMS and critical-equipment tests; check OWS/15-ppm alarms, Oil Record Book evidence, fuel-transfer arrangements, bilges, leaks, emergency power and fire systems. Brief engineers to explain their work honestly. Demonstrate equipment under a risk-assessed procedure agreed with the Master; never bypass protections or falsify records to satisfy an inspection. Continuous improvement and safety culture For each observation: record objective evidence, correct the immediate condition, identify the underlying cause, allocate an owner/deadline and verify effectiveness. Share lessons fleet-wide. A blocked fire damper, for example, requires repair and testing plus review of inspection access, job instructions and supervision. Crew must feel able to report problems. (b) Technology and analytics Digital inspection tools: structured tablet-based records and supporting photographs improve consistency and traceability. Risk-based questions: vessel particulars and operating profile guide inspection coverage. Analytics: recurring observations, equipment defects, incidents and overdue tasks help prioritize maintenance and training. Remote evidence review: improves preparation where accepted, while physical condition and competence still require appropriate verification. Human factors: examine workload, design, supervision and the conditions shaping crew actions. Use findings to improve the work system, rather than simply blaming the last person involved. Protect inspection data through access control and sound cyber practices; poor or manipulated input produces misleading trends. Date distinction - essential for a January 2024 paper SIRE 2.0 was an emerging development in January 2024. It subsequently went live on 2 September 2024, replacing the previous SIRE inspection regime. For a present-day answer, explain its digital, risk-based and human-factor approach. SIRE reports do not constitute a universal OCIMF “pass certificate”, and vetting never replaces statutory surveys, class surveys or ISM audits. Examiner focus: Commercial decision versus inspection; practical safety improvement; technology plus human factors; correct SIRE chronology. References: [15]-[17] OCIMF SIRE 2.0 programme, launch and human-factor guidance.

Source: Jan2024_question_and_answer.pdf

2024-JAN-Q08 · Fault Tree Analysis – Engine Starting
8 + 8 MARKS Q8. Fault Tree Analysis (a) Explain Fault Tree Analysis with examples. (8) (b) Draw a fault tree for “Engine starting Failure”. (8) Question wording reconstructed from the earlier topic description. (a) Method, logic and application Fault Tree Analysis (FTA) is a deductive, top-down method: define an unwanted top event, then identify combinations of lower-level events capable of causing it. State system boundaries and assumptions; examine drawings, failure history and operating conditions; build the tree; identify critical combinations; select controls and review the result. OR gate: any input can produce the output. AND gate: all specified inputs must occur together. Rectangles represent events; circles may represent basic events needing no further breakdown. A minimal cut set is the smallest combination sufficient to cause the top event. Unlike bottom-up FMEA, FTA starts with the consequence. For independent events: P(A AND B) = P(A)P(B); P(A OR B) = P(A)+P(B)-P(A)P(B). If two independent standby pumps each have failure probability 0.01 per demand, loss of both is 0.0001 per demand. Common power loss defeats the independence assumption and needs its own branch. (b) Engine fails to reach self-sustaining firing after a valid start command ENGINE STARTING FAILURE OR Start inhibited / Insufficient cranking Cranks, but combustion command path fails torque or speed not established OR OR OR Turning gear engaged; Low starting-air pressure; Fuel absent/injection fault; stop/trip not reset; valve/distributor fault; low compression; control power/signal lost mechanical resistance wrong timing / poor ignition Assumption: a direct-air-start diesel. For electric/hydraulic starting, replace the cranking branch. “Starting failure” includes both failure to turn and failure to fire; state this boundary clearly. The leaf lists are illustrative and must follow the actual engine’s logic. Use the tree to select safe corrective action First establish whether the engine is inhibited, turning too slowly, or turning without firing. Check indications, permissives, air/control supplies and fuel readiness systematically. Coordinate with the bridge, preserve starting-air reserves and investigate abnormal resistance before another attempt. Never defeat turning-gear or protective interlocks. Isolate before opening machinery. Link findings to ISM 10.3 critical-equipment maintenance and test the repaired system safely. Examiner focus: Defined top event; correct gates; independence limitation; readable tree; practical precautions. References: Illustrative engineering analysis; ISM Code 10.3 [8].

Source: Jan2024_question_and_answer.pdf

2024-JAN-Q09 · III Code and State Responsibilities
6 + 4 + 6 MARKS Q9. IMO Instruments Implementation Code (a) State the III Code objectives, recommended flag-State strategy and instruments covered. (6) (b) State the key indicators for evaluating flag-State performance. (4) (c) Discuss Flag, Coastal and Port State responsibilities. (6) (a) Objective, strategy and scope The III Code, resolution A.1070(28), improves maritime safety and environmental protection through effective implementation of IMO instruments. States should establish a coordinated strategy, review its effectiveness and improve organizational capability. Its four parts address common areas, flag States, coastal States and port States. The principal instruments are SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW, Load Lines, Tonnage and COLREG, with applicable protocols/amendments. The State’s obligations depend on the instruments to which it is party. The Code provides the audit standard for IMSAS; mandatory audit arrangements took effect from 2016. Do not substitute the ILO MLC or the FAL Convention for this list. (b) Performance indicators - paragraphs 42-44 Paragraph 43: PSC detention rates; flag inspection findings; casualty statistics; communication/information processes; and annual loss statistics excluding constructive total losses. Other appropriate indicators may supplement these. Review trends, competence failures, pollution violations and responses to deficiencies under paragraph 44. The purpose is to test whether administration, resources and staffing meet obligations. (c) Three roles - one State may perform all three Role Principal responsibilities Flag State Implement legislation; survey/certify; ensure crew competence; oversee ROs; Ships flying its flag enforce compliance; investigate casualties and report. Coastal State Provide navigational, hydrographic, weather, communication and SAR services as Services/jurisdiction applicable; organize routing/reporting/VTS; respond to pollution and cooperate in off its coast investigations. Port State Exercise lawful PSC through competent officers; secure rectification/detention where Foreign ships visiting justified; communicate findings; provide required reception facilities and avoid its ports undue delay. How to explain implementation in an examination Describe a management cycle: identify the treaty obligation; assign a responsible authority; give it legal powers, staff and funding; deliver the service; measure the result; and correct weaknesses. Illustrative evaluation: if a fleet records 8 detentions in 200 PSC inspections, its inspection-based detention rate is 8/200 × 100 = 4%. Investigate recurring defects and corrective-action delays. This ratio is a study example, not a universal III Code formula; distinguish inspections from unique ships and account for inspection selection and fleet profile. Chief Engineer’s contribution Provide reliable maintenance and pollution records, demonstrate machinery readiness, report defects through the Master/company and assist surveyors and investigators. The Chief Engineer supports implementation onboard; State audit and RO authorization remain Administration functions. Examiner focus: Six instrument groups; three State roles; paragraph 43 indicators including CTL exclusion; practical review. References: [18]-[19] III Code and IMO implementation/audit information; [11]-[12] RO oversight.

Source: Jan2024_question_and_answer.pdf

Classification / RO / HSSC (12)
CLASS-Q01 · Role of classification societies, SOLAS II-1/3-1, and annual versus periodical HSSC surveys
QUESTION 01 (a) | 10 MARKS | Original Q8(a) Role of Classification Societies in structural, mechanical and electrical rule-making and implementation. Why SOLAS Chapter II-1 refers to Class Rules. Introduction A Classification Society is an independent technical organization that develops, publishes and maintains rules for ship structure, machinery and electrical installations, and verifies compliance through design review, construction survey and in-service surveys. Classification is a technical assessment, not a guarantee that a vessel is safe in all circumstances. [R1] A. Rule formation: areas covered Rule discipline Examples of technical requirements Hull / structural Hull-girder strength, scantlings, buckling and fatigue, corrosion additions, materials, welding, watertight integrity and structural surveys. Mechanical Propulsion engines, shafting, boilers, pressure vessels, piping, steering gear, auxiliary machinery, automation and protective devices. Electrical Generators, switchboards, emergency supply, cables, distribution, protection, short-circuit capability and automation interfaces. Class rules are updated in response to operating experience, casualties, research, technology, SOLAS changes and harmonized IACS resolutions. Relevant IACS products include UR, UI, PR and CSR (explained in Q3). [R1, R2] B. Application during ship life cycle 1. Design: Check ship drawings, scantling and strength calculations; approve machinery arrangement, propulsion/shafting, pressure systems and electrical distribution/protection; verify the submitted designs against applicable class/statutory requirements. 2. Construction: Survey approved materials, welding/NDT and fabrication; witness pressure and operational tests, machinery installation, electrical tests, alarm/protection trials and relevant sea trials. 3. Entry into class: After satisfactory compliance, assign class notation and issue classification documentation, with any outstanding items controlled under society rules. 4. In service: Carry out class annual, intermediate, docking and special/renewal surveys, machinery surveys and damage/repair surveys. Verify repairs, approved modifications and ongoing rule compliance. 5. Deficiencies: Issue relevant conditions/recommendations, specify rectification and re-survey, and suspend/withdraw class where class rules so require. C. SOLAS statutory link - important answer SOLAS Chapter II-1, Regulation 3-1 states that ships shall be designed, constructed and maintained in compliance with structural, mechanical and electrical requirements of a classification society recognized by the flag Administration under SOLAS XI-1/1, or applicable Administration national standards providing equivalent safety. [R3] • Technical depth: Convention provisions are mainly broad safety outcomes; detailed class rules supply engineering calculations, testing and acceptance criteria. • Lifecycle safety: The requirement addresses not only newbuild design and construction but continued maintenance in service. • Uniformity and verification: Recognized technical rules promote consistency, with inspection by competent organizations and flag control. • Legal distinction: SOLAS II-1/3-1 makes the referenced technical standard a statutory requirement; this does not turn every class survey or notation into a separate SOLAS certificate. EXAMINER NOTE Write SOLAS II-1/3-1, not II-1/3-10. Regulation 3-10 concerns goal-based ship construction standards and is a different provision. Also state the equivalent national-standard alternative. Primary references: [R1] IACS FAQ; [R2] IACS Resolutions; [R3] SOLAS II-1/3-1; [R4] IMO Recognized Organizations. QUESTION 01 (b) | 6 MARKS | Original Q8(b) Difference and relevance of annual versus periodical survey under HSSC. Introduction The Harmonized System of Survey and Certification (HSSC) coordinates survey windows, certificate validity and endorsements under SOLAS, MARPOL, Load Lines and relevant codes. The current IMO survey guidance is Resolution A.1207(34), HSSC 2025, adopted 3 December 2025. [R5] Particular Annual survey Periodical survey Purpose General inspection to verify continuing Certificate-specific inspection/tests to satisfactory maintenance of ship, machinery demonstrate that relevant equipment complies and equipment. and is fit for service. Survey window Within 3 months before/after applicable Within 3 months before/after anniversary certificate anniversary. prescribed for the certificate. Frequency Each anniversary when required by that Safety Equipment: 2nd OR 3rd anniversary; certificate. Safety Radio: EACH anniversary. Scope Certificate examination, visual condition and Relevant equipment examination, functional necessary tests. tests and examination of specified records/manuals. Certificate action Certificate endorsed as applicable. Certificate endorsed; may replace annual survey for Safety Equipment. Exact SOLAS applications Certificate Anniversary and type Cargo Ship Safety Equipment - SOLAS I/8 Annual survey (normally 1st and 4th); periodical survey at the 2nd OR 3rd anniversary, replacing an annual survey. Cargo Ship Safety Radio - SOLAS I/9 Periodical survey at every annual anniversary, ±3 months. Cargo Ship Safety Construction - SOLAS I/10 Annual survey plus intermediate survey at 2nd OR 3rd anniversary, replacing an annual survey. IOPP / Load Line IOPP: annual plus intermediate at 2nd or 3rd. Load Line: annual. Do not incorrectly call all intermediate surveys periodical surveys. Relevance • Combines surveys into coordinated attendance and minimizes unnecessary duplication/off-hire. • Establishes predictable anniversary windows and certificate endorsements. • Supports continuous ship safety while permitting common survey and certification cycles. EXAMINER NOTE Periodical survey ≠ five-year renewal. The Cargo Ship Safety Equipment periodical is at the second or third anniversary; Radio periodical is at each anniversary. CLASS-Q02 · Why is a Class Certificate required when statutory certificates exist?
QUESTION 02 | 4-6 MARKS | Previously discussed oral question Why is a Class Certificate required when statutory certificates are already available? Direct answer: Statutory certificates show conformity with specified treaty/flag requirements; a Class Certificate documents a vessel’s technical compliance with the classification society’s rules for hull, machinery and electrical installations throughout its life. They are complementary, not interchangeable. Classification certificate Statutory certificate Issued by society to record assigned classification and Issued by flag or its authorized RO for a defined Convention applicable notations. obligation. Basis: society rules, class surveys, approved repairs, conditions Basis: SOLAS, MARPOL, Load Line, etc., and flag legislation. of class. Loss of class may have major commercial/insurance Statutory certificate validity and endorsement controlled by implications. relevant Convention and flag. • Legal link: SOLAS II-1/3-1 refers to recognized Class Rules OR equivalent flag national standards. • Practical need: Owners, insurers, financiers, charterers and terminals frequently require acceptable class status. • RO distinction: A society can also issue statutory documents only within its flag authorization; not all classed ships automatically enjoy all statutory certifications. Primary references: [R1], [R3], [R4]. CLASS-Q03 · IACS structure, UR, UI and PR: preparation and significance
QUESTION 03 | 8-16 MARKS | Previously asked IACS structure / making procedure Enumerate the structure of IACS; distinguish UR, UI and PR; explain their preparation and significance for shipowners/operators. IACS structure and functions IACS (International Association of Classification Societies) is an association of member classification societies. It develops common technical resolutions and quality arrangements. IACS itself does not class ships or issue individual ship class certificates. • Council: highest governing body responsible for overall association policy. • General Policy Group (GPG): develops and coordinates technical policy and approves applicable resolutions. • Technical Panels / expert working groups: prepare, review and maintain resolutions across hull, machinery, electrical and other domains. • Quality and Secretariat arrangements: support QSCS, coordination, communications and records. UR versus UI versus PR Type Meaning and use Example UR - Unified Minimum technical class requirement; subject to member ratification Hull strength, machinery, Requirement and incorporation in applicable society rules. Members may set stricter electrical installation or survey rules. requirements. UI - Unified Uniform interpretation of unclear IMO Convention provisions; applied Interpretation of SOLAS, Load Interpretation when flag has not issued definite instructions. Does not supersede the Line or MARPOL technical Administration. wording. PR - Procedural Common procedure for IACS members to implement in their own PR1B (double/dual class); PR17 Requirement practices. (reporting ISM-related deficiencies). Typical formation/implementation sequence Issue identified (IMO change, technology or operational problem) → technical panel drafts a proposal → review and consultation among members → GPG approval under IACS procedures → implementation through society rules/practices with applicable effective dates → periodic revision. For UR, the member governing body ratifies and normally implements approved applicable requirements in its rules within the IACS-prescribed period. UI is circulated as appropriate to IMO/Administrations, and definite flag instructions govern. PR is incorporated into society procedures in the agreed implementation period. [R2, R7] Significance to owner/operator • Reduces divergent baseline technical requirements and improves survey predictability. • Assists acceptance by charterers, insurers and industry. • Supports common interpretations of international statutory regulations. • Permits owners to anticipate technical updates, survey requirements, retrofits and investment. Primary references: [R1] IACS FAQ; [R2] IACS Resolution descriptions; [R7] IACS Unified Interpretations. Official reference list and authoritative links [R1] IACS - Frequently Asked Questions, classification, UR and CSR [R2] IACS - Resolutions, UR / UI / PR [R3] SOLAS II-1, Regulation 3-1 (text as reproduced by IMO Rules) [R4] IMO - Recognized Organizations, framework and authorization [R5] IMO Resolution A.1207(34) - HSSC Survey Guidelines 2025 [R6] SOLAS Chapter I, Regulations 8, 9, 10 (HSSC survey schedules) [R7] IACS - Unified Interpretations [R8] IMO MSC.349(92) - RO Code full adopted text [R9] DG Shipping - Comprehensive Safety Management Report 2025, RO oversight section [R10] IACS PR1B - Double and Dual Class, version status [R11] IACS PR17 - Reporting deficiencies affecting ISM implementation [R12] IMO - Port State Control / Procedures A.1206(34), 2025 [R13] IMO - Ship construction and ESP overview Preparation standard: Short direct introductions, subpart-by-subpart response, comparison tables for distinctions, numbered procedures and explicit IMO regulation numbers. CLASS-Q04 · Recognized Organizations: RO Code, authorization and flag oversight
QUESTION 04 | 8-16 MARKS | Earlier RO Code / administration question What is a Recognized Organization (RO)? State the three parts of the RO Code and explain authorization and Flag-State oversight. RO: A competent organization formally recognized and authorized by a flag Administration to carry out specified statutory surveys, audits and certification on its behalf. The flag State retains ultimate responsibility for treaty compliance. [R4] RO Code legal foundation The Code for Recognized Organizations was adopted by MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65); relevant amendments made Parts 1 and 2 mandatory from 1 January 2015 under applicable IMO instruments, while Part 3 is recommendatory guidance. [R8] Part Subject Status 1 General purpose, scope, delegation, communication and references. Mandatory framework 2 Recognition and authorization: independence, competence, resources, documented Mandatory QMS, surveys, audits, reporting, flag arrangements. requirements Part Subject Status 3 Flag-State oversight: ship visits, RO audits, performance monitoring and follow-up. Non-mandatory guidance Flag authorization procedure 1. Administration assesses RO eligibility, technical competence, geographical coverage, independence and quality systems. 2. Defines statutory tasks and limits of authority by written agreement or equivalent legal instrument. 3. RO executes authorized surveys and certification in accordance with mandatory instruments and flag instructions. 4. RO reports significant defects, detentions, incidents and certification decisions as required. 5. Flag assesses performance, audits work, requires corrective actions and can restrict or withdraw authorization. Oversight methods • Office and process audits, shipboard supplementary inspections, survey witnessing and review of issued statutory certificates. • Vertical Contract Audits (VCAs), performance metrics, PSC detention trends, complaints, casualties and trends in deficiencies. • Corrective action requests and verification of close-out; closer oversight for weak performers. • The flag should maintain a documented risk-appropriate oversight programme; RO Code Part 3 does not prescribe a single universal annual/biennial flag-audit interval. EXAMINER NOTE Do not confuse RO internal VCA annually (mandatory RO Code Part 2) with the frequency of every flag Administration’s external oversight audits (determined by its programme and applicable national agreement). [R8, R9] Primary references: [R4] IMO RO overview; [R8] MSC.349(92); [R9] DG Shipping Safety Management Report 2025. Official reference list and authoritative links [R1] IACS - Frequently Asked Questions, classification, UR and CSR [R2] IACS - Resolutions, UR / UI / PR [R3] SOLAS II-1, Regulation 3-1 (text as reproduced by IMO Rules) [R4] IMO - Recognized Organizations, framework and authorization [R5] IMO Resolution A.1207(34) - HSSC Survey Guidelines 2025 [R6] SOLAS Chapter I, Regulations 8, 9, 10 (HSSC survey schedules) [R7] IACS - Unified Interpretations [R8] IMO MSC.349(92) - RO Code full adopted text [R9] DG Shipping - Comprehensive Safety Management Report 2025, RO oversight section [R10] IACS PR1B - Double and Dual Class, version status [R11] IACS PR17 - Reporting deficiencies affecting ISM implementation [R12] IMO - Port State Control / Procedures A.1206(34), 2025 [R13] IMO - Ship construction and ESP overview Preparation standard: Short direct introductions, subpart-by-subpart response, comparison tables for distinctions, numbered procedures and explicit IMO regulation numbers. CLASS-Q05 · Vertical Contract Audits and RO office audit frequencies
QUESTION 05 | 6 MARKS / ORAL | Previously asked exact-frequency cross-question Explain Vertical Contract Audit (VCA) and how often RO office audits and Flag oversight are carried out. VCA traces a particular job/contract vertically from application and plan review through work instructions, survey evidence, decisions, certificate issue and records to determine whether the RO delivered its service in compliance with the RO Code and its QMS. Frequency: specific language from the RO Code Activity Frequency / rule RO Vertical Contract Audits (Part 2, para 6.3.1) Annually for each of: plan approval; new construction survey; in-service periodical survey/audit; type approval or other equipment/material survey where applicable. RO internal office/location audits (Part 2, para Audit periods set by RO, and each location audited at least once per 3 years. 6.2.3) Flag oversight audits (Part 3) Flag develops oversight programme based on authorization scope and risk; not one universal fixed RO Code interval. Indian practice described in DGS report 2025 DGS report describes annual audits of ROs under RO agreements and supplementary/vertical audits; confirm current programme and agreement for a specific case. Difference: VCA vs vertical survey/inspection An RO VCA audits the RO’s delivery process and selected contract records. A flag supplementary ship inspection checks the actual ship and authorized RO work onboard. They may be used together but are not synonymous. CLASS-Q06 · Dual class versus double class: purpose, procedure and duration
QUESTION 06 | 6-8 MARKS | Earlier dual-class oral question What is dual class? Why is it arranged, what is the procedure, and how long does it normally last? Dual class Double class Two class societies with written agreement sharing agreed Two societies each class the vessel independently; each survey work; each society maintains its own class decision and conducts its own complete survey programme. certificate. Formal bilateral agreement for existing ship; newbuild may No reliance on a survey-sharing arrangement; duplicate involve trilateral agreement including yard. workload/cost generally greater. Reasons • Charterer/financier/insurer preferences or requirements; owner fleet policy. • Specialist technical services, regional access or newbuilding arrangements. • Commercial and technical flexibility where acceptable to both societies. Procedure 1. Owner applies to the second society, discloses existing class status, outstanding conditions and survey history. 2. Societies exchange necessary technical and survey information under IACS PR1B and agree responsibilities. 3. For an existing ship, enter the written bilateral survey-sharing agreement (or appropriate newbuild arrangement). 4. Complete additional plan checks or surveys needed to satisfy the incoming society; close outstanding requirements. 5. Each society assigns/maintains class and issues its own certificate; owner maintains compliance with both. Duration: No IMO-wide or IACS PR1B fixed one-month or one-year duration for the dual-class relationship. It lasts while both societies maintain the arrangement and each ship complies. A PR1B one-month communication deadline is a procedural deadline, not the maximum dual-class duration. [R10] EXAMINER NOTE As at Oct 2026, IACS PR1B Rev.8 (Nov 2024) is current; Rev.9 is indicated for 1 Jan 2027 entry into force. [R10] Primary references: [R10] IACS PR1B status and procedure. CLASS-Q07 · Classification surveys, statutory surveys, PMS and Conditions of Class
QUESTION 07 | 6-8 MARKS | Previous class / survey / PMS question Distinguish classification surveys, statutory surveys and maintenance; explain how PMS records and Conditions of Class fit in. Activity Authority and purpose Examples Class survey Classification society rules; retain class notation, Annual hull/machinery, docking, special assess hull/machinery/electrical fitness. survey, shaft/boiler surveys as applicable. Statutory survey Convention and flag law; performed by flag or SOLAS Safety Construction, MARPOL IOPP, authorized RO. Load Line, safety equipment. PMS / routine maintenance Owner/operator procedures and ISM section 10; Clearances, calibration, filter service, machinery kept effective between surveys. generator tests and defect records. Role of PMS • Plan and record jobs, condition checks, calibrations, repairs, testing and spares. • Provide evidence to attending class/statutory surveyors. • Routine PMS completion does not automatically earn classification survey credit. Credit is only available under approved survey arrangements and subject to society rules and surveyor verification. Conditions of Class (CoC) A Condition of Class is an outstanding requirement placed by the society for a deficiency or required work, with a stated action/due date under its rules. Non-compliance may lead to restrictions, suspension or withdrawal of class. The Master/Chief Engineer should notify the company, plan permanent repair and obtain required re-survey and formal closure. CLASS-Q08 · HSSC survey types and the five-year certificate cycle
QUESTION 08 | 6-8 MARKS | Survey types and HSSC oral follow-up Enumerate HSSC survey types and show the harmonized five-year survey/certificate cycle. HSSC survey Meaning / timing Initial Before putting the ship into service or before first issue of relevant statutory certificate. Annual Applicable certificate anniversary ±3 months; general continuing-maintenance verification. Intermediate Normally 2nd OR 3rd anniversary ±3 months for specified certificates, replacing one annual survey. Periodical Safety Equipment 2nd OR 3rd; Safety Radio EACH anniversary; scope specific to that certificate. Renewal Before certificate renewal, normally at intervals not exceeding five years, subject to Convention exceptions. Bottom inspection Cargo ship normally at least two inspections of outside bottom within any five-year period; max interval generally 36 months, subject to applicable provisions. Additional After important repairs/renewals or where otherwise prescribed. Five-year certificate example (Safety Equipment) Initial Year 1 Year 2 Year 3 Year 4 Expiry Issue Annual Annual OR periodical Periodical OR annual Annual Renewal before expiry Periodical survey is performed at one of Year 2 and Year 3, with a normal annual survey at the other. The certificate anniversary date is a defined statutory date; survey window is not recalculated by simply adding one year to the actual last survey attendance date. [R5, R6] Example: anniversary is 15 June Normal annual or prescribed periodical window is 15 March through 15 September for the relevant anniversary. For the 2nd-or-3rd survey, choose the relevant year and apply the ±3-month window. The certificate and endorsements remain subject to the survey rules and any exceptional extensions. Primary references: [R5] A.1207(34), 4.1-4.8 and appendices; [R6] SOLAS Ch I. CLASS-Q09 · IACS PR17 reports and their connection with PSC and ISM
QUESTION 09 | 6 MARKS | Previous PR17 / PSC question When does an IACS PR17 report arise, who raises it, and how does it connect with PSC and ISM? IACS PR17 is an internal class-society procedure titled Reporting on deficiencies possibly affecting the implementation of the ISM Code on board. A surveyor may report deficiencies observed at class/statutory attendance, PSC-related additional attendance or Flag-State inspections when they could indicate breakdown of SMS implementation. [R11] Typical trigger groups 1. Technical: Several deficiencies suggesting that company maintenance procedures are not effectively implemented. 2. Operational: Deviation from required operating practices and safety procedures. 3. Documentation/reporting: Missing or defective required records, reporting or corrective-action processes. 4. Other serious safety/environment issues suggesting possible ineffective SMS implementation. Procedure RO/Class surveyor detects deficiency → considers PR17 criteria and records facts → submits report through society reporting process → responsible SMS audit organization is informed/follow-up arranged as appropriate → company corrects deficiencies and addresses underlying SMS failure. PSCO role: PSC officer records PSC findings/detention under PSC procedures. A PSCO does not issue an IACS PR17 form; the attending class/RO surveyor does so in accordance with society procedures. Not every ordinary wear-and-tear item automatically merits PR17. EXAMINER NOTE PR17 is an ISM-related reporting pathway, not a replacement for a PSC deficiency form, detainability assessment or full ISM audit. Primary references: [R11] IACS PR17 Rev.3 (Dec 2024) official status; [R12] IMO PSC Procedures 2025. Official reference list and authoritative links [R1] IACS - Frequently Asked Questions, classification, UR and CSR [R2] IACS - Resolutions, UR / UI / PR [R3] SOLAS II-1, Regulation 3-1 (text as reproduced by IMO Rules) [R4] IMO - Recognized Organizations, framework and authorization [R5] IMO Resolution A.1207(34) - HSSC Survey Guidelines 2025 [R6] SOLAS Chapter I, Regulations 8, 9, 10 (HSSC survey schedules) [R7] IACS - Unified Interpretations [R8] IMO MSC.349(92) - RO Code full adopted text [R9] DG Shipping - Comprehensive Safety Management Report 2025, RO oversight section [R10] IACS PR1B - Double and Dual Class, version status [R11] IACS PR17 - Reporting deficiencies affecting ISM implementation [R12] IMO - Port State Control / Procedures A.1206(34), 2025 [R13] IMO - Ship construction and ESP overview Preparation standard: Short direct introductions, subpart-by-subpart response, comparison tables for distinctions, numbered procedures and explicit IMO regulation numbers. CLASS-Q10 · ESP and Common Structural Rules in classification
QUESTION 10 | 6 MARKS | Related structural/class survey question What are ESP and Common Structural Rules (CSR), and how do they connect with classification? ESP: The 2011 ESP Code, as amended, contains enhanced survey requirements for relevant bulk carriers and oil tankers, with detailed planning, close-up surveys, thickness measurements and structural condition assessment. It is a mandatory SOLAS XI-1/2 framework as applicable; class/RO may carry out authorized surveys. CSR: IACS Common Structural Rules specify comprehensive minimum hull structural rules for covered bulk carriers and oil tankers, implemented by IACS member societies. CSR concerns structural design/construction, while ESP mainly concerns continued structural condition assessment of ships in service. [R1, R13] • Hull checks: coating condition, cracking, deformation, corrosion and gauging results. • Substantial corrosion: defined/assessed according to applicable ESP/Class criteria; calls for closer measurement, reassessment and repair as needed. • Chief Engineer: support ballast/cargo tank preparation, safe access, isolation, permits and equipment readiness; ensure survey/repair findings are reported and closed. Primary references: [R1] IACS CSR explanation; [R13] IMO ESP Code overview. CLASS-Q11 · Chief Engineer duties before, during and after class/RO surveys
QUESTION 11 | 6-8 MARKS | Previously discussed Chief Engineer practical survey duties What should the Chief Engineer do before, during and after Class/RO survey, and after a serious machinery defect? Before attendance 1. Study the survey status, certificate anniversary, outstanding Conditions of Class and SMS defect records. 2. Prepare PMS reports, alarm and trip tests, calibration certificates, machinery history, oil/boiler tests and approved drawings. 3. Coordinate safe isolation, permits, drydock support, contractors, surveyor attendance and required spare parts with Master/company. During survey 1. Demonstrate actual operation of safety-critical machinery; cooperate with surveyor, explain system arrangements and provide objective test evidence. 2. Disclose defects accurately and record findings; do not conceal failures or sign unperformed tests. After survey or major defect 1. Enter findings and deadlines into survey tracker and PMS; notify Master, superintendent, DPA and class/RO/flag as required. 2. Where equipment is unsafe, stop/restrict operation, arrange safeguards and obtain approval of any temporary measures. 3. Repair, function-test, document and obtain surveyor endorsement/closure. Review repeated defects under ISM sections 9 and 10. EXAMINER NOTE The Chief Engineer may manage maintenance and evidence, but cannot independently close a class condition or authorize a statutory certificate endorsement unless the relevant rules expressly permit it. Primary references: [R3], [R4], [R5], ISM Code sections 9-10. 2025-07-JUL-Q7 · July 2025 Q7 — Substantial corrosion, structural surveys and class requirements
MEO CLASS I | ENGINEERING MANAGEMENT | JULY 2025 Q7 DG SHIPPING / MMD INDIA | MEO CLASS I - ENGINEERING MANAGEMENT July 2025 - Q7: Substantial Corrosion Question: Define substantial corrosion in the context of ship structure and classification requirements. What criteria are used to assess substantial corrosion during inspections? Discuss the steps taken to address substantial corrosion during a renewal survey, including inspection techniques, repair methods, and the role of the classification society in ensuring compliance. Answer for written examination: Corrected and reorganised from the four-page handwritten notes. References are numbered and updated to October 2026. 1. Meaning and applicable regulations Substantial corrosion is a corrosion condition in which assessment of the corrosion pattern indicates wastage exceeding 75% of the allowable corrosion margin, but still within the acceptable limit. This is not 75% loss of the original plate thickness. [2, 3] For a ship built to the IACS Common Structural Rules (CSR), the measured thickness lies in the interval from the renewal thickness, tren, up to tren + 0.5 mm. The renewal thickness is the minimum permissible thickness; steel measured below tren requires renewal/approved corrective action under the applicable class rules. [2, 3] Statutory/class basis: SOLAS regulation XI-1/2 makes the 2011 ESP Code mandatory for applicable bulk carriers and oil tankers (normally 500 GT and above). Apply the current ESP Code, applicable IACS UR Z10-series, class rules and approved renewal criteria. [1-3] Simple numerical illustration If original thickness = 12.0 mm and minimum permissible thickness = 10.0 mm, allowable wastage = 2.0 mm. At an actual measured thickness of 10.3 mm, wastage is 1.7 mm or 85% of the allowable margin: it is substantial corrosion but is not automatically beyond the renewal limit. At 9.8 mm, the renewal limit has been exceeded. This is an illustration; actual scantling limits are decided from the ship-specific class criteria. 2. Criteria used by the surveyor to assess corrosion 1. Visual and coating condition: inspect ballast tanks, cargo holds/tanks, shell plating, deck, bottom structures, longitudinals, frames, stiffeners, bulkheads, welds and joints for scale, rust, coating failure, pitting, grooving, edge corrosion, cracks, buckling and deformation. POOR coating means general coating breakdown over 20% or more of the area, or hard scale over 10% or more. This prompts further examination; it does not by itself establish substantial corrosion. [2] 2. Thickness measurement (TM): use calibrated ultrasonic gauging and compare the actual measured thickness with the as-built/original thickness and the applicable allowable or renewal thickness. Evaluate the extent, severity and distribution of corrosion, including local pitting and grooving. [2, 3] 3. Survey/history evidence: review earlier TM reports, coating condition, repair history, structural plans, class conditions and locations known to experience rapid wastage. Record suspect areas (locations already showing substantial corrosion or considered by the surveyor prone to rapid wastage) and critical structural areas. [2] 4. Structural significance: determine whether reduced scantlings, fractures, buckling or distortion compromise hull-girder/local strength, or watertight/weathertight integrity. Such defects may require prompt repair even apart from the nominal thickness threshold. [2] Verified answer | Original handwritten scan: 4 pages | Updated Oct 2026 Page 1 MEO CLASS I | ENGINEERING MANAGEMENT | JULY 2025 Q7 3. Procedure during a renewal (special) survey A. Before starting the survey - preparation and safety 1. The owner and classification society prepare/agreed a written survey programme using previous survey and TM records; identify suspect/critical areas and agree the scope of overall survey, close-up survey, TM and tank testing. Conduct the survey-planning meeting with the attending surveyor and TM firm. [2] 2. Arrange tank cleaning, sufficient lighting, safe access (scaffolds/staging, ladders or other accepted means), ventilation, safe enclosed-space entry, atmosphere testing, permit to work, rescue arrangements and communication. Any remotely operated tool is used only where accepted under the applicable survey arrangements. [2] B. Examination and gauging 3. Perform the overall survey to determine general hull condition, followed by close-up surveys of required and suspect/critical areas. Close-up survey means details are within close visual range, normally within reach of hand. [2] 4. Have the qualified, certified thickness-measurement firm carry out calibrated UT gauging simultaneously with close-up surveys where required, under the control/witness of the class/Administration surveyor. Mark or sketch measurement positions on plans so the findings are traceable. [2, 4] 5. Compare each relevant measurement with the approved acceptance/renewal criteria; calculate percentage wastage where appropriate; distinguish general corrosion, pitting, edge and grooving corrosion. Where substantial corrosion is detected, extend the close-up examination and TM to the extent prescribed by the ESP Code/class rules before the survey is credited complete. [2, 3] 6. If cracks or suspected structural defects are found, apply further NDT (e.g. magnetic-particle, dye-penetrant or ultrasonic flaw detection) as directed by the surveyor. Report all unacceptable findings immediately. [2] 4. Repair methods, monitoring and acceptance Condition found Correct action Steel below approved renewal limit; Carry out prompt, thorough permanent repair to the surveyor's unacceptable fracture, buckling, satisfaction, usually by cropping and renewing plating, stiffeners or structural grooving or strength/watertight members. Use accepted material, approved repair details/welding procedures, defect suitable NDT and required tests. [2, 3] Substantial corrosion, but thickness Area is identified, documented and kept under enhanced examination / remains within acceptance limits and thickness monitoring. Corrosion control may include cleaning and restoring no unsafe structural defect an approved hard protective coating; the class surveyor decides permitted follow-up. Coating alone is not a universal substitute for steel renewal. [2, 3] Isolated defect where permanent A temporary repair may be accepted only in the limited circumstances repair is not immediately practicable provided by applicable class rules, with a condition of class and specified deadline; unsafe service must not continue. [2] CSR bulk-carrier qualification: For identified substantial-corrosion areas in certain CSR bulk-carrier arrangements, prescribed annual monitoring may be by maintaining an approved coating in GOOD condition or by annual thickness measurements. This ship-type-specific provision should not be applied as a general exemption to all tankers. [3] 5. Role of classification society / authorised RO 1. Survey planning: agree the renewal-survey programme; study previous findings and plans; select suspect/critical locations and approve/require further examination as necessary. Verified answer | Original handwritten scan: 4 pages | Updated Oct 2026 Page 2 MEO CLASS I | ENGINEERING MANAGEMENT | JULY 2025 Q7 2. Independent verification: attend the survey, control/witness gauging as required, check calibration and the qualifications/certification of the TM firm, request additional readings and evaluate remaining scantlings. [2, 4] 3. Repair acceptance: apply class and ESP acceptance criteria, approve the repair approach, inspect completed steelwork and tests, and require corrective action or impose class conditions where permitted. [2] 4. Reporting and follow-up: review the signed TM report and countersign it, record corrosion/repairs/suspect areas in the survey records and Condition Evaluation Report / Executive Hull Summary; schedule future checks and credit the survey only when applicable requirements are met. [2] 5. Flag-state role: when undertaking statutory surveys as a recognised organisation, act within the flag Administration's authorisation, including applicable reporting and certification requirements. 6. Chief Engineer / ship-management responsibilities • Notify the Master, superintendent/owner and class surveyor promptly of significant corrosion, cracking or buckling; protect affected spaces and equipment as necessary and assess operational risks. • Supply historical reports, original thickness/scantling drawings, corrosion-control records and a safe, clean, gas-tested, accessible survey space; coordinate the gauging/repair contractors and safety permits. • Implement class-approved steelwork and coating repairs, preserve photographs/sketches/TM readings, verify test results and close out corrective actions in the planned-maintenance/SMS records. • Monitor identified suspect locations at the intervals imposed by the survey programme or class; never conceal defects with coating before their extent and remaining thickness are established. 7. Important corrections made to the handwritten answer Original shorthand / potential Correct exam wording error "75% corrosion" or "75% loss of plate" More than 75% of allowable corrosion margin, not 75% loss of nominal steel thickness. "Poor coating = substantial corrosion" POOR coating (20% breakdown or 10% hard scale) is a separate indicator; confirm steel wastage by TM. "Any substantial corrosion needs Steel below renewal/acceptance limit, or structurally unsafe defects, needs immediate steel renewal" approved repair. Substantial but acceptable wastage triggers additional examination and future monitoring. "Apply epoxy instead of permanent Epoxy coating may control corrosion in eligible and accepted cases; it does repair" not restore lost steel scantling. "All coating-restored areas may skip Exceptions depend on ship type, CSR applicability, class/ESP provisions and annual TM" acceptance of coating condition; not a general permission. "Argonaut TM" software is prescribed No particular commercial software name is imposed by the cited provisions. Use a traceable, approved TM report format. 8. Current regulatory update - 1 January 2026 Resolution MSC.553(108) amended the 2011 ESP Code procedures concerning approval/certification of firms engaged in hull thickness measurement, including auditing by the Administration to verify that the firm is properly organised, managed and competent. The amendment entered into force on 1 January 2026. For MEO Class I answers, mention the Administration/authorised RO framework rather than describing these firms as informal/private gauging companies. [1, 4, 5] Verified answer | Original handwritten scan: 4 pages | Updated Oct 2026 Page 3 MEO CLASS I | ENGINEERING MANAGEMENT | JULY 2025 Q7 9. Conclusion - examiner-friendly Substantial corrosion is an early warning stage before the renewal limit is exceeded. The surveyor assesses it by overall and close-up survey, calibrated ultrasonic thickness measurements, coating assessment and previous survey history. A renewal survey therefore requires planned access and survey, extended measurements in affected areas, approved steel repair whenever limits or integrity demand it, corrosion prevention and future monitoring, all verified and recorded by class / the Administration. 10. Quick revision - 20-25 minute exam writing plan Suggested sequence (not an official marking scheme): 3 lines of definition and regulatory basis; 4 clear inspection criteria; 6 numbered renewal-survey actions; separate steel repair versus accepted monitoring; 4 specific duties of class and a one-line conclusion. Reading from gauge / survey Correct interpretation and action More than 75% of allowable margin Record substantial corrosion if still within accepted scantling limits; used (general definition) expand measurements and apply required follow-up. CSR: tren to tren + 0.5 mm Substantial-corrosion range; confirm extent and class-prescribed survey/repair or monitoring action. Below tren, or otherwise unsafe structural Class-approved repair/renewal and tests before service in accordance condition with class requirements. Memory chain: PLAN - ACCESS - INSPECT - GAUGE - COMPARE - EXTEND - REPAIR/MONITOR - RECORD - FOLLOW UP. This follows the action verbs in the question and covers the Chief Engineer and class responsibilities. References (primary rules and official current IMO material, accessed October 2026) [1] IMO - 2011 ESP Code; SOLAS XI-1/2, with January 2026 supplement. [2] IACS UR Z10.4 (Hull Surveys of Double-Hull Oil Tankers): definitions, repairs, special/annual surveys, gauging and reporting. [3] IACS UR Z10.2 (Hull Surveys of Bulk Carriers): extent of gauging and CSR coating/annual survey qualification. [4] IMO Resolution MSC.553(108), adopted 23 May 2024, effective 1 January 2026. [5] IMO - Shipping rules in force from 1 January 2026 (official overview). [6] IACS - Common Structural Rules for Bulk Carriers and Oil Tankers. Casualty Investigation (1)
2026-04-APR-Q8 · April 2026 Q8 — Marine casualty investigation: SOLAS, MARPOL and Casualty Investigation Code
APRIL 2026 - Q8: MARINE CASUALTY INVESTIGATION Typed-text transcription of the supplied handwritten notes Q8. State the applicable Regulation of SOLAS and MARPOL under which it is mandatory for a Flag State to conduct an investigation into any Maritime Casualty. Under SOLAS Regulation I/21 Each Flag State must investigate any casualty involving its ship if the investigation may help find what changes are needed in the SOLAS Convention. The Flag State must also give relevant information about the findings to the IMO. The purpose is to improve maritime safety and prevent similar incidents in future. The reports are submitted to IMO without naming the ship or fixing blame on any person. Under MARPOL Article 8 The Flag State has the duty to investigate any pollution incident involving its ship, especially when the incident is serious or when the Administration thinks an investigation will be useful. The aim is to find the exact cause of the pollution, identify any contributing faults or negligence, and take steps to prevent the same type of incident from happening again. The investigation is important because it helps improve shipboard safety, pollution-control procedures and compliance with MARPOL. Under MARPOL Article 12 The Party must also send the relevant information, data and findings of the investigation to IMO. In this way, IMO can study the case, collect lessons learned and improve international pollution-prevention standards for the future. NOTE - GISIS / MCI reporting Reporting to IMO is done electronically through the Global Integrated Shipping Information System (GISIS), which has a Maritime Casualties and Incidents (MCI) module database containing a unique collection of MCI data, as defined by circular MSC-MEPC.3/Circ.4/Rev.1. The MCI module, which is accessible to the public, contains all marine safety investigation reports submitted to the Organization and all casualty analyses that have been approved by the III Sub- Committee. (b) Illustrate the salient points of the Casualty Investigation Code and the recommended practices for a safety investigation into a marine casualty or marine incident. SALIENT POINTS OF THE CASUALTY INVESTIGATION CODE 1. Mandatory Investigation of Very Serious Casualties A safety investigation is compulsory for every "very serious marine casualty" involving total loss of the ship, a death, or severe damage to the environment. The Flag State is primarily responsible. 2. Notification Requirements The Flag State (high seas), or both Flag and Coastal State (territorial seas), must notify all "substantially interested States" as soon as practicable. The notification shall contain as much of the following information as is readily available: 1. Name of the ship and its Flag State. 2. The IMO Identification Number. 3. Nature of marine casualty. Text transcription - 2026-04 APRIL Q8 Casualty 4. Location of marine casualty. 5. Time and date of marine casualty. 6. Number of any seriously injured or killed persons. 7. Consequences of the marine casualty to individuals, property and the environment. 8. The identification of any other ship involved. 3. Investigator Powers and Independence Investigators must have the power to board ships, interview personnel and acquire evidence. Investigations must be independent and free from any external direction or interference from parties affected by the outcome. 4. Protection of Seafarers Seafarers' human rights must be upheld. They must be informed of the nature of the investigation and allowed access to legal advice regarding their right against self-incrimination before giving evidence. 5. Reporting Standards Draft reports should be shared with substantially interested parties/States for comments. The final report must be submitted to IMO for a very serious marine casualty and made available to the public and the shipping industry. 6. Dissemination of Safety Information Reports and lessons learned should be widely disseminated to prevent recurrence and improve safety of life at sea and the protection of the marine environment. RECOMMENDED PRACTICES FOR A SAFETY INVESTIGATION 1. Administrative Independence The investigation should have functional independence from parties involved in the casualty and from anyone who might take disciplinary action or judicial proceedings. 2. Priority of Investigation A safety investigation should, as far as possible, be afforded the same priority as a criminal investigation to ensure investigators are not prevented from accessing vital evidence. Examples of vital evidence noted in the source include:  Survey/other records held by the Flag State, Class and owner.  All recorded data, including VDR.  Evidence that may be provided by VTS, government surveyors, Coast Guard, PSC, etc. 3. The "Chain of Responsibility" Scope The investigation should look beyond the immediate (proximate) cause to identify underlying conditions, which may be remote from the site of the marine casualty or marine incident. Examine failure across the whole chain of responsibility, including organizational or human factors. 4. Evidence Collection and VDRs Make effective use of the Voyage Data Recorder (VDR). Do not unnecessarily detain the vessel or remove original documents unless essential. If a State lacks facilities to read VDR data, other States with the capability should offer assistance. 5. Confidentiality of Safety Records The State must protect marine safety records (witness statements, medical information, etc.) from disclosure in criminal or civil proceedings, unless there is an overriding public interest in the administration of justice. 6. International Co-operation Text transcription - 2026-04 APRIL Q8 Casualty States should agree on which State will lead the investigation to avoid conflicting demands on witnesses. Substantially interested parties should be allowed to participate by interviewing witnesses and viewing evidence to the extent practicable. 7. Re-opening Investigations If new and significant evidence is presented which could materially alter the findings, the investigating State should consider re-opening the case. Text transcription - 2026-04 APRIL Q8 Casualty

IMO · Conventions & Codes (3)
2026-06-JUN-Q1 · June 2026 Q1 — Goal-Based Ship Construction Standards: bulk carriers and oil tankers
JUNE 2026 - Q1 GOAL-BASED SHIP CONSTRUCTION STANDARDS (GBS) Question (a) Discuss concept of Goal Based Ship Construction Standard for bulk carriers and oil tankers, emphasizing their significance in enhancing safety and environmental protection. (b) Discuss the implementation process of Goal Based Standard considering the involvement of various stakeholders such as ship designer, shipbuilder and class society. Answer - Part (a) Background: Late 1990s catastrophic structural hull failures in ageing tankers ERICA & PRESTIGE, causing devastating oil spill, highlighted that existing construction standards/law were not ensuring vessel structural integrity over its entire operational life. Concept: Goal Based Ship Construction Standard was a fundamental shift in IMO regulatory philosophy from traditional prescriptive rules (dictating exact dimensions, scantlings and materials) to a goal-oriented, performance-based framework. GBS sets high-level safety and environmental objectives, allowing flexibility in how those objectives are met and encouraging innovation in design and construction methods. Specific international GBS for bulk carriers and tankers were adopted by IMO through:  Resolution MSC.287(87) - Tier 1 & Tier 2.  Resolution MSC.290(87) - new SOLAS regulation II-1/3-10, making it mandatory. Application of Goal Based Standard:  Oil tankers and bulk carriers of 150 m length and above.  Ships for which the building contract is placed on or after 1 July 2016. Text transcription - June 2026 Q1 GBS GBS - Five-Tier System Tier 1 - Goals (High Level) Ship design and construction to be safe and environmentally friendly throughout its specified design life, in both intact and specified damage conditions. Tier 2 - Functional Requirements (Detailed Performance Criteria) Set of requirements to meet the above goal. Design requirements  25-year design life.  North Atlantic environment.  25-year fatigue life in North Atlantic.  Watertight and weathertight integrity.  Protection against corrosion; coating life.  Structural redundancy.  Human element considerations. Construction requirements  Construction quality procedure.  Survey. In-service requirements  Survey and maintenance.  Structural accessibility. Recycling requirement  Recycling. Tier 2 specifies the features a ship must have to achieve Tier 1 goals. Tier 3 - Verification of Conformity Procedure for independent verification of classification society rules or national rules meant to actually deliver the Tier 1 and Tier 2 requirements. Text transcription - June 2026 Q1 GBS Tier 4 - Rules & Regulations for Ship Design and Construction (Blueprint)  Developed by IMO, Administration and/or RO / Classification Society.  Applied by National Administration and/or RO acting on its behalf to the design and construction of the ship.  Tier 4 rules are verified by the IMO audit team (Tier 3) to ensure Tier 1 and Tier 2 are achieved. Tier 5 - Industry Practice & Standards Practical, ground-level implementation of the rules and regulations in Tier 4 on an everyday basis in the shipyard, to achieve Tier 1 and Tier 2. Tier 5 dictates how workers physically perform to ensure the goal is achieved. Areas covered include:  Welding quality.  Steel alignment.  Surface preparation. Significance of GBS in Enhancing Safety & Environmental Protection  Harmonized IACS rules verified by IMO, also known as Common Structural Rules for bulk carriers and oil tankers; minimum structural requirements to build a safe hull in BC & OT must be complied with.  Explicit consideration of residual strength after collision, grounding or flooding, reducing the risk of collapse and total loss.  Mandates better accessibility for inspection and maintenance, improving long-term safety.  Structural redundancy is mandatory: if a single structural member fails, surrounding structure can absorb the redistributed weight and stress.  Ship Construction File (SCF): mandatory file containing critical design information that can be accessed by ship crew, surveyors and shipyard personnel. Text transcription - June 2026 Q1 GBS Significance for Environmental Protection  Probability of structural failure reduces, so the vessel is less prone to cargo or bunker pollution.  Material and construction methods to be in compliance with the Hong Kong Convention for recycling.  GBS has given IMO direct oversight of the safety and pollution-prevention performance of the vessel. Answer - Part (b): Implementation Process Implementation of GBS is happening through the five-tier hierarchy: Step 1 - IMO establishes the baseline IMO establishes the baseline through Tier 1 and Tier 2. Step 2 - Verification and alignment The technical rulebook created by the Flag State Administration or RO, i.e. Classification Society, is officially audited by the IMO GBS audit team before ground work starts. Step 3 - Engineering & design validation Using the approved rules, the naval architect and ship designers create the physical blueprint of the vessel, integrating Tier 1 and Tier 2 requirements. Step 4 - Physical construction and quality control The shipyard carries out the ground-level implementation (Tier 5), adhering to verified industry workmanship and ship standards. Text transcription - June 2026 Q1 GBS Step 5 - Completion of Ship Construction File (SCF) During and upon completion of the build, all structural data, as-built drawings and critical-area maps are archived in the Ship Construction File. The same is maintained throughout the operational life of the ship and is used by the classification society and Flag State during survey and repair. Involvement of Various Stakeholders Stakeholder Role / Involvement IMO Sets goals; sets functional requirements; appoints auditors; grants final approval to rules made by Flag State Administration or RO/Class Society. Flag State Administration Implements SOLAS II-1/3-10; authorizes RO; develops/verifies own national rules. RO / Classification Society Develops detailed technical rules meeting GBS; surveys during construction and in service; acts as the technical bridge between IMO goals and the shipbuilder. Ship Designer / Naval Architect Translates class rules into actual ship design; demonstrates rule compliance with GBS requirements. Shipyard Ground-level implementation of the approved ship design and rules; quality control over materials, welding, alignment and coating application. Shipowner Ensures the SCF is properly prepared, maintained and retained on board. Insurers Direct beneficiary of reduced structural risk and lower insurance premium. Note: This document is a typed transcription of the handwritten source. The wording has been preserved as closely as legible; it is not a technical verification or correction of the notes. Text transcription - June 2026 Q1 GBS

2024-10-OCT-Q5 · October 2024 Q5 — FAL Convention: objectives, implementation and digitalisation
FAL CONVENTION Convention on Facilitation of International Maritime Traffic, 1965 EXAM QUESTIONS FROM THE NOTES Q1. Discuss the key provisions and objectives of the Convention on Facilitation of International Maritime Traffic (FAL Convention). Briefly discuss the impact of the FAL Convention on enhancing efficiency and security in international maritime traffic. Discuss the significance of the FAL Convention in promoting global cooperation and standardisation in maritime trade. Q2. Discuss the recent amendments to the FAL Convention and analyse their impact on modern shipping operations, particularly in relation to digitalisation and sustainability. CORE IDEA The FAL Convention addresses red-tapism - the administrative burden created by excessive paperwork - with the aim of preventing unnecessary delay to ships, persons and property on board. Adopted: April 1965 | Entered into force: March 1967 Objectives of the FAL Convention ●Prevent unnecessary delay to ships, persons and property during ship/port operations. ●Achieve the highest practicable degree of uniformity in formalities, documentary requirements and procedures in global marine transport. ●Simplify and reduce formalities, documentary requirements and procedures worldwide. ●Promote cooperation among governments and agencies so that the same information need not be repeatedly submitted to different authorities. Exam memory line FAL = Facilitation through standardisation, simplification, cooperation and reduced delay. FAL Convention - MEO Class I Revision Note Page 1 Structure of the Convention As recorded in the uploaded notes: Convention 16 Articles and one Annex Articles General obligations and scope Annex Contains mandatory Standards and Recommended Practices; the notes state the Annex has 7 sections. When a Government The notes indicate that the Government should notify cannot comply with a IMO. mandatory Standard Seven Sections of the Annex - as listed in the notes ● Definitions. ● Arrival, stay and departure of ships. ● Arrival, stay and departure of persons. ● Stowaways. ● Arrival, stay and departure of cargo. ● Public health and quarantine. ● Miscellaneous provisions. IMO FAL Forms - 7 FAL Form 1 IMO General Declaration FAL Form 2 Cargo Declaration FAL Form 3 Ship's Stores Declaration FAL Form 4 Crew's Effects Declaration FAL Form 5 Crew List FAL Form 6 Passenger List FAL Form 7 Dangerous Goods Manifest / list Three additional declarations/information items noted ● Security-related information under SOLAS Chapter XI-2 - e.g. ISSC and current security level. ● Advance cargo information for Customs risk assessment. ● Advance notification form for waste delivery to port reception facility. FAL Convention - MEO Class I Revision Note Page 2 Impact of FAL on Efficiency and Security EFFICIENCY SECURITY ● Standardised IMO FAL forms reduce ● Risk-based, data-driven control allows bureaucratic delay and repeated port authorities and Customs to use paperwork. advance digital information for ● Maritime Single Window (MSW) enables screening. one-time electronic submission and ● Automated risk assessment helps access by the relevant agencies, authorities target high-risk ships, cargo reducing duplication and redundant and passengers for physical inspection. paperwork. ● Low-risk traffic can be cleared faster, ● Recognising crew as key workers which supports both security and supports accelerated visa/immigration efficient port operations. processing and access to welfare and shore leave. Simplified Information Flow MARITIME SINGLE AUTHORITIES WINDOW SHIP → → Customs - Immigration - Port one-time data - Health - Police submission ANSWERING THE EXAMINER'S 'HOW DOES FAL IMPROVE BOTH?' Efficiency: less paperwork, faster clearance, one-time data submission and reduced administrative burden. Security: better advance information, risk assessment, targeted inspection and coordinated enforcement. Balance: FAL seeks faster legitimate maritime traffic without removing necessary border, port, customs or security controls. FAL Convention - MEO Class I Revision Note Page 3 Significance in Global Cooperation and Standardisation GLOBAL COOPERATION ● Aids Government cooperation: multiple Governments and authorities work together for smoother maritime trade. ● Broad international coverage: the notes emphasise that FAL covers many countries, supporting common procedures across borders. ● Continuous implementation review: the FAL Committee meets regularly to review implementation, exchange ideas and update rules. ● International cooperation: common procedures reduce inconsistency and administrative friction between ports and States. ● Protection of maritime workers: faster clearance and access to medical care are highlighted in the notes. STANDARDISATION ● Global uniformity in documentation through standard forms and harmonised information requirements. ● Limits unnecessary paperwork through standardised data requirements. ● Maritime Single Window supports one electronic portal for ship data. ● Mandatory Standards establish minimum common rules that Contracting Governments are expected to follow. ● Recommended Practices encourage progressive improvement beyond the minimum level. Chief idea for a 16-mark answer: FAL converts many different national clearance practices into a more uniform international system. This reduces duplication while preserving the right of authorities to apply necessary controls. FAL Convention - MEO Class I Revision Note Page 4 Recent Amendments - Converted from the Handwritten Notes The points below reproduce the content and framing of the uploaded notes. They are not independently verified in this conversion. 2022 AMENDMENTS ● Resolution FAL.14(46) - the notes state entry into force on 1 January 2024. ● Maritime Single Window (MSW) made mandatory for all Members / Contracting Governments as recorded in the notes. ● India example in the notes: use of the Sagar Setu module launched by the Shipping Ministry. ● Health emergency response: seafarers and port workers recognised as 'key workers'. ● Anti-corruption measures: Member States to assess risks and take preventive action to improve integrity and accountability. 2023 AMENDMENTS ● Resolution FAL.15(47) - the notes state entry into force on 1 January 2025. ● Tighter coordination and risk-based controls to combat illegal smuggling and wildlife trafficking. ● The notes refer to Recommended Practice 7.11. ● Examples given: non-intrusive X-ray scanning of containers and use of threat-detection software with Customs to trigger alerts for suspicious cargo routes. 2026 DEVELOPMENT MENTIONED IN THE NOTES ● FAL 50 draft / development - marked in the notes as not in force. ● Cybersecurity for digital platforms / Maritime Single Window. ● Facilitation of crew changes and medical care in emergencies. 2025-10-OCT-Q7 · October 2025 Q7 — III Code objectives, flag State performance and IMSAS

MEO Class I - III Code Q7 | Structured from the supplied DGMA model-answer notes Page 2 Q7 - IMO INSTRUMENTS IMPLEMENTATION CODE (III CODE) Exam-style text answer based on the supplied DGMA model-answer PDF FULL QUESTION (clean transcription from the handwritten source) As per IMO III Code: (a) Briefly discuss the objective and strategy recommended to Flag State, and state the IMO instruments covered in this Code. (b) What are the key performance indicators for evaluation of Flag State performance? (c) Discuss Flag State, Port State and Coastal State responsibilities. INTRODUCTION / MODEL-ANSWER NOTE The supplied notes state that the IMO Instruments Implementation Code (III Code) was adopted by IMO Resolution A.1070(28) and became mandatory from 1 January 2016 through amendments in SOLAS, MARPOL and the Load Line Convention. The answer below follows the content and framing of the supplied model answer. (a) OBJECTIVE OF THE III CODE IMO develops safety rules and environmental-protection rules through conventions and instruments such as SOLAS, MARPOL, STCW and the Load Line Convention. These requirements must be put into practice by each country according to the role it performs - Flag State, Port State or Coastal State - and the conventions it has agreed to join by ratification/approval. The III Code recognizes that each State has different responsibilities depending on its role: the Flag State for ships flying its flag, the Port State for ships visiting its ports, and the Coastal State for ships navigating near its coastline. A State can perform more than one of these roles. STRATEGY RECOMMENDED TO THE STATE 1. Develop strategy - prepare a clear plan to meet international duties and responsibilities as Flag State, Port State and Coastal State. The supplied notes link this to integrating IMO instruments into the State's domestic law. 2. Strategy monitoring - build a system to monitor whether the strategy is actually working; in other words, whether the international rules adopted/written into domestic law are in force in practice. 3. Review and improve strategy - no strategy is complete; it should be reviewed and continuously improved so that the State improves how it performs its Flag, Port and Coastal State duties. MEO Class I - III Code Q7 | Structured from the supplied DGMA model-answer notes Page 3 Q7 - PART (a) CONTINUED & PART (b) III Code instruments, Flag-State KPIs and IMSAS IMO INSTRUMENTS COVERED IN THE SUPPLIED MODEL ANSWER 1. SOLAS - International Convention for the Safety of Life at Sea. 2. MARPOL - International Convention for the Prevention of Pollution from Ships. 3. International Convention on Load Lines. 4. International Convention on Tonnage Measurement of Ships. 5. STCW - International Convention on Standards of Training, Certification and Watchkeeping for Seafarers. 6. COLREG, 1972 - Convention on the International Regulations for Preventing Collisions at Sea. (b) KEY PERFORMANCE INDICATORS FOR FLAG-STATE PERFORMANCE 1. Port State Control detention rates. 2. Flag-State inspection results. 3. Casualty statistics. 4. Communication and reporting to IMO, including incidents. 5. Annual loss / total loss of ship. 6. Cases of incompetence or misconduct of certified personnel. IMSAS - IMO MEMBER STATE AUDIT SCHEME The supplied notes state that State-performance data are shared/submitted to IMO and are analysed under IMSAS. Member States are audited to check their legal framework, enforcement capability and resources. • The country follows the requirements of the III Code. • The country has integrated IMO conventions into its national law. • National law is properly enforced and implemented. • Where the country delegates functions to a Recognized Organization (Class Society), it maintains effective control over that delegation. • The country monitors and supervises its own survey and certification system as well as the Recognized Organization system. MEO Class I - III Code Q7 | Structured from the supplied DGMA model-answer notes Page 4 Q7 - PART (c): STATE RESPONSIBILITIES Flag State vs Port State vs Coastal State - model-answer structure FLAG STATE RESPONSIBILITIES 1. Registration of ships. 2. Issue national law and guidance so that international safety and pollution-prevention conventions are enforced on ships flying its flag. 3. Carry out ship audits/inspections and monitor the Recognized Organization (Class Society) authorized for surveys and certification. 4. Appoint Flag-State surveyors for Flag-State inspections and conduct investigations into maritime casualties. 5. Carry out certification of seafarers and training with the help of approved departments and institutes. 6. A ship flies the flag of the country/Flag State and therefore must comply with the laws of that Flag State/country. India example from the supplied notes: DGS/DGMA (Directorate General of Shipping) carries out responsibilities on the ground through MMD (Mercantile Marine Department) offices at major ports across India. PORT STATE RESPONSIBILITIES 1. Provide adequate reception facilities for ships visiting its ports, as required under IMO conventions/instruments. 2. Keep a register of bunker/fuel suppliers operating in the port. 3. Under the Port State Control programme, inspect foreign ships to check compliance with IMO instruments relating to safety and pollution prevention, as per the applicable MOU. 4. Train and ensure PSC officers are qualified and are free from pressure that could affect the fairness of an inspection. India example in source: Port-State duties are shared among DGS/DGMA, MMD, Port Trust/Port Authority, Port Health Officer, Customs and Immigration authorities. COASTAL STATE RESPONSIBILITIES 1. Provide essential maritime services to ships passing the coast, including weather warnings, Search and Rescue, hydrographic services, Vessel Traffic Services and aids to navigation. 2. Ensure ships passing the coast can navigate safely and without causing environmental problems. 3. The supplied notes use India as an example and indicate that coastal-State duties are shared among relevant maritime, coast-guard, navigation and meteorological authorities. MEO Class I - III Code Q7 | Structured from the supplied DGMA model-answer notes Page 5 EXAM-READY RECALL SHEET Use this page for last-minute MEO Class I revision 1. OBJECTIVE Ensure IMO safety and environmental instruments are effectively put into practice by States according to their Flag, Port and Coastal State roles. 2. STRATEGY Develop strategy -> Monitor implementation -> Review and improve continuously. 3. KPI PSC detention rate; Flag-State inspection results; casualty statistics; IMO reporting; annual/total ship loss; incompetence/misconduct of certified personnel. 4. IMSAS Audits legal framework, enforcement capability/resources, III Code implementation, national-law integration, control of RO delegation and survey/certification oversight. ORAL CLOSING LINE “III Code is about effective implementation: the State must legislate, enforce, monitor and continuously improve its Flag, Port and Coastal State responsibilities.” MEO Class I - III Code Q7 | Structured from the supplied DGMA model-answer notes Page 6
